===== ICLE-US-IND-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong personal engagement with the topic and a clear moral position. You care deeply about environmental harm and corporate accountability, and that passion gives the essay energy. For revision, focus on sharpening your argument, improving organization, and strengthening your evidence and style.

Clarify and focus your thesis
Right now, the essay opens with “Money has been said to be the root of all evil,” but the paper is really about Exxon’s responsibility for the Exxon Valdez spill and the need for corporate accountability. Your main claim should say that directly.

A stronger thesis might be:
The Exxon Valdez disaster shows how corporate negligence and poor emergency planning can devastate ecosystems and communities, which is why large corporations must be held accountable through stricter safety standards and environmental reparations.

This gives your essay a clearer purpose than the broad statement about money and evil.

Improve organization
The essay has strong ideas, but they sometimes repeat or appear out of order. Try organizing it into clear parts:

Suggested structure
Introduction
Briefly introduce the Exxon Valdez spill
State your argument about corporate negligence and accountability

Body Paragraph 1: The disaster and its impact
Explain what happened in March 1989
Describe environmental and human consequences

Body Paragraph 2: Exxon’s response
Show how the response was slow and ineffective
Explain why the public reacted so negatively

Body Paragraph 3: Corporate responsibility
Discuss maintenance, planning, and safety failures
Explain what Exxon should have done differently

Conclusion
Restate why Exxon should be held accountable
Suggest broader lessons for environmental policy and business regulation

This structure will help readers follow your reasoning more easily.

Use stronger evidence and analysis
You mention important points—slow response, captain error, ship maintenance, long-term harm—but many of these claims need more specific support. For example:
What evidence shows Exxon’s response was “slow, disorganized and unacceptable”?
What court records or published notes show maintenance problems?
What specific impacts did the spill have on wildlife, fisheries, and Native communities?

Instead of only stating that the damage was “devastating” or “unbelievable,” include a few concrete facts. This will make your argument more persuasive.

Also, avoid relying too much on emotion alone. Your anger is understandable, but in an argumentative essay, readers are persuaded most by facts plus analysis. After presenting evidence, explain how it supports your claim about negligence or accountability.

Reduce repetition
Several ideas are repeated:
Exxon is a large corporation
The spill was devastating
You were angry at Exxon

You do not need to say these points multiple times. Instead, say them once clearly and then build on them with new information. Repetition can weaken the impact of your strongest points.

Maintain a more academic tone
The essay often uses personal and emotional language, such as:
“I loathed the corporation”
“rape our land”
“catastrophe beyond belief”

These phrases express strong feeling, but they can make the essay sound more like a personal reaction than an academic argument. Try using a more formal tone:
“I loathed the corporation” → “I was deeply disturbed by the corporation’s actions.”
“rape our land” → “severely damage the environment.”

Also, the first-person perspective (“I feel,” “I watched,” “I grew angry”) could be reduced unless the assignment specifically invites personal response. The paper will sound stronger if it emphasizes the issue rather than your feelings about it.

Fix sentence-level issues
There are some grammar and wording problems that should be revised for clarity:
“I personally witnessed first hand” → “I witnessed firsthand”
“Prince William sound” → “Prince William Sound”
“the native people of Alaska as well” → could be more specific, such as “Alaska Native communities”
“the Captain in charge the vessel” → “the captain in charge of the vessel”
“the grounding the Exxon” → “the grounding of the Exxon Valdez”
“monies” → usually better as “funds”
“The publishing of Exxon's notes and court transcriptions, prove” → subject-verb agreement issue; better: “The publication of Exxon’s notes and court transcripts proves...”

There is also a placeholder in the essay: “.” This needs to be replaced with the missing quotation or removed.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion has a clear message: corporations should be accountable. That is a good ending. To make it stronger, connect it directly back to the Exxon Valdez case and your thesis. Emphasize the broader lesson: environmental disasters caused by negligence require both compensation and prevention.

Revision priorities
Write a more precise thesis.
Reorganize the essay into clear body paragraphs.
Add specific factual evidence.
Reduce repetition and emotional overstatement.
Edit for grammar, clarity, and formal tone.

Your essay has a strong foundation because the issue matters to you and your position is clear. With more focused argumentation and evidence, it can become much more convincing and effective.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear moral claim—crime may seem profitable at first, but it ultimately destroys lives—and that gives the paper a strong central message. The personal example also helps make your argument concrete and emotionally powerful. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your thesis, organization, development, and sentence-level correctness.

Main strength: clear position
Your essay clearly argues that people who try to “get rich quick” through crime suffer serious consequences. The story about the man involved in real-estate fraud gives your essay a specific case that supports your point. This is much stronger than speaking only in general terms.

Improve the thesis
Right now, your main idea is repeated several times (“crime does not pay”), but the thesis is not presented in a precise, argumentative way early in the essay.

A stronger thesis should:
make a clear claim,
suggest the reasons you will discuss,
avoid vague wording.

For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although crime may offer quick money, it does not truly pay because it leads to punishment, destroys families, and ruins a person’s future.*

This gives your essay direction and helps readers know what to expect.

Strengthen organization
Your essay currently moves from general comments about crime to one personal example, then to a conclusion. The basic structure is there, but the introduction is somewhat repetitive and unclear in places.

Suggested structure:
Introduction
Introduce the idea that some people pursue wealth illegally.
Explain why this is tempting.
End with a clear thesis.

Body Paragraph 1
Explain why some people turn to crime for money.
Be careful not to generalize too broadly.

Body Paragraph 2
Present the real-estate fraud example in a focused way.
Show how the crime appeared successful at first.

Body Paragraph 3
Analyze the consequences: arrest, loss of money, damage to family, ruined reputation.
Connect these consequences directly to your thesis.

Conclusion
Restate the argument in fresh words.
Emphasize that short-term gain leads to long-term loss.

Develop analysis more fully
The example is effective, but much of the paragraph is narrative summary. To strengthen the essay as an argument, add more analysis after the story. Explain how the example proves that crime does not pay.

For instance, after describing the arrest, you could discuss:
He lost his freedom.
His family lost financial security.
His reputation was destroyed.
The money he gained did not last.
Emotional consequences were as damaging as legal ones.

This kind of explanation turns a story into evidence.

Clarify and refine your language
Some sentences are hard to follow because of grammar, missing words, or awkward phrasing. There are also several spelling errors. Careful editing will make your argument more credible.

Examples of issues to correct:
“societies rules” → *society’s rules*
“expectable” → probably *acceptable* or *normal*
“see and easier way” → *see an easier way*
“catigory” → *category*
“not being will” → *not being willing*
“way very proud” → *was very proud*
“The one day” → *Then one day*
“beleive” → *believe*
“families money” → *family’s money*

Also watch for comma splices and sentence openings. For example:

Original:  
*Crime does not pay, is a very true statement.*

Better:  
*The statement “crime does not pay” is true.*

Maintain an academic tone
Your paper uses phrases like “I know several people” and “at least not in my mind.” First-person can be acceptable in some assignments, especially when using personal experience, but try to keep the tone more formal and focused.

For example:
Instead of “I know several people who fit in this category”, say  
  *Some people choose illegal ways of making money because they want quick results.*
Instead of “at least not in my mind”, say  
  *From an outside perspective, he had no financial need to commit fraud.*

Avoid overgeneralization
Some of your opening claims are too broad, such as “Most of the people that turn to crime…” Unless you have evidence, avoid making large claims about “most people.” You can revise with more cautious language:

*Some people turn to crime because…*
*In some cases, individuals are influenced by…*

This sounds more accurate and more academic.

Final revision priorities
When revising, focus on these steps in order:
Write a sharper thesis.
Reorganize the essay around clear body paragraphs.
Add analysis explaining why the example proves your claim.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, punctuation, and word choice.

Overall
This essay has a strong core idea and a compelling example, but it needs clearer structure and more polished language to make the argument fully effective. With revision, you can turn this into a much stronger argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting and important argument: pay reflects what a society truly values, and child care exposes a gap between stated beliefs about equality and actual social priorities. The paper has a thoughtful core idea, but it would be stronger with a clearer thesis, more precise organization, and more direct explanation of how each paragraph advances the argument.

What the essay does well

It addresses a complex issue thoughtfully.  
You move beyond the simple claim that “equal work deserves equal pay” and ask a deeper question about how society defines value. That shift gives the essay intellectual depth.

The child care example is effective.  
Focusing on child care gives your argument a concrete case through which to explore larger social and economic values.

The tone is appropriately academic.  
Your language is formal and analytical, which suits argumentative writing.

Main areas to revise

Clarify your thesis early
The opening is interesting, but the central claim remains somewhat indirect. Readers need a precise thesis that tells them exactly what you are arguing.

For example, your essay seems to argue something like:  
American society claims to value equality and children, but the low wages of child care workers reveal that society does not truly value caregiving labor, even though it is essential to the economy.

A thesis like this would help unify the essay and guide the reader.

Improve paragraph focus and progression
Right now, some paragraphs contain several large ideas at once, and the logic sometimes feels compressed. Try to make each paragraph do one clear job:

Paragraph 1: Introduce the issue and present your thesis.
Paragraph 2: Explain how pay and status reflect social value.
Paragraph 3: Use child care as evidence of the mismatch between declared values and actual rewards.
Paragraph 4: Discuss why this matters economically and socially.
Conclusion: Reinforce the argument and suggest implications.

This structure would make the essay easier to follow.

Explain your reasoning more directly
At several points, your ideas are strong but underdeveloped. For example, you suggest that child care is essential because it supports a diverse workforce, but you do not fully explain that connection. Spell it out:

Parents cannot participate fully in the workforce without reliable child care.
Therefore, child care workers make other forms of labor possible.
Yet their wages and status remain low.
This contradiction shows that market pay does not always reflect true social importance.

Making each step explicit will strengthen your argument.

Use more concrete support
The essay is mostly conceptual. To make it more persuasive, add specific evidence or examples. You might include:

average wages for child care workers,
comparisons with other professions,
labor force participation of parents,
public spending on education versus child care,
or examples of policy debates about child care access.

Even one or two well-chosen facts would make the argument more convincing.

Sentence-level issues

Watch for awkward or unclear phrasing
Some sentences are difficult to follow because they are too long or abstract. For example:

“The idea that  seems at first to be in harmony...”
  - This opening is incomplete because the phrase after “that” is missing.
“Although it is likely that most Americans, if asked, would profess to the belief...”
  - Better: “Most Americans would probably say that equal work deserves equal pay.”
“including women - of all ages - as well as man with family responsibilities”
  - This should be “men”, not “man.”

Try to prefer direct, clear wording over complicated phrasing.

Edit for grammar and word choice
A few errors distract from the argument:

“pereniel” → “perennial”
“profess to the belief” → “profess the belief” or “claim to believe”
“contribution to the society they live in” is wordy; consider “contribution to society.”
There is an extra space in “without  involving”

Careful proofreading will improve readability.

Manage quotation marks and emphasis
You put quotation marks around terms like “valuable” and “low value.” This can be useful, but overusing quotation marks may make the prose feel uncertain. If these are your own analytical terms, you may not need quotation marks every time.

Suggestions for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one develops one main point.
Expand the explanation of why child care is economically essential.
Add at least 1–2 specific pieces of evidence.
Edit for clarity, grammar, and concision.

Final thought

This essay has a strong underlying insight: a society’s wage structure reveals its true hierarchy of values. That is a compelling argument. Your next step is to present that insight more clearly and concretely so readers can follow and be persuaded by your reasoning from beginning to end.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important and still relevant question: why does society financially reward paid labor while undervaluing caregiving and homemaking? The paper has a clear moral concern and a recognizable argumentative structure. You establish the issue, compare paid and unpaid work, and end by proposing possible solutions. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, strengthening your evidence, and improving precision in style and logic.

Strengths

Clear central issue.  
You identify a real social contradiction: caregiving is essential, yet it is often unpaid and culturally undervalued.

Engaged voice.  
The essay sounds invested in the topic, which helps persuade readers that the issue matters.

Comparative reasoning.  
Your comparison between marketplace jobs and domestic labor is useful because it highlights the difficulty of measuring “value” only in monetary terms.

Main revision priorities

A. Clarify your thesis
Right now, the essay moves between two different claims:

homemaking/caregiving should receive direct financial compensation;  
society should rethink how it defines value and contribution.

These are related, but they are not identical. Your final paragraph presents them as alternatives, which makes the essay feel undecided.

Revise by choosing one main argument, or by clearly stating that the essay will argue both:
unpaid domestic labor should be socially recognized, and
one way to recognize it is through specific economic policy.

For example, a stronger thesis might be:
> Because caregiving and homemaking are essential to social stability and child development, society should recognize their value not only culturally but also materially through policies that compensate or support full-time caregivers.

This gives you a clear position and a direction for body paragraphs.

B. Develop stronger evidence
Much of the essay relies on assertion rather than proof. Statements such as “many of the ills of society” or “study after study” need actual evidence. Without support, readers may see the claims as too broad.

Consider adding:
research on the economic value of unpaid domestic labor;
evidence about childcare costs;
studies on parental caregiving and child development;
examples of countries that provide caregiver stipends, tax credits, paid parental leave, or family allowances.

This would make your argument more persuasive and less dependent on emotional appeal.

C. Address counterarguments
A good argumentative essay should acknowledge objections. For example:
Who would pay homemakers?
How would compensation be measured?
Would paying for caregiving reinforce traditional gender roles?
What about households without children?

If you raise these issues and respond to them, your essay will sound more balanced and intellectually rigorous.

Organization

Your essay generally follows a logical progression, but the paragraphs could be more focused.

Paragraph 1: Good introduction to the issue, but the thesis should be more direct and specific.

Paragraph 2: This paragraph mainly explains that paid careers are socially valued. That works as background, but it could connect more explicitly to your argument about unequal recognition.

Paragraph 3: This is one of your stronger paragraphs because it asks what “equal work” means. However, the comparison to a neurosurgeon needs more careful framing; otherwise, it can seem exaggerated. Rather than arguing that caregiving and neurosurgery are identical, argue that both are socially necessary in different ways.

Paragraph 4: This paragraph introduces social stigma and gender expectations, which is important. You could expand this point with clearer topic sentences and evidence.

Paragraph 5: The conclusion introduces new ideas—paying the couple or family rather than the individual. Because this appears suddenly, it feels underdeveloped. If this is your main solution, introduce and support it earlier.

Style and language

Your style is thoughtful, but some phrasing is dated, wordy, or imprecise.

Watch for outdated or distracting language
“first-world cultures” may sound outdated; consider “wealthier societies” or “industrialized nations.”
“Indian chiefs” is informal and potentially insensitive in this context.
“the at-home female” sounds impersonal and outdated; use “stay-at-home parent” or “full-time caregiver.”

Fix grammar and wording issues
A few examples:
“an neurosurgeon” → “a neurosurgeon”
“this contribution to society in so immense” → “this contribution to society is so immense”
“it, of course, the worker is married” → “if, of course, the worker is married”

Also, some sentences are quite long. Breaking them into shorter units will improve clarity.

Ideas to deepen the argument

You could strengthen the essay by distinguishing between recognition and payment. Social value does not always have to take the form of a salary. You might explore several policy options:
tax credits for caregivers;
paid family leave;
retirement credit for years spent caregiving;
childcare subsidies;
universal basic income or caregiver stipends.

This would make your proposal more practical and nuanced.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, aim to:
write a precise thesis that takes a clear position;
support major claims with evidence, not generalizations;
consider counterarguments and respond to them;
update wording and correct sentence-level errors;
develop your proposed solution earlier and in more detail.

Your essay has a meaningful core argument and a strong ethical concern. With a clearer thesis and better evidence, it could become a much more convincing argumentative paper.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay takes on a difficult proverb and does something promising: instead of accepting the saying “money is the root of all evil,” you interrogate its logic. The strongest aspect of the draft is its ambition. You are trying to expose the conceptual problems in linking money, desire, virtue, happiness, and evil, and that gives the essay intellectual energy. To revise effectively, however, you need a clearer thesis, more controlled organization, and more precise use of terms.

Clarify the central argument

At present, I can see several possible claims:

the saying is logically flawed;
desire for money cannot be measured;
wealth does not correlate neatly with virtue or vice;
history shows that wealth and power can produce either oppression or generosity.

All of these are interesting, but they are not yet unified. Your opening claim—“the saying is meaningless or at least impractical”—seems closest to your true thesis. If that is your argument, state it directly and early:

Possible revised thesis:  
*The proverb “money is the root of all evil” fails as a serious moral principle because it confuses money with desire, assumes virtue can be measured by wealth, and ignores the historical fact that money can serve both generous and destructive ends.*

A thesis like this gives your reader a roadmap.

Define key terms consistently

Much of the essay’s difficulty comes from shifting definitions. You discuss:

money
desire
evil
virtue
happiness

But these terms do not remain stable. For example, happiness is described as a psychological state, then as contentment, then as a counterpart to virtue. That makes the argument hard to follow.

Choose a workable definition for each important term and stick to it. For instance:

Money = a tool or resource, morally neutral in itself.
Desire for money = ambition, greed, or pursuit of security, depending on degree.
Virtue = moral character or ethical action.
Evil = harmful, unjust, or corrupt action.

Once those are clear, your reasoning will be stronger.

Improve organization and paragraph structure

Right now, the essay reads more like a series of reflections than a developed argument. Consider reorganizing into a clearer structure:

Introduction
Present the proverb.
Briefly explain why it seems persuasive.
State your thesis.

Body Paragraph 1: Money is not the same as evil
Argue that if money itself caused evil, poverty should produce virtue, which is clearly false.
Show that moral character exists across classes.

Body Paragraph 2: The real issue is desire, not money
Distinguish between money as an object and greed as a motive.
Explain why desire is difficult to measure and not limited to the rich or poor.

Body Paragraph 3: Historical complexity
Use examples showing that wealth and power have enabled both oppression and philanthropy.
This would support your final point much more effectively.

Conclusion
Reaffirm that the proverb is too simplistic.
Offer a more accurate claim, such as: *unchecked greed, not money itself, contributes to evil.*

Use evidence or examples

The essay gestures toward “the unfolding of history,” but it never gives examples. This weakens your authority. Even one or two concrete references would help. For instance, you might mention:

wealthy philanthropists who used money for public good;
powerful rulers or institutions that used wealth to oppress;
examples of cruelty or corruption among those without wealth.

You do not need many examples, but you do need some.

Refine style for clarity

Your prose has a serious, philosophical tone, which suits the topic, but some sentences are too abstract or grammatically tangled. For example:

“Desire cannot be measured on some utilitarian scale of pleasure.”
“The desire for money would-must-coincide and flourish with a desire for virtue…”

These sentences sound impressive, but they are hard to parse. Aim for shorter, clearer sentences when expressing complex ideas.

Also, avoid rhetorical overloading. Too many dashes, interruptions, and qualifications can obscure your point.

Edit for grammar, spelling, and mechanics

There are several errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:

privelaged → *privileged*
acruement → *accruement* or better *accumulation*
wheighed → *weighed*
serrendipitously → *serendipitously*
“a least” → *at least*
“where in which” → choose one: *where* or *in which*

Also revise punctuation around quotations and dashes. For example, “meaningless-or a least impractical” should be punctuated more cleanly.

Revise the ending

Your conclusion has strong potential, especially this idea: those with wealth and power “could be our saints” but are often “oppressors as [often as] saviors.” That is a compelling insight. Build toward it more directly. Right now, the symbol “” disrupts the flow and makes the ending feel incomplete. Replace it with a proper transition and conclude decisively.

Final recommendation

Your essay has an ambitious intellectual core, but it needs revision for focus, definition, structure, and clarity. The best next step is to rewrite around one clear thesis and support it with a few concrete examples. If you can make your abstract ideas more precise and organized, this could become a thoughtful and persuasive argument.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting question: whether Marx’s principle of social obligation can realistically function in modern Western society. You show awareness of several important tensions—between family and society, religion and secular life, and individual freedom and collective responsibility. To strengthen the paper, focus on clarifying your thesis, developing your argument with evidence, and improving organization and precision.

Clarify the thesis
Right now, the essay seems to move between several claims:
Marx’s idea is natural at the family level.
Western society preserves similar egalitarian values through Christianity.
Applying the principle to society as a whole creates problems of reward, freedom, and relativism.
Therefore, the ideal is admirable but unlikely to succeed in Western culture.

These ideas are related, but the central argument is not stated clearly enough at the start. Your conclusion suggests the real thesis: Marx’s ideal works in small communities such as families but cannot be consistently applied across modern Western society because of individualism, disputes over value, and moral relativism. If that is your position, state it directly in the introduction.

Also, the opening is incomplete because the key quotations are missing (“”). Since your argument depends on Marx and Christianity, you need to include the exact quotations and explain them.

Strengthen the structure
The paper would benefit from a more deliberate argumentative structure. Consider organizing it like this:

Introduction
   - Introduce Marx’s principle.
   - Provide your thesis.

Why the principle seems persuasive
   - Family as a model of need-based support.
   - Christian ethics as a cultural reinforcement of care for others.

Why the principle becomes difficult at the societal level
   - Problem of measuring contribution and reward.
   - Conflict with individual freedom.
   - Moral relativism and lack of shared standards.

Conclusion
   - Reaffirm that the principle is admirable but difficult to implement broadly in Western society.

This structure would help readers follow your reasoning more easily.

Develop each paragraph with evidence and analysis
Many of your claims are broad but not fully supported. For example:
“In Western society, the primary guide is Christianity.”
“The modern person lives in a world where relativism reigns supreme.”
“These avenues are the only paths to tempering rampant moral relativism.”

These are large claims that need support. You could strengthen them by:
Referring to specific teachings, thinkers, or historical examples.
Explaining how Christianity shaped Western moral views.
Defining what you mean by “moral relativism” and showing why it matters here.
Giving examples of policies or social expectations that reflect the tension between individual freedom and collective obligation.

Without evidence, the argument sounds more like opinion than analysis.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay considers opposing views. For example, someone might argue:
Modern societies already practice versions of Marx’s principle through taxation, welfare, public education, and healthcare.
Individualism does not necessarily undermine social responsibility.
Shared civic values can exist even in secular societies.

If you briefly acknowledge these points and explain why you still disagree, your essay will become more persuasive.

Improve precision and tone
At times, the essay uses abstract language without enough specificity. Phrases like “egalitarian goal,” “the concept provided,” and “such an ethic” need clearer reference. Be explicit about what “it” or “this notion” means.

Also, some claims seem overstated. For instance, saying relativism “reigns supreme” may sound too absolute unless you can prove it. A more precise phrase such as “is increasingly influential in some parts of modern Western culture” would sound more academically careful.

Sentence-level and grammar issues
There are several proofreading issues that should be corrected:
“on e” → “one”
“who it to define” → “who is to define”
“primarily form the family” → “primarily from the family”
Extra spacing: “The  religious”
Use “and” instead of “&” in formal academic writing
“one whose ends will unlikely be achieved” would be smoother as “one whose ends are unlikely to be achieved”

Also watch pronoun consistency. You shift among “one,” “I,” and “his/her.” Try to maintain a more consistent academic voice.

Use quotations correctly
Because your essay begins and builds on quotations, the missing quoted material is a major issue. Make sure to:
Include the exact Marx quotation.
Include the New Testament quotation.
Cite the sources properly if required.
Explain how each quotation supports your point instead of assuming the reader will make the connection.

Revision priorities
Add the missing quotations and introduce them properly.
State a clear thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize the body around distinct reasons supporting your thesis.
Support broad claims with examples or evidence.
Address at least one counterargument.
Proofread carefully for grammar, wording, and formal style.

Your essay has a thoughtful central concern and a promising line of argument. With clearer focus and stronger support, it could become a much more convincing and polished argumentative paper.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central question and an interesting controlling idea: whether crime “pays” depends on how we define “pay.” This is a strong argumentative starting point because it moves beyond a simple yes/no answer and introduces a cost-benefit framework. The essay also maintains focus well; nearly every paragraph connects back to the idea of gains versus losses.

What is working well

Clear focus and structure  
You organize the essay logically. The introduction defines the issue, the body paragraphs consider different kinds of costs, and the conclusion returns to the original question. This makes the argument easy to follow.

Strong use of examples  
The opening jewelry-store example is effective because it immediately shows that “profit” is more complicated than the amount stolen. Later examples about time, equipment, injuries, and legal fees continue this practical line of reasoning.

Thoughtful expansion of the term “pay”  
Your best insight is that “pay” includes not only money but also time, physical safety, mental health, and legal consequences. That gives the essay depth and shows analytical thinking.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your thesis is implied rather than sharply stated. You suggest that crime should be judged by comparing gains to multiple forms of loss, but the essay would be stronger if you made your claim explicit near the end of the introduction.

For example, you might revise toward something like:  
“Crime rarely pays because any short-term financial gain is often outweighed by losses in time, money, health, and personal freedom.”

A clearer thesis will help the reader understand exactly what you are arguing.

Strengthen paragraph unity and development
Some body paragraphs introduce good points but do not fully develop them. For instance, the paragraph about time investment raises an important idea—opportunity cost—but could explain it more directly. Why does comparing criminal earnings to legal earnings matter? How does this show that crime often does not pay?

Try giving each body paragraph:
a clear topic sentence,
one main type of “cost,”
explanation of how that cost weakens the profitability of crime.

This will make the argument more systematic.

Clarify tone and word choice
At times, the essay uses language that sounds casual or unintentionally humorous, especially in the final sentence: “However, he does better the legitimate businessman on one count: no taxes.” This ending undercuts the seriousness of the essay and also contains a grammatical error (“better than the legitimate businessman”).

If you want to keep the irony, make it sharper and more formal. For example:  
“The only apparent financial advantage is the avoidance of taxes, but that advantage is minor compared with the risks of injury, prosecution, and imprisonment.”

Address counterargument more directly
Your conclusion says, “In some cases it may,” which is a good acknowledgment that crime can produce short-term rewards. However, this point deserves fuller treatment. A stronger argument often considers the opposing view: yes, some crimes may be profitable in the short term, but long-term consequences usually outweigh those gains.

Adding a brief counterargument paragraph or expanding the conclusion would make the essay more persuasive.

Style and sentence-level concerns

Grammar and mechanics
There are several errors that should be corrected in revision:

“equiptment” → “equipment”
“get-away cars” → usually “getaway cars”
“monies earned” → “money earned” is more natural
“lessen both a person's life span” → “shorten a person’s lifespan” is more precise
“he does better the legitimate businessman” → “he does better than the legitimate businessman”

Pronoun consistency and inclusivity
You repeatedly refer to the criminal as “he” or “his.” In academic writing, it is usually better to avoid assuming gender. You might use:
“the criminal”
“they” / “their”
“a person”

Diction and formality
Phrases like “small businessman criminal” and “Larger criminal corporations” are vivid, but they may oversimplify or sound awkward. If you use the business comparison, try to make it more precise and controlled throughout the essay.

Suggested revision priorities

Write a direct thesis that states your main claim clearly.  
Reorganize body paragraphs so each focuses on one kind of cost: financial, time/opportunity, health, legal consequences.  
Develop explanations more fully instead of listing examples.  
Revise the conclusion so it reinforces your argument in a serious, polished way.  
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and word choice.

Final comment

This essay has a solid foundation and an original angle. Your cost-benefit approach is effective and gives you a strong framework for argument. The next step is to make the thesis sharper, deepen the analysis in each paragraph, and polish the language so the essay sounds more formal and persuasive. With revision, this could become a clear and compelling argument that crime does not truly “pay” once all costs are considered.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central position: money itself is not evil; the love of money is what causes harm. This is a strong argumentative foundation, and you maintain that position consistently throughout the essay. The piece also shows thoughtful moral reflection and uses examples from family life, crime, and society to support your view.

What is working well

Clear stance
From the opening, you take a definite position and do not drift away from it. That gives the essay a strong sense of purpose.

Logical contrast
Your main distinction between money as a practical necessity and greed as a destructive force is effective. This contrast is the core of the essay and gives it structure.

Range of examples
You refer to individuals, families, companies, and nations. This broadens the discussion and shows that you are thinking beyond a single situation.

---

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis and introduction
Your opening begins abruptly with “This idea is completely erroneous,” but the reader does not yet know exactly what “this idea” is. State the issue directly and present your thesis in one clear sentence.

Possible revision:
> Some people argue that money is the root of all evil, but this view is mistaken. Money is a necessary tool for daily life; it is the excessive love of money, not money itself, that leads to selfishness, corruption, and suffering.

This would make your argument clearer from the start.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay reads as a series of related thoughts rather than a carefully structured argument. Try organizing it into clear paragraphs with a specific purpose:

Introduction: present the debate and thesis
Body paragraph 1: explain why money is necessary
Body paragraph 2: explain how greed distorts healthy needs
Body paragraph 3: give examples of the damage caused by the love of money
Conclusion: restate the argument and its significance

This structure will make your reasoning easier to follow.

Develop evidence more fully
Many of your points are persuasive, but they remain general. For example, when you mention families breaking apart, companies failing, or nations crumbling, try to explain how greed causes those outcomes. You do not necessarily need formal research in this piece, but you do need more concrete explanation.

For instance, instead of saying:
> “This love of money urges them on...”

you could expand:
> “When people value wealth above relationships or ethics, they may neglect their families, exploit coworkers, or engage in fraud and theft. In this way, greed damages both personal lives and society.”

That kind of explanation makes the argument more convincing.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrasing is conversational or exaggerated for an academic essay, such as:
“How many times have we seen on the evening news...”
“the ‘right’ car”
“love affair with money”

These expressions are vivid, but a more formal style would strengthen the argument.

For example:
Replace rhetorical questions with statements.
Avoid emotionally loaded phrases unless you explain them carefully.
Use precise wording such as materialism, greed, financial obsession, corruption.

Clarify overstatements
Your final sentence says that the love of money “is truly the root of all evil.” That is a very broad claim and may weaken your credibility because not all evil clearly comes from greed. Consider softening this to something more defensible, such as:
> “The excessive love of money is a major cause of many forms of personal and social harm.”

This still supports your argument without overstating it.

---

Sentence-level issues to correct

There are several grammar, punctuation, capitalization, and word-choice problems that need attention. A few examples:

“all together” → altogether
“capitivates” → captivates
“is money the root of all evil?” → Is money the root of all evil?
“It can be sued for good.” → It can be used for good.
“it’s needs met” → its needs met
“4-5 year-old” → 4- or 5-year-old
“he begins” → capitalize if starting a sentence; also consider gender-neutral language such as a child begins
Avoid “&” in formal writing; use and

Also watch comma use. For example:
> “Money perhaps, is a necessary evil...”
would be better as
> “Money, perhaps, is a necessary evil...”
or more simply
> “Money is a necessary part of daily life.”

---

Final advice for revision

As you revise, focus on three priorities:

Write a direct thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize into clear paragraphs with one main idea each.
Support each claim with fuller explanation and a more formal style.

You already have a solid central idea. With better structure, more precise language, and careful editing, this essay could become a much stronger and more persuasive argument.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0009.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises a provocative question—whether the saying “crime does not pay” is actually true—and it does so with clear skepticism and several concrete examples. The central strength of the draft is that it challenges a familiar moral cliché rather than simply repeating it. That gives the essay argumentative energy. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, strengthening your logic, and improving organization and precision.

Clarify the thesis
Your conclusion suggests the real claim: the phrase is “wishful thinking” rather than a truism because crime can benefit criminals and others. This is an interesting argument, but it appears too late and is not stated precisely enough at the beginning.

Consider making the thesis explicit in the introduction. For example:
*Although the phrase “crime does not pay” functions as a moral warning, it is misleading because some crimes produce material, social, or institutional rewards for offenders and for the systems built around punishment.*

This version clarifies what “pay” means and previews your main lines of argument.

Define key terms
A major issue is ambiguity. What does “pay” mean?
financial profit?
reduced punishment compared with the gain?
fame or restored status?
benefits to institutions such as prisons?

You move among these meanings without clearly marking the shift. For instance, murder “paying” because a person spends “only” 15 years in prison is not the same kind of payoff as a financial scam yielding profits. Likewise, crime paying for the prison industry is different from crime paying for the criminal.

To strengthen the essay, define the term early and perhaps divide the argument:
crime may pay for some offenders,
crime may pay for elites more often than ordinary people,
crime may pay for institutions that profit from punishment.

That structure would make the essay more coherent.

Improve logical development
The essay has a strong central question—“If crime does not pay, then why does it persist?”—but the reasoning after that needs more care.

The claim that “behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist” is too broad as stated. Crime may persist for many reasons besides straightforward payoff: desperation, addiction, coercion, poor judgment, lack of alternatives, or low perceived risk. If you keep this point, qualify it. For example:
*One reason crime persists is that, for some offenders, the perceived rewards outweigh the likely costs.*

That is more defensible and fits your evidence better.

Similarly, the paragraph about murder risks sounding morally blunt because it frames killing in cost-benefit terms without enough nuance. If you want to argue that penalties may fail to deter, say so directly rather than implying murder can be “cost effective.”

Strengthen evidence and accuracy
Your examples are vivid, but some need stronger support or more precise handling.

“Michael Milliken” appears to refer to Michael Milken; be careful with names and factual accuracy.
Calling Richard Nixon “certainly a criminal” may be rhetorically forceful, but academically it would be stronger to specify what you mean: abuse of power, obstruction, or violation of public trust.
The prison industry paragraph would benefit from evidence rather than generalization. Phrases like “states now compete” and “for most” need support.
The statistic about convicted murderers spending 15 years in prison should be cited or framed more cautiously.

In revision, either add evidence or use more limited wording such as “in some cases” or “according to X.”

Organize body paragraphs more clearly
Right now, the essay develops by association rather than by a clear argumentative sequence. A stronger structure might be:

Paragraph 1: introduce the phrase and conventional meaning.
Paragraph 2: argue that some offenders still benefit because rewards can exceed penalties.
Paragraph 3: show that wealthy or powerful offenders often recover status or retain gains.
Paragraph 4: argue that institutions and local economies may also profit from crime and incarceration.
Conclusion: restate that the slogan is morally useful but factually unreliable.

This would help readers follow the progression of your argument.

Refine tone and style
Your writing is generally fluent, but some sentences are wordy or awkward. A few examples:
“with some satisfaction when he reads” could be shortened.
“Parents of would-be miscreants” sounds overly loaded and slightly sarcastic.
“which, after, not only saves money” should be “which, after all, not only saves money.”

Be especially careful with tone when discussing serious crimes. A more measured academic voice will make the argument more persuasive.

Consider a counterargument
The essay would be stronger if you acknowledged why people still use the phrase. For example, you might concede that:
many criminals do suffer severe losses,
the phrase serves as moral instruction,
even when crime appears profitable, long-term social and personal costs may remain high.

Then explain why, despite this, the saying is still misleading as a universal truth. Addressing a counterargument will make your position more sophisticated.

Revision priorities
State a precise thesis in the introduction.
Define what “pay” means and keep categories distinct.
Tighten logic, especially around persistence and deterrence.
Replace broad claims with supported, accurate evidence.
Reorganize body paragraphs around clear points.
Add a brief counterargument.

Overall, this draft has a strong idea and clear argumentative potential. With a more precise thesis, better evidence, and sharper organization, it could become a compelling critique of a common moral slogan.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: people should be financially rewarded for individual effort. The draft has a consistent point of view and a logical general structure from past to present to hypothetical future. You also show engagement with the topic by trying to explain *why* reward matters, not just stating that it does. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your argument, improving precision, and correcting sentence-level issues.

Main strengths

Clear stance:  
Your position is evident from the introduction and remains consistent throughout the essay.

Logical progression:  
You move from early human survival, to modern society, to a hypothetical equal-outcome society. This gives the essay an understandable structure.

Awareness of motivation:  
One of your strongest ideas is that rewards can motivate effort and innovation. This is a solid argumentative foundation.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis currently says it is “vital” that men and women be financially rewarded for individual efforts. This is clear, but broad. A stronger thesis would explain *why* this is vital.

For example:  
“In modern society, people should be financially rewarded for individual effort because such rewards encourage productivity, innovation, and personal responsibility.”

This gives your essay a roadmap and makes later paragraphs easier to organize.

B. Use stronger evidence and reasoning
Right now, much of the essay depends on general statements and speculation. For an argumentative essay, you need more support. For example:

“the harder you worked, the greater the reward”
“we would become lazy and stagnate people”
“the world would become divided or even destroyed”

These claims are very broad and not fully explained. Ask yourself:

Is this always true?
Can I give an example?
Can I explain the cause-and-effect more carefully?

You do not necessarily need statistics, but you should provide more concrete reasoning or examples from work, education, business, or history.

C. Address counterarguments fairly
You imagine a society where everyone is treated equally regardless of effort, but this section oversimplifies the opposing view. People who argue for greater equality do not usually mean that no one should work or that everyone should receive unlimited rewards without contribution.

A stronger essay would acknowledge a more realistic counterargument, such as:
society should guarantee basic needs, but still reward extra effort;
opportunity is not equal for everyone, so rewards do not always reflect effort alone.

If you address this fairly, your argument will seem more thoughtful and credible.

D. Improve paragraph unity
Some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. For example, paragraph four moves from equality, to lack of motivation, to jealousy, to violence, to cultural decline. This makes the paragraph feel rushed.

Try to give each body paragraph one main point:
Reward and survival in early societies  
Reward and specialization in modern societies  
Why financial incentives encourage effort and innovation  
Acknowledgment of counterargument and response

Style and clarity

Avoid overgeneralizations
Phrases like “Life is about rewards” are dramatic, but too absolute. Consider a more precise wording:
“Rewards play an important role in motivating human effort.”
“Financial compensation is one important way societies recognize contribution.”

Use formal academic tone
A few phrases sound conversational:
“I would venture to guess”
“Worst case scenario”
“but is it really about money?”

These can be revised into a more formal style:
“It is likely that…”
“In an extreme case…”
“However, financial reward also represents recognition, security, and opportunity.”

Grammar and sentence issues to correct

Here are some important corrections:

“financially reward” → “financially rewarded”
“took up of majority” → “took up the majority”
“In order to feed your family you had to plant.”  
  More precise: “In order to feed a family, people had to farm or hunt.”
“held us meet” → “help us meet”
Comma splice:  
  “...with little or no effort on our part, it would appear.”  
  Revise to: “...with little or no effort on our part, or so it would appear.”
“treated equal” → “treated equally”
“as advanced of a society” → “as advanced a society”
“jealously” → “jealousy”
“selfish and hoarding” → “selfish and hoarding resources” or “selfish and possessive”
“stagnate people” → “stagnant people”

Also watch for repetition of “needs” and “society.” Varying vocabulary will improve style.

Revision priorities

When you revise, focus on these steps in order:

Rewrite the thesis so it clearly lists your main reasons.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one central claim.
Add specific examples or fuller explanations to support your points.
Include a fair counterargument and respond to it.
Edit grammar and wording for clarity and formal tone.

Final thought

This draft has a solid core argument, but it needs more precision and development to become persuasive at an academic level. Your main idea—that financial rewards encourage effort and social progress—is promising. If you support it with clearer reasoning, more balanced treatment of opposing views, and careful editing, the essay will become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting question about whether people can or should be paid according to their “contribution to society.” You clearly recognize that this idea is difficult because value is subjective, and you try to explore both economic inequality and moral attitudes toward wealth. The essay has a strong topic, but it needs a clearer argument, tighter organization, and more careful sentence-level editing.

Clarify your thesis

At the moment, your main claim is not fully precise. In the introduction, you suggest that equal compensation is impossible because “contribution to society” is subjective. By the end, you argue that we “cannot give pay equal to contribution to society.” This seems to be your thesis, but the essay sometimes shifts into discussing whether wealthy people morally deserve their money.

To strengthen the essay, state your position directly and early. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although many people believe pay should reflect social contribution, it is impossible to distribute income fairly by that standard because social value is subjective, economic systems reward market demand rather than moral worth, and people do not begin with equal opportunities.*

This would give your essay a clear direction and set up body paragraphs.

Improve organization

Right now, the essay moves from salary expectations, to 1920s investors, to inherited wealth, to democracy, to doctors. These points are interesting, but the connections between them are not always clear.

A stronger structure would be:

Introduction: define the issue and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain why “contribution to society” is hard to measure.
Body paragraph 2: discuss examples of unequal reward (migrant workers, doctors, investors, inherited wealth).
Body paragraph 3: explain how unequal opportunity makes “people deserve what they earn” an incomplete argument.
Conclusion: restate why equal pay for social contribution is not realistic.

Try to begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence that links back to your thesis.

Develop your examples more carefully

Your essay includes several potentially strong examples, but many are not fully explained.

1920s investors: This historical example could work well, but you need to explain how it supports your argument. Does it show that wealth can be disconnected from social usefulness? If so, say that explicitly.
Migrant worker example: This is one of your strongest examples because it highlights the gap between hard work and high pay. Expand it and connect it directly to your claim that market systems do not reward contribution equally.
Doctors: Be careful here. You write that “everyone seems to think that doctors make much too money,” but this is too broad and sounds unsupported. Some readers may disagree. If you use doctors, explain more precisely: for example, society may debate whether high salaries reflect training, responsibility, or market scarcity rather than pure social contribution.

In general, avoid unsupported generalizations like “everyone seems to think.” Instead, use more cautious phrasing such as “many people believe” or “some critics argue.”

Address logic and nuance

A few parts of the argument need refinement.

You suggest that because contribution is subjective, equal compensation is impossible. That is a reasonable point, but it needs more explanation. Why does subjectivity make fair measurement impossible? Could societies still create approximate standards?
Your conclusion jumps quickly from examples to the claim that equal pay cannot be achieved unless society controls private lives or gives “undeserved” money to the poor. This is too abrupt and may sound like a false choice. There may be intermediate positions, such as progressive taxation, wage protections, or public benefits.
The discussion of democracy is interesting, but underdeveloped. If democracy is central, explain whether you mean equality of opportunity, political freedom, or the right to keep what one earns.

Style and tone

Your writing has some strong phrasing, but at times it becomes overly conversational or moralizing. For an academic argumentative essay, aim for a more precise and formal tone.

For example:
“But do they have the right to live this way?” could become  
  *This raises the question of whether wealth without labor is ethically justified.*
“slave away in a field” is vivid, but a more formal alternative may be better depending on your audience.

Also, avoid rhetorical questions unless you answer them directly.

Grammar and proofreading

There are several sentence-level errors that distract from your argument. Some examples:

“In the 1920s in American” → in America
“equally’” → mismatched quotation mark
“A migrant work” → A migrant worker
“The answer this question” → The answer to this question
“Democracry” → democracy
“responsibly” → responsibility
“much too money” → too much money

Also watch for comma splices and awkward phrasing. Reading the essay aloud may help you catch unclear sentences.

Final advice

Your essay has a thoughtful central idea: income does not easily correspond to social value. For revision, focus first on making your thesis explicit, then reorganizing the essay around 2–3 clear reasons, and finally strengthening your examples and proofreading carefully. If you do that, your argument will become much more persuasive.

To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, strengthening logic and evidence, and improving sentence-level clarity.

What is working well

Clear position
   You take a definite stand from the introduction onward. This helps the reader understand your purpose immediately.

   Each body paragraph centers on a different reason why crime can “pay”: historical examples, monetary rewards, and the justice system. This gives the paper coherence.

Engaging style
   The essay uses vivid phrasing and rhetorical energy. Lines such as “Apparently crime can be quite profitable if one has the right agent” show confidence and a sense of audience.

A stronger version might be:

This version is more specific and sets up your body paragraphs more clearly.

O.J. Simpson: This example could work better if you explain how publicity increased wealth or market value.

Use more credible and specific evidence
Right now, many claims rely on generalization. For an argumentative essay, your points will be stronger if you include more concrete support.

For example:
instead of saying criminals “have often become celebrities,” mention specific cases and what they gained;
instead of saying crime statistics “rise continuously,” provide a source or avoid the claim;
instead of “No one can argue…,” explain why people commit crimes despite risks.

Avoid absolute claims unless you can prove them.

Was the gain financial, political, or cultural?

Your ideas will be more persuasive if you analyze rather than just list.

“speak-eases” → usually “speakeasies.”
“Los Vegas” → “Las Vegas.”
“monetary rewards gained” → repetitive; use “financial rewards.”
“if it were not why would people commit them” → awkward and missing punctuation; also “them” should refer clearly to “crimes.”
“convicted killers autobiographies” → “convicted killers’ autobiographies.”
“Finally, is the issue” → remove the comma: “Finally, there is the issue…”
“should they become arrested again” → better: “if they are arrested again.”
“tes.” at the end appears to be a typo.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses a controversial issue and clearly takes a position: you argue that feminism has helped some women but has also pressured others and challenged what you see as natural differences between men and women. That gives your paper a recognizable argument. For revision, focus on strengthening clarity, logic, evidence, and tone.

Main strengths
Clear stance: Readers can tell what you believe.
Engagement with the topic: You try to discuss work, family, military service, harassment, and social change.
Willingness to make claims: This is useful in argument writing, because it gives you something to support and refine.

Main issues to revise

A. Your thesis is unclear and shifts
Right now, the essay begins by agreeing that women faced unfair treatment, but later argues that traditional roles were good and feminism has gone too far. Because of this, the reader is not sure of your exact claim.

Try to write one precise thesis that explains:
what you agree with,
what you disagree with,
and why.

For example, a revised thesis might be:
*Although feminism helped expand women’s opportunities, it can become harmful when it pressures all women to reject traditional roles, because not every woman wants the same kind of life.*

This is only one option, but it is clearer and more focused than the current draft.

A major weakness is that the essay makes broad assertions without support. Examples:
“Women just can’t fight in a war very well.”
“Many employers are the victims.”
“Divorce is at fifty percent and rising.”
“Women are having more heart attacks…kids are being more neglected.”

These are strong claims, but you do not provide data, sources, or examples. In academic argument, readers expect evidence, not just opinion. To revise:
Use statistics from reliable sources.
Refer to studies, laws, or historical examples.
Explain how the evidence supports your point.

If you cannot support a claim, either soften it or remove it.

C. Some reasoning relies on stereotypes
Several arguments depend on generalizations about all women or all men. For instance, saying women cannot fight well because they are not strong enough assumes all women are physically weaker than all men and that strength alone determines military effectiveness. This weakens your argument because readers may see it as biased rather than reasoned.

Instead, try to make careful, specific claims. For example, if you want to argue that some jobs have physical requirements, discuss those requirements directly rather than making a blanket statement about women.

Similarly, the reference to rape if captured is emotionally charged but not well developed as an argument. If you include this point, you would need to explain why that risk should determine policy and whether men also face sexual violence in war.

D. Tone and wording may alienate readers
Phrases like “these people were crazy” reduce the essay’s credibility. In academic writing, strong disagreement should still sound respectful and reasoned.

Replace emotional or dismissive language with neutral language:
Instead of “these people were crazy,” write: *I disagree with that position because…*
Instead of “women just can’t fight in a war very well,” write: *I question whether all combat roles are equally suitable for all applicants, given differing physical demands.*

This does not mean weakening your position; it means presenting it more effectively.

E. Organization needs clearer structure
The essay moves quickly from one topic to another: workplace equality, feminism, military service, sexual harassment, religion, divorce, stress, and children. Because so many points appear in a short space, none is fully developed.

A stronger structure would be:
Introduction with thesis
Paragraph on gains from feminism
Paragraph on pressure against traditional roles
Paragraph on workplace equality/harassment
Paragraph on differences between equality of opportunity and sameness
Conclusion

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and focus on one main idea.

Sentence-level concerns

There are also frequent grammar and word-choice issues. A few examples:
“societies role” → society’s role
“work place” → workplace
“verses” → versus
“The are not strong enough” → They are not strong enough
“piers” → peers
“women should be created equal” → probably treated as equal or considered equal
“nature intended they to do” → nature intended them to do

You also use informal phrases such as “a lot.” Try more precise wording:
“many”
“some”
“increasingly”
“a significant number”

What to do in your next draft
Write a clear thesis that captures your actual argument.
Choose 2–3 main points instead of many scattered ones.
Add evidence for each major claim.
Remove stereotypes and overgeneralizations or qualify them carefully.
Use a more academic tone.
Proofread for grammar and word choice.

Final note
Your essay has the beginnings of an argument, but it currently reads more as a set of opinions than a developed academic case. The most important revision is to move from assertion to argument supported by evidence. If you do that while sharpening your thesis and organization, your paper will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: crime may seem rewarding at first, but it leads to worse behavior, harm to others, and heavier punishment. That central idea is strong and gives you a good foundation for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on clarity, organization, tone, and sentence control.

Main Strengths
Clear opinion: You firmly argue that “crime does not pay.”
Relevant example: The robbery scenario helps show how a “small” crime can escalate into something far more serious.
Moral reasoning: You emphasize both legal consequences and harm to innocent people, especially children, which gives the essay emotional force.

Biggest Areas to Revise

A. Create a stronger thesis
Right now, your opening is personal and conversational, but the main argument is not stated as clearly as it could be. A thesis should directly tell the reader what you will prove.

Possible revised thesis:
> Although crime may offer short-term rewards, it ultimately leads to escalating behavior, serious legal punishment, and harm to innocent people.

This is clearer and more academic than “crime does not pay” alone.

B. Improve organization
Your essay currently moves from personal reflection to a general claim to a long hypothetical story. Try organizing it into clear paragraphs with one main idea each:

Introduction – Present topic and thesis  
Body Paragraph 1 – Crime can become addictive or habitual  
Body Paragraph 2 – Crimes bring legal consequences  
Body Paragraph 3 – Crimes can escalate and harm innocent people (use your robbery example)  
Conclusion – Reinforce why crime does not pay

This structure will make your argument easier to follow.

C. Use a more academic tone
The essay often sounds like casual speech: “a cool million,” “run like hell,” “guess what happens,” “sounds like trouble to me.” While these phrases create voice, they weaken the formal tone expected in academic writing.

Replace conversational wording with more precise language:
“a cool million” → “a large sum of money”
“run like hell” → “run as fast as possible”
“guess what happens” → simply state the event directly

Also, avoid centering the essay too much on “I” unless the assignment specifically welcomes personal opinion. In argumentative writing, your claims sound stronger when grounded in reasoning rather than personal reaction.

D. Shorten and focus the hypothetical example
The robbery example is your strongest evidence, but it is too long and repetitive. Some details distract from the point. Condense the scenario so it clearly supports your argument:

robbery begins as property crime
criminal uses a gun during escape
innocent child is injured
charges become much more serious

That is enough to prove escalation without narrating every movement.

E. Strengthen sentence structure and grammar
Many sentences are run-ons or very long, which makes the essay hard to read. Break long sentences into shorter ones.

For example, this sentence:
> “For an individual that starts out doing small crimes... and the cycle goes on and on.”

could become:
> A person who begins with minor crimes may become bolder each time they avoid punishment. As a result, the crimes may increase in seriousness, and the cycle continues.

Also review:
spelling: *commits, individual, deterrent, persistent, starts, happened, simple*
apostrophes: *Its's* should be *It’s* or *Its* depending on meaning
word choice: *convenient store* should be *convenience store*
agreement: “someone could definitely headed” should be “someone could definitely be headed”

Development and Support
Your essay makes strong claims, but most support comes from personal belief and one hypothetical example. If possible, add broader reasoning or evidence:
explain how repeated offending can lead to habitual criminal behavior
mention that using a weapon increases criminal charges
explain that innocent bystanders often suffer when crimes escalate

Even if you do not use outside sources, make your reasoning more explicit.

When revising, try this order:

Write a clear thesis.
Divide the essay into 4–5 paragraphs.
Condense the robbery example.
Replace slang and conversational expressions.
Edit for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

Final Thought
You have the basis of a persuasive essay with a meaningful message. The main improvement needed is to present your ideas in a more organized and formal way. If you tighten the structure and revise the language, your argument that crime leads to greater consequences will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear central idea: money itself is neutral, but people’s attitudes toward it can lead to harmful behavior. That is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay because it moves beyond the cliché in the opening proverb and offers a more nuanced claim. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your thesis, improving organization, and correcting sentence-level issues.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis
Right now, the essay begins with “Money is the root of all evil,” but later argues that money is not evil in itself; rather, greed and misuse are the problem. This creates some inconsistency. Your final claim seems to be:

money represents power,
power can be used for good or evil,
greed and addiction make money destructive.

Try to state that clearly in one sentence near the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although money provides security, status, and opportunity, it becomes harmful when people treat it as the ultimate source of power and allow greed to override moral values.*

This makes your position more precise and argumentative.

Improve organization
Your essay has thoughtful points, but they are not fully developed. Right now, each paragraph introduces important ideas, but the argument ends before those ideas are explored. A stronger structure might be:

Introduction  
   Define money as power and present your thesis.

Body Paragraph 1: The positive power of money  
   Explain how money helps people survive and access resources, education, healthcare, and social opportunities.

Body Paragraph 2: The danger of emotional dependence on money  
   Discuss addiction to status, possessions, or lifestyle.

Body Paragraph 3: Greed and moral compromise  
   Explain how the pursuit of money can lead people to ignore ethics or harm others.

Conclusion  
   Reinforce that money itself is neutral; human values determine whether it leads to good or evil.

This structure would help your reader follow your reasoning more easily.

Develop your evidence and examples
The essay makes broad claims, but they need support. For example, when you mention gambling, drugs, prostitution, and social prestige, you list them without explaining how they connect to your argument. To strengthen the essay, add specific examples or scenarios.

For instance:
A person who becomes obsessed with wealth may neglect family relationships.
A company may exploit workers in pursuit of profit.
A politician may accept bribes because money becomes more important than public duty.

These examples would make your points more concrete and persuasive.

Use transitions to connect ideas
Some sentences feel abrupt. Transitions will help the argument flow. For example:
“In addition to meeting basic needs, money also influences social status.”
“However, the danger begins when money becomes more than a tool.”
“As a result, greed can push people to ignore ethical boundaries.”

These small additions make the essay sound more polished and logical.

Revise sentence clarity and grammar
There are several grammar and mechanics issues that weaken the authority of your argument. Here are a few key corrections:

“Money is the root of all evil is an ancient saying”  
  → *“‘Money is the root of all evil’ is an ancient saying…”*

“The key is in the definition of term money”  
  → *“The key lies in the definition of the term ‘money.’”*

“Ones's attitude”  
  → *“One’s attitude”*

“when the need for money becomes the driving force”  
  Capitalize *When* if it begins a sentence.

“Values and ethics play very little importance”  
  → *“Values and ethics play very little role”*  
  or *“are of very little importance”*

“in structure of a greedy persons life”  
  → *“in the structure of a greedy person’s life”*

“Money does good or evil”  
  → *“Money can be used for good or evil”*

Also watch for run-on sentences and comma splices. For example:
“Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive.”  
  This should be split or joined properly:
  *“Enough money gives you the power to get what you need to survive, while a lack of money may mean that you cannot survive.”*

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion is currently only one sentence, so it feels abrupt. Use the ending to reinforce your main point in a memorable way. You might conclude by emphasizing that greed, not money itself, corrupts human behavior.

Final Thoughts
Your essay has a strong philosophical idea at its center: money is a form of power, and power reflects the character of the person who holds it. That is an insightful argument. The next step is to make the essay more convincing by clarifying the thesis, organizing the body more fully, adding examples, and editing carefully for grammar and style. With revision, this could become a thoughtful and persuasive argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear personal position: you support some goals of feminism but believe traditional dating etiquette and chivalry have declined. The piece has a strong sense of voice and conviction, which can be an asset in argumentative writing. For revision, focus on making the argument more precise, balanced, and evidence-based.

What’s Working
Clear stance: Readers can identify your main claim: feminism has had positive effects in some areas, but has also contributed to confusion about dating norms.
Acknowledgment of complexity: You do not reject all feminist ideas. This helps avoid a purely one-sided argument.
Personal engagement: Your use of lived experience gives the essay energy and authenticity.

Main Areas to Revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your central claim appears gradually and somewhat loosely. Try stating it more directly in the introduction.

For example:
> While feminism has helped women gain important rights and opportunities, changing expectations about gender roles have also made traditional courtship practices less common, leaving some people uncertain about how to behave in dating relationships.

This version is more specific and avoids blaming all women or all feminists.

Replace broad generalizations
The essay often makes sweeping claims about “women in general,” “men,” or “feminists.” These generalizations weaken your credibility because they imply that all members of a group think and act the same way.

Examples:
“feminists in this country”
“women in general are offended”
“Men were gentlemen in the 60’s”

These statements are too absolute. Many women still appreciate traditional gestures; many men in the 1960s were not respectful; and feminism itself includes many different viewpoints.

Revision strategy: Use qualifiers such as:
“some women”
“many men I have spoken to”
“in my experience”
“certain forms of modern dating culture”

This will make your argument more accurate and fair.

Separate personal experience from proof
Your essay relies heavily on anecdotal evidence: your own dating history and comments from male friends. Personal experience is valuable, but in academic argument it usually needs support from broader evidence.

To strengthen the essay, consider adding:
research on changes in dating culture over time
surveys about attitudes toward paying for dates or opening doors
evidence about how gender expectations have shifted since the 1960s

This would help you move from “this is what I have seen” to “this is a broader social pattern.”

Clarify the relationship between feminism and misconduct
One problematic section suggests that men fear being accused of “date rape” or “sexual harassment” if they are “too forward.” This is a sensitive and serious issue. As written, it risks implying that accusations are mainly misunderstandings caused by feminism, rather than responses to real harmful behavior.

If your point is that changing social expectations require clearer communication, say that directly. For example:
> One consequence of changing gender roles is that some people are less certain about dating expectations, which makes communication and mutual respect more important than ever.

This keeps the focus on consent and respect rather than treating those issues dismissively.

Improve organization
The essay moves between ideas without a strong structure. A clearer organization would help.

A possible structure:
Introduction: Present your thesis.
Paragraph 1: Explain positive gains of feminism.
Paragraph 2: Explain how dating norms have changed.
Paragraph 3: Discuss confusion around courtship and expectations.
Conclusion: Argue for preserving respect and thoughtfulness in modern dating.

This structure would make your argument easier to follow.

Use a more academic tone
The conversational style gives personality, but some phrases reduce formality:
“Believe me”
“this is bunk!”
“Miss Susie Homemaker”
“you men out there”

In an argumentative essay, a more measured tone is usually more persuasive. Try replacing emotional or casual language with precise wording.

For example:
“Believe me” → “In my experience”
“this is bunk” → “I disagree with this assumption”

Edit for sentence clarity
Some sentences are long and difficult to follow. Breaking them into shorter sentences will improve readability. Also review punctuation, especially parentheses and quotation marks, which are sometimes overused.

For instance, the paragraph beginning “Nowadays, it takes two incomes…” could be shortened and simplified into two or three clearer sentences.

Final Advice
Your strongest revision goal should be to turn a personal opinion piece into a more persuasive academic argument. Keep your main idea, but:
state the thesis clearly,
avoid overgeneralizing,
use more balanced language,
add evidence beyond personal experience,
and adopt a more formal tone.

With revision, this essay could become a more nuanced and convincing argument about the tension between gender equality and traditional courtship.
