Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear overall position: crime has serious costs, not only for victims but also for offenders, taxpayers, and communities. You also show awareness that the phrase “crime does not pay” can be interpreted in several ways—financially, emotionally, and socially. That gives you a good foundation for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: clarity of thesis, development of argument, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the thesis and introduction
The opening begins with a rhetorical question, but the phrase “The phrase ” is unclear and incomplete. You seem to be discussing the saying “crime does not pay.” State that directly and make your central claim more specific.

A stronger introduction would:
identify the phrase clearly,
explain what it means,
preview your main reasons.

For example, your thesis could be something like:

> Although some people may believe crime brings short-term benefits, crime ultimately “does not pay” because it leads to financial costs, emotional consequences, and long-term social penalties for both offenders and the wider community.

This would give the essay a sharper argumentative focus.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Your body paragraphs each touch on a different consequence of crime, which is good. However, some ideas repeat or drift. Try to give each paragraph one main claim and support it fully.

Possible structure:
Financial costs to society — incarceration, taxes, court costs.
Emotional and moral costs to offenders and victims — guilt, remorse, lasting harm.
Costs to communities and law-abiding citizens — lower property values, fear, neighborhood decline.
Long-term consequences for convicted individuals — employment, housing, social stigma.

Add clear topic sentences so the reader knows the point of each paragraph right away.

Example revision of a topic sentence:
Current: “The law abiding citizen also knows that crime does not pay.”
Better: “Crime also harms law-abiding citizens by reducing property values and damaging community stability.”

Develop your evidence and reasoning
Right now, many of your points are stated generally, but not fully explained. In argumentative writing, each claim needs support. You can strengthen the essay by:
giving examples,
explaining cause and effect,
using facts or statistics if available,
showing why each point proves your thesis.

For instance, when you discuss incarceration, you could explain that imprisonment involves expenses for housing, security, food, and administration, all funded by taxpayers. When discussing job discrimination after conviction, explain how a criminal record limits access to stable employment and makes reintegration difficult.

Also consider acknowledging the opposing view briefly: some may think crime “pays” because criminals can gain money or power in the short term. Then rebut that idea by showing how temporary gains are outweighed by lasting consequences. This would make your argument more persuasive.

Work on style and tone
Your essay has a serious tone, which suits the topic, but some sentences are wordy or awkward. Aim for more direct and precise phrasing.

For example:
“in the world crime actually costs money” → “crime has real financial costs”
“The criminal will have to live with his or her actions as long as they live” → “A criminal may live with the consequences of those actions for life.”

Avoid overgeneralizations such as “for the most part” or absolute claims that may not always be true. For example, not every criminal feels remorse, so be careful with broad statements.

Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation
Sentence-level errors currently distract from your ideas. Proofread carefully for:

subject-verb agreement:  
  “The phrase has been repeated many times but have you…” → “The phrase has been repeated many times, but have you…”

word choice:  
  “to often” → “too often”  
  “so to will” → “so too will”

spelling:  
  “freinds” → “friends”  
  “acquantances” → “acquaintances”  
  “oppurtunity” → “opportunity”  
  “their is no escape” → “there is no escape”

capitalization and punctuation:  
  “so even though…” should begin with a capital “S” if it starts a new sentence.  
  Several long sentences need commas or should be split into shorter sentences.

apostrophes/possessives:  
  “the one's own freedom” is incorrect; revise to “one’s own freedom” or rewrite the phrase.

Ending more effectively
The final paragraph introduces an important point about lifelong stigma, but the essay ends abruptly. Add a conclusion that does more than repeat. A good conclusion should:
restate the main argument,
synthesize the major consequences,
leave the reader with a strong final thought.

For example, you might conclude that crime may appear profitable in the short term, but its financial, emotional, and social consequences show that it ultimately harms everyone involved.

Revision priorities
Replace the unclear opening with a direct thesis.
Organize each body paragraph around one clear argument.
Add explanation and examples to support your points.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
Write a fuller conclusion.

You have a solid core idea here. With clearer structure, stronger support, and careful editing, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a strong sense of voice and clear interest in the topic. You sound engaged, confident, and personally invested, which helps create energy on the page. The distinction you make between “organized” and “unorganized” crime gives the essay a useful structure. For revision, though, you need to strengthen the essay as an *academic argument*: clarify your thesis, support claims with credible evidence, refine definitions, and adopt a more formal tone.

Clarify your main argument
Right now, the essay seems to argue that organized crime is more profitable, more protected, and more sustainable than unorganized crime because money buys influence and protection. That is an interesting claim, but it is not stated directly and early enough.

Try revising the introduction so it ends with a clear thesis, such as:

> Although both organized and unorganized crime harm society, organized crime is more durable and dangerous because it is profit-driven, collaborative, and often protected through corruption.

A thesis like this will give the paper direction and help readers understand what you are trying to prove.

Improve organization and focus
The essay currently moves from personal reflection to definitions, then to broad claims about police corruption, and finally to a personal biography. The final autobiographical paragraph does not support the argument and should be removed unless the assignment specifically asks for personal background.

A clearer structure would be:

Introduction: define the issue and state your thesis  
Paragraph on unorganized crime: define it, describe typical features, explain why it is less profitable or less stable  
Paragraph(s) on organized crime: define it, explain how it operates, discuss profit and corruption  
Conclusion: restate the argument and explain why the distinction matters

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that supports the thesis.

Use evidence instead of broad assertions
Many of your claims are interesting, but they need evidence. For example:

“These are petty offenses because they do not hold a high dollar value.”
“The police and other government officials are willing to overlook the illegal acts because of the payoff they are receiving.”
“In the United States, the average amount of money spent on illegal gambling alone is $30 per person.”

These statements are serious and need sources. In academic writing, personal belief (“I know this”) is not enough. You should cite course materials, scholarly articles, crime statistics, or government reports. Also, some of your claims are too sweeping. For example, saying police generally protect organized criminals is a major accusation and should either be carefully qualified or strongly documented.

Refine your definitions
Your paper depends on the difference between organized and unorganized crime, so your definitions need to be precise. Right now, the categories blur.

For instance, you write that an “unorganized criminal must be very good and have considered all aspects” in order not to get caught, but later you suggest such criminals are careless and unsophisticated. Also, crimes like robbery, assault, rape, and theft are not necessarily “petty offenses.” Some are violent felonies with severe social consequences.

Be careful with terms. You might define:
Unorganized crime as spontaneous or loosely planned acts, often committed by individuals
Organized crime as coordinated, ongoing criminal activity conducted by groups for profit

This would make your comparison more accurate.

Adopt a more formal academic tone
Your voice is vivid, but some phrasing is too informal for an argumentative essay. Examples include:
“save the best for last”
“MONEY!”
“BIG sum of money”
“whomever coined this term is on point exactly”

These expressions create emphasis, but in academic writing they can weaken credibility. Aim for precise, controlled language instead.

For example:
Replace “save the best for last” with “I will next turn to organized crime.”
Replace “MONEY!” with “financial gain is the central enabling factor.”

Avoid unsupported personal authority
You mention being “truly educated on both sides of the track” and refer to your course as proof of knowledge. This may be sincere, but in academic writing, authority comes from reasoning and evidence, not self-description. Let your analysis and sources establish your credibility.

Sentence-level revision
There are several grammar and style issues to address:
Avoid sentence fragments and overly conversational transitions.
Watch pronoun agreement and word choice: “will to deal with others” should be “willing to deal with others.”
“that is ongoing” should be “that are ongoing” if referring to “individuals.”
Be careful with punctuation; some dashes and parentheses are distracting.

Also, try to reduce repetition of phrases like “This type of crime” and vary sentence structure.

Final recommendation
Your strongest idea is that money and coordination make organized crime more resilient than spontaneous individual crime. Build the revision around that argument. Remove unrelated material, define your terms carefully, support key claims with evidence, and shift to a more formal style.

With those changes, your essay can become a much more persuasive and academically credible argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a strong emotional core and a clear personal investment in the topic. The narrative is vivid, and your main claim—that crime does not pay because legal consequences eventually catch up with offenders—is consistent throughout. The personal experience gives your argument authenticity and helps readers understand the long-term effects of crime on victims. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening the essay’s argument, organization, and sentence-level correctness.

Main strength: strong personal evidence
Your essay’s greatest strength is its use of firsthand experience. The details about discovering the robbery, living in fear afterward, and dealing with the court system make the issue feel real rather than abstract. This helps persuade readers emotionally.

However, because this is an argumentative essay, you should make sure the story supports a clear argument rather than reading mostly as a narrative. Right now, the essay sometimes feels more like a personal account than a fully developed argument.

Clarify the thesis early
The introduction needs a clearer thesis statement. You begin with your parents’ lesson that stealing is wrong and that thieves are punished, but your position is not stated directly enough.

Consider revising the introduction so that it clearly announces your argument, such as:

Possible thesis:

This gives the essay a direct claim that the rest of the paragraphs can support.

Improve organization
The essay follows a chronological structure, which works well for your story, but each paragraph should also connect more explicitly to your argument.

A possible structure:
Introduction: present belief that “crime does not pay” and your thesis
Body paragraph 1: describe the robbery and immediate impact
Body paragraph 2: explain emotional and practical consequences for you and your family
Body paragraph 3: describe the legal process and your frustration with plea bargaining
Body paragraph 4: explain how the final sentencing confirmed your argument
Conclusion: reflect on what the experience taught you about justice

This would help the essay feel more purposeful and less like a simple retelling of events.

Develop the argument, not just the story
To make the essay more argumentative, explain *why* your story proves your point. For example:
Crime did not pay because the thieves were caught.
Crime did not pay because prison time outweighed any benefit they got from stolen goods.
Crime creates suffering not only for victims but also serious consequences for offenders.
The legal system, though imperfect and slow, can still deliver justice.

Right now, these ideas are implied, but they should be stated more directly.

There are many sentence-level errors that weaken the essay’s credibility. These include missing words, verb tense problems, pronoun issues, and punctuation errors.

Here are some examples:

“my mother and father always taught me the stealing was wrong”  
  → “my mother and father always taught me that stealing was wrong”

“I always believed them and they saying that .”  
  This sentence is incomplete and needs to be rewritten or removed.

“my husbands firearms”  
  → “my husband’s firearms”

“it was our home they stolen property came from”  
  → “it was our home the stolen property came from”

“prosecutors office”  
  → “the prosecutor’s office”

“two men and one women”  
  → “two men and one woman”

Also watch for comma splices and overly long sentences. Breaking long sentences into shorter ones will make your ideas clearer and more powerful.

Maintain formal tone
Because this is an academic essay, some phrases should be made more formal:
“old car” may not be necessary unless it matters to your point.
“awful people” could be replaced with “the offenders” or “the burglars.”
“I mean everything” is emotionally effective, but you may want to phrase it more formally: “Nearly everything in our home had been taken.”

You do not need to remove emotion, but balancing emotion with formal language will strengthen your credibility.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion returns nicely to your father’s phrase, “crime doesn’t pay,” which creates a sense of closure. To make the ending stronger, go beyond repeating the lesson and explain its significance. For example, you might emphasize that justice matters not only as punishment for criminals but also as recognition of victims’ suffering.

When revising, focus on these steps:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Make each body paragraph support that thesis directly.
Reduce pure storytelling by adding explanation and analysis.
Correct grammar, punctuation, and word choice errors.
Strengthen the conclusion so it reflects on the larger meaning of your experience.

Overall, this essay has a compelling foundation. Your personal experience gives you strong material; now your task is to shape it into a clearer and more polished argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: feminism originally sought equality, but some contemporary uses of the term have distorted that purpose. That gives your argument a recognizable structure and a strong sense of personal investment. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening the argument with evidence, improving fairness and precision, and correcting style issues.

Main strengths

Awareness of complexity: You do not simply reject feminism; you acknowledge its benefits, such as suffrage and increased attention to violence and discrimination.
Engaged voice: The essay sounds committed and thoughtful, which is important in argumentative writing.

Main issues to revise

Feminism originally meant equality.
The term has been misused.
Radical feminists have damaged public perceptions.
The movement has still done more good than harm.

These ideas can fit together, but you need a single precise thesis. For example:

Some phrases seriously weaken your credibility. For instance:

“a big chunky lesbian who hates all men”
“a bunch of whining women”
“Feminists are becoming anarchists”

that feminism is now seen as a “dirty word”
that “many radical women” misuse feminism
that “men are not taking the feminist cause seriously anymore”
that women now have “partial equality”

historical evidence about early feminism
examples of feminist achievements
specific cases or sources when discussing radical groups or public perception

D. Define key terms
Your essay uses words like feminism, radical, equality, and dominance, but they remain vague. A strong argument depends on definition. Ask yourself:
What exactly counts as feminism?
What makes a feminist view “radical”?
What would “equality” look like in practice?

Without definitions, readers may feel you are criticizing an unclear target.

E. Improve logical balance
You argue that feminism has done important good, but you also suggest the movement has become a shelter for extremists. The problem is that the essay often treats “some feminists” and “feminism as a whole” as if they are the same. Be careful not to overgeneralize. Distinguish between:
the broader movement,
particular groups,
public misconceptions.

This will make your argument more nuanced and credible.

Organization and paragraphing
The essay generally moves from history to present-day concerns, which is useful. However, some paragraphs are too long and contain multiple ideas. Try this structure:

Introduction: present the issue and thesis.
Historical background: explain feminism’s original goals.
Achievements of feminism: voting rights, workplace access, awareness of abuse/discrimination.

Conclusion: restate your position and suggest what feminism should return to.

Each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence.

Word form: “to an extremes” should be “to extremes”
Clarity: some sentences are long and repetitive

Revision priorities

Remove stereotypical or inflammatory language.
Add specific evidence and examples.
Define your key terms.
Revise for clarity, grammar, and paragraph structure.

Topic sentence
Historical example
Explanation of public reaction
Link back to your main claim about perception

Introduction: stereotypes about movements; thesis about feminism and public perception

Body paragraph 2: media focus on radical images in the 1960s–70s
Body paragraph 3: how those stereotypes continue to affect views of feminist goals today
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why this misunderstanding matters

This will help the essay feel more logical and persuasive.

Sentence-level concerns

You have several grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors that distract from the argument. Examples include:
“Whenever a movement is began” → “Whenever a movement begins”
“oppostion” → “opposition”
“therfore” → “therefore”
“readiliy” → “readily”
“promote” → “promoted”
“in lieu of” is used incorrectly here; you likely mean “in favor of” or “because of attention to”

Also watch comma use:
“This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists, made it seem…”  
  Remove the comma after “feminists.”

Final revision advice

As you revise, focus on three priorities:

Write a precise thesis early in the essay
Replace weak or inaccurate examples with specific, reliable ones
Explain each example in depth so it clearly supports your argument

You have the foundation of a thoughtful argument here. The key now is to make it more historically accurate, better organized, and more formally argued. With stronger evidence and clearer structure, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a confident voice. You aim to connect the history of feminism in the United States to a larger global discussion, and your opening gives the piece a clear occasion and purpose. The strongest part of the essay is your sense of historical importance: you highlight women’s suffrage, second-wave feminism, and expanded access to education and professions. These are strong starting points for an argumentative essay.

To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument, evidence and development, and style/accuracy.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Right now, your essay gestures toward an argument, but it does not fully state one in a precise, debatable way. Your final sentence—“America continues to set an example for women around the world”—seems to be your main claim, but it appears only at the end and is not fully defended.

Try stating your thesis earlier and making it more specific. For example:

Possible revised thesis: “Although gender inequality persists, the American feminist movement has significantly expanded women’s political, educational, and professional opportunities, making the United States an influential—though imperfect—model for women’s rights worldwide.”

This version helps in two ways:
It acknowledges complexity rather than sounding one-sided.
It gives you clear categories for body paragraphs: political rights, education, and employment.

Develop the argument with evidence
Your essay makes several broad claims, but many need support. For example, you say that feminist leaders influenced “how America reacts and responds to its women” and that opportunities have “expanded greatly.” These points are promising, but they need examples, facts, or brief historical references.

Consider adding evidence such as:
the passage of the 19th Amendment for suffrage;
expanded access to higher education after the women’s movement;
workplace reforms related to discrimination and equal pay;
examples of women entering professions once dominated by men.

Even a few specific examples would make your argument more convincing. Since this is an argumentative essay, readers need more than general statements—they need proof.

Also, your essay introduces the World Conference on Women in Beijing, but then the focus shifts almost entirely to the United States. If you want to keep the global frame, briefly explain why the American case matters internationally. Otherwise, narrow the introduction so it matches the essay’s actual focus.

Address complexity and counterargument
A strong argumentative essay usually acknowledges that the issue is not simple. Your essay suggests that America is a positive example, but it does not consider limitations. For instance, if the “battle” continues, what inequalities remain? Equal pay? Underrepresentation in leadership? Social resistance to feminism?

Including a brief counterargument would strengthen the essay:
Some critics might argue that legal gains do not guarantee full equality.
Others might point out that feminism has benefited some women more than others.

You do not need a long section on this, but even 2–3 sentences would make your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Improve organization
Your essay currently reads as one developing discussion rather than a structured argument. To improve flow, organize it more clearly:

Introduction: global context + clear thesis
Body paragraph 1: suffrage and political equality
Body paragraph 2: second-wave feminism and social change
Body paragraph 3: education and workplace opportunities
Conclusion: restate the main claim with nuance

Also, one sentence contains a missing quotation or incomplete idea:  
“They have always preached the same sermon, from the same pulpit: .”  
This must be completed or removed.

Revise tone and wording for academic style
Your voice is energetic, but some phrasing is too emotional or repetitive for formal academic writing. For instance:
“battle cries”
“same sermon, from the same pulpit”
repeated use of “women of America” and “men of America”

Aim for more precise, less dramatic language. For example:
instead of “battle cries,” use “demands for political equality”
instead of “preached the same sermon,” use “advanced a consistent argument for gender equality”

Also, avoid overgeneralizations such as “No one today would say…” since some readers may disagree. A more accurate phrasing would be:
“Voting rights are now legally equal for women and men in the United States.”

Correct factual and mechanical errors
There are several issues with correctness that should be fixed in revision:

“World Conference Women” → likely “World Conference on Women”
“suffragates” → “suffragists” or “suffragettes”
“Gloria Steinam” → “Gloria Steinem”
Add a space after the period in “.Throughout”
Watch for sentence length; some sentences are overly long and could be divided for clarity.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid topic and clear commitment to the subject. The next step is to turn it from a general reflection into a stronger argument. Focus on:
stating a precise thesis early,
supporting claims with specific evidence,
acknowledging ongoing inequalities,
improving organization, and
correcting factual and stylistic issues.

With these revisions, your essay can become much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that money itself is not the root of all evil and that individual moral choice is more important. This gives your paper a strong central disagreement with the statement. You also show awareness of nuance by admitting that money can contribute to some evil. That balance is a good starting point.

What is working well

Clear stance
You clearly disagree with the claim that money is “the root of all evil.” Readers can identify your position quickly.

Use of qualification
You do not argue that money is harmless in every case. Instead, you say money can be connected to evil when people pursue it through lying, stealing, or murder. This makes your argument more thoughtful.

Strong concluding idea
Your conclusion that evil comes from individual choice is the most compelling idea in the essay. It gives the paper a clear alternative thesis.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen and clarify the thesis
Right now, the introduction is somewhat confusing. You begin by saying the phrase is incorrect, then say you believe “this corrected statement” is true, but the corrected statement is never fully explained. A reader may not understand whether you are arguing against money or against the love of money.

Try stating your thesis directly and early. For example:

> Money itself is not the root of all evil; rather, evil comes from the choices people make about how they value and pursue money.

This is clearer and sets up the rest of your essay.

Improve organization
Each body paragraph has a point, but the overall structure could be tighter. Consider this order:

Introduction: explain the saying and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: money is a neutral tool and can be earned honestly.
Body paragraph 2: evil actions connected to money come from human greed and dishonesty, not from money itself.
Body paragraph 3: money can also be used for good, such as charity and social programs.
Conclusion: moral choice, not money, is the deeper cause of evil.

This structure would help your ideas build logically.

Develop your reasoning more fully
Some claims need more explanation. For example, when you say, “If money were the root of all evil, we all would have to be evil because we all have money,” that point is interesting, but it may oversimplify the issue. Someone could argue that simply possessing money is different from loving or abusing it.

To strengthen your reasoning, explain *why* money should be seen as neutral. You might say that money is a tool that can be used responsibly or irresponsibly, just like power, knowledge, or influence.

Use more precise wording
There are several places where word choice could be sharper:

“Most money is attained in honest ways” could become “Most people earn money through legitimate means such as work, business, or inheritance.”
“How can honestly be associated with evil?” should be “How can honesty be associated with evil?”
“worth-while” should be “worthwhile.”

Also, your sentence “Other things as well can be the root of evil: attitudes, morals (lack of), greed...” would sound more academic as:

> Other factors can lead to evil as well, including greed, corruption, and a lack of moral principles.

Address the opposing view more carefully
You mention that people have committed terrible acts for money, which helps acknowledge the other side. However, you could make this even stronger by directly addressing why some people believe money is the root of evil. Then explain why that view is incomplete.

For example:

> Some people argue that money causes evil because it often motivates crime, betrayal, and exploitation. However, money is only the object being pursued; the true cause is the person’s greed or lack of ethics.

This kind of counterargument will make your essay more persuasive.

Grammar and style issues to correct

A few sentence-level issues weaken the essay’s credibility:

“wold” → would
“honestly” → honesty
“If money was the root” → If money were the root  
Be careful with commas and semicolons; some sentences are long and could be divided for clarity.
Avoid casual phrasing like “I am sure that most if not all...” unless you can support it. In academic writing, it is better to sound measured: “Many reputable organizations depend on charitable donations to serve the public.”

Revision goal
As you revise, focus on this main improvement:

Make your thesis precise and organize every paragraph around proving that money is a neutral tool, while human choices determine whether it is used for good or evil.

Possible revised thesis
Here is one version you might use:

> Although money can motivate harmful behavior, it is not the root of all evil; rather, greed, dishonesty, and individual moral choices are what turn money into a cause of wrongdoing.

This thesis matches your strongest ideas and gives your essay a clearer argumentative direction.

Overall, your essay has a solid central idea and several relevant examples. With a clearer thesis, stronger organization, and more precise language, it could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central concern: money is necessary in modern life, but it can also become controlling or corrupting. This is a strong topic for an argumentative essay because it addresses both practical and moral dimensions of money. You also use memorable contrasts such as “money can be either a slave for us or it can make us slaves,” which gives the piece a thoughtful tone.

What is working well

Clear engagement with the topic  
   You take the saying seriously and question whether it still makes sense in today’s world. This shows independent thinking.

Awareness of complexity  
   Rather than simply praising or condemning money, you argue that money is necessary but dangerous when it becomes an obsession. That balance gives your essay potential.

Some effective phrasing  
   Lines such as “We are to be the masters and use it for our needs and good” and the contrast between “use it to live” and “live to use it” are strong and memorable.

Main areas to revise

Clarify your thesis
Right now, the essay begins with the idea that money is essential, then shifts into power, corruption, work ethic, and success. The reader can follow the general theme, but your main argument needs to be stated more directly.

Ask yourself: What exactly am I arguing?  
A possible thesis might be:

*Although money is necessary for survival in modern society, it becomes harmful when people treat it as the measure of power, success, and personal worth.*

This kind of thesis would help unify the whole essay.

Improve organization
Your paragraphs contain interesting ideas, but the progression is sometimes unclear. Try organizing the essay so that each paragraph develops one main point.

A possible structure:
Introduction: explain the saying and present your thesis
Paragraph 1: money is necessary in modern society
Paragraph 2: money can lead to power and corruption
Paragraph 3: people value earned money differently from inherited/easy money
Paragraph 4: society often measures success by wealth, but there are better measures
Conclusion: money should serve people, not rule them

Using topic sentences at the start of each paragraph will make your argument easier to follow.

Develop your reasoning more fully
Some claims are interesting but underexplained. For example:
“Once ones basic and comfort needs are met, what’s next, power...”
“Anything that comes easy most probably won’t be as appreciated...”
“The person who rates success on substantive accomplishments can stand alone...”

These are good ideas, but they need explanation or examples. Why does wealth lead some people to seek power? Are there exceptions? Can you give a real-life or hypothetical example? Strong argument essays do not just state opinions—they support them.

Maintain a more formal academic tone
Your voice is sincere, but some expressions are too conversational for an academic essay. For example:
“the almighty dollar”
“nothing works”
“what’s next”
“a must”

These can be revised into more formal language. For instance:
“money is essential”
“modern life depends heavily on money”
“people may then seek influence or control”

Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling
There are several sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. Examples include:
“some where” → somewhere
“currupts” → corrupts
“absolute” → probably absolutely
“Once ones basic” → Once one’s basic
“begats” → begets
“cicle” → cycle
“sence” → sense
“sucess” → success
“one heir” → likely on their or by their
“indespencable” → indispensable

Also watch for:
apostrophes: *one’s basic needs*
comma use around quotations
sentence boundaries: some sentences are run-on or awkwardly joined

For example, this sentence could be revised:
Original: *This begats the will for more power and the cicle goes on.*
Revised: *This often creates a desire for even more power, and the cycle continues.*

Specific revision suggestions

Here is one possible revision of your opening idea:

Original:  
*It's difficult to justify this saying in a world where money is power and power is everything.*

Revised:  
*It is difficult to defend this saying in a modern world where money often determines access to security, comfort, and influence.*

This keeps your point but sounds more precise and academic.

Final advice
Your essay has a meaningful message: money is necessary, but it should not define a person’s values or control their life. That is a solid argument. In revision, focus on:
writing a clear thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one point,
explaining your ideas with more detail,
using a more formal tone, and
correcting grammar and spelling.

With those changes, your essay will become much stronger and more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you argue that money itself is not evil, but people’s desire for it and misuse of it can lead to harmful behavior. That central idea is understandable throughout the paper, and you use a moral and religious framework consistently. With revision, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear position
You clearly answer the main question in your conclusion: money is not evil in itself; evil comes from how people use or pursue it.

Logical contrast
You effectively contrast two ideas:
a life centered on money and power
a life centered on God and moral values

This gives your essay a clear structure and helps the reader follow your reasoning.

Recognition of complexity
A strong point in your essay is that you do not argue that money is always bad. You acknowledge that money can help people by providing food, housing, and education. This makes your argument more balanced.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your thesis is implied rather than sharply stated. The introduction asks several questions, but it should lead to one clear argumentative claim.

A stronger thesis might be:
“Money is not the root of all evil; rather, the selfish pursuit of money and power can lead people away from moral and spiritual values.”

This gives your essay a precise focus and sets up the argument more effectively.

Improve organization
Your essay has good ideas, but some paragraphs repeat or drift. Try using a clearer structure:

Introduction – explain the saying and present your thesis  
Body paragraph 1 – explain why people connect money with evil  
Body paragraph 2 – discuss how greed can damage relationships and moral judgment  
Body paragraph 3 – explain why money itself is morally neutral  
Body paragraph 4 – show how money can be used for good  
Conclusion – restate the argument clearly

This will help your ideas build more logically.

Develop your evidence
Your essay makes broad claims, but it needs more support. For example, when you say that chasing money can lead people to neglect family or turn to gambling, explain how and why this happens. You could include:
a real-life example
a historical example
a religious reference, such as the biblical verse often paraphrased as “the love of money is the root of all kinds of evil” (1 Timothy 6:10)

Using specific evidence will make your argument more convincing.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some sentences are too absolute. For example, “If you lead a life that is based on how much money you have, you are always searching for the next dollar” suggests that this happens in every case. Try softer, more precise language such as:
“can lead people to…”
“may cause some individuals to…”
“often results in…”

This makes your writing more accurate and academic.

Sentence-level issues to correct

Grammar and word choice
There are several errors that should be revised:

“you always want more that you have” → “more than you have”
“christian” → “Christian”
“subservant” → “subservient”
“can be lead” → “can be led”
“mear” → “mere”
“can not” → “cannot”
“educated the uneducated” → “educate the uneducated”

Clarity and phrasing
Some sentences are awkward or imprecise. For example:

“It is the person whose judgements, decisions, and actions that can be evil.”
  - Better: “It is people’s judgments, decisions, and actions that can be evil.”

“it is all in how it is used and how you allow it to use you”
  - This is a strong idea, but you could phrase it more clearly:
  - “What matters is how money is used and whether a person allows the pursuit of money to control their life.”

Punctuation
Be careful with comma use. For example:
“I think not, it is all in how it is used…” is a comma splice.
You could fix it like this:
“I think not. It all depends on how money is used…”

Style and academic tone

“people”
“individuals”
“some people”
“a person”

Final advice
As you revise, focus on three priorities:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction
Support each claim with examples or evidence
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity

You already have the foundation of a thoughtful argument. The next step is to make it more precise, organized, and formally developed so that your reader is fully convinced.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0027.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a consistent argument: you argue that “crime does pay” by comparing the criminal life to the life of an average worker. The essay is easy to follow because it is organized around several advantages you believe criminals have: job security, higher income, flexible hours, and earlier retirement. That clear structure is a strength. For revision, however, you need to strengthen your reasoning, improve your tone, and correct several grammar and usage errors.

Main issue: argument and logic

Your paper argues only one side and treats criminal success as guaranteed. This weakens the essay because it ignores obvious counterarguments. For example, you claim that “a criminal will always be employed” and can “retire a lot faster,” but the essay does not consider major risks such as arrest, imprisonment, fines, violence, addiction, injury, or death. Because those consequences are central to the topic, leaving them out makes the argument seem unrealistic.

Also, many of your claims are unsupported or overgeneralized:
“Many criminals live far better than the average American”
“The average criminal can work one hour a day and make $300.00”
“A criminal will always be employed”

These statements need evidence. Who is the “average criminal”? What kind of crime are you discussing? Street theft, fraud, drug trafficking, and white-collar crime all differ greatly in risk and financial reward.

Revision advice:  
Either:
Keep your current claim, but make it more nuanced and support it with credible evidence; or  
Reframe the essay to argue that crime may seem profitable in the short term, but the long-term consequences make it a false form of success.

That second option would likely produce a more convincing academic argument.

Use of evidence

The essay relies on invented or unverified numbers. For example, your wage and deduction figures seem arbitrary, and the criminal earnings estimate is not documented. In academic writing, readers expect evidence from reliable sources, not assumptions.

Revision advice:
Use current wage data from government or labor sources.
If you compare legal work and crime, use research on criminal earnings, incarceration rates, recidivism, or economic mobility.
Explain your calculations clearly and make sure they are realistic.

Without evidence, the paper reads more like opinion than argument.

Tone and audience

At times the essay sounds as if it is endorsing crime rather than analyzing it. Phrases such as “you’re your own boss” and “you can retire well before the age of 65” create a promotional tone. In an academic essay, you want to sound analytical, not approving.

Revision advice:  
Adopt a more formal and neutral tone. For example, instead of saying crime has “advantages,” you might say it can offer “short-term financial incentives” while also carrying “serious legal and personal consequences.”

Organization

Your overall structure is solid: introduction, body paragraphs with separate points, and conclusion. However, the transitions could be stronger, and each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence. Right now, some ideas are repeated across paragraphs.

Revision advice:  
Try a structure like this:
Introduction: present the claim and define what “pay” means.
Body 1: perceived economic benefits of crime.
Body 2: instability and risks of legal employment versus illegal work.
Body 3: counterarguments—legal penalties, social harm, and long-term costs.
Conclusion: weigh both sides and make a final judgment.

Adding a counterargument paragraph would greatly improve the essay.

Grammar, word choice, and mechanics

There are several sentence-level errors that need correction. A few important examples:

“it’s advantages” → “its advantages”  
  (“It’s” = “it is”; “its” shows possession.)

“hard working person” → “hard-working person”

“rather or not” → “whether or not”

“laid off work” → “laid off” or “laid off from work”

“There is never a lack of work are a means” → likely “There is never a lack of work or a means of continuing employment.”

“will costs them” → “will cost them”

“specific numbers of hours” → “specific number of hours”

“If he/she invest” → “If he or she invests”

You also shift between “he/she,” “his/her,” and “they.” Choose one form and use it consistently. In modern academic writing, singular “they” is often the smoothest choice.

Style

Some sentences are wordy or awkward. For example:
“Let's take time to compare the criminal life to the life of the hard working person.”

This could be revised to:
“A comparison between criminal activity and lawful employment highlights differences in income, stability, and risk.”

That version is more formal and precise.

Final recommendation

Your essay has a clear thesis and a logical basic structure, but it needs more credible evidence, more balanced reasoning, and careful editing. The biggest improvement would come from addressing the long-term consequences of crime and acknowledging that apparent short-term profits may be outweighed by serious risks. If you revise with a more academic tone and stronger support, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear argument: for some inner-city youths, crime appears financially rewarding because poverty limits alternatives and punishment is inconsistent. The paper has a strong sense of purpose and a direct style, but to revise effectively, you need to strengthen your reasoning, tone, and evidence.

Clarify and refine your thesis
Your main claim appears in the final paragraph: “It is obvious that crime pays.” This is arguable, but in its current form it is too absolute. It also risks oversimplifying a complex social issue.

Consider revising the thesis so it is more precise and defensible. For example:
“For some low-income urban youths, crime can appear to offer faster financial rewards than legitimate work because of poverty, limited opportunity, and weak deterrence.”

This version avoids claiming that crime always pays and instead focuses on perception and circumstances.

Reduce overgeneralization
A major weakness in the essay is that it treats “inner-city youth” and “criminals” as broad, uniform groups. Statements such as:
“In many cases the answer is simple: crime.”
“Police are no threat.”
“There are few alternatives for low-income youths.”

These claims are sweeping and need qualification. Not all visibly wealthy young people obtained money through crime, and not all low-income youth see crime as their best option. Overgeneralization can weaken credibility and make the essay sound biased.

Use language such as:
“in some cases”
“for certain youths”
“may seem”
“can create the perception”

This will make your argument more persuasive and academically responsible.

Use evidence instead of assumption
Right now, the essay relies almost entirely on observation and assertion. You describe luxury cars, clothes, and public attitudes, but you do not provide statistics, studies, or examples to support your points.

To strengthen the essay, add evidence such as:
poverty and unemployment rates in urban areas
recidivism or incarceration data
research on deterrence and policing
wage comparisons between low-wage work and illegal markets
sociological studies about opportunity, status, and crime

For example, instead of saying “crime pays,” you could show how certain illegal activities may produce quick cash compared with minimum-wage labor, while also acknowledging the long-term risks.

Address counterarguments
An argumentative essay becomes stronger when it recognizes opposing views. Your paper currently assumes the issue is straightforward. A reader may object that:
crime also leads to imprisonment, violence, trauma, and death
many poor youths reject crime despite hardship
luxury goods do not prove criminal income
education, community programs, and work do offer alternatives

Addressing these points will make your argument more balanced. You might say that while crime may appear profitable in the short term, it often carries severe long-term costs. This would also help you avoid sounding as if you are endorsing criminal behavior.

Reconsider tone and word choice
Some language in the essay sounds judgmental, stereotypical, or sensationalized. Phrases like “thug,” “killing babies,” and “smoking as much weed as he or she can get his or her hands on” undermine the seriousness of your analysis. Academic writing works best when it is controlled and precise.

Also, your opening relies on brand names and visual stereotypes. While vivid, this approach may come across as anecdotal rather than analytical. Try opening with the larger issue—poverty, opportunity, and incentives—rather than with assumptions about appearance.

Improve organization and development
The essay has a recognizable progression, but several paragraphs repeat the same core point: crime offers money and the justice system does not stop it effectively. To improve structure, consider organizing by clear reasons:

Economic pressure and limited legitimate opportunities
The visible appeal of criminal success
Weak deterrence and inconsistent punishment
Why this perception persists despite the risks

This structure would help each paragraph do a distinct job.

Sentence-level revisions
There are several grammar and mechanics issues that should be corrected:
“wether” → whether
“The only danger that exists for a criminal come” → comes
“The public calls for more police but refuse” → refuses
“an overbearing boss who is stuck in a service job with no en in sight” → likely no end in sight
Extra punctuation: “Guess? clothing” and “. .”
Some sentences are wordy or awkward and would benefit from simplification.

Also watch consistency with pronouns and phrasing; repeated “he or she” constructions can sound cumbersome.

Suggested next step
As you revise, aim to shift from a forceful opinion piece to a supported academic argument. Keep your central insight—that crime may appear economically rational under certain social conditions—but present it with nuance, evidence, and balance.

Final assessment
Your essay has a strong central concern and engages an important social issue. Its biggest strengths are clarity of position and urgency. Its main areas for revision are evidence, fairness, tone, and precision. If you qualify your claims, support them with research, and organize your reasoning more carefully, the essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting central idea: computers have made life easier physically while creating new mental, social, and ethical problems. That tension gives you a strong basis for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, sharpening your argument, and improving tone, organization, and evidence.

Main strengths
Clear topic and relevance: You address an important issue with broad social significance.
Debatable claim: The idea that computers are both beneficial and harmful is arguable and worth discussing.
Attempt at balance: You do not present computers as simply good or bad; this gives the essay nuance.

Thesis and argument
Your current thesis is: “The computer has made life easier from a physical standpoint and harder from a mental standpoint.” This is a workable starting point, but it is still somewhat vague.

How to improve it
Make the thesis more precise by naming the specific “mental” consequences you want to discuss. For example:
“Although computers have increased efficiency in offices and factories, they have also encouraged overreliance on automated calculation and contributed to worker anxiety and job displacement.”

This version gives readers a clearer roadmap for the body paragraphs.

Organization
The essay has an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion, but the body needs a more logical progression. Right now, some ideas repeat or jump too quickly.

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - Brief background on the rise of computers
   - Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Physical/economic benefits
   - Faster office work
   - Automation and efficiency
   - Everyday convenience

Body Paragraph 2: Intellectual/educational drawbacks
   - Overreliance on computers for calculations
   - Reduced understanding of underlying processes
   - Risk of accepting outputs uncritically

Body Paragraph 3: Social and emotional consequences
   - Job displacement
   - Worker insecurity
   - Anxiety about technological change

Conclusion
   - Restate argument
   - Emphasize need for education and critical use of technology

Using clearer topic sentences will also help readers follow the progression of your ideas.

Use of evidence
The biggest weakness in the essay is the lack of concrete evidence. Many claims are broad but unsupported, such as:
computers have eliminated many jobs
mathematical models are “absolutely riddled with errors”
negative emotions have risen with computer usage

To revise
Add specific examples, studies, or historical cases. For instance:
A factory or industry transformed by automation
An example of spreadsheet or software errors causing real-world problems
Evidence about technological unemployment or worker retraining

Without evidence, the essay sounds more opinion-based than argumentative.

Tone and word choice
Some phrases weaken your credibility because they sound exaggerated, informal, or insensitive.

Problematic examples
“everyone already knows” — dismissive and unnecessary
“except for people in 2nd & 3rd world countries” — outdated and inappropriate wording
“needless computations” — unclear and judgmental
“humans are ignorant” — too broad and harsh

Better approach
Use precise, academic language:
“Computers are widely used across many societies, though access remains unequal.”
“Users may rely on computational tools without fully understanding the underlying methods.”
“Human error in programming or data input can lead to inaccurate results.”

A more measured tone will make your argument stronger.

Paragraph development
Several paragraphs list ideas rather than fully developing them. Try using this pattern:
Topic sentence
Explanation
Example
Analysis connecting back to the thesis

For example, in the paragraph about mathematical computations, you could explain not only that computers make advanced calculations easier, but also why that ease may discourage deeper understanding and how that creates risks.

Style and grammar
There are recurring sentence-level issues.

Common problems
Wordiness: “there have been many technological advances and also there has been a tremendous impact on society”
Informal abbreviations: “eg” and “etc” are overused
Possessives/plurals: “1940's” should be 1940s
Agreement and phrasing: “Due to this errors frequently exist” should be “As a result, errors frequently occur”
Repetition: “effects,” “errors,” and “computer” appear too often without variation

Revision tips
Prefer shorter, clearer sentences.
Avoid “etc” in formal essays; be specific instead.
Proofread for punctuation and grammar.
Vary sentence openings to improve flow.

Your conclusion points toward education as a solution, which is promising. However, it feels somewhat tentative because of phrases like “probably” and “in either case.” End more confidently by tying your solution directly to your thesis.

For example:

Revision priorities

Rewrite the thesis to make it more specific.
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct claims.
Add concrete evidence and examples.
Replace exaggerated or informal language with academic phrasing.
Edit for grammar, precision, and concision.

Overall, your essay has a solid core idea, but it needs stronger support and a more academic style to become persuasive. With a clearer thesis and better-developed evidence, you can turn this into a thoughtful argument about the complex impact of computers on society.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear and engaging topic: the computer has changed human life in both positive and negative ways. The strongest aspect of the paper is that you do not take an overly simple position. Instead, you acknowledge benefits such as efficiency and convenience while also raising concerns about depersonalization and overreliance on machines. That balanced approach gives your essay potential for a strong argumentative structure.

What is working well

Clear focus on the topic  
From the introduction onward, the essay stays centered on how computers affect people’s lives. This consistency helps the reader follow your ideas.

Balanced perspective  
You identify both advantages and disadvantages. For example, you discuss personal computers, ATMs, and medical technology as benefits, while also pointing to job loss, reduced human interaction, and dependence on machines as drawbacks.

Relevant examples  
Your examples are familiar and concrete, especially banking technology and safety systems in cars. These examples help make the argument understandable.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your thesis is broad: computers have changed life in good and bad ways. That is true, but it is not yet a strong argumentative claim. A stronger thesis should make a clearer position about the overall impact.

For example, you might revise it to something like:

“Although computers have made daily life faster, cheaper, and more efficient, they have also reduced human interaction and increased people’s dependence on technology; therefore, society should use computers carefully rather than allow them to replace human judgment.”

This version is more specific and argumentative.

Improve organization
Your essay currently has an introduction, a paragraph on benefits, and a paragraph on drawbacks, which is a good start. However, some ideas in the final paragraph-like section are packed together and could be organized more clearly.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: background + thesis  
Body Paragraph 1: computers improve efficiency and convenience  
Body Paragraph 2: computers reduce human interaction and replace jobs  
Body Paragraph 3: computers create risks when people rely on them too much  
Conclusion: restate argument and suggest a balanced approach

This would help each paragraph develop one main idea fully.

Develop analysis more fully
At times, the essay states opinions without explaining them enough. For example:

“People soon stop thinking, and allow machines to do the work for them.”
“Computers have also made live transpire much quicker than before.”

These claims need more explanation. Ask yourself: Why? How? What evidence or example shows this?  
For instance, if you argue that computers reduce independent thinking, you could explain how GPS, autocorrect, or automated systems make people less likely to solve problems on their own.

Use a more formal academic tone
The essay is readable, but it relies heavily on phrases like “I feel,” “I miss,” “I hope,” and “EVERYTHING.” In an academic argument, your points will sound stronger if you present them more directly and formally.

For example:

Instead of “I feel the invention of the computer has significantly changed people's lives,” write “The invention of the computer has significantly changed people’s lives.”
Instead of “It seems these days EVERYTHING is run by computers!” write “Many parts of modern life now depend on computers.”

This makes your argument sound more confident and polished.

Revise sentence clarity and correctness
There are several grammar, spelling, and word-choice issues that distract from your ideas. Here are some important ones:

“people’s life styles” → “people’s lifestyles”
“live transpire much quicker” → likely “life move much more quickly”
“ATM” → “ATMs”
“proceed with the same transactions” → “perform the same transactions”
“Interraction” → “Interaction”
“obstical” → “obstacle”
“an on-coming obstical is approaching” is repetitive; better: “when an oncoming obstacle is nearby”
“incapable, in some instances to function” → “incapable, in some instances, of functioning”

Also watch comma use. For example:
“These changes resulting from the invention of the computer, have been…”  
  The comma after “computer” is unnecessary.

Suggested revision priorities

Write a stronger, more specific thesis
Separate your ideas into clearer body paragraphs
Replace informal phrasing with a more academic tone
Add explanation after each major claim
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and punctuation

Final comment

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a sincere and humane argument: greater cultural awareness and the unlearning of prejudice are among the most important developments of the modern world. Your values are clear, and your writing shows genuine commitment to equality and peace. That strong sense of purpose is a good foundation. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: clarifying your thesis, strengthening your argument, and editing for grammar and word choice.

Clarify the central argument

Right now, your essay begins by saying that “the most significant discovery or invention will depend on the individual.” This makes your argument sound uncertain. Then you shift to your own choice: cultural awareness and understanding prejudice/discrimination. Because this is an argumentative essay, you should make a clear, direct claim early on.

For example, instead of beginning with a general statement about individual opinion, you could say something like:

“One of the most significant developments of the twentieth century was the growth of cultural awareness and the recognition that prejudice and discrimination must be challenged.”

This is stronger because it states your position clearly and prepares the reader for the rest of the essay.

Improve focus and organization

Your essay has one main paragraph that contains many ideas: racism in society, media influence, historical inequality, social progress, and the importance of accepting difference. These are all relevant, but they need clearer structure.

Try organizing the body into separate points, such as:

Why prejudice and discrimination were such major social problems
How people began to challenge these ideas
Why cultural awareness is important for the future of humanity

This would make your essay easier to follow. Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and then develop one main idea.

For example:

Paragraph 1: Explain how discrimination was historically accepted and embedded in institutions and media.
Paragraph 2: Discuss social movements or courageous individuals who challenged inequality.
Paragraph 3: Show why cultural awareness benefits society and supports peaceful coexistence.

Use more specific support

At the moment, your essay relies mostly on broad statements. These ideas are thoughtful, but argument writing becomes more convincing when you include specific examples.

You mention discrimination against African Americans, other ethnic groups, and women. You could strengthen this by referring to examples such as:

the Civil Rights Movement
the women’s rights movement
anti-discrimination laws
increased multicultural education

Even one or two concrete examples would make your argument much more persuasive.

Be careful with word choice and historical framing

One important issue is that “cultural awareness” is not exactly a discovery or invention in the usual sense. Since the prompt appears to ask about a “discovery or invention,” you need to explain more clearly why your topic fits. You might describe it as a major social development, transformation in human understanding, or important change in modern society.

That way, you acknowledge that your topic is different from a scientific invention while still defending its significance.

Revise sentence structure for clarity

Some of your sentences are thoughtful but too long or grammatically unclear. Breaking them into shorter sentences will improve readability.

For example, this sentence:

“People are understanding that prejudice and discrimination are not consciously learned but they must be unlearned.”

could be revised to:

“People are beginning to understand that prejudice is often absorbed from society, and that it must be actively unlearned.”

This is clearer and more precise.

Edit grammar, spelling, and mechanics

There are several errors that distract from your ideas. Here are some examples:

“world wide” → worldwide
“peoples lives” → people’s lives
“discriminitive” → discriminatory
“more then” → more than
“excepted” → accepted
“enought” → enough
“breed sterotypes” → deeply rooted stereotypes
“burried” → buried
“forms” → perhaps forums
“avalibility” → availability
“existance” → existence

Also watch for comma splices, where two complete ideas are joined only by a comma.

Strengthen the conclusion

Your final sentences are meaningful, but the ending could more powerfully restate your argument. Rather than simply saying we need each other, connect back to your main claim:

“For this reason, the growth of cultural awareness and the rejection of prejudice may be considered one of the most important developments of the twentieth century, because they create the possibility of a more just and peaceful world.”

Final thoughts

Your essay’s greatest strength is its moral seriousness. You clearly care about the topic, and that sincerity gives the piece emotional force. For revision, work on making your position more precise, adding concrete examples, dividing your ideas into clearer paragraphs, and correcting grammar and spelling. With those changes, your essay will become much more persuasive and polished.

Clarify your thesis

A stronger thesis might be:

This gives your essay a clear argument and helps readers understand what you will prove.

Lack of geography knowledge
Weak language education
Limited awareness of other cultures

Conclusion
Reaffirm that technology should be used to expand understanding, not narrow it

This structure will help your reader follow your reasoning.

You use personal experience well, but some claims are too broad without enough support. For example:

“a great majority of Americans live in their own personal closets”
“Geography classes rarely, if ever, exist”
“Our awareness of other people, other cultures, and other languages is very low”

These are strong claims, but they need either:
specific examples, or  
more careful wording

Instead of making absolute statements, you could qualify them:

“This can leave students less prepared to understand other cultures.”

This sounds more credible and academic.

Explain the connection to technology more directly

One of the biggest revision needs is that the essay sometimes criticizes education or American attitudes, but does not always show exactly how technology causes or worsens these problems. That connection must be made more explicit.

For example, do you mean that technology:
makes people feel they do not need to learn about the world because information is instantly available?
encourages entertainment and self-centered habits?
creates the illusion of connection without real understanding?

Choose one or two of these ideas and explain them clearly. Otherwise, the essay risks sounding like a criticism of American schooling rather than an argument about technology.

Revise style for academic tone

You have vivid language, but some phrases are too dramatic or informal for an academic argumentative essay.

Examples:
“This thing, technology”
“the greatest Nemesis”
“personal closets”
“selfish self-satisfaction”

These phrases show emotion, but they can weaken precision. Aim for language that is strong but controlled.

For instance:
“Technology has become both a powerful benefit and a serious challenge.”

Comma splices/run-on sentences
Unclear pronoun reference
Incorrect or awkward phrasing
Hyphenation and punctuation

Better:
> “Science and technology have transformed life in the United States in ways that are difficult to fully describe.”

Original:
> “Since I was twelve, I had an intense desire to learn Spanish…”

Better:
> “Since I was twelve, I had wanted to learn Spanish…”

Original:

Final advice

Replace sweeping generalizations with qualified, specific claims
Edit for grammar, clarity, and academic tone

Thesis and argument

   - Why dependence on computers can be risky

   - End with a final thought about modern dependence on technology

computers manage inventory,
organize financial records,
send information instantly,
automate repetitive tasks.

How?
Why?
For example?

Repetition
You repeat ideas such as:

computers make tasks easier,
people rely on computers.

Feedback Report

Your essay has an ambitious and interesting central concern: you argue that Einstein’s theory of relativity changed not only science but also humanity’s worldview, and that its most visible social consequence has been the development of atomic weapons and a global politics shaped by fear. That is a compelling idea. The draft shows intellectual curiosity and a willingness to connect science, philosophy, and politics. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving accuracy, and organizing your ideas more clearly.

Clarify the main argument

Right now, the essay raises several large claims:
relativity changed human philosophy,
time is a human construction,
Einstein’s ideas have not had their full impact,
atomic bombs are the main social consequence of Einstein’s work,
humanity should move from fear to peace.

These ideas are potentially related, but the essay does not yet explain their connection clearly enough. Try to state one main claim in the introduction and let the rest of the essay support it.

For example, a stronger thesis might be:
“Einstein’s theory of relativity transformed modern thought by challenging absolute views of time and knowledge; however, the twentieth century applied scientific discovery more readily to warfare than to philosophical self-understanding.”

This kind of thesis gives your paper a clearer direction.

Improve organization

The essay currently moves quickly from relativity, to philosophy, to time, to atomic bombs, to global politics. Readers need clearer transitions and paragraph focus.

A possible structure:
Introduction: introduce Einstein and your main argument.
Relativity and philosophy: explain how relativity challenged older ideas of certainty and absolutes.
Time and human perception: discuss your point that ideas of time are socially constructed and changed by relativity.
Historical consequences: explain the link between Einstein, modern physics, and atomic weapons.
Conclusion: return to your main claim and argue for a more humane use of scientific knowledge.

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that tells the reader its main point.

Support big claims with explanation

Many of your ideas are intriguing, but they remain too abstract. For example:
“What Einstein did to humanity’s philosophy was change the notion that closure could be obtained.”
“The philosopher’s stone was pulverized into utopic and nostalgic dust.”
“Fear has been the main point of departure for the global community construction.”

These are vivid statements, but they need explanation. Ask yourself:
What exactly do you mean by “closure”?
Which older philosophy are you referring to?
How did relativity change human thinking in practical terms?
In what ways did fear shape the postwar world?

Whenever you make a large claim, follow it with an example or fuller reasoning.

Check factual and conceptual accuracy

Some statements need revision for accuracy:
Einstein is spelled Einstein, not “Einstien.”
Stephen Hawking is spelled Hawking, not “Hawkings.”
Relativity did not simply “bring” quantum mechanics; they are distinct developments in modern physics.
Atomic bombs were influenced by developments in physics, but it is too simplistic to call them Einstein’s “invention.” Be more precise: Einstein’s work contributed to modern physics, and his letter to Roosevelt is historically relevant, but he did not design the bomb.
The claim about “his and his wife’s” theory needs strong evidence if included. If you mean Mileva Marić, this is a debated issue and would require documentation.

Because your essay is argumentative, credibility matters. Be careful not to overstate historical connections.

Strengthen style and tone

Your voice is thoughtful, but some phrases are informal or unclear for an academic essay:
“came onto the scene”
“grasping for closure”
“if you will”

Also, rhetorical fragments such as “Many things have been generated: namely fear!” can be effective in speech, but in formal writing they should usually be integrated into complete sentences.

Aim for precise, direct sentences. For instance:
Instead of “Personally, I find that relativity hasn’t had its full impact,” write:
  “I argue that relativity’s broader philosophical implications have not yet been fully absorbed by society.”

Revise grammar and mechanics

There are several sentence-level issues:
possessives: “history’s recorded history” / “histories” is incorrect
capitalization: “Socratic” should be capitalized
punctuation: avoid unnecessary semicolons and random capitalization, such as “And” in the middle of a sentence
spelling: “nostalgic,” “shortcomings,” “Einstein”
fragments: “The Socratic idea that all was known.” is not a complete sentence

Read the paper aloud during revision; this will help you hear where sentences are incomplete or awkward.

Final advice

Your essay’s strength is its ambition. You are trying to connect scientific theory to the human condition, which is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. In revision, do three things above all:
write a precise thesis,
organize each paragraph around one clear point,
support your claims with accurate explanation and evidence.

If you do that, your essay will become much more persuasive while keeping its original intellectual energy.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: shopping malls have harmed Kalamazoo more than they have helped it. That central claim gives the paper direction, and you also show strong awareness of how geography, transportation, class, and business development are connected. The essay’s best quality is that it tries to explain *why* malls matter socially, not just economically. That is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear argumentative stance  
   You take a definite position in the introduction: malls are “more of a detriment than a blessing.” This gives the reader a clear sense of your purpose.

Use of local example  
   Focusing on Kalamazoo makes the essay more concrete. Your discussion of downtown, suburbs, colleges, and festivals helps ground the argument in a specific place.

Cause-and-effect thinking  
   You do more than state an opinion; you trace a process: transportation changes -> suburban movement -> relocation of businesses -> decline of downtown -> harm to poorer residents. That logical chain is promising.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen and narrow the thesis
Right now, your thesis states your opinion, but it could be more specific about *how* malls are harmful. Try revising it so it names your main reasons.

For example:  
“Although shopping malls offer convenience, they have damaged Kalamazoo by drawing businesses and wealth away from downtown, increasing class division, and leaving poorer residents with fewer economic opportunities.”

This version gives the essay a clearer roadmap.

Improve organization
The essay currently moves through several ideas, but some paragraphs feel loosely connected. A stronger structure would help your reader follow the argument.

Consider this structure:
Introduction: present thesis
Body paragraph 1: explain how downtown once functioned as a shared center
Body paragraph 2: show how automobiles, suburbs, and malls shifted businesses outward
Body paragraph 3: explain who is most harmed and why
Body paragraph 4: discuss counterpoints, such as colleges and festivals helping downtown somewhat
Conclusion: restate argument and emphasize broader consequences

Also, avoid single-sentence fragments such as:  
“Everyone was near the central city - so everyone could use the central city.”  
That idea is useful, but it should be integrated into a full paragraph.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some parts sound conversational or emotional rather than analytical. For example:
“Why should we, say those with cars…”
“and still could be!”

Instead of rhetorical questions and exclamations, explain the point directly. For example:
“Residents with cars often prefer malls because they offer convenience, safety, and a concentration of stores in one location.”

That keeps your voice persuasive while sounding more academic.

Clarify and support your claims
Many claims are interesting, but they need clearer explanation or evidence. For example:
Did malls directly cause downtown decline, or were they one factor among several?
What kinds of businesses left downtown?
How exactly were poor residents harmed?

Try to support claims with examples, statistics, historical details, or even local observations. Even one or two specific details would make the argument stronger.

For instance, instead of saying downtown festivals “always attract those from the suburbs,” you could say:
“Downtown festivals temporarily bring suburban residents back into the city, but these events do not provide the consistent commercial activity that year-round local businesses need.”

Address word choice and correctness
There are several grammar, spelling, and usage issues that weaken the paper’s credibility. Some examples:

“town home town” -> “hometown”
“the could keep” -> “they could keep”
“clientel” -> “clientele”
“There neighborhoods” -> “Their neighborhoods”
“flu from the inner cities” -> likely “flight from the inner cities”
“many business try” -> “many businesses try”
“office building” -> “office buildings”

Be especially careful with:
apostrophes
plural nouns
sentence fragments
capitalization
spacing and punctuation

Avoid overgeneralizations
A few statements are too broad:
“Throughout history, the trend is for the rich to get richer and the poor remain poor.”
“If they could have more money and leave, the majority would.”

These may express frustration, but they are too sweeping unless you prove them. Try using more precise language:

Final revision advice

When revising, focus first on argument and structure, then on style and editing. A good plan would be:

Rewrite the thesis so it lists your main reasons.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one has one clear main point.
Replace rhetorical or informal language with direct analysis.
Add specific support and explanation.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice.

You have the basis for a thoughtful essay about class, space, and urban change. With clearer organization, stronger evidence, and cleaner style, your argument will become much more persuasive.

What is working well

Clear thesis and consistent focus

studies about television viewing habits,

or even a more developed explanation of how and why television affects behavior.

Without support, some statements sound like opinion rather than argument.

Not all viewers use television the same way, and not all television programming is shallow entertainment. A more precise argument would be stronger. For instance, you say television informs society but also makes people “too lazy to think about this new information.” That is an interesting idea, but it needs explanation. “has potential” → “has the potential”
“than cannot be given by newspaper” → “that cannot be given by newspapers”
“integrety” → “integrity”
“dificulties” → “difficulties”
“becomed” → “become”
“00 years ago” → likely “100 years ago”
inconsistent use of TV / T.V. / television — choose one form and use it consistently
extra spacing and punctuation issues appear throughout
