Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central claim: the polio vaccine was one of the most important inventions of the twentieth century because it reduced suffering and helped shape later medical progress. That is a strong argumentative direction, and your writing shows genuine engagement with the topic. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument development, evidence and logic, and style/mechanics.

Strengthen the thesis

Right now, your thesis is present but broad and somewhat overstated:

> “The effects of such a vaccine are countless and inequivalent to any other invention of the 20th century.”

This makes a very large claim that would be difficult to prove. A stronger thesis should be specific, arguable, and supportable. For example:

Possible revision:  
*The polio vaccine was one of the most significant medical developments of the twentieth century because it dramatically reduced child disability and death, eased public fear, and encouraged confidence in modern vaccination research.*

This version gives you clear points to develop in the body paragraphs.

Improve organization and paragraph focus

Your essay currently has an introduction, a long middle paragraph, and a conclusion, but the body paragraph tries to cover too many ideas at once: public fear, generational differences, the Baby Boom, population growth, disease control, and medical progress.

Try organizing your essay so that each paragraph develops one reason from your thesis.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction: background on polio + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: how polio affected children and families before the vaccine  
Body paragraph 2: how the vaccine reduced death and disability  
Body paragraph 3: how the vaccine advanced public trust in medicine and future vaccine research  
Conclusion: restate significance

This will make your argument easier to follow.

Use stronger evidence and avoid unsupported claims

The essay often relies on general statements rather than evidence. For example:

> “The polio vaccine stopped death in its path.”  
> “Usually disease plays a role of population control.”  
> “Every few years a new one comes along to normalize an overpopulated world.”

These claims sound dramatic, but they are too sweeping and can weaken your credibility. In academic argument, you should avoid statements that seem speculative, emotional, or scientifically unsupported.

Also, the connection to the Baby Boom is not convincing as written. You admit “whether it had anything to do with the Baby Boom is for each individual to decide,” which suggests uncertainty. If you cannot support that point with evidence, it would be better to remove it and focus on claims you can prove more clearly.

Instead, include concrete facts such as:
how widespread polio was before the vaccine
how many children were paralyzed or killed
when Salk’s vaccine was introduced
how case numbers dropped after vaccination

Even one or two specific facts would make your argument much stronger.

Clarify your tone and word choice

Your voice is passionate, which is a strength, but some phrases are too informal or conversational for an academic essay:

“For sure”
“I’m sure”
“Things weren’t impossible any more”
“the new dreaded ailment is AIDS”

Aim for a more formal tone. Also, some word choices are inaccurate:
“inequivalent” is not the best word here; perhaps *unmatched* or *unequaled*
“mamed” should be *maimed*
“future’s children” would be better as *future generations*

Be careful with comparisons. Calling polio “the greatest invention and discovery” is confusing because a vaccine is an invention/product, while the science behind it is a discovery/development.

Revise sentence structure and grammar

There are many run-on sentences, awkward phrasing, and punctuation issues. A few examples:

“For centuries before, millions of people were left dead, or permanently mamed because of disease.”  
  Better: *For centuries, millions of people died or were permanently maimed by disease.*

“Unfortunately this still occurs, but with increasing medical & technological advances starting at the middle of the century many people have been rescued from the torment of disease.”  
  Better: *Although this still occurs, medical and technological advances in the mid-twentieth century saved many people from debilitating disease.*

Other things to fix:
Avoid using &; write and
Watch unnecessary capitalization, such as CAUSE
Remove repeated words: “never never”
Be careful with spacing and punctuation around quotation marks and hyphens

Make the conclusion more persuasive

Your conclusion is brief and somewhat vague:

> “Thanks to Dr. Salk we are a little bit more protected than we would have been before...”

This understates your own argument. If the vaccine was transformative, say so directly. A stronger conclusion should remind readers of the vaccine’s historical importance and broader legacy.

Possible revision:  
*The polio vaccine changed modern history by preventing paralysis and death, reducing fear for families, and proving that scientific research could defeat once-terrifying diseases. Its success helped lay the groundwork for later medical advances and remains one of the clearest examples of the power of public health.*

Final priorities for revision
Write a more precise thesis.  
Remove weak or speculative claims, especially about population control and the Baby Boom unless you have evidence.  
Add specific historical evidence.  
Divide the body into clearer paragraphs with one main idea each.  
Edit for formal tone, grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

You have a meaningful topic and a strong sense of its importance. With clearer structure and stronger evidence, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a sensible historical progression: you begin with the discovery of penicillin, move to the expansion of antibiotic research, and end with its broader effects on medicine and industry. The piece shows that you understand penicillin’s importance and that one scientific discovery can transform both healthcare and research. For revision, the main areas to improve are argument, accuracy, specificity, and style.

Strengthen the central argument
Right now, the essay reads more like a brief overview than an argumentative essay. You describe what happened after penicillin’s discovery, but you do not clearly state a debatable claim.

Consider developing a thesis such as:
*The discovery of penicillin not only revolutionized treatment of bacterial infections but also reshaped pharmaceutical research by encouraging the large-scale development and synthesis of antibiotics.*
Or, if you want a more critical argument: *Although penicillin transformed medicine and launched the modern antibiotic industry, its widespread use also changed public attitudes toward medicine in ways that created new problems.*

A stronger thesis will help the reader understand what you are trying to prove, not just what you are summarizing.

Add specific evidence
The biggest weakness is the lack of concrete support. You make broad claims, but they need examples, dates, names, or facts.

For example:
When was penicillin discovered, and by whom?
How did it reduce deaths from infection?
What specific antibiotics followed penicillin?
What evidence shows that the pharmaceutical industry “increased tremendously”?
Why is Upjohn important here?

Adding even a few well-chosen details would make your claims more persuasive. For instance, mentioning Alexander Fleming, the later development of mass production during World War II, or examples of semi-synthetic antibiotics would give your essay authority.

Improve historical and scientific accuracy
A few points need revision for accuracy:

Penicillin was discovered from mold, not “a small bacteria.” The producer is generally identified as *Penicillium* mold.
“Medicinal chemistry” may not be the best term for the original discovery; “medicine” or “modern antimicrobial therapy” may fit better unless you specifically discuss chemical development.
Antibiotics do not treat the common cold, which is caused by viruses. This sentence is especially problematic because it suggests inappropriate antibiotic use. If you want to discuss public attitudes, you could revise it to say that antibiotics became widely sought even for illnesses they could not effectively treat.
“Naturally-occurring antibiotics that could also be cultured ‘in vitro’” is somewhat imprecise. The microorganisms are cultured; the antibiotic compounds are produced or isolated.

These corrections will make your essay more credible.

Clarify and refine your wording
Several sentences are understandable but vague or awkward. Examples:

“The discovery of one compound led to the increase of health of millions of people”  
  Better: *The discovery of penicillin improved the health and survival of millions of people worldwide.*

“research of their uses”  
  Better: *research into their uses*

“It was also attemped”  
  Correct spelling: *attempted*

You also rely heavily on passive voice (“It was discovered,” “It was attempted,” “were formed and grew”). Passive voice is not always wrong, but too much of it makes the prose less direct. Try using stronger subjects:
*Chemists discovered…*
*Pharmaceutical companies expanded…*
*Researchers developed…*

Develop paragraph focus
Your paragraphs generally follow chronology, which is good, but each one could have a clearer purpose.

A possible structure:
Introduction: Present penicillin and your thesis.
Medical impact: Explain how penicillin transformed treatment of bacterial infections.
Scientific and industrial impact: Show how it led to antibiotic discovery, synthesis, and growth in pharmaceutical research.
Conclusion: Explain the long-term significance and, if desired, note complications such as overuse or resistance.

This would help your essay feel more organized and argumentative.

Make the conclusion more powerful
Your conclusion summarizes the essay, but it could do more than repeat earlier points. Try ending with a broader insight. For example:
Why does penicillin still matter today?
How did it shape modern drug development?
What lessons does its history offer about science and medicine?

A strong conclusion should leave the reader with the significance of your argument.

Revision priorities
Write a clearer, more debatable thesis.
Add specific historical and scientific evidence.
Correct factual inaccuracies, especially about bacteria/mold and the common cold.
Replace vague or passive phrasing with more precise language.
Organize paragraphs around distinct argumentative points.

You have a solid foundation and a worthwhile topic. With a stronger thesis and better evidence, this essay could become much more convincing and academically effective.

Strengths

Logical organization: The essay moves through three main areas of impact: entertainment, communication, and family/family values.
Balanced approach: You do not simply praise television; you also consider negative consequences, which strengthens the argument.

Your introduction gives a general claim that television changed entertainment, communication, and family life. However, the thesis could be more precise and arguable. Right now, the essay sometimes sounds descriptive rather than argumentative.

This version is more specific and clearly argumentative.

live news broadcasts,
emergency weather warnings,

television as a shared family activity, and  
television as a source of reduced conversation and harmful content.

That would allow you to develop each point more fully.

The essay makes many claims, but they are mostly based on opinion. In academic writing, your argument becomes stronger when you include:
historical examples,
statistics,
research findings,
or at least more concrete illustrations.

For example, instead of saying television “creates problems with values,” explain how or provide a specific example of concern about violent programming.

Your essay is readable, but some word choices are too informal for academic writing.

“T.V.” → in formal writing, use “television” consistently
“it is anyone’s guess” → too conversational; replace with “the debate remains unresolved”

Also, avoid phrases like “Of course” and “I would like to discuss,” which are unnecessary. Academic introductions are usually stronger when they begin directly with the topic.

Spelling/word form

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: modern inventions like the washing machine make life easier, but they may also contribute to physical inactivity. This is an interesting argument because it moves beyond simply praising technology and tries to consider a hidden cost. The essay also has a lively, humorous tone, especially in lines such as people “clutching the machine tightly to their chests.” That voice makes the piece engaging. For revision, focus on making the argument clearer, more formal, and better supported.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, the essay begins by praising invention, then jokes about dependence on washing machines, and finally argues that labor-saving devices reduce exercise and may contribute to weight problems. The final claim seems to be your real argument, but it appears quite late.

Try stating your main point directly in the introduction. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although washing machines and other household technologies save time and reduce physical labor, they also encourage a more sedentary lifestyle, which can have negative effects on health.*

This would help readers understand the direction of the essay from the start.

Strengthen organization
The essay currently reads more like a series of reflections than a fully developed argument. A clearer structure would make it more persuasive.

You might organize it like this:
Introduction: Present the topic and thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Explain the benefits of washing machines (time-saving, convenience, hygiene).
Body paragraph 2: Discuss how dependence on labor-saving devices reduces physical activity.
Body paragraph 3: Address a counterargument—why convenience still matters, especially for busy people.
Conclusion: Weigh both sides and restate your position.

This would give the essay a stronger argumentative shape.

Develop your evidence
The biggest weakness is that the essay relies mostly on exaggeration and rhetorical questions rather than evidence. For example, the claim that modern devices contribute to obesity is plausible, but you do not prove it. Similarly, “was weight watchers ever an issue?” is memorable, but it oversimplifies history and does not function as evidence.

To improve, add:
examples from everyday life,
historical comparison,
facts or statistics about sedentary lifestyles,
or explanation of how less physical labor changes health habits.

Even one or two concrete examples would make the argument more convincing.

Adjust tone for an academic argument
Your humor and exaggeration make the essay entertaining, but some parts weaken the seriousness of the argument. For instance, the image of people saving a washing machine in a house fire is amusing, but it distracts from the point. Similarly, phrases like “millions and millions” and “What ever would a college student do” sound informal.

Try to keep some voice, but make it more controlled. Academic arguments usually work best when they balance style with precision.

Also be careful with terms like “man” and “housewives.” These sound outdated and may unintentionally exclude readers. Consider using:
people / humans / society
many adults / many households

Improve sentence-level correctness
There are several spelling, punctuation, and usage issues that you should correct in revision. Some examples:

continously → continuously
necesity → necessity
with out → without
What ever → Whatever
handclothes washing → perhaps hand-washing clothes
Avoid &; use and
Avoid excessive punctuation such as “?....”

You should also watch subject-verb agreement and phrasing. For example:
“The washing machine, in those houses that own them…”  
  This is awkward because washing machine is singular, while them is plural.  
  Better: “In households that own one, the washing machine has become almost a necessity.”

Make the conclusion more precise
The ending raises an interesting issue but feels abrupt and somewhat overstated. Instead of suggesting that older times had no health concerns, you could end by acknowledging complexity:

*While devices such as washing machines improve daily life, they also reduce routine physical effort. Rather than rejecting modern technology, society should recognize the need to balance convenience with healthy activity.*

This gives the essay a more mature and balanced conclusion.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis early in the introduction.
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct points.
Replace some exaggeration with real explanation or evidence.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Correct spelling, punctuation, and word choice errors.

You have a strong underlying idea and an engaging voice. With clearer structure and stronger support, this could become a thoughtful and persuasive argumentative essay.

Your essay has a clear position and an engaging, personal voice. Strong use of examples

Natural organization

Your essay has a clear central claim: the computer is the most influential invention of the 20th century because it changed how people live and work. The topic is strong, and your conclusion shows enthusiasm and a sense of historical importance. To revise effectively, focus on making the argument more precise, better supported, and more formally organized.

What is working well

Main areas to improve

how computers changed workplaces (banking, accounting, communication, design, research)
how they affected education, medicine, or science
examples of daily-life changes, such as email, online shopping, or digital records

This structure will help your reader follow your reasoning and make the essay feel more complete.

Current idea: The computer is the most influential invention of the 20th century because it changed the way people live and work.

You could revise it to something like:
Possible revision: The computer is the most influential invention of the 20th century because it revolutionized communication, increased workplace efficiency, and transformed access to information.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases sound informal or exaggerated:
“expensive monsters”
“new and exciting improvements”
“20th century miracle”
“looks ‘bright’ indeed”

In an argumentative essay, a more academic tone is usually stronger. For example:
“large and expensive machines”
“continuous technological improvements”
“a transformative invention”
“its future influence remains significant”

Also, the final lines become speculative and emotional. Predictions can work, but they should still connect clearly to your argument.

“their predecessors” is missing a space in the original text

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: to argue that AIDS changed society’s attitudes toward sexuality, public health, and mortality, while also exposing prejudice and the need for education and compassion. The topic is important, and you make several strong points, especially about stigma, fear, and the role of public awareness. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving organization, and developing your evidence more carefully.

Clarify and strengthen your main argument
Right now, your essay begins with: “One of the most important discoveries of the 20th Century would have to be AIDS.” This is not quite accurate, because AIDS was not a “discovery” in the sense of a beneficial invention or breakthrough; it is a disease whose emergence and identification transformed society. Consider revising the opening to reflect that distinction.

Also, your thesis is somewhat broad. You mention that AIDS changed “how people think, live, and view mortality,” but the essay later focuses more specifically on stigma, public education, and acceptance. A stronger thesis would name those points directly.

For example:
“The emergence of AIDS in the 1980s transformed society by exposing prejudice, increasing public awareness about sexual health, and forcing communities to confront mortality and compassion.”

This gives your essay a clearer argumentative direction.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Your essay generally follows a logical progression, but some paragraphs contain several different ideas at once. Try organizing each body paragraph around one main claim.

A possible structure:
Paragraph 1: Early reactions to AIDS and the stigma toward gay men and IV drug users  
Paragraph 2: Public fear and changing attitudes when children such as Ryan White were affected  
Paragraph 3: Education, prevention, and the debate over abstinence vs. condom access  
Paragraph 4: Conclusion about acceptance, public responsibility, and respect for AIDS victims

This structure would help the reader follow your argument more easily.

Develop evidence more carefully
You make compelling claims, but many need more support or explanation. For example:
“No one really cared that gays would die” is a very strong statement. It may capture widespread indifference, but it should be phrased more precisely and supported.
“Children are becoming sexually active at the age of eleven” also needs evidence if you want to include it.
“Most people are more careful about who they sleep with” is another broad generalization.

In revision, either support these claims with evidence or qualify them:
“Many people responded with indifference because AIDS was associated with marginalized groups.”
“Public health debates also reflected concern that some young adolescents were becoming sexually active before receiving adequate education.”

This will make your argument sound more credible and academic.

Use a more formal academic tone
Your essay is passionate, which is good, but some wording is too informal or sweeping for an argumentative essay. Examples include:
“How wrong they were.”
“It’s too bad that it takes a superstar like Magic Johnson…”
“no one really cared”

These phrases sound conversational. Try replacing them with a more formal tone:
“This assumption proved dangerously false.”
“Public attention increased dramatically when well-known figures such as Magic Johnson disclosed their diagnoses.”

A more academic tone will strengthen your authority as a writer.

Address word choice and historical accuracy
Be careful with terms and phrasing:
Use “affect” instead of “effect” in “they thought AIDS would never effect them.”
Consider saying “people living with AIDS” rather than “AIDS victims,” unless you specifically want to emphasize suffering. “Victims” can sound limiting or dated.
“contract AIDS” may be better as “contract HIV” in medically precise language, though depending on the context of the essay, your current usage reflects common public discussion.

Also, be cautious when saying AIDS was “widely publicized” first through Ryan White. You may want to say he became one of the most visible public cases, which is more accurate.

Revise sentence clarity and punctuation
Several sentences could be smoother and more polished. For example:
“It was seen everywhere (and still is)- on t.v., magazines, radio, & in the schools.”
  - More formal revision: “Public education campaigns appeared everywhere—on television, in magazines, on the radio, and in schools.”

Capitalization such as “20th Century” should usually be “20th century.”
Watch apostrophes: “80's” should be “1980s.”

“Even if a cure is never found.”
This should be connected to the next sentence:

Your conclusion has a strong moral message, but it could do more to tie back to your thesis. Instead of only ending with acceptance, remind the reader of the larger social impact of AIDS: it revealed prejudice, changed public health education, and forced society to confront human vulnerability.

Overall
This essay has a meaningful argument and strong emotional force. Your best ideas concern the relationship between fear, discrimination, and education. For revision, focus on:
creating a sharper thesis,
organizing paragraphs around clear claims,
supporting broad statements,
adopting a more formal tone,
correcting grammar and usage.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear moral perspective and a strong personal voice. You are trying to make a bold argument: that among twentieth-century inventions, only medical inventions have significantly improved people’s lives. This is an interesting and debatable claim, which is a good starting point for an argumentative essay. To revise successfully, focus on making your argument more precise, better supported, and more logically developed.

Clarify and narrow your thesis

Your main claim appears in the first paragraph:

> “the only inventions of the twentieth century that have significantly changed people's lives for the better are those that have occured in the medical field.”

This is a strong thesis, but it is also extremely broad and absolute. Because you say “only”, you must prove that all other major inventions have not improved life, which is very difficult. Consider softening or refining the claim. For example:

*Medical inventions have improved human life more consistently than most other twentieth-century inventions.*
*Among twentieth-century inventions, medical advances have had the most clearly beneficial impact on human life.*

A more focused thesis will be easier to defend.

Distinguish personal reflection from argument

Your introduction begins with personal reflection:

> “Interestingly enough, this topic is one that is very important to me.”

This creates voice, but in an academic argument, you should move more quickly to the issue and your claim. The opening currently spends a lot of time on general ideas about human nature, fear, and domination before reaching the thesis. Try to shorten this material and connect it more directly to the prompt.

For example, instead of discussing all technology as an expression of fear and insecurity, introduce the debate about whether inventions improve life, then present your position.

Develop your reasoning with evidence and examples

The biggest weakness in the essay is the lack of concrete support. Your claims are philosophical, but they remain general. For instance:

“We continue to destroy the plants, animals, and the earth itself and our new inventions help us to do it in more efficient ways.”
“We invent better weapons and better economic systems geared for killing and depriving other people.”
“Medical inventions… save lives.”

These points need examples. A strong revision should name specific inventions and explain their effects. For example:

Negative technological examples: nuclear weapons, industrial machinery that accelerates pollution, pesticides, or surveillance technologies.
Medical examples: antibiotics, vaccines, insulin, X-rays, MRI, organ transplantation.

Then explain *how* these inventions changed people’s lives. Evidence can come from historical examples, widely known facts, or logical explanation.

Address counterarguments

A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge that some non-medical inventions have clearly benefited people. For example, electricity, refrigeration, telephones, computers, and safer transportation have all improved daily life in major ways. Right now, your essay ignores these examples, which makes the argument feel one-sided.

You do not need to agree with them fully, but you should address them. For instance, you could argue:

while communication and transportation inventions have brought convenience, they have also increased environmental harm, inequality, or warfare;
medical inventions are different because their central purpose is preserving life.

This would make your essay more balanced and persuasive.

Strengthen paragraph structure

Each body paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence and then develop one main idea with explanation and examples. Right now, the paragraphs are short and underdeveloped.

A possible structure:

Paragraph 1: Many inventions damage nature; explain with examples.
Paragraph 2: Many inventions are used to dominate or harm other people; explain with examples.
Paragraph 3: Medical inventions differ because they directly preserve life and reduce suffering; provide examples.

This would help the essay feel more organized and complete.

Improve precision and avoid overgeneralization

Some of your claims are too sweeping:

“the majority of other inventions do”
“cannot be used to dominate nature or other people”
“one of the only truely ‘kind’ expressions of mankind’s creativity”

These statements may be challenged easily. For example, medicine can also be tied to power, profit, or unequal access. Be careful with absolutes unless you can fully support them. More precise wording will make your argument sound more credible.

Edit for grammar, spelling, and style

There are several sentence-level errors that should be corrected in revision:

“occured” → occurred
“affect” → effect (in this context)
“truely” → truly
“0the century” → likely 20th century
Remove extra spaces before punctuation, as in “For these reasons ,”
“peacefully coexistent with nature” could be revised to peacefully coexist with nature

Also, some sentences are repetitive in their use of “dominate.” Varying your language would make the prose stronger.

Final suggestion

You have the foundation of a compelling argument, especially because your position is ethically ambitious and clearly felt. To improve the essay, focus on specific examples, a more manageable thesis, and fuller development of each paragraph. If you support your moral claims with concrete historical evidence, your essay will become much more convincing.

If you revise, aim to answer this question in each paragraph: What exact invention am I talking about, and how does it prove my claim?

Feedback Report

Your essay has an interesting topic and a clear sense that the satellite dish transformed communication, politics, business, and entertainment. You also attempt to weigh both benefits and drawbacks, which is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, and supporting claims with evidence.

Strengthen the thesis

Right now, your essay suggests many ideas: the satellite dish changed communication, helped create the Information Age, influenced government, business, entertainment, and even harmed intelligence. Because so many points appear at once, the argument feels scattered.

Try to develop one clear central claim. For example:

*The satellite dish transformed global communication by making news, commerce, and entertainment more immediate and international.*
Or, if you want a balanced argument: *Although the satellite dish has increased global access to information and entertainment, it has also contributed to political dependence on mass media and the commercialization of culture.*

A strong thesis will help readers understand exactly what you are arguing.

Improve organization and paragraph structure

Your paragraphs often introduce a topic but do not fully develop it. For example, the paragraph about CNN mentions presidents, dictators, and political decisions, but it moves quickly without explaining how satellite technology specifically enabled that change.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction: Introduce the satellite dish and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Satellite dishes and global news (CNN, faster transmission of information).
Body paragraph 2: Business and economics (international markets, corporate communication).
Body paragraph 3: Entertainment and culture (MTV, globalization of media).
Body paragraph 4: Drawbacks or counterargument (media dependence, excessive television consumption, military spending).
Conclusion: Restate the main argument and explain why it matters.

Within each paragraph, use a topic sentence, explanation, and evidence.

Use evidence more carefully

Many claims are interesting, but some are too general or unsupported. Examples include:

“Decisions... are being made, based on that nights news CNN commentator.”
“40% of U.S. millionares are entertainers.”
“The intelligence of an average teenager is lowering itself due to cable viewing.”
“Naval programs have wasted exceeding amounts of money... due to the satellite dish.”

These statements need proof, and some may be exaggerated or inaccurate. In academic writing, avoid claims unless you can support them with facts, examples, or sources. If you do not have evidence, revise to make the point more cautious. For example:

Instead of saying television lowers intelligence, you might say: *Some critics argue that expanded cable television encourages passive consumption rather than active learning.*

This sounds more credible and analytical.

Clarify your analysis

At times, you mention examples without explaining how they support the argument. For instance, Ted Turner, MTV, and “STARWARS” are all potentially useful examples, but the connection to your main claim needs to be clearer.

Ask yourself after each example: What does this prove?  
For example:

CNN shows how satellite technology made live international news possible.
MTV shows how satellite broadcasting spread entertainment culture across borders.
Military or space programs could show how satellite-related technology influences national priorities and spending.

Make those connections explicit.

Revise tone and word choice

Some phrases sound dramatic but not precise enough for an academic essay, such as:

“changed the size of the world”
“The only observation which can be made”
“heightened an entertainer’s salary”

Try to use more formal and specific language. For example:

*reduced the effective distance between countries*
*significantly influenced global communication*
*increased the profitability of entertainment industries*

Also be careful with broad phrases like “average person” or “average teenager,” which can sound vague.

Correct grammar, spelling, and mechanics

There are many sentence-level errors that make the essay harder to read. Some common issues:

its/it’s: “it’s” means “it is”; use “its” for possession.
receive not “recieve”
basis not “bases”
affect/effect: usually *effect* is a noun, *affect* is a verb
millionaires not “millionares”
pros not “pro’s”
Capitalization: *Panasonic* should be capitalized; *country* should not be abbreviated as “ctry”
Avoid random capitalization, such as “The Information Age”
Possessive/plural errors: “people lives” should be “people’s lives”

Also watch sentence clarity. The opening sentence is vivid, but it is awkward and confusing. Aim for shorter, clearer sentences.

Suggested revision priorities

Write a clear thesis
Reorganize around 3–4 main points
Add or improve evidence and explanation
Remove unsupported exaggerations
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling

Final thought

You have a promising topic and several strong examples. The main revision challenge is turning a list of observations into a focused argument. If you narrow your claim, explain your examples more fully, and support them with evidence, your essay will become much more persuasive and effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central claim: television radically changed people’s lives by shaping culture, commercialization, and access to information. You also organize the body around three main effects, which gives the essay a strong basic structure. For revision, focus on sharpening the argument, improving evidence, and editing for clarity and correctness.

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction identifies three effects of television, which is a good start. However, the thesis is somewhat broad and descriptive rather than strongly argumentative. Try to make a clearer claim about *how* or *why* these effects matter.

For example, instead of:
> “The presence of television has brought about several crucial trends in our culture…”

you might argue:
> “Television transformed American life by standardizing cultural values, turning daily life into a target for advertising, and accelerating the spread of information in ways that both connected and manipulated viewers.”

This version is more precise and signals your essay’s critical perspective.

Develop each body paragraph with stronger evidence
Your three body paragraphs each introduce an important point, but many of the examples remain general. To make the essay more persuasive, add specific evidence and explain it more fully.

Cultural standardization
Your discussion of “ideal” family life on television is effective, especially your references to shows like *Father Knows Best* and *Green Acres*. However, phrases like “we all received the same information” are too sweeping. Not everyone watched the same shows in the same way. A stronger approach would be to say television *popularized shared images* of family, gender, and class across the country.

Also, be careful with claims like:
> “To white people, Ghetto life is slightly less of a mystery.”

This wording risks sounding reductive and imprecise. If you want to argue that television exposed viewers to social realities beyond their own communities, say that directly and carefully.

Commercialization
This paragraph has a good idea, but it is underdeveloped. The sentence about advertisements becoming “an art form” is interesting, yet you do not explain how that changed people’s lives. Did television create consumer desire? Did it shape identity through products? Did it blur entertainment and marketing? Add an example—such as children’s advertising, Super Bowl commercials, or how brands became household names through TV.

Information and speed
This is your strongest point because you show how television changed the speed and emotional impact of news. The Challenger explosion and the Vietnam War are effective examples. To improve this paragraph, make the connection to your argument more explicit: television does not just provide information faster; it changes how people *experience* distant events, making them feel immediate and personal.

Improve paragraph coherence
Some paragraphs move quickly from one idea to another without enough transitions. Try using a clear topic sentence, explanation, example, and concluding sentence in each paragraph.

For instance, in the first body paragraph:
Topic sentence: Television helped create a shared national culture.
Explanation: Families across the country saw similar images of “normal” life.
Example: Sitcom families presented consistent ideals of gender and behavior.
Conclusion: As a result, regional and social differences were filtered through a common media lens.

This pattern will make your reasoning easier to follow.

Refine tone and word choice
Your essay has energy, but some wording is too informal or imprecise for academic writing. Examples include:
“the tube”
“w/”
“&”
“must-have”
“blown-up”

Use formal academic language instead. Also avoid overgeneralizations such as “we all” and “the American population is made to laugh, or to cry, & often times to buy.” These can be revised into more careful claims:
“many viewers”
“television advertisers sought to influence consumers emotionally”

Address complexity
Your conclusion usefully notes that television can both “stimulate” and “manipulate.” This is one of the most interesting ideas in the essay. Bring that complexity into the body paragraphs as well. For example, television can unify culture but also flatten differences; it can inform citizens but also sensationalize events; it can entertain while promoting consumerism. Acknowledging both benefits and dangers will make your argument more mature.

Edit grammar, spelling, and mechanics
There are frequent sentence-level errors that distract from your argument. Revise carefully for:
Apostrophes: “1950s,” not “1950’s”
Spelling: “groups,” “breadth,” “led,” “people’s,” “living rooms,” “Vietnam,” “enhance”
Capitalization: “United States,” “Vietnam War”
Sentence boundaries and punctuation
Missing words: “The television is a powerful and dangerous tool.”

Also, avoid using “&” and abbreviations like “w/o” in formal writing.

Suggested revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis to make it more arguable.
Expand each body paragraph with specific examples and analysis.
Use more formal, precise language.
Edit closely for grammar and mechanics.

Overall, your essay has a solid framework and a strong topic. The next step is to turn broad observations into a sharper, better-supported argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a strong personal voice and clearly conveys how emotionally significant the fall of the Berlin Wall felt to you. The opening is vivid and memorable, and your central claim—that the fall of the Wall acted as a catalyst in the end of the Cold War—is clear by the final paragraph. To revise this into a stronger argumentative essay, focus on sharpening the thesis, improving organization, and making the argument more evidence-based.

What’s working well

Strong opening
Your first paragraph captures excitement and historical importance effectively. The repeated reactions (“Have you heard?”, “I can’t believe it!”) create immediacy and draw the reader in.

Clear sense of significance
You successfully communicate that this event mattered not only politically but emotionally and symbolically. That helps the essay feel meaningful rather than purely factual.

Personal connection
The anecdote about your classmate’s father gives the topic human depth. It reminds the reader that the Cold War affected real people’s lives.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis explicit earlier
Right now, your main argument only becomes fully clear near the end. In an argumentative essay, the thesis should appear clearly in the introduction.

For example, you could end the first paragraph with something like:
“The fall of the Berlin Wall was more than a dramatic symbol; it was a catalyst that inspired resistance across Eastern Europe and accelerated the end of the Cold War.”

This gives the reader a clear roadmap from the start.

Separate personal reflection from historical argument
Your essay currently blends memoir, reflection, and argument. That can be effective, but the balance leans too heavily toward personal reaction in the first half. Since this is an argumentative essay, your personal experience should support the claim, not replace evidence.

A possible structure:
Paragraph 1: Introduction and thesis
Paragraph 2: Why the Wall mattered in Germany
Paragraph 3: How it influenced other Eastern Bloc countries
Paragraph 4: Counterargument or qualification (for example, that communism was already weakening)
Paragraph 5: Conclusion

Use stronger historical evidence
Your essay makes broad claims, but many need support. For instance:
“the people of Hungary, Tchekoslovaquia, Poland and Roumania rose against communist regimes as well”
“it was the catalyst force that led to the end of the Cold War”

These are important claims, so they should be backed up with specific evidence. You might mention:
Poland’s Solidarity movement
Hungary opening its border with Austria in 1989
The Velvet Revolution in Czechoslovakia
The Romanian uprising against Ceaușescu

Also, your argument would be stronger if you acknowledged that the Berlin Wall did not alone end the Cold War. Reforms in the Soviet Union, economic weakness, and years of resistance also played major roles.

Improve logic in the third paragraph
There is a contradiction in this section:
You say that without the Berlin Wall events, history “probably would not have taken a very different course”
But then you say the Wall was “the catalyst force” that led to the end of the Cold War

If events would likely have turned out similarly, then calling the Wall *the* catalyst may be too strong. You could revise to a more precise claim:
“Although communism was already weakening, the fall of the Berlin Wall became the defining symbol and accelerating force behind the collapse of communist regimes in Eastern Europe.”

That is more nuanced and persuasive.

Style and language issues

Formality
Some phrases are too conversational for an academic argument, such as:
“our now as-President Bush”
“the ‘other side’”
“we were mesmerized by the images of the TV”

Try for a more formal tone while keeping your voice.

Word choice and accuracy
There are several spelling and naming issues:
“mesmirized” → mesmerized
“historic moment” should be historic moments
“Tchekoslovaquia” → Czechoslovakia
“Roumania” → Romania
“events of Berlin Wall” → events surrounding the Berlin Wall or the fall of the Berlin Wall
“catalyst force” is awkward; use catalyst or driving force

Sentence control and punctuation
Many sentences are connected by dashes, which makes the prose feel breathless and sometimes unclear. Use periods more often. For example, your opening can be divided into shorter, more controlled sentences for greater impact.

Also watch subject-verb and grammar issues:
“one of those historic moment” → one of those historic moments
“the people had suffered” is fine, but be consistent with tense throughout

Final revision advice

As you revise, aim to transform this from a powerful personal reflection into a persuasive historical argument. Keep the vivid opening and the personal anecdote, but connect them more directly to a clearly stated thesis and support that thesis with concrete historical examples. If you add evidence, clarify your claim, and tighten the organization, this essay will become much more convincing.

Your essay already has passion and a strong sense of historical importance. The next step is to give that passion a clearer argumentative structure.

This is a strong argumentative topic, and your paper shows that you understand the broad social impact of computers. To revise effectively, focus on making your argument more precise, better organized, and more formally supported.

Strengths

Clear topic: The essay stays focused on the computer and its importance.
Logical general direction: You move from early computers, to the personal computer, to future impact.
Sense of significance: You communicate that computers changed daily life and information processing in major ways.

These are good foundations for a stronger argumentative essay.

Main revision priority: strengthen the thesis

Right now, your introduction gives a general claim, but it is too broad and somewhat repetitive. A stronger thesis should make a specific, arguable point and preview your main reasons.

Current idea:
computers had a great impact on society and business information

Possible revision:
The personal computer is one of the most important inventions of the 20th century because it transformed how individuals and businesses store information, communicate, and complete daily tasks efficiently.

This version is clearer and gives you three body points:
storing/organizing information  
communication/access to information  
efficiency/productivity

Improve organization

At the moment, the essay reads more like a long explanation than a structured argument. Try organizing it into clearer body paragraphs, each centered on one main point.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: background + thesis
Body paragraph 1: early computers and why personal computers were a turning point
Body paragraph 2: computers made information storage and organization easier
Body paragraph 3: computers improved communication and access to information
Body paragraph 4: computers increased productivity at home and work
Conclusion: restate argument and explain long-term importance

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects back to the thesis.

Example topic sentence:
One major reason the computer changed society is that it made storing and organizing information much faster and easier.

Add evidence and examples

Your essay makes many claims, but most are general. Argumentative writing becomes more convincing when you include specific examples, facts, or comparisons.

For example, instead of saying:
“It was a very powerful tool indeed.”

You could say:
Word processors replaced typewriters, spreadsheets simplified accounting, and databases allowed businesses to store and retrieve large amounts of information in seconds.

You could also mention examples such as:
businesses using spreadsheets and databases
students writing papers and doing research
email replacing slower forms of communication
portable computers allowing work outside the office

Even if you are not required to use formal research, concrete examples will make your argument stronger.

Work on style and conciseness

Many sentences are too long and repetitive. This makes your ideas harder to follow. Try to say each point once, clearly.

For example:
“What this meant was that you now had a faster tool that could do more, receive more, and analyze more information than ever before.”

This could become:
As computers improved, they became faster and more capable of storing, receiving, and analyzing information.

Also avoid phrases like:
“just about anything”
“very powerful tool indeed”

These sound informal and vague. Replace them with more precise academic language.

Subject-verb agreement
“There has been many inventions” → “There have been many inventions”

Awkward phrasing
“responds to and, understands everyday information”
Better: “processes and understands everyday information”

Comma use
You often insert commas in awkward places:
“The computer however in this, the information age…”
Better: “However, in this information age, the computer has had a great impact…”

Capitalization and spelling
“Modem” → “modem”
“recieve” → “receive”

Word choice

“tap into information sources through the phone on the other computers” is unclear

Final advice

For your revision, focus on:
writing a more specific thesis  
dividing the essay into clear argument paragraphs  
adding concrete examples  
cutting repetition and improving sentence clarity  
correcting grammar and punctuation errors

You have a solid topic and a clear overall claim. With stronger structure and more precise writing, this essay could become a much more persuasive argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear sense of urgency and a strong emotional voice. You want readers to see nuclear energy as dangerous and to feel responsible for environmental action. That persuasive energy is a real strength. To revise successfully, focus on making your argument more precise, better supported, and more logically organized.

Clarify your main argument

Right now, your essay makes several related claims:
atomic energy is the most powerful and terrible invention of all time,
nuclear weapons are uniquely destructive,
nuclear power harms the environment,
world leaders should stop nuclear use,
citizens should pressure government officials.

These ideas are connected, but the essay needs one central thesis stated clearly near the beginning. For example:

*Because nuclear technology creates catastrophic risks both in warfare and in energy production, governments should reduce reliance on it and invest in safer alternatives.*

A thesis like this gives your paper a clear direction and helps readers follow your reasoning.

Improve accuracy and nuance

Some statements are too broad or factually questionable, which weakens your credibility.

Examples:
“J. Robert Oppenheimer unveiled to the world his invention...”  
  Oppenheimer did not single-handedly invent the atomic bomb; he led the Manhattan Project’s scientific effort.
“blowing holes in our sky” and linking ozone depletion directly to nuclear energy  
  This is vague and scientifically inaccurate. Ozone depletion is primarily associated with chemicals such as CFCs, not ordinary nuclear power generation.
“we have many other power source alternatives”  
  This may be true, but you need evidence and explanation.

When writing an argumentative essay, strong emotion works best when paired with accurate, specific claims. If you exaggerate, readers may doubt the rest of your position.

Use evidence, not only emotional appeals

At present, the essay relies mostly on strong language: “terrible,” “appalling,” “tragedy,” “imperative.” That creates passion, but argument also requires proof.

You should add:
historical evidence about Hiroshima and Nagasaki if discussing nuclear weapons,
examples of nuclear accidents such as Chernobyl or Fukushima if discussing nuclear power risks,
facts about radioactive waste, reactor safety, or long-term contamination,
evidence about alternative energy sources if you argue they should replace nuclear power.

Ask yourself: How do I know this is true? What example or fact can prove it?

Strengthen organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer paragraph structure. Right now, it shifts between weapons, power plants, pollution, world leaders, and environmental decline without enough transitions.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: present the topic and thesis.
Paragraph 1: explain the destructive power of nuclear weapons.
Paragraph 2: discuss risks of nuclear energy production.
Paragraph 3: explain why governments still use nuclear energy and respond to that reasoning.
Conclusion: propose action and restate why the issue matters.

This kind of organization will make your argument easier to follow and more persuasive.

Develop logical connections

Some of your ideas need fuller explanation. For instance, you move from “nuclear use” to general environmental damage, but you do not always explain exactly how nuclear energy causes the harms you list. Be careful not to group all environmental problems together unless you can directly connect them.

Also, consider acknowledging the opposing view. Some people support nuclear energy because it produces large amounts of electricity with low carbon emissions. If you briefly address that argument and explain why you still oppose nuclear use, your essay will seem more thoughtful and balanced.

Revise sentence style and grammar

There are several grammar, punctuation, and usage issues throughout the essay. A few examples:

“The discovery of the Atomic energy, is undoubtedly the the most awesome...”  
  Remove unnecessary capitalization, the extra comma, and repeated “the.”
“The reason behind the fear that this energy causes is routed in...”  
  “rooted,” not “routed.”
“It is also however, a problem...”  
  Use commas correctly: “It is also, however, a problem...”
“irreversable”  
  Correct spelling: “irreversible.”
“what is to become of our children.”  
  This should be a question mark: “What is to become of our children?”

You should also watch for unnecessary capitalization of words like “Atomic,” “The,” and “It” in the middle of sentences.

Make the conclusion fit the essay

Your final line—“Write your local congressman”—has energy, but it feels abrupt. Build toward it more clearly. Explain what specific policy action you want: ending nuclear weapons programs, reducing nuclear power, funding renewable energy, or strengthening environmental regulation. A call to action is strongest when it is concrete.

Revision priorities

Write a clear, arguable thesis.
Decide whether your main focus is nuclear weapons, nuclear energy, or both.
Add specific evidence and examples.
Correct factual inaccuracies and avoid exaggeration.
Improve paragraph organization and transitions.
Proofread for grammar, punctuation, spelling, and capitalization.

Your essay has conviction and an important topic. With a clearer thesis, stronger evidence, and more precise reasoning, it could become much more persuasive.

Right now, your thesis is somewhat broad and repetitive. Try to make it more specific by naming the main reasons television is so influential.

For example, instead of saying television is important because it changed how we live, you could argue:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Television was one of the most significant inventions of the 20th century because it transformed access to live information, shaped public opinion during major events such as war, and made distant parts of the world feel immediate and familiar.*

This gives your essay a clearer roadmap.

Improve paragraph structure
Your body paragraphs have strong ideas, but they need clearer topic sentences and more focused development. Each paragraph should cover one main reason.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: present the topic and thesis.
Body paragraph 1: television delivers immediate visual news.
Body paragraph 2: television influences public opinion, especially during war.
Body paragraph 3: television makes the world feel smaller and more connected.
Body paragraph 4 (optional): acknowledge negative effects and explain why they do not outweigh its importance.
Conclusion: restate the argument and significance.

At the moment, some ideas are repeated across paragraphs, especially the idea that television “shrinks” the world.

Use more specific evidence
Your essay would become much more persuasive with concrete examples. You mention the Vietnam War and Somalia, which is a good start, but the discussion stays general.

Ask yourself:
What exactly did television coverage of the Vietnam War show?
How did that affect American public opinion?
What does Somalia illustrate about global awareness?

You do not need many examples, but each one should be explained clearly.

Clarify and refine sentences
Some sentences are awkward, vague, or grammatically unclear. For example:

“One of the most significant inventions of the 20th century has to do with the speed at which information is now communicated throughout the globe.”  
  This is indirect. Say the point more simply.

“There are, however, several downsides to the television, and, like all inventions can be used in a useless way.”  
  This sentence is grammatically incomplete and the phrase “used in a useless way” is vague.

“The greatest effect that television has had is that it has made smaller the dimensions of the planet.”  
  The meaning is understandable, but the phrasing is unnatural. “Television has made the world seem smaller” is clearer.

Try to prefer direct, concise wording over abstract phrasing.

Maintain formal academic style
Some phrases sound conversational or imprecise, such as:
“has to do with”
“really see”
“very living room”
“a harmful thing”

Replace these with more formal alternatives:
“concerns”
“directly witness”
“their homes”
“a harmful medium” or “a harmful influence”

Grammar and mechanics issues to fix

Be careful with the following:

Capitalization: “vietnam war” should be Vietnam War.
Word choice: “led to much of the protesting” would be better as contributed to widespread protests.
Sentence control: avoid long sentences with too many clauses.
Comma use: there are several unnecessary commas.
Pronoun consistency: “people live their lives” and “one’s sense” shift between plural and singular points of view.

Revision advice
As you revise, try this process:

Rewrite the thesis to name 2–3 specific reasons.
Give each body paragraph one main claim.
Add one clear example per paragraph.
Cut repetition.
Edit sentence by sentence for clarity and correctness.

Example of a stronger opening
Here is one possible revision of your introduction:

*Among the many communication technologies developed in the 20th century, television stands out as one of the most influential. More than simply transmitting information quickly, television combined sound and moving images to bring world events directly into people’s homes. Because it transformed news reporting, shaped public opinion, and made distant places feel immediate, television became one of the century’s most significant inventions.*

This version is more direct, specific, and argumentative.

Final comment
You have a solid central idea and a good instinct for why television matters historically. The next step is to make your argument more focused, better organized, and more precise. With clearer structure and stronger evidence, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear main idea: the airplane is an important 20th-century invention because it transformed travel, cultural understanding, and international relations. The topic is strong, and your personal example about visiting Spain helps make the argument more relatable. For revision, focus on making your thesis more precise, improving organization, and strengthening grammar and style.

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction presents the topic well, but the thesis should be more direct and specific. Right now, the main claim is somewhat broad and repetitive (“significantly changed people’s lives,” “astronomical”). A stronger thesis would clearly list the main reasons you will discuss.

For example:
“The invention of the airplane has transformed human life by making long-distance travel faster, increasing cross-cultural understanding, and improving international political and business relations.”

This gives your essay a clear structure and helps the reader follow your argument.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Your essay has three paragraphs, but each body paragraph could be more clearly centered on one main point.

Paragraph 1 (Introduction): Introduce the airplane and present your thesis.
Paragraph 2: Focus only on travel and cultural exchange.
Paragraph 3: Focus only on international relations, business, and communication.
Conclusion: Add a short concluding paragraph that restates the main argument and explains why the invention still matters today.

Right now, the essay ends somewhat abruptly. A conclusion would make the essay feel complete.

Develop your argument with more evidence
You make good claims, but some ideas need more explanation or examples.

For instance:
You say airplanes broadened people’s perceptions of the world. This is a strong point, but explain how: tourism, study abroad, migration, easier cultural exchange.
You mention international relations and business. Try to give a concrete example, such as diplomats traveling quickly to negotiate agreements or businesses transporting workers and goods internationally.

Your personal experience in Spain is effective, but make sure it supports the main point rather than taking over the paragraph. You can connect it more explicitly to your argument:
“My visit to Spain showed me that direct contact with another culture teaches more than reading or watching the news ever could.”

Use a more formal academic tone
The essay is generally understandable, but some phrases are too informal or imprecise for academic writing.

Examples:
“opened doors to traveling” → “made travel more accessible”
“astronomical” → “enormous” or “profound”
“basically speeded up” → “greatly accelerated”
“Life with airplanes is more rapid paced” → “Air travel has contributed to a faster-paced global society.”

Also avoid overstatement, such as “completely impossible concept.” Humans could not fly naturally, but the idea of flight existed long before airplanes. A more accurate phrase would be:
“made human flight a practical reality.”

Correct grammar and word choice
There are several language errors that you should revise carefully. Here are some important ones:

“The extend to which” → “The extent to which”
“long distance traveling” → “long-distance travel”
“time consuming” → “time-consuming”
“One other door is international relations” → awkward; try “Another major impact of the airplane is on international relations.”
“the world has increased close contact between political systems” → unclear; try “the airplane has increased direct contact among nations and political systems.”
“The frequency that which foreign countries are able to communicate” → incorrect; try “The frequency with which countries can communicate”
“has improved drastically the establishment of international trade” → “has drastically improved international trade”
“postal communication system also” → “postal system as well”

Also watch punctuation. For example, in this sentence:
“One could travel by boat but this was inconvenient and often dangerous because of various things like disease, hunger, and threatening weather; also as noted above, it was extremely time consuming.”
This is too long and awkward. Break it into two sentences.

Suggested revision strategy
When revising, try this order:
Rewrite the thesis.
Separate your ideas into clear body paragraphs.
Add a conclusion.
Replace informal or vague expressions with more precise academic language.
Proofread for grammar, spelling, and sentence structure.

Overall assessment
This essay has a solid central idea and good potential. Your best strength is that you clearly understand the significance of the airplane and connect it to real human experience. The next step is to express that argument in a more organized, formal, and polished way. With clearer structure and careful editing, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows a clear concern with a serious public-health issue and argues that education is essential in preventing AIDS when medicine is limited. That central idea is promising. To strengthen the essay, focus on creating a clearer thesis, using accurate and respectful language, and organizing your ideas more logically.

Main strength
Your strongest idea is this one: if AIDS cannot yet be fully controlled by medicine, public knowledge and prevention matter greatly. That is an arguable claim and could become the foundation of a strong argumentative essay.

Thesis and argument
Right now, the essay reads more like a series of observations than a developed argument. Your thesis appears to be:

AIDS remains a major threat despite medical advances.
Education can help prevent its spread.

That is a useful start, but the thesis should be more specific and direct. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although twentieth-century medicine made major advances, the fight against AIDS showed that prevention through public education, safe practices, and accurate information is just as important as medical treatment.*

This version clearly states your claim and suggests the points the essay will develop.

Organization
Your essay would be stronger with a clearer structure:

Introduction
Introduce the topic and end with a precise thesis.

Body Paragraph 1: Limits of medicine
Explain that AIDS challenged medicine because treatment was limited, especially in earlier years.

Body Paragraph 2: Importance of education
Discuss how hospitals, schools, and public campaigns can teach people how HIV/AIDS spreads and how it can be prevented.

Body Paragraph 3: Behavior and prevention
Explain how risky behaviors such as needle sharing and unprotected sex increase transmission.

Conclusion
Restate the main argument and emphasize that education and medical research must work together.

Right now, some paragraphs jump from one idea to another too quickly. Try using topic sentences so each paragraph has one main focus.

Use of evidence
An argumentative essay needs support beyond general statements. Many of your claims are broad, such as “it’s killing our population” or “many schools have begun inviting victims of AIDS.” Add specific evidence:

facts about HIV/AIDS transmission
examples of public education campaigns
statistics from reliable medical or historical sources
brief references to how awareness changed over time

Without evidence, the argument sounds emotional but not fully convincing.

Accuracy and tone
This is the area that most needs revision. Some statements are misleading, outdated, or overly broad. For example:

“homosexuals are not the only possible carriers” reinforces stigma. HIV/AIDS is not limited to one group.
“dentists who ‘forget to put on their plastic gloves’” is not an appropriate or well-supported example.
“victims of AIDS” may be better phrased as people living with AIDS or people with HIV/AIDS.
“AIDS viruses” should be the AIDS virus or, more accurately, HIV, the virus that causes AIDS.

Be careful not to suggest blame or fear in ways that stigmatize people. A stronger academic tone would emphasize public health, prevention, and compassion.

Style and diction
For formal academic writing, avoid informal abbreviations and symbols such as:

“&” → write and
“w/” → write with

Also revise unclear or awkward wording. Examples:

“many ailing human beings” → many patients
“interact sexual” → engage in sexual activity
“selling their lives short” → putting themselves at serious risk
“drugs effect” → the effects of drugs

Aim for precise, formal language throughout.

Grammar and mechanics
There are several sentence-level problems to fix:

Semicolons
You often use semicolons incorrectly. A semicolon should join two closely related independent clauses, not replace every pause.

Example:
“Due to the ineffectiveness of medicines; the population has needed…”  
Better: “Due to the ineffectiveness of medicines, the population has needed…”

Apostrophes and word forms
“it’s killing our population” = correct if you mean “it is”
“its population” = possessive
“peoples lives” → people’s lives
“can not” → usually cannot

Subject-verb agreement
“The AIDS viruses was discovered” → The AIDS virus was discovered

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are long and crowded with ideas. Break them into shorter, clearer sentences.

Suggested revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis so it is clear and arguable.  
Reorganize the essay into introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion.  
Remove stigmatizing or inaccurate claims and replace them with precise information.  
Add evidence from reliable sources.  
Edit for formal style, grammar, and punctuation.

Final advice
You have chosen an important topic and identified a meaningful argument: education can be a powerful tool in fighting disease. Your next step is to make the essay more academic by improving accuracy, support, and organization. If you revise with those goals in mind, your essay will become much more persuasive and credible.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear personal voice and strong enthusiasm for the topic. The reader immediately understands that contact lenses matter to you, and your specific examples about basketball, swimming, and daily routines make the essay relatable. This personal engagement is one of the paper’s strengths. To revise effectively, focus on making the argument more academic, more logically developed, and more polished in style.

Clarify the argumentative purpose
Right now, the essay reads more like a personal reflection than an argumentative essay. You claim that contact lenses are “one of the most important inventions of the 20th century,” but most of the paper explains why they are important to you personally.

To strengthen the argument, ask:
Why are contact lenses important beyond my own experience?
Compared with what other inventions?
What criteria make an invention “important”? Convenience? Medical benefit? Quality of life? Accessibility?

A stronger thesis might be:
> Contact lenses are one of the most significant 20th-century inventions because they improved vision correction, expanded freedom in sports and daily life, and increased comfort and convenience compared with traditional glasses.

This gives you clear points to develop in the body paragraphs.

Improve organization
Your essay has a beginning, middle, and end, but the ideas could be arranged more logically. Right now, it moves from general praise to your personal story, then to disposable lenses, then to colored lenses, and finally to a conclusion. Try organizing by main reasons instead.

A possible structure:
Introduction: present the claim that contact lenses are a major invention.
Body Paragraph 1: explain how they improve vision correction and mobility.
Body Paragraph 2: discuss how they help in sports and active lifestyles.
Body Paragraph 3: explain advances such as soft and disposable lenses.
Conclusion: restate why they are historically important.

This structure would help your essay feel more focused and persuasive.

Use a more academic tone
Your voice is lively, but some phrases are too informal for academic writing. Examples include:
“I can’t even begin to explain”
“a bit ridiculous!”
“who am I to say!”
“glasses are a thing of the past!”

These expressions sound conversational and sometimes exaggerated. Also, the final statement that glasses are “a thing of the past” is too broad and not really true, since many people still rely on glasses.

Try replacing informal language with more precise wording. For example:
Instead of “I can’t even begin to explain what a difference they make,” write:  
  Disposable lenses greatly increased convenience for users.
Instead of “glasses are a thing of the past,” write:  
  For many people, contact lenses have become a preferred alternative to glasses.

Develop evidence more fully
You include vivid personal examples, which is good, but an argumentative essay usually needs broader support. Consider adding:
a brief explanation of how contact lenses changed vision care,
mention of how they benefit athletes or people with active jobs,
discussion of comfort improvements from hard to soft to disposable lenses.

Even if you are not required to use research, you can still explain your points more analytically. For example, instead of simply saying disposable lenses are wonderful, explain why: they reduce cleaning time, may feel more hygienic, and make vision correction more practical.

Revise style and mechanics
There are many sentence-level issues that distract from your ideas:
unnecessary capitalization,
inconsistent verb forms,
incorrect punctuation,
use of “&” instead of “and,”
spelling errors such as “lense” instead of “lens” or “lenses.”

Examples:
“hard lenses were the only kind made” → fine, but the next sentence should avoid “I’ve heard from others” if you want a stronger academic tone.
“the ‘lense craze’ caught rapidly” → awkward phrasing; better:  
  the popularity of contact lenses increased rapidly.
“another type of lenses were made accessible” →  
  another type of lens was introduced or disposable lenses became available.

Also, be careful with punctuation. You use many exclamation marks, quotation marks, dashes, and semicolons in ways that make the essay feel less controlled. Aim for cleaner, simpler sentences.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion should do more than repeat your opinion. It should show why the issue matters. You might conclude by emphasizing that contact lenses did not merely improve appearance; they transformed independence, comfort, and participation in everyday activities for millions of people.

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis so it makes a clear, arguable claim.
Reorganize body paragraphs around reasons, not just memories.
Reduce informal language and exaggeration.
Expand support beyond personal experience.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

You already have a strong foundation because your topic is clear and your examples are memorable. With a more formal argument and cleaner organization, this essay could become much more persuasive.
