Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: feminism can be understood as one of the major “discoveries” or advances of the modern era because it has changed how society thinks about women and equality. This is an interesting and original framing, and your conclusion returns effectively to that idea by calling feminism a “discovery in progress.” That gives the essay a sense of unity. For revision, the main areas to strengthen are historical accuracy, development of evidence, clarity of argument, and sentence-level polish.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your main claim is present, but it could be more specific. You suggest that feminism is an important discovery because it has changed the lives of men and women, but you do not fully explain how or why.

A stronger thesis might state the main reasons more directly, for example:
feminism has expanded women’s political, economic, and social rights;
it has challenged traditional gender roles;
it continues to shape society because full equality has not yet been achieved.

This would give your body paragraphs a clearer structure.

Improve historical accuracy
Several statements are too general or factually uncertain, which weakens your credibility.

Examples:
“the 0th century” appears to be a typo and should be corrected.
“women winning the right to vote in the 1800s” is inaccurate in the U.S.; women gained the constitutional right to vote nationally in 1920 with the 19th Amendment.
“The first significant equal rights movement in the U.S. was in the 70s with the Equal Rights Amendment” is also inaccurate. There were important women’s rights movements much earlier, including the Seneca Falls Convention (1848) and the suffrage movement.
“60 cents for every dollar” may once have been a common claim, but if you use a statistic like this, you should either verify it or phrase it more carefully.

When revising, make sure your examples are correct and, if possible, more precise. Accurate evidence will make your argument much more persuasive.

Develop each paragraph with clearer support
Your paragraphs introduce important points, but they often stay at the level of broad assertion. Try to expand each point with examples or explanation.

For instance:
In paragraph 2, when discussing changes in women’s roles, you could mention voting rights, access to higher education, workplace participation, legal protections, or reproductive rights.
In paragraph 3, when discussing image and representation, you could explain more specifically how media, language, or pornography shape cultural attitudes toward women.
In paragraph 4, you could briefly address why feminism still matters today if it has not yet become “mainstream.”

A good revision strategy is to ask after each main claim: What example proves this? How can I explain it more fully?

Clarify your use of the word “discovery”
The most original feature of your essay is the idea that feminism is a “discovery.” However, some readers may question whether feminism is really a discovery in the same sense as nuclear energy or the telephone.

To strengthen this comparison, explain what you mean by “discovery.” Do you mean:
a new social awareness?
a recognition of women’s inequality?
a new understanding of gender roles and justice?

If you define the term more carefully, the essay’s central comparison will feel more convincing rather than just metaphorical.

Refine organization and transitions
The essay generally moves from introduction to examples to conclusion, but the progression could be smoother. Consider organizing around three clear areas of impact:
political progress,
economic/workplace equality,
cultural/image-related change.

This would make the argument easier to follow. Add transitions that show how one paragraph builds on the previous one.

For example:
“In addition to changing women’s legal rights, feminism has also transformed expectations about work and family.”
“Beyond politics and economics, feminism has also challenged the cultural image of women.”

Revise style and grammar
There are several sentence-level issues to correct:

Typo: “0th century.”
Quotation marks: You use quotation marks around “discovery” and “discovered” inconsistently. If this is your key metaphor, either explain it clearly or avoid overusing quotation marks.
Agreement and phrasing: “how much things as varying as pornography or the language we use helps” should be “how much things as varied as pornography and the language we use help.”
Awkward phrasing: “it was still the most typical as well as the ideal” could be simplified to “it was still considered both normal and ideal.”
Word choice: “the woman is still only earning” would be better as “women still earn less than men on average.”

Try to make your sentences more direct and precise.

Final revision goal
Your essay has a thoughtful idea and a strong sense of purpose. The next step is to make it more persuasive by grounding it in accurate history, expanding your evidence, and clarifying your central metaphor. If you revise with those goals in mind, the essay will become much stronger.

Priority checklist for revision:
Correct factual inaccuracies.
Rewrite the thesis to make your argument more specific.
Add concrete examples to each body paragraph.
Clarify what you mean by feminism as a “discovery.”
Edit for grammar, wording, and clarity.

Sense of importance

Main areas to revise

For example:

Improve organization

Government and public safety: nuclear weapons, nuclear waste, laws against dumping

  Revise to: “countries that have many weapons”

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear main idea: computers have significantly changed everyday life, both personally and socially. You also use concrete examples from your own experience, which is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on thesis clarity, organization, style, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengths

Clear topic: You stay focused on the impact of computers.
Relevant personal examples: Storing school records, balancing a checkbook, editing documents, and travel arrangements all help make your point concrete.
Sense of significance: The essay shows that computers affect both individual convenience and larger institutions.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the essay begins generally: “Computers have definitely effected peoples lives.” This states a topic, but not yet a strong argumentative claim.

Try to make your central argument more precise. For example:

Possible revision: “Computers have transformed modern life by making information storage, financial tasks, communication, and travel arrangements faster, more accurate, and more organized.”

This gives your essay a clearer direction and signals the main points you will discuss.

B. Improve organization
The essay has many good ideas, but they are presented in a long flow rather than in clearly structured paragraphs. Consider organizing it like this:

Introduction
   - Introduce the topic
   - Present your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Personal organization and storage
   - Explain how computers reduce paper clutter
   - Show how digital storage improves organization

Body Paragraph 2: Speed and accuracy
   - Use the checkbook example
   - Discuss editing documents with a word processor

Body Paragraph 3: Communication and larger systems
   - Mention online ticket purchasing
   - Explain how institutions like universities and airlines depend on computers

Conclusion
   - Restate the importance of computers
   - End with a broader comment about their impact on modern society

This structure will make your argument easier to follow.

C. Develop analysis, not just description
Many of your examples are useful, but sometimes you stop at describing what computers do. Push further by explaining why this matters.

For instance, instead of only saying that computers store more information, explain that this changes how people work, study, and manage their lives. Instead of saying booking flights seems impossible without computers, explain how computers make complex international coordination efficient and reliable.

Ask yourself after each example: So what? Why does this example prove my argument?

Style and Tone

A. Use formal academic language
Because this is an academic essay, avoid casual abbreviations such as:

“&” → write and
“w/” → write with
“w/out” → write without
“info” → write information
“on line” → usually online

These shortcuts make the essay sound informal.

B. Avoid overstatement
Some phrases are too exaggerated or vague, such as “the power of computers, for me, is incomprehensible.” Try to be more precise and analytical.

For example:
“Although I have only used a small portion of what computers can do, I recognize that they offer far more possibilities than I currently understand.”

Sentence-Level Corrections

There are several grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors to correct.

Common issues:
effected → affected
peoples lives → people’s lives
my life has change → my life has changed
had to this → had to do this
form the US → from the US
would inevitably inundated → would inevitably be inundated

Example of a revised opening:
Original:
> “Computers have definitely effected peoples lives. I have a computer and my life has change because of it.”

Revised:
> “Computers have definitely affected people’s lives. My own life has changed because of the computer I use every day.”

Watch sentence length
Some of your sentences are very long and contain too many ideas. Break them into shorter sentences to improve clarity.

For example, the sentence beginning “My father has the same program...” could be divided into two sentences.

Evidence and Argument

Your essay relies almost entirely on personal experience, which is acceptable for a short response, but the argument would be stronger if you framed those experiences as evidence of broader social change. For example, when discussing the university application process or airline booking, emphasize that computers do not just help you personally—they help institutions manage large amounts of information efficiently.

Conclusion

Your final sentence is a good attempt at a broad concluding statement, but it would be stronger if it directly reflected your thesis. For example:

“Overall, computers have had a profound impact on modern life by improving organization, reducing errors, and making communication and travel more efficient.”

Final Advice

In revision, focus on:
Writing a specific thesis
Dividing the essay into clear paragraphs
Replacing informal abbreviations with formal language
Correcting grammar and spelling
Explaining how each example supports your argument

You already have strong material; now the goal is to shape it into a clearer, more formal, and more persuasive essay.

Your essay has a clear position and strong energy. You argue that the satellite is a transformative invention because it gives the world immediate access to events as they happen. Consider this structure:

Introduction: Present the claim and define what makes satellites so important.
Body Paragraph 1: Explain “nowness” and how satellites differ from radio, TV, or videotape.
Body Paragraph 2: Use Tiananmen Square as evidence of satellites’ global impact.
Body Paragraph 3: Discuss the broader effect: truth, public pressure, and global responsibility.
Conclusion: Reinforce your thesis and explain why this invention matters today.

This structure would also help you avoid repetition and make each paragraph do one clear job.

Develop your evidence carefully
Your Tiananmen Square example is the strongest part of the essay, but it needs more explanation and accuracy. You should explain exactly how satellite broadcasting allowed international audiences to witness the protests and why that mattered. Be careful not to overstate what the satellite alone accomplished. For example, saying that without satellites “the movement for democracy... would have been lost” is too absolute. The event may still have been reported through journalists, newspapers, and recordings, though perhaps less immediately.

A more balanced claim would be:

“Satellite broadcasting allowed the protests to be seen worldwide in real time, making the Chinese government’s actions visible to the international community.”

This sounds more credible and academic.

Strengthen style and tone
Your essay has passionate language, which gives it force, but at times it becomes vague or overly dramatic. Academic argument works best when emotional intensity is supported by precise wording.

For example:
“the tank of injustice made its way into the lives of most of the world’s people” is vivid, but unclear.
“the satellite keeps a watchful eye on all of us” is powerful, but personifies the technology in a way that may sound exaggerated.

Aim for language that is strong but controlled. Instead of relying on slogans, explain the cause-and-effect relationship between media technology and public awareness.

Also, avoid conversational phrases like “we’ve all witnessed” and rhetorical questions like “who wouldn’t want to help his neighbor?” unless you use them very deliberately.

Correct grammar, spelling, and word choice
There are many sentence-level errors that distract from your ideas. Revising these carefully will make your argument much easier to follow. A few examples:

“evention” → *inventions*
“preceed” → *precede*
“invention” → *inventions*
“openned” → *opened*
“litterally” → *literally*
“Teniman Square” → *Tiananmen Square*
“sensorship” → *censorship*
“a like” → *alike*
“mor” → *more*

There are also punctuation and sentence-fragment problems. For example:

“As it happens- the most profound byproduct of the satellite.”  
  This is a fragment. You could revise it to:  
  “Its most profound byproduct is the ability to show events as they happen.”

Also be careful with abbreviations like “T.V.” and symbols like “&”; in formal academic writing, write television and and.

Revise for precision
Some phrases are hard to understand, such as:
“Freedom US. the tank of injustice”
“it has influenced now the world functions”

These ideas likely contain strong meaning, but they need rewriting for clarity. Ask yourself: *What exactly do I mean here?* Then express it directly.

For example:
“It has influenced how the world functions.”
“Images of the protester standing before the tank became a symbol of resistance around the world.”

Final advice
You already have the foundation of a persuasive essay: a strong opinion, a meaningful example, and a sense of why the issue matters. In revision, focus on three priorities:

State a precise thesis
Organize the essay into clear paragraphs
Edit carefully for grammar, clarity, and accuracy

If you do that, your argument will become much more convincing and powerful.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: television is a major 20th-century invention because it changes how people receive information, connect across cultures, and learn through entertainment. You have several strong ideas here, especially the argument that television allows people to witness events directly and respond more quickly. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, organizing your ideas more clearly, and improving sentence control and style.

What’s Working

Clear topic and strong relevance
You answer the prompt directly by identifying television as an invention that changed people’s lives. The essay stays focused on that subject.

Good main arguments
You offer more than one reason television matters:
it delivers news quickly,
it creates shared access to information,
it connects cultures,
it provides entertainment that can teach lessons.

This gives your essay substance and shows thoughtful engagement with the topic.

Awareness of complexity
A strong feature of the essay is that you do not present television as purely positive. You acknowledge problems such as distortion, unrealistic portrayals, and glorified violence. This makes the argument more balanced and credible.

Main Areas to Revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your opening says television has “significantly changed people’s lives,” but it does not clearly state how in a concise, arguable way. Try ending the first paragraph with a stronger thesis that previews your main points.

For example:
> Television has significantly changed people’s lives by transforming access to news, connecting people across cultures, and shaping how audiences understand both reality and entertainment.

This gives the essay a clearer roadmap.

Improve organization
At the moment, some ideas run together in long paragraphs. Consider using a structure like this:

Introduction: identify television and present thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: television and instant news/information.
Body Paragraph 2: television as a shared cultural/global medium.
Body Paragraph 3: television as entertainment and moral/social education, including its limitations.
Conclusion: restate significance and give final judgment.

This would help readers follow your argument more easily.

Control very long sentences
Your biggest writing issue is the use of run-on sentences and overloaded punctuation. Several sentences contain too many ideas at once, connected by commas, dashes, parentheses, and ampersands. This makes your argument harder to follow.

For example, this sentence is too long:
> While previously people had to wait days, or even weeks for news ... now they can see it happening...

Break it into two or three sentences. Shorter sentences will sound more authoritative and make your points clearer.

Use a more formal academic style
To strengthen the essay’s tone:
Replace “&” with “and.”
Avoid conversational phrases like “well, at least everybody who watches.”
Be careful with exaggerations such as “every person in the world can watch.” This is too absolute and not accurate.

A more precise version would be:
> Television allows millions of people to witness major events almost immediately.

Academic writing becomes stronger when it is precise rather than overstated.

Clarify and refine reasoning
Some claims need more explanation. For example:
When you say television gives people the “same inflow of information and ideas,” is that always beneficial?
When you say people can “make their own, informed judgment,” you might also consider that televised images can still be edited or framed.

You do not need to argue both sides equally, but acknowledging these limits briefly would deepen your analysis.

Conclusion needs expansion
Your essay ends rather suddenly. Add a short conclusion that reinforces your overall claim. You might emphasize that despite its flaws, television remains transformative because it changes how people experience the world beyond their immediate surroundings.

Sentence-Level Notes

Pay attention to:
Comma splices/run-ons
Subject-verb agreement
Word choice and grammar

A few examples:
“this allows” should begin with a capital letter if it starts a new sentence.
“through television entertainment” is awkward; consider “through television programs.”
“making life seem the good guy always wins” should be revised to something like “making it seem that the good guy always wins.”
“lives thru dangerous situations” should be “survives dangerous situations” or “lives through dangerous situations.”

Revision Priorities

Write a clear thesis with 2–3 main points.
Split the essay into clear body paragraphs by topic.
Revise long sentences into shorter, grammatical ones.
Replace informal punctuation and wording with formal academic style.
Add a brief conclusion.

Final Thought

You have the basis of a thoughtful argument with good insight into both the benefits and drawbacks of television. The next step is not inventing new ideas, but presenting your existing ideas more clearly and formally. If you revise for structure and sentence control, this essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central topic—the impact of radio—and you make a strong attempt to connect technology, politics, information, and entertainment. You also use concrete everyday examples, which helps the reader see why the invention matters. To revise successfully, though, you need a sharper argument, better organization, and more precise language.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your essay begins with an interesting anecdote about a teacher’s claim that the machine gun and radio enabled repressive government. However, the paper then shifts into a much broader discussion of radio as information and entertainment. Because of this, the essay feels like it starts with one argument and ends with another.

Ask yourself: What exactly are you trying to prove?

Possible directions:
If your focus is political power: argue that radio became one of the most influential inventions of the 20th century because it allowed governments to shape public opinion through mass communication.
If your focus is broader social impact: argue that radio transformed modern life by changing how people receive news, experience entertainment, and relate to mass media.

Choose one. Then make that claim explicit in your introduction.

A stronger thesis might be:
“Although radio could be used as a tool of propaganda, its greatest impact in the twentieth century was transforming everyday life through instant news and mass entertainment.”
Or: “Radio became one of the most powerful inventions of the twentieth century because it gave governments and media the ability to influence millions of people at once.”

Improve organization
Your body paragraphs currently move from dictatorship, to news, to television, without a clear structure. Try organizing around two or three main points that directly support your thesis.

For example:

Introduction
Hook/anecdote
Background on radio
Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Radio and information
Instant communication
Weather, news, public awareness
Comparison with newspapers

Body Paragraph 2: Radio and political influence
Propaganda under dictatorships
Ability to reach millions at once
Why this made radio powerful

Body Paragraph 3: Radio and entertainment / television as an offshoot
Family life, leisure, popular culture
Transition from radio to television
Broader cultural effect

Conclusion
Restate thesis
Emphasize why radio changed modern life

This kind of structure will help your ideas build logically.

Develop analysis more fully
Many of your points are interesting, but they need more explanation. For instance, you write that radio allows people to become “passive observers rather than active participants.” That is an insightful claim, but you do not explain it. Why does radio encourage passivity? How is hearing news different from helping create change?

Similarly, when you mention dictatorship and propaganda, that point deserves development because it connects strongly to your opening. You could explain how radio allowed leaders to speak directly to the population and influence public opinion more effectively than print.

Try to move beyond general statements like “Nowadays everybody watches T.V.” Instead, explain how and why these technologies changed behavior.

Use stronger evidence and examples
Your personal examples (weather reports, your señora watching TV) make the essay relatable, but the argument would be stronger with broader historical or social evidence. For example:
Radio gave people immediate access to war news and national events.
Governments used radio broadcasts to unify or manipulate populations.
Radio dramas and music programs changed home entertainment before television became dominant.

Even one or two historical examples would make your essay more persuasive.

Watch accuracy and focus
Be careful with claims that may be too broad or inaccurate. For example:
“The machine gun has not changed all that many lives” is difficult to defend, especially given its role in war.
“It was not until the invention of radio that the power of the media became evident” overlooks the major influence of newspapers before radio.

Instead of absolute claims, use more qualified language:
“Radio expanded the influence of media by making communication immediate and widespread.”
“Unlike newspapers, radio could reach millions instantly.”

Edit for style, grammar, and mechanics
There are a number of sentence-level issues that distract from your ideas. In revision, pay attention to:

Spelling: “Marshall Law” should be martial law; “forcast” should be forecast.
Apostrophes and spacing: “it' s,” “don' t” should be it’s, don’t.
Capitalization: “20th century” does not need capitalization in the middle of a sentence.
Word choice: “civil peace” may be less precise than public order.
Punctuation: Some sentences are run-ons or awkwardly joined.
Symbols: Avoid using “&” in formal essays; write and.
Tone: Phrases like “Nowadays everybody watches T.V.” sound too informal and exaggerated for academic writing.

Final advice
Your essay has the beginnings of a strong argument, especially because you identify radio as more than just a device—it is a force that shaped politics, news, and entertainment. For your next draft, focus on one clear thesis, organize each paragraph around that thesis, and support your claims with fuller explanation and more precise examples. If you do that, your paper will become much more convincing and coherent.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and an engaging personal voice. You show genuine interest in the impact of cellular telephones, and your examples help readers understand why this invention seemed so remarkable. The paper also attempts to present both positive and negative effects, which is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. Clarify your thesis

Much of the paper describes how useful and impressive cell phones are, and the conclusion sounds admiring rather than argumentative. As a result, the essay feels divided between two positions:
cell phones are amazing and transformative
cell phones are unnecessary luxuries

Choose the main argument you want to defend and shape the whole essay around it. For example:

If your position is negative: argue that cell phones are convenient but remain unnecessary because they are expensive, distracting, and encourage overdependence.
If your position is balanced: revise the thesis to reflect that, such as: *Although cellular telephones improve communication and safety, they also create expense and distraction, making them a mixed technological advance.*

Your thesis should match the body paragraphs and conclusion.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from convenience, to accidents, to cost, to a personal anecdote, and then to robots. The ideas are interesting, but the structure feels loose.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction  
   Introduce cell phones and state your thesis clearly.

Body Paragraph 1: Benefits  
   Explain convenience, emergencies, travel, and staying reachable.

Body Paragraph 2: Drawbacks  
   Discuss distracted driving and cost.

Body Paragraph 3: Evaluation  
   Weigh the benefits against the drawbacks and explain why this supports your thesis.

Conclusion  
   Restate your argument and leave the reader with a final thought about technology.

Try to give each paragraph one central idea. The anecdote about calling your father could fit well in the “benefits” paragraph as evidence of how communication technology connects people across distances.

Develop the argument more fully
Some points need more explanation. For example, you say cell phones are “a complete luxury,” but why exactly? Is it because pay phones and home phones were enough? Because only wealthy people could afford them? Because most “important calls” can wait? Explain your reasoning.

Similarly, the claim about accidents would be stronger if you explained the cause-and-effect more carefully. Instead of simply saying this happens often, explain how divided attention affects driver reaction time and road awareness.

When revising, ask yourself after each paragraph: How does this prove my thesis?

Use a more formal academic tone
Your voice is lively, but some phrases are too conversational for an academic essay. Examples include:
“To me, it is a bit ridiculous”
“lots of people”
“The other day”
“The scary thing is”
“it’s just around the corner”

These can be revised into a more formal style:
*This dependence may seem excessive.*
*many people*
*For example*
*This development may be unsettling*
*may soon become reality*

Also avoid using &; write and instead.

Strengthen sentence clarity and grammar
There are several sentence-level issues to fix:

Sentence fragments / awkward punctuation:  
  “People are able to use their car phones in case of an emergency; if they are stranded or broken down.”  
  Better: *People can use car phones in emergencies, such as when they are stranded or their car breaks down.*

Parallel structure:  
  “They are expensive to buy, to install and to use.”  
  This is close, but make punctuation consistent: *They are expensive to buy, install, and use.*

Pronoun and agreement issues:  
  “Although most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone, can afford to pay the bills!”  
  Remove the comma and exclamation mark: *Most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone can also afford to pay the bills.*

Spacing/apostrophes:  
  “it' s” should be *it’s.*

Capitalization and consistency:  
  Choose either *cell phone* or *cellular telephone* and use it consistently.

Revise the conclusion
Your conclusion shifts away from the essay’s main argument and introduces robots, which feels unrelated. A conclusion should reinforce your thesis, not open a new topic. For example:

*Cellular telephones have transformed modern communication by making people reachable almost anywhere. However, their cost and their role in distracted driving show that they are not purely beneficial. Although they are an impressive technological development, they remain more of a luxury than a necessity.*

Final Advice
Your essay has strong potential because the topic is clear and your examples are memorable. In revision, focus on one clear argument, organized paragraphs, and a more formal style. If you make those changes, your paper will feel much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay raises an important and emotionally difficult issue, and you clearly care about the topic. You also attempt to engage with moral, legal, religious, and personal dimensions of euthanasia, which gives your paper range. For revision, your biggest priorities are: clarifying your thesis, organizing your argument, developing evidence, and correcting sentence-level errors.

Clarify your position
At the moment, your essay seems to argue two different positions:

that euthanasia should be a personal choice for suffering patients
that euthanasia conflicts with God’s gift of life and should be restricted

These ideas can coexist, but you need to explain *how*. Right now, readers may feel unsure whether you support euthanasia, oppose it, or support it only in limited circumstances.

To revise:
Write a thesis that clearly states your exact claim. For example:

Qualified support: “Although euthanasia raises serious moral and religious concerns, competent terminally ill patients should have the legal right to choose it in limited cases of unbearable suffering.”
Opposition: “Because euthanasia violates the sanctity of life and risks moral abuse, it should not be legalized.”
Middle-ground position: “Euthanasia should remain highly restricted and permitted only when a patient is competent, terminally ill, and suffering without hope of recovery.”

Choose one and make the whole essay support that position.

Improve organization
Several paragraphs repeat the same point—that euthanasia is debated as a moral issue—and the essay sometimes moves abruptly between ideas. A stronger structure would help.

Possible structure:
Introduction  
   Define euthanasia, explain why it is controversial, and state your thesis.
Moral/religious concerns  
   Discuss sanctity of life, church teaching, and values.
Arguments in favor of euthanasia  
   Patient autonomy, freedom from suffering, dignity.
Legal/medical concerns  
   Dr. Kevorkian, medical ethics, regulation, consent.
Your evaluation  
   Explain why your position is the most reasonable.
Conclusion  
   Restate the argument and leave the reader with a final thought.

Use clear topic sentences so each paragraph has one main purpose.

Develop evidence more fully
You mention articles, pro-life activists, “The Last Goodbye,” the Cruzan case, and Dr. Kevorkian, but these examples are often too vague. Some sentences are incomplete because of the  marks, and several references are not explained enough for a reader unfamiliar with them.

To revise:
Identify each source clearly.
Summarize the source’s argument.
Explain how it supports your claim.
If you use quotations, introduce and analyze them.

For example, instead of saying an article was “melancholy,” explain how its language encourages readers to see euthanasia as a moral tragedy rather than a legal choice.

Strengthen analysis
Often the essay summarizes what “some say” without fully analyzing why those views matter. Your paper becomes strongest when you interpret ideas rather than just listing them.

For example:
You write that euthanasia is treated as “an issue of value more than consequence.” This is a promising insight. Push it further:
Why do writers frame it as a values issue?
What consequences get ignored when that happens?
Does this framing strengthen or weaken public understanding?

That kind of analysis will make your argument more original and persuasive.

Address contradictions carefully
Near the end, you say life is God’s gift and not society’s choice to end, but then you also say that if a person is suffering greatly, euthanasia should be the patient’s personal decision. That tension could become a strength if you discuss it openly.

You might say something like:
“While my religious beliefs make me cautious about euthanasia, I believe personal autonomy in extreme suffering should still be respected.”
  
That would sound thoughtful rather than inconsistent.

Revise style and grammar
There are many sentence-level problems that make the essay hard to follow. Focus on:

Word choice: “an complex issue” should be “a complex issue”
Agreement: “someone else life” should be “someone else’s life”
Comparatives: “than that should be” should be “then that should be”
Spelling: “ethic” → “ethics,” “opposers” → “opponents,” “Kervokian” → “Kevorkian”
Sentence clarity: some sentences are awkward or repetitive
Fragments/incomplete thoughts: especially where  appears

Also avoid overgeneralizations such as “society always has interest...” unless you can support them.

Tone and academic voice
Your essay sometimes shifts between academic discussion and personal reaction (“Of course this is not true”). In an argumentative essay, keep a more formal tone. You can still use first person if allowed, but make sure your personal views are presented analytically.

Instead of:
“Of course this is not true.”

Try:
“This claim seems overstated because it assumes widespread public acceptance of mercy killing without sufficient evidence.”

Final advice
Your essay has a strong topic and shows genuine engagement, but it needs a clear central argument and more controlled development. Start by deciding exactly what you believe about euthanasia. Then reorganize the essay so every paragraph helps prove that position. Finally, revise for clarity, grammar, and completeness.

If you do those things, your paper will become much more persuasive and coherent.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position, a serious topic, and a recognizable argumentative structure. You show that you understand both sides of the debate and that your main claim is that the anti-death-penalty position is stronger. That is a solid foundation. For revision, focus on three priorities: completing and supporting your evidence, sharpening your thesis and organization, and improving tone and precision.

Main strengths

Clear stance: You consistently argue against capital punishment.
Engagement with opposing views: You do not ignore the pro-death-penalty side; instead, you summarize key claims such as justice and deterrence.
Moral focus: The idea that capital punishment lowers the value of human life gives your essay a central theme.

Most important revision need: missing evidence

The biggest problem is that several quotations are left as “”. As written, the essay cannot fully support its claims because the reader cannot see the evidence.

What to do:
Insert the actual quotations or paraphrases from Hoyler, Nygaard, Conway, and Reiman.
Introduce each source clearly and explain how it supports your point.
Cite sources in the style your class requires.

Example:
Instead of:
> Robert Hoyler supports this claim by stating, .

Write:
> Robert Hoyler argues that capital punishment provides a sense of justice for victims’ families because it treats murder as a crime deserving the highest penalty. This view strengthens the retributive argument, though it does not prove that execution benefits society as a whole.

Notice that the source is not just dropped in; it is explained.

Thesis and argument focus

Your thesis is somewhat repetitive and broad. You say the anti-death-penalty argument is “stronger and more sensible,” but you should specify why.

Current problem:
The introduction spends a lot of time setting up the issue, but your exact argument could be more precise.

Revise toward a thesis like:
> Although supporters of capital punishment defend it as justice and deterrence, the death penalty is ultimately unjustifiable because it devalues human life, rests too heavily on revenge, and lacks convincing evidence that it deters violent crime better than life imprisonment.

This version gives the reader a clear roadmap for your body paragraphs.

Organization and paragraph development

Your essay has a logical broad structure, but some paragraphs repeat the same idea instead of developing it.

Suggestions:
Paragraph 1 after introduction: Explain pro-death-penalty argument #1: retribution.
Paragraph 2: Explain pro-death-penalty argument #2: deterrence.
Paragraph 3: Refute retribution by arguing that revenge is not justice and that state killing devalues life.
Paragraph 4: Refute deterrence using evidence showing weak or inconclusive support.
Conclusion: Reinforce your claim without introducing overly dramatic new language.

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a clear topic sentence and ends by connecting back to your thesis.

Tone and style

At times, your language becomes too emotional or absolute for an academic argument. Phrases like:
“heinous action”
“society simply craves violence”
“effective destruction of civilization”
“barbaric”
sound forceful, but they can weaken your credibility if they seem exaggerated.

Aim for a more analytical tone:
Instead of:
> Society simply craves violence...

Try:
> Public support for capital punishment may partly reflect a desire for retribution, which can blur the distinction between justice and revenge.

This sounds more measured and persuasive.

Precision and logic

Some claims are too sweeping or unsupported. For example:
“It is a known fact that for most people, the biggest fear in life is the fear of death.”
“Anyone who is placed in their shoes would wish for the death penalty...”
“Society and the government have no right to take the life of another man.”

These are strong assertions, but they need either evidence or more careful wording.

Revise by qualifying claims:
“Many people fear death intensely...”
“Some victims’ families may support execution as a form of justice...”
“Opponents argue that the state should not claim the moral authority to take life...”

This will make your argument more credible.

Sentence-level concerns

A few style and grammar issues to address:
Avoid unnecessary gendered language like “another man”; use person, individual, or human being.
Watch agreement: “society... they” shifts between singular and plural.
Fix spacing/typing errors such as “indivi dual.”
Reduce repetition of phrases like “value of human life” by occasionally varying wording.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, concentrate on:
Replacing every placeholder with real evidence
Strengthening your thesis
Reducing repetition
Using a more academic tone
Explaining your evidence instead of only stating opinions

Your essay already has a strong moral center and a clear position. With fuller evidence and more precise reasoning, it could become a much more convincing academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on an important and difficult topic, and it has a clear overall position: affirmative action remains necessary. You also show awareness that strong argumentative writing must engage opposing views, not simply assert a claim. That is a solid foundation. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: thesis and purpose, use of evidence, and organization/style.

Clarify the thesis and narrow the focus
Right now, your introduction promises several different things: to define affirmative action, explain its legality, show both sides, argue it is necessary, and explain how supporters can improve their arguments. That is too much for one essay, and it makes the paper feel divided between arguing your own view and analyzing how others argue.

Choose one main purpose. Most likely, your strongest paper would argue:
Affirmative action remains necessary because racial inequality persists, and carefully designed policies are constitutional and help counter ongoing discrimination.

If you want to keep the “how advocates can improve their argument” section, make it a brief concluding point rather than a major part of the essay.

Replace placeholders with real evidence
The biggest problem is that key quotations are missing, marked as “.” Because of this, your argument often relies on assertion rather than proof. In revision, you need to:

insert the actual quotations or paraphrases,
identify each source clearly,
explain how each source supports your claim.

For example, when you discuss Kennedy’s Executive Order, quote the relevant wording and then explain how it defines affirmative action. When you refer to Shaw, Gillespie, or Beer, summarize their arguments accurately and connect them to your own.

Also, be careful with broad claims such as:
“The proponents realize that without laws to insure a certain number of minorities be hired, many would never be hired.”
“Opponents state that it is up to blacks to raise their morals...”

These are sweeping statements and need specific support. Avoid making the opposition sound simplistic unless you can document that exact view.

Strengthen the structure of the argument
The essay currently moves through several topics, but the logic is not always smooth. A clearer structure might be:

Introduction
   - brief context
   - definition of affirmative action
   - clear thesis

What affirmative action is / is not
   - distinguish between goals, outreach, and quotas
   - explain that affirmative action is broader than quotas

Legality
   - present the opposition’s constitutional concern
   - respond with evidence showing that affirmative action is not automatically unconstitutional

Necessity
   - argue that persistent racial inequality justifies continued intervention
   - use evidence rather than generalizations

Counterargument and response
   - address “reverse discrimination” and “stigma” concerns seriously
   - explain why these concerns do not outweigh the need for the policy

Conclusion
   - restate the argument clearly
   - avoid introducing new claims

This structure would help readers see your reasoning step by step.

Improve fairness and precision
You are most persuasive when you acknowledge complexity. At times, though, your language becomes too absolute or emotionally charged. For example, saying advocates would have a case “nearly infallible” overstates your position. In academic argument, it is better to sound measured and precise.

Similarly, some claims risk overgeneralization:
“Opponents demand blacks to get out of jail...”
“The only way to guarantee they get into the system is through affirmative action programs.”

Try revising in a more careful way:
“Some critics argue that social progress depends more on individual responsibility than on policy intervention.”
“Supporters argue that affirmative action remains one important tool for increasing access.”

This makes your essay sound more credible and scholarly.

Work on paragraph development
Several paragraphs begin with a good topic, but then shift quickly or end before the idea is fully developed. In revision, make sure each paragraph does one job:

start with a clear topic sentence,
provide evidence,
explain the evidence,
connect it back to the thesis.

For instance, the paragraph beginning “Now that we have established the legality...” is very short and mostly transitional. Expand it with actual reasoning and evidence, or combine it with the next paragraph.

Edit for sentence-level issues
There are a number of grammar, spelling, and wording problems that weaken the essay’s authority. Examples include:

“tents” → tenets
“achie ved” → achieved
“caucasians” should usually be capitalized if you capitalize “blacks,” or use parallel terms such as White people and Black people
“stope” → stop
“writer’s need” → writers need

Also watch for awkward phrasing:
“achieve of their own merit” → achieve on their own merit
“legislation to insure the rights” → legislation to ensure the rights

Final recommendation
Your essay has a strong central conviction and shows real engagement with both sides of the debate. To make it persuasive, revise toward one clear thesis, specific evidence, and more precise, balanced analysis. If you replace the missing source material, narrow the paper’s purpose, and organize the argument more clearly, this draft could become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that the case against teaching New Age beliefs is stronger than the case for it. You also show engagement with sources and try to evaluate arguments in terms of values, consequences, credibility, and evidence. That gives you a solid starting point. For revision, the main work is to improve focus, fairness, organization, and use of evidence.

Clarify your thesis and central claim

A more focused thesis might look like this:
> Although advocates of New Age practices present them as harmless or beneficial, the arguments against teaching New Age beliefs are more persuasive because they rely on clearer moral frameworks, stronger evidence of consequences, and more credible sources.

That kind of thesis tells the reader exactly what the essay will prove.

Distinguish “teaching New Age beliefs” from “teaching about New Age beliefs”
This is one of the most important issues in your essay. Sometimes you seem to argue against promoting or endorsing New Age beliefs, while at other times you seem to oppose teaching about them in a school or academic setting. Those are very different claims.

Teaching New Age beliefs = encouraging students to adopt them
Teaching about New Age beliefs = studying them as part of religion, culture, or history

Your essay should decide which one you mean and stay consistent. Right now, that ambiguity weakens your argument.

Improve your use of sources
The biggest weakness is that many quotations are missing and replaced with “.” Because of that, readers cannot evaluate your evidence. In revision:

Include the actual quotations.
Introduce each source clearly.
Explain how the quoted material supports your point.
Cite the author, title, and publication consistently.

But popularity does not automatically equal credibility, and a magazine’s religious perspective may also make it biased. Similarly, you dismiss pro–New Age arguments too quickly as “opinion.” For a stronger essay, evaluate both sides by the same standards:
evidence
reasoning
accuracy
expertise
bias

Avoid relying only on personal reaction
Some sections use personal response in place of analysis. For example, the paragraph beginning “I honestly ran out of my room…” is vivid, but it is not strong academic evidence. Instead of emphasizing your reaction and your neighbors’ opinions, explain:
why the source’s claim is inaccurate,
what evidence disproves it,
and how that inaccuracy damages the source’s credibility.

Academic argument is strongest when it uses verifiable support rather than anecdote.

Reconsider unsupported assumptions
Several claims need more support or a more careful tone. For example:
“People were tired of rules and went searching for something that could unify everyone.”
“The whole new age movement is becoming very popular… due to extreme political correctness.”
“The Bible is very real and true.”

These may reflect your beliefs, but in an argumentative essay, they need evidence or qualification. Also, if your audience does not already share your religious assumptions, simply stating that the Bible is true will not persuade them. If you want to use the Bible, frame it more carefully: for example, as an authoritative text within a Christian worldview, not as unquestioned proof for all readers.

Strengthen organization and paragraph structure
Some paragraphs are long and move through several ideas at once. Try this structure for body paragraphs:

Topic sentence stating the paragraph’s claim  
Evidence from a source  
Analysis of that evidence  
Connection back to thesis  

For example, one paragraph could focus only on credibility of sources, another only on consequences in schools, and another only on value systems.

Revise tone and style
Your voice is energetic, but parts of the essay sound conversational or dismissive. Academic writing works better with a more precise tone. Examples to revise:
“that little green guy”
“stupendous job”
“for all I know”
“making things up himself”

Also watch for grammar and mechanics:
capitalize consistently (“Furthermore,” not “furthermore” mid-sentence unless needed)
correct spacing/typing errors (“te aching,” “can not” → usually “cannot”)
fix agreement and phrasing (“one author’s opinion,” “many times” not “many time”)

Insert and properly cite all missing quotations.  
Replace personal reactions with source-based analysis.  
Evaluate both sides more fairly and consistently.

You have a strong sense of conviction and a clear attempt to compare arguments. With better focus, stronger evidence, and more careful reasoning, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: to compare how opponents and proponents of nuclear power argue, and to show that proponents rely more on facts and evidence. You also maintain a consistent focus on persuasion, which gives the paper direction. The essay is organized into recognizable sections—introduction, discussion of opponents, discussion of proponents’ main points, and conclusion—which is a solid foundation to build on.

What is working well

Clear central claim  
Your thesis appears in the introduction: opponents appeal to values and beliefs, while proponents use facts and figures. This gives the essay an argumentative frame rather than just listing pros and cons.

Logical overall structure  
You divide the body into major categories—cost, safety, environmental effects, and waste—which helps readers follow your discussion.

Awareness of rhetoric  
One of the essay’s strongest ideas is that the debate is not just about energy, but about *how* each side persuades the public. That rhetorical focus is interesting and worth keeping.

Main areas to revise

Clarify the thesis and match the body to it
Right now, the thesis says the essay will compare methods of persuasion, but much of the body shifts into arguing that nuclear power is actually good. Decide which paper you want this to be:

Option A: a rhetorical analysis of how each side argues
Option B: an argumentative essay supporting nuclear power

At present, it tries to do both. A stronger revision would choose one main purpose. If you keep the rhetorical analysis focus, each paragraph should explain not only *what supporters claim* but *how those claims persuade*.

Improve accuracy, evidence, and credibility
Many statistics and claims appear without sources, such as cost comparisons, lives saved, and radiation claims. Because your paper emphasizes “facts and figures,” it especially needs reliable evidence.

For revision:
Add credible sources for all numerical claims.
Avoid broad statements that seem unsupported, such as “the general public becomes instantly closed minded.”
Be careful with historical and scientific claims. For example, the opening sentence about a nuclear bomb’s explosive power is distracting and may be inaccurate or irrelevant to a discussion of nuclear energy.

Make the argument more balanced and nuanced
The paper sometimes presents opponents as simply fear-driven or uninformed. That weakens your credibility because serious concerns about nuclear safety, waste storage, and cost do exist. A stronger essay would acknowledge that opponents may also use evidence, not just emotion.

For example, instead of saying opponents “take advantage” of public unfamiliarity, you might say they emphasize risks that resonate with public safety concerns. This sounds more analytical and less biased.

Strengthen paragraph focus
Several body paragraphs begin with a clear topic but drift into general commentary about the media or public opinion. Try to keep each paragraph centered on one point:

Topic sentence
Evidence/example
Analysis of how the evidence persuades
Link back to thesis

For instance, in the safety paragraph, much of the discussion turns to media reputation. If media framing is important, it may deserve its own paragraph.

Style and sentence-level concerns

Tone and wording
Some phrasing is too informal or emotionally loaded for academic writing. Examples include:
“closed minded”
“beat the nuclear waste issue to death”
“flat out”
“shot down from the start”

Replace these with more formal alternatives.

Grammar and usage
There are frequent sentence-level errors that interfere with clarity. Some patterns to fix:

Word choice: “do to” should be due to
Comparatives: “more cost-efficient then” should be than
Verb forms: “counter act” should be counteract
Agreement and phrasing: “the amount of precaution took” should be the precautions taken
Spelling: “meggawatts,” “radio active,” “rail road”
Article/adjective issues: “common An American” appears to be a typo

Sentence boundaries and punctuation
Some sentences are run-ons or comma splices, such as:
“Thus, a large group of opponents to nuclear power are formed, they fear this new entity…”

Break these into separate sentences or use proper conjunctions.

Suggested revision plan

Rewrite the introduction so it presents the issue more directly and avoids the nuclear bomb opening.
Refine the thesis to state one clear purpose.
Revise each body paragraph to connect evidence to rhetorical strategy.
Add sources for all factual claims.
Acknowledge counterarguments fairly to sound more credible.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

Final thought

This essay has a strong underlying idea: public debates are shaped not just by facts, but by emotional appeals and rhetorical choices. If you sharpen the thesis, support your claims with credible evidence, and revise for a more formal and balanced tone, this could become a much stronger academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important and complex issue: whether school integration improves education and race relations. You show a clear interest in the topic, and one of the strongest features of the paper is that you try to balance benefits and drawbacks rather than treating the issue as simple. Your personal experience with Chapter 220 also gives the essay a distinctive voice. Try to make that claim explicit in the introduction. For example:

Possible revision:  
*School integration can improve educational opportunity and racial understanding, but its success depends on the age at which students enter integrated schools, the climate created by teachers and administrators, and the support of the wider community.*

A clearer thesis will help unify the essay and guide each paragraph.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Your essay has a reasonable general structure: educational benefits, possible harms, race relations, and community influence. However, some paragraphs are long and contain multiple ideas. That makes the argument harder to follow.

For revision:
Give each paragraph one main point.
Start each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.
Use transitions that show how the next paragraph relates to the previous one.

For instance, the paragraph beginning “Even though there are some individuals...” discusses self-esteem, teacher climate, minority status, and adult attitudes. These could be better organized into two paragraphs:
how school climate affects achievement  
how minority status and adult attitudes affect self-esteem and performance

Use evidence more precisely
You refer to studies and books, which is good, but many references are too vague. For example:
“According to a thesis written by Howard Fuller, .”
“A study done by two researchers from New York...”
“According to the book *Desegregation and Beyond*...”

Because some quotations or citations are missing, the reader cannot evaluate the evidence. In revision:
Complete all missing citations or quotations.
Identify sources more specifically.
Explain how each source supports your claim.

Also, be careful not to rely too heavily on personal experience as proof of general claims. Your Chapter 220 example is useful, but it should support—not replace—broader evidence.

Reconsider tone and fairness
This is the most important area for revision. Some passages make broad claims about racial groups in ways that can sound biased or unsupported. For example:
“many of them were rebellious”
“Many black students took advantage...”
“This led to abuse of the rules... by some black students”

These statements risk weakening your credibility because they generalize from personal observation and may appear to blame one group rather than analyzing the structural problems of the program.

Instead, revise toward more precise, analytical language. For example:
focus on how students may have felt isolated when entering at an older age
discuss how inadequate support, poor communication, or community tension contributed to conflict
distinguish between individual behavior and larger patterns

This will make your argument stronger and more academically persuasive.

Strengthen analysis
Often the essay describes what happened but stops short of analyzing why it matters. Push each paragraph further by explaining:
why early placement helps students adapt
how teacher expectations shape student achievement
why community resistance undermines school policy
what this suggests about the conditions necessary for successful integration

In other words, don’t just report evidence—interpret it.

Revise sentence-level issues
There are many grammar and style problems that distract from your ideas. Common issues include:
word choice: “conductive” should be conducive; “hinderance” should be hindrance
awkward phrasing: “The ethical dispute over school integration has based itself...” is unclear
comma/splice problems: “As of 1970 forty-four percent...” needs punctuation after the introductory phrase
agreement and article errors: “a hinderance” / “As can seen” should be As can be seen
overly long sentences: several sentences should be divided for clarity

Read the essay aloud during revision. That often helps identify sentences that are too long or unclear.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion points to an important idea: integration needs support beyond the schools themselves. This is a strong insight. Make the ending more forceful by tying it directly back to your thesis and summarizing the key conditions for success.

Revision priorities
Write a clear, arguable thesis.  
Replace generalizations with precise, balanced analysis.  
Complete and clarify missing evidence/citations.  
Reorganize body paragraphs around single claims.  
Edit carefully for grammar and wording.

You have the basis for a thoughtful argument here. With a clearer thesis, more careful evidence, and a more analytical tone, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central concern: racism on predominantly white campuses is real, damaging, and should be addressed in ways that promote integration rather than further separation. That gives your paper a strong argumentative purpose. You also show ambition by engaging a difficult issue from multiple angles: stereotypes, campus climate, historical context, and student organizations. For revision, the main work is to make your argument more precise, better supported, and more ethically balanced.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis
Right now, the essay’s thesis is present but somewhat diffuse. You seem to argue two things:

Black students on white campuses experience racism and alienation.
Separate black campus organizations are not the best solution because they risk reinforcing division.

That is a workable argument, but you should state it more directly in the introduction. For example:  
“Although black students often create separate organizations in response to racism on predominantly white campuses, these organizations can unintentionally deepen division; universities should instead pursue forms of inclusion that address racism while fostering meaningful cross-racial engagement.”

This kind of thesis is more specific, arguable, and easier to prove.

Fill in missing evidence
Several places in the essay contain placeholders (“”), which makes the argument incomplete. More importantly, even where you refer to examples, they are often too vague. For instance:

the Marquette student example
the Penn State study
the Isaiah Thomas example
references to Steele

These need to be introduced clearly, cited accurately, and explained. Don’t assume the reader knows them. After each source or example, explain how it supports your claim.

A useful structure is:
make a claim
present evidence
interpret the evidence
connect it back to the thesis

Without this, the paper relies too heavily on assertion.

Improve logical balance
A major revision issue is fairness. Your essay sometimes shifts from criticizing racism to placing the burden of change mainly on black students. For example, when you argue that black students “must be the ones to start the change,” the reasoning may sound unjust, especially since your essay has already shown that they are responding to exclusion.

Also, your treatment of black organizations is too one-sided. You argue that they encourage separation, but you do not seriously consider their positive functions: support, safety, cultural affirmation, political advocacy, and community-building. A stronger essay would acknowledge these benefits and then argue why integrationist solutions are still preferable.

That makes your position more persuasive because it shows you understand the complexity of the issue.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some claims are too broad and need qualification. Examples include:

“the white majority in this country have held themselves in higher respect”
“the white population is not going to be the one to step forward”
“separate black organizations are segregation”

These statements risk sounding absolute or reductive. Try using more careful language such as:
“many white institutions have historically privileged white experiences”
“some white students and administrators fail to address racism”
“these organizations may unintentionally contribute to social separation”

Academic arguments are usually stronger when they avoid sweeping claims.

Strengthen organization
The essay has a general progression, but some paragraphs repeat ideas instead of developing them. Try reorganizing around a clearer structure:

Introduction: context + thesis  
Campus racism is real: examples and studies  
Effects on black students: alienation, inferiority, pressure, exclusion  
Why separate organizations emerge: understandable response to racism  
Why separation may have limits: may reduce dialogue, allow institutions to avoid reform  
Better alternative: integration plus institutional action  
Conclusion

This structure will help the reader follow your reasoning.

Revise style and tone
There are many sentence-level problems that make the paper harder to read:
awkward phrasing (“what is would be like”)
word choice issues (“felling” instead of “feeling”)
repetition
comma misuse
unclear pronoun reference
grammatical inconsistency (“ones” should be “one’s”)

Some sentences are also overly charged or imprecise, such as “the way to end this race war.” That phrase may distract from your argument. Aim for a more analytical tone.

Example revision:
Original: “The African American on a predominantly white campus is the subject of racial dispute and discrimination caused by stereotypes, and experiences.”
Revised: “On predominantly white campuses, African American students often face discrimination shaped by racial stereotypes and exclusionary experiences.”

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion currently repeats the main idea but does not fully synthesize the essay. A stronger conclusion should:
restate the thesis in fresh language
emphasize the complexity of the issue
propose what universities should do

For instance, you might argue that universities should confront racism directly through inclusive policies, shared spaces, dialogue, and support systems that do not isolate students by race.

Revision priorities
Complete all missing evidence and citations.
Rewrite the thesis to make the argument specific.
Present black organizations more fairly before critiquing their limits.
Reduce overgeneralizations and absolutist claims.
Edit carefully for grammar, clarity, and formal tone.

You have an important topic and a clear desire to make a serious argument. The next step is to make the essay more precise, balanced, and well-supported so that readers can trust your reasoning.

Relevant ethical issue: You address emotional strain, parental rights, and the Baby M case, all of which are important to the topic.
Attempt to engage opposing views: You do not ignore the pro-surrogacy side, which is important in an argumentative essay.

Most important revision priorities

A. Complete missing material
The biggest problem is that the essay contains multiple “” placeholders. These make the argument incomplete and difficult to follow. Before anything else, replace each placeholder with:

a quotation,
a paraphrase from a source,
or a transition/explanation.

Right now, the reader cannot fully understand your evidence or how your sources support your claims.

B. Strengthen the thesis
Your thesis is present, but it could be sharper and more precise. At the moment, the essay seems to argue several things at once: biological parenthood, emotional suffering, and possible baby-selling. Choose the main reasons and state them clearly.

For example:
“Surrogate motherhood is ethically problematic because it can commodify women’s bodies, create emotional harm for the surrogate and intended parents, and produce conflicts over parental rights.”

This kind of thesis gives your paper a stronger structure.

C. Improve organization
Several paragraphs repeat similar ideas rather than developing one clear point each. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Define surrogacy
   - Briefly mention Baby M
   - Present thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Emotional harm to the surrogate
   - Pregnancy creates physical and emotional bonds
   - Separation after birth may cause distress
   - Use evidence

Body Paragraph 2: Problems for intended parents
   - Infertility grief, possible disappointment, parental uncertainty
   - Tension if the surrogate changes her mind

Body Paragraph 3: Ethical concern of commodification
   - Explain the “renting a womb” or “baby selling” concern carefully
   - Avoid overstatement; support with evidence

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument and rebuttal
   - Present the case for reproductive freedom and helping infertile couples
   - Then explain why you still find surrogacy unethical

Conclusion
   - Summarize the argument without repeating the same wording

You refer to “the author,” “Health services,” and “Annas,” but the quotations or ideas are incomplete. Make sure each source is:
clearly introduced,
accurately quoted or paraphrased,
and followed by your explanation of how it supports your argument.

Also, avoid making broad claims without proof. For example:
“They do not see the surrogate mother as a person who has feelings” is too sweeping unless you provide evidence.
“Many times the mother may feel left out” may be true in some cases, but you need support.

Try using more cautious academic phrasing:
“Some intended parents may overlook…”
“Critics argue that…”
“This suggests that…”

Tone and fairness
Your argument becomes stronger when you avoid emotionally charged phrasing such as “walking womb.” Although it expresses your concern vividly, it may sound more accusatory than analytical. In academic writing, a measured tone is usually more persuasive.

Instead of:
“just a walking womb”

Try:
“reduced to a reproductive function”
“treated instrumentally rather than as a full person”

These choices sound more formal and credible.

Sentence-level issues

Grammar and wording
There are frequent errors in:
subject-verb agreement (“services… argues”),
possessives (“couples lives” → “couple’s lives”),
apostrophes (“fathers” → “father’s,” depending on meaning),
word choice (“bore” should be “borne” in this context),
spacing (“mother hood” → “motherhood”).

Examples:

“Others feels” → “Others feel”

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
Filling in all missing sections,
creating a precise thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one claim,
supporting claims with evidence,
revising for grammar and academic tone.

You have the basis of an argument here, but it needs fuller development and clearer support to become persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and important argument: prescription drug prices can harm patients, and federal regulation may be necessary. You also show strong engagement with the topic and use specific examples, which is a major strength. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, strengthening organization, improving evidence, and editing for accuracy and style.

Main strengths
Clear concern and purpose: You strongly communicate that high drug prices affect real people, especially the elderly and those with fixed incomes.
Specific examples: Depo-Provera, Coumadin/Percocet, and Clozaril show that you are trying to support your claims with concrete cases.
Sense of audience: Your appeal to public health and fairness is persuasive and emotionally compelling.

Thesis and argument
Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction: federal intervention is necessary through a price regulation review board. This is a solid arguable claim, but it needs to be stated more precisely.

Right now, the essay sometimes shifts between:
arguing that drug prices are too high,
arguing that pharmaceutical companies are unethical,
arguing that price boards in other countries work.

Try revising the thesis so it previews your main reasons. For example:

Possible revision:  
“Because pharmaceutical companies have raised prices far beyond inflation, imposed unnecessary costs through packaging and distribution practices, and charged far more than regulated markets in Canada and Europe, the federal government should establish a drug price review board to protect patients and healthcare providers.”

This gives your essay a clearer structure.

Organization
Your essay would benefit from a more consistent paragraph structure. Right now, some paragraphs begin with one idea and move into another. A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: background on medical advances + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: drug prices have risen faster than inflation  
Body paragraph 2: out-of-pocket costs hurt consumers and hospitals  
Body paragraph 3: international comparison—Canada and Europe  
Body paragraph 4: example of Depo-Provera  
Body paragraph 5: example of packaging/unit-dose pricing  
Body paragraph 6: Clozaril monitoring requirement  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why regulation matters

Also, avoid ending with rhetorical questions alone. Your conclusion should leave the reader with a firm final claim, not just a question.

Evidence and development
You use numbers and examples, which is good, but many claims need clearer explanation or better support.

Important issues:
There are missing parts marked by “.” These gaps weaken your credibility and must be filled.
Some statistics are presented without enough explanation. For example, the paragraph about healthcare expenditures needs clearer logic: how does the 55% out-of-pocket figure directly support your claim about drug overpricing?
Some examples need more context. Explain exactly how each example proves the need for federal regulation.

Suggestions:
After each statistic or example, add 2–3 sentences explaining why it matters.
Make sure all examples connect back to your thesis: not just “this was expensive,” but “this shows why the market alone does not protect patients.”

Accuracy and credibility
This is the area that most needs attention. Several details appear inaccurate or inconsistent, which can seriously weaken an argumentative essay.

Examples:
Depo-Provera is an injectable contraceptive, not an oral contraceptive.
Clozaril/clozapine is generally used for treatment-resistant schizophrenia, not senile dementia.
You mention a “$9000 heart monitor,” but clozapine is associated more commonly with blood monitoring; this detail should be checked carefully.
“Recieving,” “agrues,” and other misspellings reduce trust in your research.
You refer to “the Senate Special Committee on Aging reported in 1991 in 1991” — this repetition should be corrected.

Before revising, verify each drug example with a reliable source. If an example is incorrect, replace it rather than trying to defend it.

Tone and style
Your tone is passionate, which helps, but some wording is too emotionally charged for academic argument. Phrases like:
“price blackmail”
“blatant overpricing”
“skyrocketing of already outrageous prices”

can make the essay sound more like a speech than a researched argument. Use a more formal tone and let the evidence carry the force.

For example:
“price blackmail” → “coercive pricing practices”
“outrageous prices” → “excessive prices”

Also, avoid first-person comments such as “As a nursing student…” unless the assignment specifically welcomes personal perspective. In most academic arguments, evidence is stronger than personal testimony.

Sentence-level revision
There are many grammar, punctuation, and word-choice problems that need editing. Common issues include:
spelling: “argues,” “receive,” “proper,” “can’t”

awkward repetition: “wouldn’t have lived otherwise”
sentence clarity and run-ons

Revised:  
“Many drugs have extended the lives of patients who otherwise might not have survived.”

Revision priorities
Correct factual errors and fill in missing sections.
Rewrite the thesis to make the argument more specific.
Reorganize body paragraphs around clear reasons.
Explain how each example supports federal regulation.
Edit for grammar, spelling, and academic tone.

You have the foundation of a persuasive essay here. With stronger accuracy, clearer structure, and more polished prose, your argument will become much more convincing.
