Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure. The introduction establishes the debate, and the body paragraphs are organized around three main claims: deterrence, constitutional problems, and ethics. This gives the paper a solid foundation. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening evidence, clarity, paragraph development, and academic style.

Strongest aspects
Clear thesis: You clearly argue that the death penalty should be abolished.
Logical organization: The essay follows a consistent structure, with each body section addressing a distinct reason.
Awareness of counterargument: You acknowledge the pro–death penalty position before arguing against it, which is important in argumentative writing.

Most important revision priority: incomplete and unsupported evidence
The biggest issue is that many sentences contain placeholders such as “”, which means key evidence is missing. Because your argument depends heavily on studies, cases, and quotations, these gaps seriously weaken the paper. Before revising anything else, complete all missing material.

Also, even when sources are mentioned, they are often introduced vaguely:
“A study conducted by… showed that…”
“Time magazine reported that…”
“Newsweek reported that…”

You need to explain what exactly the study found, when it was published, and why it is credible and relevant. Avoid simply naming a source and moving on.

What to do
For each source:
Identify it fully.
Summarize the key finding accurately.
Explain how it supports your claim.
Cite it in the style required by your course.

Improve analysis, not just summary
At times, the essay lists studies or examples without fully analyzing them. For instance, in the deterrence paragraph, several studies are mentioned one after another, but the paragraph does not always explain how they fit together or why they are more convincing than opposing evidence.

Try using this pattern in body paragraphs:
Topic sentence
Evidence
Analysis of evidence
Connection to thesis

For example, after presenting a study, explain:
Does it challenge the deterrence theory?
Does it suggest the death penalty has no effect, or a harmful effect?
Why should readers trust this evidence?

This will make your argument sound more thoughtful and persuasive.

Address counterarguments more fully
You mention supporters’ views, especially in the ethics section, but the rebuttal is sometimes too quick. A stronger argument would engage more directly with major opposing claims, such as:
the death penalty deters murder,
it delivers justice for victims,
it is constitutional because the law allows it.

Rather than saying those arguments “do not stand up,” explain why they fail, using evidence and reasoning. Academic argument is strongest when it fairly represents the other side before refuting it.

Style and tone
Your tone is generally appropriate, but some sentences are too informal or absolute. For example:
“fair is fair”
“it is clear”
“one would think”

These expressions can sound conversational or overstated. In academic writing, aim for more precise phrasing, such as:
“Supporters often rely on retributive justice…”
“The evidence presented here suggests…”
“These findings indicate…”

Also avoid broad claims that are not supported, such as “courts are so eager to find a scapegoat.” That may be arguable, but it needs evidence.

Sentence-level issues
There are several grammar and mechanics problems that should be corrected in revision:

Capitalization: “both sides” should begin with “Both sides.”
Word choice: “put a stop to it all together” should be “altogether” or better, “end its use entirely.”
Spacing/typos: “nu merous” should be “numerous.”
Repetition: The phrase “It is reported” or “It is stated” appears often. Vary your sentence openings.
Clarity: Some pronouns are vague, such as “They believe, .” Be specific about who “they” are.

Read the essay aloud during revision; this will help you catch awkward or repetitive phrasing.

Paragraph development
Some paragraphs are too long and cover too many ideas. The first body paragraph, for example, includes abolition statistics, the brutalization effect, publicized executions, and state-level comparisons. Consider dividing it into two paragraphs:
evidence that the death penalty does not deter crime,
evidence that it may increase violence.

This would improve readability and help you develop each point more fully.

Conclusion
Your conclusion clearly restates the thesis, but it mostly repeats earlier points. Try ending with a broader final insight: for example, what the issue reveals about justice, state power, or the purpose of punishment.

Final advice
For your next draft, prioritize:
Fill in all missing evidence and citations
Add deeper analysis after each example
Strengthen rebuttals to opposing views
Revise for formal academic tone
Proofread for grammar, spelling, and clarity

You already have a workable structure and a strong central claim. With fuller evidence and more careful analysis, this essay could become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0013.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you compare Wall and Pattullo and conclude that Pattullo makes the stronger case. The paper also shows engagement with the assigned readings and attempts to evaluate reasoning rather than merely summarize. To revise effectively, focus on 1) sharpening the thesis, 2) using evidence accurately and fairly, 3) strengthening logic, and 4) improving style and correctness.

Clarify and focus your thesis
Your introduction builds context, but the central claim is wordy and grammatically awkward. It also seems to decide too early that the answer is “obvious” and then reverses itself.

Current idea:
Wall argues for civil rights.
Pattullo is more convincing because he addresses realistic consequences.

A stronger thesis would be more precise:
“Although Wall frames military service as a civil-rights issue, Pattullo appears more persuasive because he addresses concerns about privacy, unit cohesion, and military discipline in greater detail.”

This version avoids absolute language like “it will be ultimately decided” and makes your criteria for judgment clear.

Use textual evidence directly and accurately
The biggest weakness in the essay is the lack of actual quoted evidence. Several places use placeholders like “,” which makes it impossible for readers to judge your interpretation. In a comparison essay, you need specific quotations or paraphrases from both authors.

For each body paragraph:
Make a clear claim.
Introduce a quotation or detailed paraphrase.
Explain how that evidence supports your point.
Compare the two writers’ approaches.

For example, instead of saying Pattullo “merely addresses the very real concern,” show exactly how he frames that concern. Then explain whether his reasoning is based on evidence, assumption, or emotional appeal.

Strengthen your analysis of reasoning
Right now, some paragraphs repeat Pattullo’s claims without examining them critically. Since this is an argumentative analysis, you should assess how well each author argues, not just whether you agree.

Questions to ask in revision:
Does Pattullo provide evidence, or does he rely on hypothetical fears?
Does Wall address counterarguments?
Are either author’s claims based on stereotypes or unsupported assumptions?
What values underlie each argument: equality, privacy, morale, efficiency?

For instance, when you say shared barracks would be uncomfortable, that is an assertion, not proof. You need to explain why this discomfort should justify exclusion and whether the same logic would apply to other groups. Similarly, the claim that allowing gay soldiers would “destroy” relationships is very strong and needs evidence. Without support, it sounds overstated.

A stronger analytical move would be:
“Pattullo’s argument is effective rhetorically because he appeals to readers’ concerns about privacy and cohesion; however, some of his claims depend on predicting misconduct rather than proving it.”

This kind of sentence shows mature critical thinking.

Improve organization
Your essay generally alternates between Pattullo and Wall, which is useful, but the structure could be clearer.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: context + thesis
Body Paragraph 1: compare how each author frames the issue
Body Paragraph 2: privacy/barracks concerns
Body Paragraph 3: unit cohesion and morale
Body Paragraph 4: civil rights vs. military practicality
Conclusion: evaluate which argument is stronger and why

Use topic sentences that signal comparison:
“Pattullo and Wall differ most sharply in how they define the central issue.”
“On the question of barracks privacy, Pattullo offers a practical concern, while Wall minimizes that concern.”

Address tone and fairness
Some wording sounds biased or imprecise, such as “the obvious answer,” “gay-free,” or “worsen it with the presence of homosexuals.” Academic argument works best when the tone is measured and fair. Try to describe positions rather than endorse loaded language.

Also, avoid assuming that homosexuality automatically creates sexual threat or misconduct unless the source provides evidence. Otherwise, your essay risks repeating assumptions instead of analyzing arguments.

Correct grammar and sentence-level issues
There are several errors that weaken credibility. Examples:
“there should be a ban gays serving” → “there should be a ban on gays serving”
“will make on change their mind” → “will make one change one’s mind”
“Wall’s articles does” → “Wall’s article does”
“there was not reason” → “there was no reason”
“claims has had an affect” → “claims have had an effect”

Also watch for:
subject-verb agreement
article use (“a barrack” should be “a barracks” or “the barracks”)
awkward phrasing
comma placement around titles

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a relevant topic: mandatory drug testing in sport. Your essay shows strong concern for fairness, athlete safety, and the influence of role models. These are persuasive points, and your introduction gives readers a clear sense of the issue. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your evidence, organization, and style.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: Your central claim is easy to identify: mandatory drug testing is ethical and necessary.
Relevant reasons: You offer three strong lines of argument—fair competition, health protection, and the social influence of athletes.
Awareness of counterargument: You recognize the privacy objection, which is important in argumentative writing.

Most important revision priority: evidence
The biggest problem is that many quotations are missing and replaced by “”. Because of this, several paragraphs make claims without proof. In an argumentative essay, your credibility depends on showing exactly what your sources say.

What to do:
Replace every “” with the actual quotation or paraphrased evidence.
Introduce each source clearly: author, article title, and why the source is credible.
Explain each quotation after you present it. Do not assume the evidence speaks for itself.

Example revision pattern:
Instead of:
> “Dr Paul Thompson states, .”

Try:
> “In ‘In Track and Field Time for Naked Truth,’ Dr. Paul Thompson argues that steroid use undermines the integrity of competition because it rewards chemical enhancement rather than training and talent. This supports the idea that testing is necessary to preserve fairness.”

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Right now, some paragraphs repeat the same idea, and some include several different points at once. A stronger structure would make your argument easier to follow.

Suggested structure:
Introduction
   - Hook
   - Background on steroids/performance-enhancing drugs
   - Thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Fairness in competition
   - Explain how drug use creates unfair advantage
   - Use evidence from sports officials or governing bodies
   - Connect back to mandatory testing

Body Paragraph 2: Health and safety
   - Discuss short-term and long-term side effects
   - Use medical evidence
   - Explain why sports organizations have a duty to protect athletes

Body Paragraph 3: Role models and social influence
   - Explain how professional and student athletes affect others
   - Use examples such as Ben Johnson
   - Show why prevention matters beyond elite sport

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument and rebuttal
   - Present privacy concerns fairly
   - Then explain why limited, regulated testing is justified

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis in new words
   - Summarize key reasons
   - End with a strong final thought

Develop your counterargument more fairly
You mention privacy, but the discussion is brief and not fully developed. A strong argumentative essay should present the opposing side accurately before refuting it.

For example:
Why do some people see random drug testing as invasive?
What rights do athletes give up when they compete?
Under what conditions is testing ethical?

Then rebut:
Testing is justified because athletes compete under agreed rules.
Fairness and safety are compelling interests.
Procedures can be designed to reduce unnecessary invasions of privacy.

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, which is good, but some sentences are informal or rhetorical in ways that weaken the academic voice.

Examples:
“What will it take for athletes to think differently about steroids.”
“What are fans of great athletes supposed to think...”

These questions could be revised into stronger statements:
> “The deaths and public downfalls associated with steroid abuse should serve as warnings to athletes and fans alike.”

Also avoid emotional wording unless supported by evidence. For example, “devastating” can work, but it is stronger when followed by specific medical facts.

Sentence-level issues to edit
There are several grammar, spelling, and word-choice problems. Some examples:

“athletes rights” → athletes’ rights
“National Collegiate Association of Athletes” → likely National Collegiate Athletic Association (NCAA)
“lesson” → lessen
“meathod” → method
“strickter” → stricter
“On example” → One example
“Lial Alsado” → likely Lyle Alzado
“drug testings” → drug tests or drug testing
“unan nounced” → unannounced

Also check sentence clarity. Example:
> “By mandatory drug testing athletes it would lesson...”
could become:
> “Mandatory drug testing would reduce, and potentially prevent, the use of performance-enhancing drugs.”

Final advice
When you revise, focus first on:
Adding real evidence
Reorganizing by clear argument points
Strengthening the counterargument
Editing for grammar and accuracy

You have the basis for a solid argumentative essay. With stronger source integration and clearer organization, your paper will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear goal: you compare different positions on prayer in public schools and argue that a “moment of silence” is the best solution. That gives your paper a definite argumentative direction, which is a strong starting point. You also show awareness of multiple perspectives rather than discussing only one side. For revision, focus on three main areas: clarity of thesis, use of evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the thesis and overall argument
Your introduction presents the issue and names the groups involved, but the thesis is too long and difficult to follow. It tries to do several things at once: compare articles, evaluate bias, criticize weak solutions, and propose a third option. A clearer thesis would help organize the whole essay.

Current idea: the Civil Liberties articles are stronger than the Conservative Religious articles, but both sides have weak solutions, and Anthony Lewis offers the best answer.

Suggested revision:  
*Although the pro-school-prayer argument appeals to tradition and free speech, the anti-school-prayer argument is better supported by constitutional and ethical reasoning; however, the most practical solution is a moment of silence, which protects both religious freedom and minority rights.*

This version is more focused and gives you a roadmap for your body paragraphs.

Improve paragraph structure
Most body paragraphs begin with a good main point, but many become repetitive because they summarize, then simply say “this is a good argument” or “this is weak reasoning.” Instead, each paragraph should follow this pattern:

Topic sentence stating the claim  
Evidence from the article  
Analysis explaining why that evidence is strong or weak  
Connection back to your thesis

For example, instead of writing that an author is biased, explain how the bias appears: emotionally loaded language? lack of evidence? one-sided examples? You need more analysis and less general evaluation.

Use quotations and evidence correctly
The biggest problem in the draft is that many quotations are missing and replaced with “.” Without the actual evidence, your claims cannot be evaluated. In revision, insert the quotations or paraphrased details from the articles and cite them clearly.

Also, do not rely on vague phrases like:
“This is a good statement”
“This is good reasoning”
“He uses fallacy”
“The author is not stating directly…”

Instead, be specific:
What kind of fallacy?
What evidence is missing?
Why does the quotation support or weaken the argument?

For example:  
*The claim that school prayer would reduce crime and teenage pregnancy is weak because the author provides no statistical evidence linking public prayer to social behavior.*

That kind of sentence is much stronger.

Develop the “moment of silence” section
Your final position is promising, but it needs more development. Right now, you mostly say that a moment of silence is “the best solution.” Explain why more fully:

How does it respect the First Amendment?
How does it allow private prayer without school endorsement?
How does it protect students of minority faiths or no faith?
Why is it more practical than organized prayer?

This should be your strongest section, since it presents your own argument. Make sure it is not shorter or less detailed than the sections summarizing others.

Maintain a more formal academic tone
Some wording is informal or imprecise. Examples:
“One, is to give students…”
“o.k.”
“bring out the event”
“trapped by other religions”

Try more formal alternatives:
*One reason is…*
*acceptable*
*refer to the 1969 Supreme Court decision*
*pressured by the religious practices of the majority*

Also, avoid phrases like “In my opinion” unless your instructor specifically wants a personal-response style. In an argumentative essay, your opinion should come through your analysis.

Correct grammar, spelling, and mechanics
There are frequent errors that distract from your ideas. Some common ones include:

Spelling: *arise, known, toward, contain, reasons, liberties, minorities, effects*
Word choice: *titles* should be *titled*; *sustain from hurting* should be *prevent harm to*
Capitalization: *God’s*, *Bible*
Sentence structure: Some sentences are run-ons or awkwardly phrased.

Example revision:  
Original: *This moment of silence will sustain from hurting anyone in a minority religion.*  
Better: *A moment of silence would prevent students from minority religions from feeling excluded or pressured.*

Proofread carefully, and read the essay aloud to catch awkward sentences.

Suggested revision priorities
Insert all missing quotations or paraphrased evidence.  
Rewrite the thesis for clarity.  
Revise each body paragraph to include deeper analysis.  
Expand the final section on the moment of silence.  
Edit for grammar, spelling, and formal tone.

Final assessment
You have the foundation of a solid argumentative essay because you compare viewpoints and end with a clear position. The main revision challenge is to move from broad summary to specific, evidence-based analysis. Once you add the missing textual support and polish the organization and language, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position against the death penalty, and you give three main reasons for that position: it does not deter crime, it is immoral, and it is flawed in practice. That gives the paper a solid argumentative structure. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your thesis, evidence, organization, and style.

Clarify and sharpen the thesis
Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction: the death penalty is unnecessary because it “will not stop our criminal problems, it is immoral, and may even hinder the development of our society.” This is a workable claim, but it can be made more precise and academic.

For example, revise toward something like:
“The death penalty should be abolished because it does not effectively deter violent crime, it raises serious moral concerns, and its unequal and error-prone application undermines justice.”

This version is more specific and previews your body paragraphs clearly.

Replace informal language with academic tone
A major weakness is the repeated use of conversational phrasing: “Well,” “to me,” “huge mistake,” “something to talk about,” and rhetorical questions such as “is it moral for the government to turn right around and murder them?” These make the essay sound personal rather than analytical.

Try to:
avoid “Well,” “basically,” and similar fillers
reduce first-person phrases like “I believe” and “to me”
replace rhetorical questions with direct claims

For example:
Instead of “Well, I believe…”, write “Capital punishment is unnecessary because…”
Instead of “No, to me no murder is right…”, write “State execution remains morally wrong because it deliberately takes human life.”

Strengthen evidence and integrate sources properly
The biggest issue in the essay is incomplete or missing source material, marked by . These gaps seriously weaken your credibility. You mention Forst, Solomon, and Jim Gittings, but you do not provide the quotations or enough context about who they are, what they studied, or why their views matter.

When revising:
fill in every missing quotation or remove it
introduce each source clearly
explain how the source supports your argument
cite sources in the required format

For example, instead of:
“In 1983 a scientist named Forst did one such study…”

Try:
“In a 1983 study, criminologist Forst found no significant evidence that executions reduced homicide rates, suggesting that capital punishment fails as a deterrent.”

Then follow with analysis:
“This finding supports the argument that harsher punishment alone does not prevent violent crime.”

Also, be careful with unsupported generalizations such as:
“the crime rate has continued to increase”
“many criminals have been able to successfully appeal”
“there have been too many cases in which someone has been falsely accused”

These claims need evidence, statistics, or specific examples.

Improve paragraph focus and logic
Your body paragraphs have clear topics, but some points drift or repeat. Make sure each paragraph centers on one main claim.

A stronger structure would be:
Deterrence argument: death penalty does not reduce homicide.
Moral argument: state killing is ethically inconsistent with justice.
Practical/legal argument: unequal representation, appeals, and wrongful convictions make it unreliable.

Within paragraphs, use the pattern:
claim → evidence → explanation → link back to thesis

For example, in the morality paragraph, the move from victims’ feelings to “society” is abrupt. Develop the connection more carefully.

Address the opposing side more fairly
You mention supporters of the death penalty, but sometimes their views are oversimplified, such as suggesting politicians support it only for political gain. That may be true in some cases, but as written it sounds dismissive.

A stronger argument acknowledges opposing views fairly:
supporters argue that the death penalty provides justice and protects society
then you explain why those arguments are insufficient

This makes your essay sound more thoughtful and persuasive.

Revise sentence-level errors
There are several grammar and wording problems that distract from your ideas. Some examples:

“increases crime” → “increased crime”
“it necessity” → “its necessity”
“full-proof” → “foolproof”
“so ciety” → “society”
“If somebody makes a horrible mistake of murdering someone else”  
  Better: “If someone commits murder”
“murdering of the murderer”  
  Better: “execution of the offender”

Also watch sentence length. Some sentences are long and loosely connected. Breaking them into shorter, more precise sentences will improve clarity.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion restates the argument, but it becomes emotional and informal at the end. Try to conclude by reinforcing the broader significance of your position.

For example:
“Rather than relying on a punishment that is ineffective, morally troubling, and vulnerable to error, society should pursue policies that reduce crime through prevention, education, and a more equitable justice system.”

Revision priorities
Fill in or replace all missing quotations and evidence.
Rewrite the thesis in clearer academic language.
Remove informal phrasing and rhetorical questions.
Organize each body paragraph around one main claim.
Correct grammar, spelling, and word choice errors.

Your essay has a strong core argument; the next step is to make it more credible, polished, and academically persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position against government-mandated prayer in public schools, and it shows strong engagement with a controversial public issue. You also attempt to address counterarguments, which is an important feature of argumentative writing. For revision, the main priorities are: sharpening your thesis, improving organization, strengthening evidence, and correcting style and grammar.

Strengths

Clear stance: Your position is consistent throughout the essay: government-written prayer in public schools should not be instituted.
Relevant topic: The essay addresses a significant political and constitutional debate.
Attention to counterargument: You recognize that some readers may believe school prayer could help children from unhealthy homes or communities.
Range of reasons: You argue that prayer should be taught by families and religious communities, that school is for academics, that forced prayer is meaningless, and that government involvement threatens religious freedom.

These are good foundations. The next step is to make the argument more precise and better supported.

Thesis and focus

Your thesis appears at the end of the first paragraph, but it is somewhat narrow and awkwardly phrased: “religion is imposed on the children who will recite the prayer with little interest to actually pray.” This sounds like only one reason, while the essay later presents several others.

Revise the thesis so it previews your main claims more clearly. For example:

> Government-mandated prayer should not be instituted in public schools because it violates religious freedom, does not produce genuine moral development, and distracts schools from their educational mission.

A thesis like this would unify the whole essay and help guide your body paragraphs.

Organization

Right now, the essay has many ideas, but the progression is sometimes repetitive or unclear. Several paragraphs make similar points about prayer being insincere or ineffective. Try reorganizing by one main claim per paragraph.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Body 1: Moral and religious education should come from families and faith communities  
Body 2: Government-written prayer in schools violates religious freedom / separation of church and state  
Body 3: Required or scheduled prayer is unlikely to be meaningful or effective  
Body 4: Counterargument—children from troubled homes—and why voluntary after-school religious groups are a better solution  
Conclusion: reinforce the broader principle of religious liberty

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a strong topic sentence and ends by tying the point back to the thesis.

Use of evidence

A major weakness is the handling of sources. Many quotations are marked as “”, so the reader cannot evaluate the evidence. As written, the essay often refers to sources without actually presenting their content. This makes the argument feel incomplete.

For revision:

Replace each placeholder with the actual quotation or paraphrased idea.
Introduce the source clearly and explain how it supports your point.
Avoid stacking references without analysis.

For example, instead of:
> Anna Quindlen… states .

Try:
> Anna Quindlen argues that meaningful prayer requires private conviction rather than public routine, which supports the idea that school-sponsored prayer would be more ceremonial than sincere.

Your essay will improve greatly if you spend more time analyzing evidence rather than just mentioning newspapers, dates, and authors.

Reasoning and development

Some claims need stronger logical support. For example, you say there is “some connection between the rise in crimes and the lack of prayer in public schools,” but you do not prove this and it may weaken your own argument. If you reject that claim, do so directly and explain why correlation does not equal causation.

Similarly, the suggestion that after-school religious activities “would not be a difficult or controversial activity to establish” seems too broad. In reality, that claim may also be debated. Be careful not to overstate. A better approach would be to say voluntary, student-led religious groups are less coercive than school-sponsored prayer.

Style and tone

The tone is generally serious, but there are places where word choice becomes inflated or imprecise, such as “the already decadent public school system.” This sounds emotionally charged rather than analytical. Academic argument is usually stronger when phrased more carefully.

Also avoid sweeping terms like “obviously,” “there can never be,” or “the majority of communities would, indeed, find agreement,” unless you can prove them.

Aim for more concise sentences. Some are long and hard to follow.

Grammar and mechanics

There are frequent sentence-level errors that affect clarity. A few examples:

Spelling/typing: “i children” should be “in children”; “This means” not “This means”; “Even is there is” should be “Even if there is.”
Agreement: “Does this means” should be “Does this mean.”
Pronouns: Keep pronoun references clear, especially when discussing “students,” “children,” “they,” and “one.”
Punctuation: Some commas are missing or misused.
Quotation format: Titles of articles should be formatted consistently, and quotations should be integrated grammatically.

Careful proofreading is necessary.

Revision priorities

Write a clearer, more comprehensive thesis.
Reorganize the body so each paragraph develops one main reason.
Replace all source placeholders with actual evidence.
Add analysis after each quotation or reference.
Remove repetition and overstated claims.
Proofread for grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity.

Overall, your essay has a strong topic and a defensible argument. With clearer structure, fuller evidence, and more polished language, it could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of animal testing and tries to engage the ethical complexity of the issue. You also attempt to consider the opposing view, which is essential in argumentative writing. To revise effectively, focus on four main areas: thesis and focus, use of evidence, organization, and style/mechanics.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Your essay’s main claim appears to be that animal testing is ethically justified when it is necessary to protect human life, though some forms of testing are unnecessary and should be limited. That is a strong, arguable position—but right now it is buried under general discussion.

A revised thesis might say something like:

> Although some forms of animal experimentation are cruel and should be eliminated, animal testing remains ethically justifiable when it is necessary for major medical advances, when no alternative exists, and when it is conducted under strict humane standards.

This gives your paper a clear line of argument and helps readers understand the conditions under which you support testing.

Improve organization and coherence
At the moment, the essay moves back and forth between points without a clear structure. For example, the first paragraph argues for animal testing, but the second begins again with a general introduction to the controversy. That makes the essay feel repetitive.

Consider organizing the essay like this:

Introduction
   - Introduce the controversy
   - Define the issue
   - Present your thesis

Ethical argument for animal testing
   - Human health and survival
   - Medical necessity
   - Lack of alternatives

Counterargument
   - Animal suffering
   - Rights-based ethical concerns
   - Examples like the Draize test

Rebuttal / qualification
   - Some testing is unjustifiable
   - Medical research differs from cosmetic or unnecessary testing
   - Humane regulation matters

Conclusion
   - Restate your position
   - Emphasize ethical limits and necessity

This structure would make your argument easier to follow.

Use evidence more carefully
You include examples, especially the “Timmy Baker” case and references to Dr. Traystman, but the essay has several problems with evidence:

Some quotations and citations are missing, marked only by .
Sources are not clearly introduced or documented.
Some claims are too sweeping, such as “Animals were put on earth for humans to do with as they please,” which many readers will reject unless carefully framed as a religious perspective rather than a fact.
The thalidomide example seems inaccurate or at least unclear. Be careful: thalidomide is better known for causing birth defects, so this example needs correction and reliable sourcing.

To strengthen your paper:
Replace placeholders with actual quotations and citations.
Verify every factual claim.
Distinguish between evidence and opinion.
Use multiple reliable sources, not only anecdotal examples.

Also, avoid relying too heavily on emotional appeals such as “Thank God for little Timmy.” Emotional language can weaken academic credibility if it replaces analysis.

Develop the counterargument fairly
You do include the views of animal rights activists, which is good. However, you sometimes present them too briefly or dismissively. A stronger argument comes from engaging the opposition seriously.

For example, you could explain that activists argue:
animals can suffer and therefore deserve moral consideration;
causing pain is wrong even if the subject is not human;
some tests are unnecessary or outdated.

Then respond by distinguishing between:
medical testing that may save lives, and
nonessential testing such as for cosmetics or minor commercial benefits.

This would make your position more nuanced and persuasive.

Revise tone and word choice
Some phrases sound overly emotional, biased, or informal for an academic essay:
“Thank God for little Timmy”
“do with as they please”
“superior species”
“who’s life is more sacred”

Try to use more neutral, precise language. For example:
“This case suggests that animal research can contribute to life-saving treatment.”
“Humans often prioritize human welfare in medical ethics.”
“The ethical question is whether preventing human suffering can justify animal experimentation.”

A more formal tone will make your argument stronger.

Correct grammar, punctuation, and sentence-level issues
There are many mechanical problems that interfere with clarity. A few patterns to fix:

Sentence fragments: “Some of them easily justified, and some are not.”
Possessives: “friends side” → “friend’s side”
Subject-verb agreement: “if human’s want” → “if humans want”
Word choice: “a medicine aides in the healing off birth defects” is unclear and incorrect
Typos: “contro versy,” “Animal researches,” “my not,” “pain less”
Comma misuse: many unnecessary commas break up sentences awkwardly

Read the essay aloud during revision; this often helps identify awkward syntax and run-on ideas.

Final revision goal
Your essay has a defendable central idea: animal testing may be ethically acceptable when it serves urgent medical purposes and no alternative exists, but not all animal experimentation can be justified. That is a promising argument. To improve, make your thesis explicit, organize your points logically, verify your evidence, and adopt a more formal academic tone.

If you revise with those priorities in mind, your essay will become much more convincing and polished.

Refute it with reasoning and evidence.

For instance, you might argue that revenue differences often result from lower institutional investment and less media exposure—not from lack of value or talent.

Style and tone

Your passionate voice is effective, but some phrases are too informal for an academic essay, such as:
“at least we don't belong in the kitchen anymore”
“the men screwed things up first”
“lower end of the totem pole”

These phrases may distract readers from your argument. Aim for a more formal tone while keeping your strong voice.

Example revision:
Instead of:
> “Women on the otherhand take all the hand-me-downs.”

Try:
> “Women’s teams are often expected to compete with outdated equipment and inferior facilities.”

Sentence-level issues to edit

There are frequent grammar, spelling, and usage problems that weaken credibility. Watch especially for:

Spelling: “supposeively” → “supposedly”
Word choice: “a women” → “women”
Agreement: “their lucky” → “they’re lucky”
Comparative form: “take competition as serious” → “take competition as seriously”
Spacing: “big  money maker” has an extra space
Possessives and plurals: “men teams” → “men’s teams”
Transitions: some paragraphs need smoother connections

Also, some sentences are run-on or awkwardly phrased. Read aloud during revision to catch unclear wording.

Thesis revision suggestion

Your current thesis is clear but long. Consider making it more concise and argumentative:

> “Although Title IX promised equal opportunity, women’s college basketball still receives less media attention, fewer resources, and less respect than men’s basketball.”

This version sets up the main categories your essay can then prove.

Final advice

For your next draft, prioritize these steps in order:

Fill in all missing evidence.
Reorganize body paragraphs around clear main points.
Strengthen counterarguments and rebuttals.
Revise for formal tone.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

You already have a strong foundation: a compelling issue, a clear position, and genuine commitment. With stronger evidence and tighter organization, this essay could become much more persuasive.

For example, instead of:

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay presents a clear central idea: business should value ethical relationships, not just profit. That is a strong and worthwhile argument, and you show awareness that business affects consumers, employees, and society. You also try to use examples and outside sources, which is important in argumentative writing. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving organization, strengthening evidence, and polishing style and grammar.

Main strength: a clear overall position
Your essay consistently argues that businesses succeed when they build trust, respect, and long-term relationships. That gives the paper unity. You also attempt to address a counterargument, which is a good feature of argumentative writing.

Biggest issue: missing and incomplete evidence
The most serious problem is the repeated use of “” where quotations or source material should appear. Because of these gaps, many paragraphs feel unfinished and your claims are not fully supported.

What to do:
Go back and insert the exact quotations, paraphrases, or facts from your sources.
Introduce each source clearly: author, title, and relevance.
Explain each quotation after you include it. Do not assume the quote speaks for itself.

Example:
Instead of:
> “According to Fortune, . This supports my claim…”

Try:
> “According to *Fortune*, companies that maintain customer trust are more likely to keep long-term buyers. This supports my claim because ethical treatment creates loyalty, which strengthens both relationships and profits.”

Thesis and focus
Your introduction discusses business and ethics broadly, but your main argument should be stated more directly and specifically. Right now, the thesis is spread across several sentences.

Suggested revision:
> “Although many people believe business is mainly about profit, ethical business practices and strong relationships with consumers, employees, and society are essential to long-term success.”

This version is clearer, arguable, and gives your paper a roadmap.

Organization and paragraph structure
Many paragraphs contain useful ideas, but the essay sometimes feels repetitive because several body paragraphs make the same general point: “relationships matter more than profit.” You need clearer paragraph purposes.

Try this structure:
Introduction: define business ethics and present thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: ethical relationships between producers and consumers.
Body Paragraph 2: honesty, fraud, and moral rules in business.
Body Paragraph 3: example of an ethical company (Sam Walton/Walmart).
Body Paragraph 4: unethical advertising and sales tactics.
Body Paragraph 5: counterargument and response (profit-first view, B.F. Goodrich case).
Conclusion: restate thesis and explain why the issue matters.

Paragraph tip:
Each body paragraph should have:
a topic sentence,
evidence,
analysis,
a link back to the thesis.

Analysis needs to go deeper
Often you make a point, but the explanation stays general. For example, you say unethical advertising “hurts the relationship,” but you should explain how and why. Does it damage trust? Cause customers not to return? Hurt a company’s reputation?

Push your analysis further:
What are the consequences?
Why does this matter in the long term?
How does this example prove your thesis?

Tone and word choice
At times, your language becomes too emotional or informal for an academic essay. For example:
“This is what irritates business”
“they are just greedy”
“money can not buy you friends”

These phrases sound more like personal opinion than formal argument.

Revise toward a more academic tone:
Instead of:
> “They are just greedy and out for self satisfaction…”

Try:
> “Such practices prioritize short-term profit over ethical responsibility and can undermine consumer trust.”

Grammar, sentence structure, and style
There are many run-on sentences, comma splices, and awkward phrases. These make the essay harder to follow.

Common problems:
Missing commas after introductory phrases
Long sentences joined incorrectly
Word choice errors (“suppose” should be supposed)
Agreement issues (“Ethics are” may work grammatically, but “ethics is” often fits better when referring to the field)

Example revision:
Original:
> “This also hurts the relationship between the business environment; the producers are lying or making their product seem better than it really is…”

Revised:

Read your essay aloud while revising. If a sentence feels too long or confusing, split it into two.

Conclusion
Your conclusion has energy, but it becomes too broad and personal. Try to end by reinforcing your argument rather than adding new ideas.

Stronger conclusion strategy:

End with the larger significance of ethical business practices.

Final revision priorities

Write a precise thesis.
Improve paragraph organization and reduce repetition.

You have a solid foundation and a meaningful argument. With clearer evidence and tighter organization, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear overall purpose: to compare two arguments about prayer in public schools and show why Shriver’s is stronger than Methvin’s. Your essay has a recognizable structure, a consistent position, and some effective use of concepts from argument analysis such as claim, support, and fallacies. The paper would improve significantly, however, if you sharpened your thesis, incorporated direct textual evidence, and revised for clarity and mechanics.

What is working

Clear stance
Your introduction establishes your position early: Shriver’s argument is stronger because it uses facts and avoids fallacies. This gives the essay direction.

Comparative organization
You separate your discussion of Methvin and Shriver into different sections, which helps the reader follow your comparison.

Use of argumentative vocabulary
You show awareness of analytical terms such as *claim*, *supporting evidence*, *appeal to authority*, and *opposition*. This is appropriate for an argumentative analysis essay.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen and narrow the thesis
Your thesis is present, but it is somewhat broad and repetitive. It would be stronger if it named the specific criteria you will use to evaluate each author.

For example, instead of:
> “Shriver's argument is a stronger argument when compared to Methvin because he presents facts that support his position rather than just the opinions of people, and he does not commit any fallacies that weaken his argument.”

You might write:
> “Shriver offers the stronger argument because his claim is clearer, his evidence is better explained, and he addresses opposing views more effectively than Methvin.”

This gives you three precise standards to organize the paper.

The biggest weakness in the draft is the repeated use of “” instead of quotations. Without the actual words from Methvin and Shriver, your reader cannot evaluate your analysis. Revision should focus on inserting key quotations from both authors.

What exactly is the author claiming?
How does this quote support or weaken the argument?
Why does this matter for your comparison?

Right now, you sometimes summarize too generally instead of closely analyzing the texts.

Be more precise in your reasoning
At times, your analysis makes claims that need stronger justification. For example, when you say Methvin commits an “appeal to unqualified authority,” you need to prove that the source is actually unqualified, not just quoted. Quoting an authority is not automatically a fallacy. A better approach would be to explain whether Methvin establishes the authority’s expertise and whether the quotation is relevant and sufficient.

Similarly, when you say Shriver presents “facts,” make sure they are actually facts rather than interpretations, religious principles, or moral reasoning. Use accurate terminology.

Improve paragraph development
Some body paragraphs begin with a useful point but then become repetitive or vague. Try this structure for each paragraph:
Topic sentence naming a criterion of evaluation  
Evidence from the text  
Explanation of how the evidence works  
Comparison to the other author, if relevant  
Link back to thesis

This will help avoid broad statements like “This is a very strong aspect to his argument” without explaining why.

Revise the introduction and conclusion
Your introduction gives useful historical context, but it is too long and contains several grammar problems. Move more quickly to the essay’s main task: comparing two authors’ arguments.

Your conclusion mostly repeats earlier points. Instead, use it to reinforce the significance of your analysis—for example, what this comparison shows about effective argumentation in general.

Style and grammar issues to fix

There are frequent sentence-level errors that affect clarity. A few patterns:

Missing or incorrect words:  
  “an influential factor,” “has become,” “people learned”
Word choice:  
  “praying in public schools” is more natural than “praying in the public schools” in repeated use
Run-on or awkward sentences:  
  “But even though it has been instrumental…” is long and tangled
Capitalization and punctuation:  
  “Although” should be capitalized at the start of the final paragraph sentence
Agreement and phrasing:  
  “Everything that he claims is supported…” is better than “Everything that he claims is supported by numerous pieces…”

Read the essay aloud slowly; this will help you catch awkward phrasing and repetition.

Suggested revision priorities

Replace all “” with actual quotations.
Revise the thesis to list clear evaluative criteria.
Strengthen your analysis of evidence rather than relying on general praise or criticism.
Clarify claims about fallacies and authority.
Edit carefully for grammar, sentence structure, and concision.

Your next step is to support your evaluation with specific textual evidence and more precise analysis. If you make those changes, the essay will become much more persuasive and academically strong.

Recognition of complexity

Main revision priorities

Complete and document your evidence

the warden’s press release,
the media groups’ legal argument,
the First Amendment,
Greg Sherwood’s statement,
Dan Morain’s explanation,

you should either quote directly or paraphrase accurately and provide a citation.

Also, be careful with a legal claim like “the media has a clear right under the First Amendment.” The First Amendment protects freedom of the press, but courts do not always interpret that as an unlimited right of access to prisons or executions. Your current opening is wordy and overly broad: “the public has a right to be informed about anything and everything…” That claim is too sweeping and easy to challenge. A stronger thesis would be narrower and more defensible.

For example:
> The media should be permitted to witness and report on executions because public oversight is essential in a democracy, although prisons may reasonably restrict cameras and other equipment for security reasons.

This version is clearer, more formal, and better matched to the evidence in your essay.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay sometimes reads like a summary of events rather than a sustained argument. Consider reorganizing into clear sections:

Introduction and thesis
Background on the Harris case
Why media access matters  
   - public oversight  
   - accountability  
   - First Amendment / public interest
Counterargument  
   - concerns about sensationalism and “high ratings”
Response / limits on access  
   - why witnessing should be allowed  
   - why filming can be restricted
Conclusion

This structure would help each paragraph contribute directly to your argument.

Develop analysis, not just reporting
Several paragraphs summarize who sued whom and what was decided, but they do not always explain why that matters to your argument. After presenting facts, add analysis.

For instance, after describing Judge Schnacke’s ruling, explain how it supports your claim:
Does it show that total exclusion of reporters was unjustified?
Does it support your distinction between observing and filming?
Does it suggest that restrictions must be based on actual security evidence?

That kind of explanation will make the essay more argumentative.

Style and sentence-level issues

There are a number of grammar and wording problems to fix. Some examples:

“there access should be restricted” → “their access should be restricted”
“One such case which receive national attention” → “One such case that received national attention”
“had made to effort” → “had made no effort”
“inmate are more tense” → “inmates are more tense”
“they are not reason to exclude” → “they are not reasons to exclude”

Also aim for a more formal tone. Phrases like “If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off” sound conversational and dismissive. A stronger ending would reinforce your principle about public oversight rather than address the reader directly.

Suggestions for revision

Replace all missing quotation placeholders.
Narrow and sharpen the thesis.
Add citations for legal and factual claims.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one advances the argument.
Expand analysis after each piece of evidence.
Revise for grammar, wording, and formal tone.

Final thought

You have the foundation of a persuasive essay, especially because you make an important distinction between press access and camera access. With fuller evidence, stronger legal precision, and clearer organization, this paper could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear sense of personal investment, and that gives it emotional force. The opening anecdote is memorable and immediately shows why the issue matters to you. You also make your position clear: because many parents and churches avoid the topic, schools should teach sex education that includes morals, values, and safety. That clarity is a strong foundation for revision.

What is working well

Strong personal voice  
Your personal story creates authenticity and shows the real-life consequences of limited sexual guidance. This helps readers understand why you care about the issue.

Clear central concern  
You consistently focus on youth sexual education and the risks of silence: pregnancy, disease, and poor decision-making.

Sense of urgency  
The essay makes it clear that this issue affects real teenagers and families, not just abstract policy debates.

Main areas to revise

Sharpen your thesis
Right now, your essay argues several things at once: abstinence, marriage, moral instruction, condom use, criticism of media, criticism of parents, and criticism of churches. Try turning these into one focused claim.

For example:  
“Because many teenagers do not receive adequate guidance about sex at home, schools should provide comprehensive sex education that includes health information, relationship ethics, and decision-making skills.”

This thesis is clearer and easier to support than a broader claim about schools teaching “morals and values.”

Distinguish between opinion and argument
Some statements are presented as facts but are really personal judgments, such as:
“Casual sex is unacceptable, degrading, animalistic”
“youngsters will inevitably go with the flow”
“the sacredness of marriage”

These claims may alienate readers who do not share your beliefs. In an argumentative essay, it is more effective to explain why certain behaviors can be harmful rather than simply condemning them. Aim for reasoning that can persuade a broader audience.

Use stronger evidence
Your draft refers to articles and studies, but several places contain missing citations: “”. This weakens your credibility. You need:
specific sources
statistics with dates
study results explained clearly
proper integration of evidence

For example, instead of saying “Results of these studies show ,” explain what the studies found. Did sex education reduce teen pregnancy? Increase condom use? Delay first sexual activity? Be precise.

Address the counterargument more fully
You mention that some people think teaching sexual morals in schools is unethical, but you dismiss them too quickly. A stronger argument would fairly represent the opposing view and then respond to it.

For example:
Some parents believe morality should be taught only at home.
Some worry schools will impose one value system on all students.

Then answer by arguing that schools can teach respect, consent, responsibility, and health without replacing family beliefs.

Improve organization
The essay sometimes repeats ideas or jumps between topics. A clearer structure might be:

Introduction: personal anecdote + thesis  
Problem: many teens do not receive enough guidance from parents/churches  
Why schools should help: health risks, pregnancy prevention, informed choices  
What schools should teach: anatomy, contraception, consent, relationships, responsibility  
Counterargument and response  
Conclusion: return to personal experience and broader social need

This structure will help your reader follow your reasoning.

Style and sentence-level revision

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too conversational or emotionally loaded for an academic essay, such as:
“live happily ever after”
“go with the flow”
“made sex sell”

You can keep your voice, but revise toward clearer academic phrasing.

Watch grammar and mechanics
There are frequent errors with:
apostrophes: “bird's and the bee's” should be “birds and the bees”
word choice: “class mate” should be “classmate”
capitalization: “Father” and “Him” do not usually need capitals
sentence boundaries and comma splices
agreement: “Educations of sex” should be “Sex education programs”
spelling: “its” should be “it’s” or better revised entirely; “Aids” should be “AIDS”
pronoun clarity: “them” and “they” sometimes have unclear referents

Avoid overgeneralizations
Statements such as “Education of sexual activity for youth is no longer taught by parents” are too absolute. Not all parents avoid the subject. Use more careful phrasing:
“Many parents struggle to discuss sex openly with their children.”
“Some teenagers receive little guidance at home.”

Most important revision priorities

Write a more precise thesis.  
Replace missing evidence with real sources and statistics.  
Reorganize body paragraphs around clear topic sentences.  
Reduce moralizing language and strengthen logical reasoning.  
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

Final encouragement

You have the basis for a meaningful argumentative essay because your topic is personal, socially important, and clearly felt. The next step is to transform strong feelings into a more focused academic argument supported by credible evidence. If you do that, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on an important ethical issue and has a clear central position: individuals should have the right to refuse life support. The paper is thoughtful and sincere, and your use of personal reflection helps show why the issue matters. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, organization, and support.

Main strength: clear engagement with the topic
You show genuine engagement with the moral complexity of life support decisions. The essay recognizes that people define life and death differently, and this idea is a strong foundation for your argument. Your personal paragraph near the end is especially effective because it makes your position concrete.

Clarify and strengthen the thesis
Right now, your main claim appears at the end of the introduction:  
“The right to refuse life-support is an individual one…”

That is a good start, but it needs to be more precise and argumentative. For example:

Stronger thesis: “Because definitions of quality of life differ from person to person, competent individuals should have the legal and moral right to refuse life-support treatment.”

This version makes your reasoning clearer: people differ in values, so the decision should belong to the individual.

Improve organization
At present, the essay sometimes moves from one idea to another without a clear progression. A stronger structure would help readers follow your argument.

Possible revised structure:
Introduction
   - Present the issue
   - Define the debate
   - State your thesis clearly

Why the decision is personal
   - People value life differently
   - Quality of life varies by individual beliefs, experiences, and goals

Defining life and death
   - Briefly explain how medical definitions have changed
   - Connect this change to the ethical uncertainty around life support

Arguments for allowing refusal of life support
   - Avoiding prolonged suffering
   - Respecting autonomy
   - Preserving dignity
   - Reducing emotional burden when wishes are already known

Role of physicians and families
   - Doctors can advise, but should not override patient wishes
   - Families benefit when decisions are made in advance

Conclusion
   - Reaffirm thesis
   - Emphasize importance of advance decisions such as living wills

Develop paragraphs with evidence and examples
Many paragraphs introduce interesting ideas but remain general. For an argumentative essay, you need more explanation and support.

For example, you write that some people “see no point in any kind of life support.” Why? You could expand with reasons such as:
fear of prolonged pain,
loss of dignity,
permanent unconsciousness,
inability to communicate,
emotional strain on family.

Similarly, when discussing definitions of death, you mention breathing, heartbeat, and brain activity. This is useful, but it needs explanation. Show how changing medical definitions make end-of-life decisions more complicated.

You could also strengthen the essay by referring to:
living wills,
advance directives,
patient autonomy,
legal rights in medical decision-making.

Even one or two specific examples would make your argument more persuasive.

Address the other side more fully
You briefly mention people who want life support at all costs, but this viewpoint needs fairer treatment. A strong argument acknowledges opposing views and responds to them.

For instance, you might say:
Some believe life should be preserved whenever possible because even a small chance of recovery matters.
However, while that choice should be respected, it should not be imposed on people who would refuse such treatment for themselves.

This helps you appear balanced and thoughtful.

Tone and style
Your tone is earnest, but at times the essay becomes repetitive, especially with phrases such as “our life, our death” and repeated rhetorical questions. Too many questions can weaken authority. Try replacing some questions with direct claims.

For example:
Instead of: “Where do you draw the line between life and death?”
Try: “The line between life and death is difficult to define, which is why individuals should be allowed to decide what forms of treatment they will accept.”

Also, avoid switching too often between “we,” “our,” and “I.” Since this is an argumentative essay, use “people,” “individuals,” or “patients” for general claims, and reserve “I” for your personal example.

Sentence-level issues to revise
There are several grammar and wording problems that need attention:

“issues need re-defining” → “issues need to be redefined”
“others now are tried and true” → awkward phrasing; revise for clarity
“what makes our life important to use” → likely typo; perhaps “important to us”
“people o want” → “people who want”
“Would we want someone else to make this decision without regarding our wish(es)”  
  → “Would we want someone else to make this decision without considering our wishes?”
“This is our life, our death that we are talking about.”  
  → add punctuation: “This is our life, our death, that we are talking about.”

Also, the  marks suggest missing material. Be sure to fill those gaps before revising further, because they interrupt meaning and coherence.

Final recommendation
Your essay has a strong moral concern at its center, but it needs clearer structure and more developed reasoning to become fully persuasive. In revision, focus on:
writing a precise thesis,
organizing ideas logically,
expanding body paragraphs with examples and explanation,
reducing repetition,
correcting grammar and incomplete passages.

With these improvements, your essay can become a thoughtful and convincing argument about patient autonomy and end-of-life choice.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear sense of urgency and a strong personal investment in the topic. You also present a definite claim: welfare reform should focus less on punishment and more on rebuilding families through support, training, counseling, and childcare. That is a promising argument. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving evidence, reducing overgeneralization, and strengthening organization and style.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Right now, your main claim is spread across several paragraphs. Try stating it in one precise sentence near the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Effective welfare reform should combine work expectations with practical support—such as job training, childcare, housing, and counseling—because punitive cuts alone weaken families rather than helping them become self-sufficient.*

This version is more specific and easier to prove than the broader claim about restoring “American family values.”

Strengthen your use of evidence
Your essay relies heavily on opinion, broad claims, and one personal anecdote. The anecdote about your visit to Social Services is vivid and useful, but by itself it cannot support large conclusions about “generations of American families” or the causes of crime.

You should add:
Historical evidence about how welfare programs developed
Policy evidence about what the “Contract with America” actually proposed
Research or statistics on welfare dependency, work transitions, childcare, and poverty
Sources for claims about outcomes for children and families

Also, be careful with historical accuracy. Welfare did not simply begin with Herbert Hoover; major federal relief and welfare structures are more strongly associated with Roosevelt’s New Deal and later Great Society programs. Check this section carefully.

Avoid sweeping generalizations
Some of your strongest problems are statements that assume too much about welfare recipients. For example:
“they have forgotten all about pride and self worth”
“have become indolent and/or chemical abusers”
“This is how the American Family... began to deteriorate”

These claims risk alienating readers because they stereotype poor families and present complex social issues as moral failings. They also weaken your credibility because they are not supported by evidence.

Instead, use more careful language:
“Some welfare policies may create disincentives to work”
“Long-term poverty can contribute to family instability”
“Reform should address barriers such as low wages, childcare costs, and substance abuse”

That kind of phrasing is more persuasive in academic argument.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Your essay has a recognizable structure, but some paragraphs mix too many ideas. A stronger structure might look like this:

Introduction: present the debate and your thesis  
Background: brief history of welfare and current reform proposals  
Problem with punitive reform: explain why cutting benefits alone can harm families  
Need for supportive reform: job training, childcare, housing, healthcare  
Role of counseling and transitional programs: parenting, addiction support, group homes  
Counterargument and response: acknowledge concerns about dependency, then explain why support plus work requirements is more effective  
Conclusion: restate your claim and its significance

Adding a counterargument would especially strengthen your essay. For example, you could concede that some critics worry welfare discourages work, then argue that this is exactly why reform must include both expectations and support.

Refine tone and word choice
Your essay is passionate, but at times the tone becomes too emotional or absolute for an academic argument. Phrases like “It is obvious” or “the greatest Country in the World” sound rhetorical rather than analytical.

Aim for a more measured tone:
Replace “It is obvious” with “These proposals suggest” or “This approach overlooks”
Replace emotionally charged claims with specific policy criticism

This will make your argument sound more thoughtful and credible.

Edit for grammar and mechanics
There are several recurring sentence-level issues:
Its/it’s: you often use “it’s” when you mean the possessive “its”
Comma errors: eg, “My reply, was” should be “My reply was”
Capitalization: words like “Country” and “Society” do not need capitalization in the middle of sentences
Quotation placeholders: the “” marks must be replaced with actual quotations or removed
Sentence clarity: some long sentences would be stronger if split into two

Also, be consistent with terms like “welfare system,” “American family,” and “work force/workforce.”

Most important revision priorities
If you only have time for major revisions, do these first:
Write a clear, specific thesis
Replace unsupported claims with evidence
Remove or soften stereotyping/generalizations
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one has one main point
Correct grammar and punctuation, especially its/it’s

Final thought
Your central insight is valuable: reform should help people move toward self-sufficiency instead of simply punishing poverty. If you support that claim with stronger evidence and a more precise, balanced tone, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: the Illinois Supreme Court made the wrong decision in transferring custody of Baby Richard to his biological father. You also show genuine engagement with the human stakes of the case, especially Richard’s emotional well-being and the broader consequences for adoption. The paper has strong passion and a recognizable argumentative purpose. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your evidence, sharpening your organization, and polishing style and mechanics.

Strengths
Clear thesis: You state your argument directly: “The Illinois Supreme Court made an immense mistake…”
Emotional engagement: The essay conveys why the issue matters and helps readers see the case as more than a legal dispute.
Awareness of broader implications: You move beyond the specific case to discuss adoption law and public trust in adoption systems.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Replace emotional appeal with stronger evidence
Much of the essay relies on emotionally charged language: “ripped away,” “tug of war,” “immense mistake,” “scar the image.” These phrases create feeling, but an academic argument needs more verifiable support.

For example:
Your claims about four-year-old development need evidence from psychology research, not only general statements.
Your comparison to Baby Jessica needs a source-based explanation of why the two cases differ.
Your claim that adoption is “already suffering” and that “more people are turning their backs on it” needs data, examples, or expert commentary.

Revision strategy: For each major claim, ask:  
What source proves this?  
If you cannot answer, either add evidence or reduce the certainty of the claim.

B. Address the counterargument fairly
Right now, the essay strongly favors the adoptive parents but gives limited attention to the legal or ethical argument for the biological father. A stronger argumentative essay should acknowledge why some people supported the court’s decision.

Possible counterarguments:
A biological parent’s rights should not be terminated unfairly.
Kirchner was deceived and acted once he learned the truth.
Courts may have been trying to protect legal principles, not just choose between two families.

If you acknowledge these points and then explain why Richard’s best interests should outweigh them, your argument will be more persuasive and more balanced.

C. Clarify your central claim
At times the essay seems to argue:
the court was legally wrong,
the court was ethically wrong,
the decision harms child psychology,
the case damages adoption as an institution.

These can work together, but right now they compete. Choose a main line of argument and organize your body paragraphs around it. For example:

Thesis: Although Kirchner’s parental rights deserved legal consideration, the court’s ruling was wrong because it ignored Richard’s psychological welfare, disrupted his established family bonds, and undermined confidence in the adoption system.

Then build one paragraph for each reason.

Organization
The essay would benefit from a more logical structure.

Current issues:
The introduction gives background, but some sentences are awkward or incomplete.
The time-line idea is introduced but not clearly developed.
The Baby Jessica comparison appears abruptly.
The final paragraphs repeat earlier points instead of building toward a conclusion.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: Brief background + clear thesis.
Background paragraph: Explain the case facts and legal situation.
Psychological argument: Use child development evidence carefully.
Counterargument paragraph: Present biological father’s rights fairly, then rebut.
Broader adoption implications: Explain how these cases affect public confidence and reform.
Conclusion: Reinforce why the “best interests of the child” should guide such decisions.

Use of Sources
Your draft includes placeholders such as “,” suggesting missing quotations or citations. These must be completed. Also, be careful not to overstate what sources say. For instance, citing Erikson can support discussion of developmental stages, but it may not prove exactly how Richard would respond unless paired with more specific child psychology research.

Style and Tone
Aim for a more formal academic tone.

Examples:
“Think back to when you were four years old” is too conversational.
“I’m not sure I can understand the ruling” centers your feelings more than the argument.
“A mother who cannot afford to keep her baby won’t have to kill it” is too blunt and may distract from your point.

Try replacing personal or emotionally loaded phrasing with precise analytical language.

Grammar and Mechanics

Capitalization: “when Daniela Janikova…” should be “When…”
Word choice: “whom are referred to” should be “who are referred to”
Spelling: “form” should be “from”; “very from state to state” should be “vary”
Punctuation and sentence boundaries: some sentences are run-ons or incomplete
Consistency: use a consistent way to refer to the case and the people involved

Final Advice
Your paper has a compelling topic and a strong sense of purpose. The next step is to make it more academic and credible by grounding your claims in evidence, organizing your reasons more clearly, and treating the opposing view fairly. If you revise with those goals in mind, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position against euthanasia and attempts to organize that position around three main arguments. The paper shows effort in addressing ethical, legal, and social concerns, and your conclusion returns to your central claim. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving accuracy and evidence, and strengthening logic.

Strengths
Clear stance: The reader always knows where you stand.
Organizational attempt: You outline three reasons in the introduction and generally return to them in the body.
Engagement with controversy: You recognize that euthanasia is ethically complex and emotionally charged.

Main issues to revise

A. Clarify and narrow the thesis
Right now, your thesis is broad and sometimes inconsistent. At times you argue euthanasia should not be allowed; at other times you argue it should not be “practiced as freely”; elsewhere you focus on legal vagueness and insufficient safeguards.

Choose one precise claim. For example:
“Euthanasia laws should be more restrictive because patient competence is difficult to assess and current legal safeguards are insufficient.”

This is stronger than arguing that America is “not ready,” which is harder to prove and sounds more like a prediction than an argument.

B. Define key terms
Your essay uses euthanasia broadly, but this topic requires precision. Are you discussing:
physician-assisted suicide,
voluntary euthanasia,
withdrawal of life support,
or all end-of-life decisions?

Without definitions, the argument becomes confusing and some claims risk being inaccurate. Early in the essay, define the term you mean and stick to it.

C. Improve logical reasoning
Several claims rely on weak or unsupported leaps:
Because some people protest killing animals, America is not ready for human euthanasia.
Because abortion led to violence, euthanasia will likely do the same.
Because a patient wants to die, the patient is probably mentally unstable.

These are not fully convincing as written. They need evidence and more careful reasoning. For example, the abortion comparison may be useful, but right now it functions mostly as analogy, not proof. Ask yourself: How exactly does this example support my claim about euthanasia law?

D. Use stronger, more relevant evidence
The biggest weakness is evidence. Some sources seem dated, unclear, or incomplete, and there are placeholder citations (“”) where quotations are missing. This makes the paper feel unfinished.

For revision:
Remove incomplete quotations.
Use credible sources on euthanasia law, medical ethics, and mental competency.
Explain each source clearly after introducing it.

You should especially support claims about:
how euthanasia is regulated in the U.S.,
whether family consent is required,
how patient competence is assessed,
and what legal “vagueness” actually means.

E. Check factual accuracy
Some claims seem questionable or oversimplified:
“Euthanasia is practiced throughout the U.S.”
“Currently, euthanasia has little restrictions on its practice.”
“The ruling on euthanasia took place in 1988.”

These statements need verification. In the U.S., laws on end-of-life decisions vary significantly by state, and “euthanasia” may not be legal in the way you describe. In an argumentative essay, factual precision is essential; otherwise readers may doubt the whole argument.

Organization and paragraph development
Your introduction is quite long and repetitive. Try this structure:
Introduce the issue briefly.
Define euthanasia.
Present your thesis clearly.
Preview your main reasons.

Each body paragraph should begin with a focused topic sentence and develop one argument. Right now, paragraphs sometimes drift into multiple ideas. For example, the third body paragraph discusses law, justice, competence, family rights, and political support all at once. Break that into smaller points or choose the strongest one.

Style and sentence-level concerns
There are many grammar and wording problems that make the essay harder to follow. Common issues include:
word choice: “there own lives” → “their own lives”; “a live is worth living” → “a life is worth living”
subject-verb agreement: “there is too much gray areas” → “there are too many gray areas”
plural/possessive errors: “all American possess” → “all Americans possess”
awkward phrasing: “Simply because that there are few people…” is unclear
capitalization: “supreme court” should be “Supreme Court”
informal or imprecise language: “snuffed out,” “full of wholes” (likely “holes”)

Read the paper aloud during revision. That will help you catch unclear or overly long sentences.

Suggestions for revision
Choose a narrower thesis focused on legal safeguards and competence.
Define euthanasia and distinguish it from related practices.
Replace weak analogies with direct evidence about law and medical ethics.
Add reliable sources and complete all citations.
Revise for factual accuracy about U.S. law.
Simplify paragraphs so each one develops one main point.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and clarity.

Final thought
Your essay has a strong sense of conviction, which is useful in argumentative writing. The next step is to match that conviction with precise definitions, accurate facts, and stronger evidence. If you revise with a narrower focus, this paper can become much more persuasive.
