Introduce each source clearly: who the person is, what text they are writing in, and why their view matters.
After each quotation, explain *how* it supports your point.

Example revision pattern:
Introduce: “Jonathan Sherman argues in ‘Pictures at an Execution’ that…”
Quote/paraphrase
Analyze: “This strengthens the opposition’s case because it gives a concrete social consequence rather than an abstract appeal to rights.”

Sharpen your thesis
Your current thesis is long and somewhat unclear: “The argument that executions should be televised… is not a strong argument because the support for the claim is not as concrete…”

You can make this more direct and specific.

Possible revision:
> Although KQED argues that banning televised executions violates freedom of the press, the opposition presents the stronger case because it offers clearer evidence about the social consequences of public exposure to executions.

This version clearly identifies:
the supporters’ claim,
your evaluation,
and the reason for that evaluation.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Some paragraphs repeat the same idea: that KQED relies too heavily on First Amendment rights and does not address consequences. This repetition makes the essay feel less controlled.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: context + thesis  
Supporters’ argument: explain KQED’s First Amendment/value claim fairly  
Why that argument is limited: show where evidence is thin  
Opponents’ argument: present their consequence-based case with examples  
Counterargument/complexity: address the idea that televising executions might educate the public  
Conclusion: restate why the opposition is stronger

Make sure each paragraph has one main point and a topic sentence that announces it.

Be more precise in your use of “value” and “consequence”
You repeatedly say KQED’s argument is a “matter of value” while the opposition’s is a “matter of consequence,” but the distinctions are not always fully explained.

Clarify the terms:
A value argument asks what is right, just, or important.
A consequence argument asks what effects or outcomes will result.

You might say:
KQED frames the issue as a value question about rights and press freedom.
Opponents frame it as a consequence question about harm, sensationalism, and increased violence.

This will make your analysis more convincing and consistent.

Reduce unsupported assumptions
At times, you make claims that are too speculative, such as “the only concern that they have at this time is how televising this execution will effect their ratings.” Unless a source directly says this, it weakens your credibility.

Similarly, the Amy story is emotionally powerful, but because it is hypothetical, it should not carry too much of the argument.

What to do instead:
Use the anecdote briefly as an opening hook.
Base the body of the essay on source-based evidence, not assumptions about motives.

Edit for style, grammar, and correctness
There are several sentence-level issues that affect clarity.

Examples to fix:
“usual routing” → usual routine
“effect their ratings” → affect their ratings
“their argument is more thoroughly defined…” should begin with a capital letter: Their
“Six-year old” → Six-year-old
“First amendment” → First Amendment

Also watch for wordiness. Many sentences could be shorter and more direct.

Example:
Original: “This is not a valid argument because only one aspect is being considered and is therefore not a valid argument.”
Revised: “This argument is weak because it considers only one aspect of the issue: press freedom.”

Final advice
Your essay has a solid central idea, but it currently reads more like a draft outline than a finished argument because the evidence is incomplete and some points are repeated rather than developed. For revision, focus first on adding the missing quotations and analysis, then reorganize paragraphs around clear claims, and finally edit for concision and correctness.

Once you support your points with complete evidence, your analysis of value versus consequence could become the strongest feature of the paper.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you argue that abortion is morally wrong and that doctors who perform abortions violate their ethical duty. The paper shows commitment to a position and attempts to support that position with biological, legal, and ethical evidence. For revision, focus on strengthening your thesis, logic, evidence, organization, and tone.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis
Your main claim appears in this sentence: abortion “cannot be justified as a moral act… because in essence it is the senseless killing of an innocent human life.” This is a strong position, but the essay would benefit from a more precise thesis that also previews your main reasons.

For example, you might revise it into something like:
“Abortion is morally wrong, I argue, because the fetus is a living human organism, it is distinct from the pregnant woman, and physicians have an ethical duty to preserve human life.”

This gives your reader a clear roadmap and helps organize the body paragraphs.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Right now, the essay sometimes repeats points rather than developing them step by step. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction: present the issue fairly, define the debate, and state your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: argue that the fetus is alive.
Body Paragraph 2: argue that the fetus is a distinct human organism.
Body Paragraph 3: explain why those facts lead to your moral conclusion.
Body Paragraph 4: discuss doctors and the Hippocratic Oath.
Conclusion: restate the argument and address the broader moral/political disagreement.

Also, several places contain missing quotations marked with . Those gaps make the paper feel incomplete and weaken credibility. Be sure either to insert the quotations properly or remove references to them.

Strengthen your use of evidence
You rely heavily on assertion and quoted authority, but the argument needs more careful explanation of how the evidence proves your claim.

For example:
Showing that a fetus has certain biological characteristics does not automatically prove that abortion is murder; you need to explain the connection between biological life and moral personhood.
The statement that abortion is “more commonly known as murder” is too simplified. Murder is a legal term, and abortion has not always been legally defined that way. If you want to make a moral argument, distinguish legal categories from ethical claims.

Try to evaluate your sources more critically. Who are Daniel Callahan, Germain Grisez, and Dr. Liley? Are they writing as scientists, philosophers, or advocates? Explain why their views matter. Also, some sources appear dated or incomplete, and some quotations are not fully introduced or cited.

Address the opposing view more fairly
You mention “pro-choice” arguments, but mostly in simplified form. To make your paper more persuasive, represent the opposing side accurately and then respond to it thoughtfully.

For instance, pro-choice arguments are not only about “doing what she wants with her body.” They often involve:
bodily autonomy,
medical privacy,
rape or incest,
threats to the pregnant person’s health,
disagreement about when personhood begins.

If you address these more seriously, your argument will seem more intellectually honest and mature.

Revise tone and word choice
Your current tone is often emotionally charged: phrases like “senseless killing,” “innocent child,” and “turn their backs” make your position clear, but they can also reduce your credibility in an academic argument if they replace analysis.

Aim for a more measured tone:
Instead of “senseless killing,” say “the deliberate ending of a human life” if that is your claim.
Instead of “liberal group,” say “those who identify as pro-choice.”

A fair, controlled tone usually persuades readers more effectively than charged language.

Fix sentence-level issues
There are a number of grammar, spelling, and style problems that need attention:
“more ninety-eight percent” → “more than ninety-eight percent”
“Or ‘endure’ the right word?” → “But is ‘endure’ the right word?”
“taking on all aspects of human life” is vague
“the woman in which it resides” → “the woman in whom it resides”
“form” the oath → “from” the oath
“re main” → “remain”

Also watch for run-on sentences and rhetorical questions. Too many questions in a row can weaken your authority. In revision, convert some of them into direct claims.

Reconsider the conclusion
Your final line, “You can’t legislate morality,” is interesting, but it may actually conflict with your essay’s argument, since much of the paper suggests abortion should be legally restricted because it is morally wrong. If you keep that line, explain it carefully. Otherwise, your conclusion may seem contradictory.

Revision priorities
Complete or remove the missing quotations.
Rewrite the thesis to make the argument more precise.
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct claims.
Add explanation showing how biological facts support moral conclusions.
Represent opposing views more fairly.
Edit for grammar, tone, and clarity.

Your essay has a strong sense of purpose; the next step is to make it more rigorous, balanced, and clearly supported.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on a difficult ethical question: whether modern medicine’s commitment to preserving life creates social, economic, and biological problems. The topic is ambitious and your draft has a clear willingness to challenge assumptions. That intellectual risk-taking is a strength. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, improving logical consistency, and strengthening evidence and tone.

Clarify your central argument

Right now, the essay raises several related claims without clearly distinguishing them:

medicine focuses too much on preserving life at all costs
symptom treatment is less valuable than prevention or cure
keeping people alive may allow harmful genes or resistant diseases to persist
health care resources are being wasted
society overvalues individual life relative to the collective good

These are not identical claims. Because they are bundled together, the essay can feel scattered and at times extreme. Revise by stating one main thesis early and making each body paragraph support it. For example:

> Modern medicine should place greater emphasis on prevention, root causes, and quality of life rather than extending life at any cost.

This would still allow you to discuss genetics, antibiotics, and cost, but under a clearer framework.

Address major logic and ethics problems

Several arguments rely on assumptions that need much more support or qualification.

A. “The medical community derives its existence solely on the preservation of life.”
This is too absolute. Medicine also aims to reduce suffering, improve quality of life, prevent disease, support autonomy, and provide comfort when cure is impossible. Overstatement weakens credibility.

B. Darwin/natural selection argument
Your PKD paragraph suggests that without treatment, people with certain genes would die earlier and not reproduce, thereby “improving” the gene pool. This is a very serious claim with eugenic implications. If you keep this section, you must handle it carefully and acknowledge ethical objections. As written, it treats human lives instrumentally and assumes that “natural selection” should guide medical policy. That is a philosophical claim, not a scientific fact.

C. Genetic counseling and parenting
The paragraph criticizing parents for having children with possible genetic defects is especially vulnerable. It makes moral judgments (“selfish act,” “disgraceful”) without sufficient evidence and ignores patient autonomy, disability ethics, and the emotional complexity of reproductive decisions. If you want to argue that genetic information should be used more responsibly, present that as a policy or ethical concern rather than condemning parents.

D. Immune weakness and inherited defects
The claim that medicine is broadly “weakening” the human immune system by keeping people alive is too sweeping and scientifically underdeveloped. Be cautious about linking survival, heredity, and societal decline unless you can support those claims with strong, credible sources.

Strengthen evidence and source use

A major issue is that many claims are asserted without enough evidence. There are also placeholder marks (“”) where evidence seems to be missing. You must fill those gaps.

You need:
credible sources for PKD prevalence and treatment outcomes
accurate medical information about antibiotics and resistance
evidence for claims about health care costs
properly introduced and explained quotations from Heimer and Bosk
support for statements about AIDS, Hantavirus, and immune systems

Also, some examples are inaccurate or imprecise. For instance, antibiotics do not treat viral infections; your sentence about “antibiotics for a viral infection in your finger” is medically incorrect. That undermines your authority. Review factual accuracy carefully.

Improve organization

The essay would benefit from a more deliberate structure. Consider this pattern:

Introduction: define the problem and present a focused thesis  
Body paragraph 1: medicine’s emphasis on life extension vs. quality of life  
Body paragraph 2: prevention and root-cause treatment (use antibiotics resistance or public health)  
Body paragraph 3: ethical limits and resource allocation  
Counterargument paragraph: why preserving life still matters; address autonomy and compassion  
Conclusion: propose a balanced shift in priorities

A counterargument is especially important here because your position is controversial. Acknowledging opposing views will make the essay more thoughtful and persuasive.

Revise tone and word choice

Your tone sometimes becomes accusatory or dismissive, which may alienate readers. Phrases like “selfish act,” “disgraceful,” and “we are presenting a continually weaker immune system to the battlefront” sound more emotional than analytical. Academic argument is strongest when it is precise, calm, and fair-minded.

Also watch capitalization and wording:
“Aids” should be “AIDS”
“Doctors” should not usually be capitalized mid-sentence
“uniformed” should be “uninformed”
“highly advance” should be “highly advanced”
“thousands years” should be “thousands of years”

Sentence-level revision

Some sentences are long and difficult to follow. Break them into shorter units and make sure each paragraph begins with a clear topic sentence. Also check agreement, punctuation, and pronoun use.

Revision priorities

Write a narrower, more defensible thesis.  
Remove or heavily revise ethically loaded claims unless you can support and qualify them.  
Replace emotional judgments with evidence-based analysis.  
Fill in missing citations and correct factual errors.  
Add a counterargument and a more balanced conclusion.

You have a provocative topic and clear passion. The next step is to turn that passion into a more precise, ethical, and well-supported academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear engagement with a difficult ethical issue and demonstrates that you understand this debate has multiple sides. You also return several times to the human stakes of the issue—pain, fear, trust in doctors, and end-of-life care—which helps the topic feel important.

2) persuade the reader toward caution about assisted suicide.

Because of this, your thesis is somewhat broad and repetitive. For example, the introduction ends by saying both sides have strengths and weaknesses, but this does not tell the reader what you ultimately argue.

Revise by making your position more specific. For example:  
*Although supporters of assisted suicide make a compelling case based on mercy and patient suffering, opponents present stronger arguments because legalization could expand beyond terminal illness, weaken trust in physicians, and distract society from improving palliative care.*

That kind of thesis gives your paper a clear line of argument.

Improve organization

Topic sentence stating the argument
Evidence from a source
Explanation/analysis
Evaluation of the argument’s strength or weakness
Link back to thesis

At present, some paragraphs drift between summary and opinion. For example, the paragraph about suicide becoming easier, HIV/AIDS, depression, and “fast food” includes several ideas that need clearer development and connection.

A stronger structure might be:

Opponents’ argument: better alternatives through hospice and pain management  
Opponents’ argument: damage to physician-patient trust  
Supporters’ argument: mercy and relief of suffering  
Counterargument and response  
Conclusion

You mention sources such as Herbert Hendin, Debra Saunders, Elana Muller Garcia, Reich, and Kevorkian, but the reader cannot see what they said.

When revising:
Insert the full quotation or paraphrase.
Introduce the source clearly.
Explain why the evidence matters.

For example:  
Instead of: *Herbert Hendin wrote, .*  
Try: *Psychiatrist Herbert Hendin argues that legalizing assisted suicide can normalize self-destruction in vulnerable patients. This supports opponents’ concerns about social consequences because it suggests the practice could spread beyond rare end-of-life cases.*

Also, be careful not to rely only on emotional claims. Some points—such as saying suicide could become “as popular as fast food”—need evidence or more cautious wording.

Is the evidence logical, emotional, or ethical?
Is the consequence likely, or is it speculative?
What would a supporter of the other side say?

This will make your analysis more sophisticated.

Some phrasing risks sounding overly dramatic or imprecise. For example:
“mercy killings”
comparing Kevorkian to Martin Luther King Jr.
“play God”

These can be discussed, but they need careful framing. The King comparison, in particular, is likely too extreme unless you fully explain why civil disobedience in a human-rights movement is relevant to medical ethics. Otherwise, it weakens your credibility.

Edit for style, grammar, and word choice

capitalization: “when” after a period should be “When”
typos: “neglection” should be “neglect” or “failure”; “i doctors” should be “in doctors”; “a doctors” should be “a doctor’s”
awkward phrasing: “Suicide is thought of as to be” → “Suicide is often seen as”
pronoun agreement and consistency
repetition of phrases like “This is significant because...”

Try reading the essay aloud to catch awkward or repetitive sentences. Also aim for more formal academic wording.

Conclusion
You have a thoughtful foundation here: you recognize that this debate involves compassion, ethics, and social risk. For revision, focus on a sharper thesis, fuller evidence, clearer paragraph structure, and more precise analysis. If you make those changes, your essay will become much more convincing and academically strong.

Suggested thesis model

evidence of harmful effects
evidence that censorship would solve the problem

Feedback Report

Your essay shows a clear interest in the ethical debate around genetic research, and you identify several important concerns: eugenics, privacy, discrimination, and genetic manipulation. You also understand that strong argumentation requires addressing counterarguments. These are solid foundations. For revision, the main task is to turn the paper from a discussion *about how to argue* into an actual argumentative essay with a clear claim, evidence, and analysis.

Clarify the thesis
Right now, your essay does not consistently argue *your own position*. Instead, it often explains what “one should” do when writing an argument. For example, you write, “One possible way of presenting the most effective argument…” This makes the paper sound like instructions for a writer rather than an argument for a reader.

Revise by stating a direct thesis early. For example:
*Although genetic research offers medical benefits, its potential for discrimination, privacy violations, and unethical human manipulation means it must be tightly restricted.*
Or, if your position is stronger: *Genetic research should not continue without strict international regulation because its risks outweigh its benefits.*

Then make every body paragraph support that thesis.

Shift from meta-commentary to direct argument
Much of the essay uses phrases like:
“one should present”
“this would be effective in an argument”
“a writer could show their reader”

These phrases keep the essay at a distance from the issue. Instead of explaining how an argument could be made, make the argument yourself.

For example, instead of:
“This obvious atrocity would be effective in an argument…”
write:
*The history of eugenics shows that genetic science can be used to justify horrifying abuses, as seen in Nazi racial policies.*

This change will make the essay more authoritative and persuasive.

Replace missing quotations and incomplete citations
The placeholders “” are a major problem because they remove crucial evidence. Since this essay depends heavily on sources, you need to include the actual quotations or paraphrased ideas and cite them properly.

Also, introduce sources more clearly:
Who are Sinsheimer, Maddox, Annas, and Aldhous?
Why are they credible?
What exactly do they argue?

A strong body paragraph should follow this pattern:
Topic sentence
Evidence from a source
Explanation of the evidence
Connection back to your thesis

Several paragraphs raise interesting points but stop too soon. For instance, your discussion of sickle-cell anemia is one of the strongest moments in the essay because it shows that removing a “harmful” gene can also remove a benefit. But the paragraph needs deeper analysis:

Does it suggest that intervention in the genome can have unintended consequences?

Push each idea further instead of just stating that it “would be effective.”

Introduction
Background on genetics
Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Historical warning
Eugenics and Nazi misuse of genetics
Why this history matters today

Body Paragraph 2: Counterargument and rebuttal
Genetic research may cure disease
But benefits come with risks, such as loss of genetic diversity and unforeseen consequences

Body Paragraph 3: Privacy and discrimination
Genetic data could be used by employers, insurers, or governments
Real-world implications

Body Paragraph 4: Gene manipulation and “designer children”
Difference between therapy and enhancement
Ethical concerns about commodifying children

Body Paragraph 5: Lack of effective regulation
International disagreement
Why weak oversight increases the danger

Conclusion
Restate thesis
Emphasize need for caution/regulation

At times, the essay sounds absolute, but your conclusion suggests caution rather than total rejection. Decide exactly what you believe:
Should genetic research stop?
Continue with restrictions?
Continue only for treatment, not enhancement?

A more nuanced position will likely be stronger. For example, you might argue that genetic research itself is not the problem; the problem is its misuse without ethical limits.

Correct style and grammar issues

Spelling/word choice: “Mendal” should be Mendel.

Agreement and grammar: “One consequences” should be One consequence.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong topic and several meaningful ethical concerns, but it needs revision to become a true argumentative paper. Focus on:
stating a direct thesis,
arguing your position rather than describing how to argue,
supplying complete evidence,
expanding analysis,
improving clarity and grammar.

With these changes, you can turn this draft into a thoughtful and persuasive essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: to compare how *Newsweek* and *JAMA* present the issue of binge drinking differently because of audience, context, and attitude. That is a strong analytical topic, and you consistently return to it throughout the paper. You also show awareness that the same facts can be framed differently depending on genre and readership, which is an important rhetorical insight.

Strengths

Clear overall claim
Your introduction establishes the main argument effectively: the two publications interpret and present the issue differently because they address different audiences. This gives the essay direction.

Strong organizational categories
Using audience, physical/social context, and attitude gives the paper a recognizable structure. Readers can follow your comparison because you repeatedly return to these categories.

Good comparative instinct
You do more than summarize the two articles; you try to explain *why* they differ. That move from description to interpretation is valuable in argumentative writing.

Main Areas to Revise

Sharpen and narrow the thesis
Right now, the thesis is somewhat broad and repetitive. It also makes a strong accusation—“popular magazines use their media power to exploit a topic to increase their readership”—that the essay does not fully prove.

Consider revising your thesis so it is more precise and easier to support. For example:

> Although both *Newsweek* and *JAMA* address college binge drinking, *JAMA* emphasizes scientific completeness and methodological detail for a professional audience, while *Newsweek* highlights dramatic consequences and emotional appeal for a general audience.

This version is more focused and matches the evidence you actually discuss.

Use stronger textual evidence
A major weakness is that many claims about the articles are general rather than supported with specific quotations or detailed examples. In several places you use placeholders like “,” which makes the analysis incomplete. To revise, add concrete evidence from each article:
exact words or phrases from headlines or opening paragraphs
specific statistics each article includes
details from visuals, charts, or layout
short quotations that reveal tone

Then explain how that evidence supports your point. Try to follow this pattern:
claim → evidence → analysis.

Avoid overgeneralizations
Some of your claims are too sweeping, for example:
“People are for some reason drawn to look at the dark side of issues.”
“Making those assumptions is what makes a good writer.”
“The problems… are really not of concern to them.”

These statements sound unsupported and may weaken your credibility. Instead, qualify your argument:
“*Newsweek* appears to assume that dramatic negative consequences will attract readers.”
“The article prioritizes attention-grabbing examples over balanced statistical coverage.”

That keeps the analysis persuasive without sounding unfair or absolute.

Improve analytical accuracy and fairness
At times, the essay seems biased in favor of *JAMA* and against *Newsweek*. A comparison is strongest when it treats both texts carefully. For example, if *Newsweek* emphasizes harmful consequences, that may not automatically mean it is “exploiting” the topic; it may be trying to raise public concern. Similarly, if *JAMA* includes more complete data, explain how that fits the expectations of scholarly discourse rather than simply calling it better.

Try to analyze rhetorical choices rather than judging motives.

Strengthen paragraph focus
Some paragraphs become repetitive, especially the sections on audience and social context. Revise so that each paragraph has one clear main idea:
Paragraph 1: audience and purpose
Paragraph 2: physical presentation/layout
Paragraph 3: social context and assumptions
Paragraph 4: tone and attitude

Then remove repeated points, especially the idea that *JAMA* is scientific and *Newsweek* is emotional, unless you are adding something new.

Style and Mechanics

There are frequent grammar and usage issues that should be corrected in revision. Some patterns include:

apostrophes and possessives: “todays” → “today’s”
word choice: “any give time” → “any given time”
affect/effect confusion: “has an affect on” → “has an effect on”
subject-verb agreement: “this make for differences” → “this makes for differences”
pronoun agreement: “how the author uses rhetoric can decide the reaction of an audience, and we will look at that” could be revised for clarity and formality
informal phrasing: “hook its audience,” “flip side,” “bad effects” could be made more academic

Also, avoid second-person or conversational phrasing such as “as any college student can tell you.” Keep the tone formal and analytical.

Revision Priorities

Replace placeholders and add quotations/examples from both articles.
Revise the thesis to be more precise and supportable.
Cut repeated ideas and give each paragraph a sharper focus.
Reduce unsupported judgments about readers and writers.
Proofread carefully for grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

Final Thoughts

This essay has a solid comparative foundation and a worthwhile argument about rhetorical situation. Your next step is to make the paper more evidence-based, precise, and fair-minded. If you support your claims with specific textual examples and revise for clarity and correctness, the essay will become much more convincing.

“have beared this scrutiny”
“She examples a follower”

Revise for standard phrasing:

“teens will not be informed”
“have withstood this scrutiny”
“She gives the example of…”

Avoid repetitive terms

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong engagement with a difficult issue and a clear effort to analyze *arguments on both sides* rather than simply take a position. That is a real strength. You also recognize an important complexity: adoption disputes cannot always be reduced to a simple “biological vs. adoptive” formula. Below are the main areas to revise.

Clarify your thesis and purpose
Right now, your introduction suggests that the essay is about who should have rights over a child, but your body mostly evaluates the quality of arguments made by each side. Those are not the same task.

Your real thesis seems to be something like:
*Both supporters of biological parents and supporters of adoptive parents rely on oversimplified values, selective examples, and weak consequences, so neither side makes a fully persuasive case.*

If that is your claim, state it directly in the introduction. Then make sure each body paragraph supports that claim.

Improve organization
The essay often repeats the same evaluation (“rare case,” “weak consequence,” “not enough evidence”) without a clear structure. Try organizing by major criteria of argument evaluation, such as:

Values used by supporters of biological parents
Consequences used by supporters of adoptive parents
Problems with media framing and extreme examples
Why case-by-case reasoning is more convincing

This will help readers follow your logic more easily.

Distinguish clearly between sides
At several points, the labels become confusing:
“proponents of biological parents”
“opponents to biological parents”
“supporters of adoptive parents”

These phrases are sometimes used inconsistently. Simplify your terminology:
supporters of biological-parent rights
supporters of adoptive-parent rights

Use those labels consistently so the reader never has to stop and figure out which side you mean.

Strengthen analysis instead of summarizing
Many paragraphs describe examples, but the analysis is sometimes too general. For instance, you often say a case is “rare” or “used for shock value,” but you should explain why rare cases weaken an argument. Is it because they are not representative? Because they appeal to emotion instead of reason? Because they cannot justify general policy?

Push your analysis one step further in each paragraph:
identify the claim,
explain the evidence,
evaluate why it is weak or strong,
connect it back to your thesis.

Address unsupported assertions
Some claims need support or qualification. For example:
“The media portrayed the adoptive parents as saints while the biological parents were evildoers”
“young children are known for their resilience”
“this point is useless”

These are strong statements, but they need evidence and a more academic tone. Avoid absolute wording unless you can prove it. Instead of “this point is useless,” try:
*this claim remains unconvincing without longitudinal evidence.*

Fix incomplete citations and placeholders
The essay includes several places marked with , which leaves key evidence missing. This seriously weakens credibility. Before revising, fill in:
quotations,
statistics,
article titles,
complete source references.

Without these, readers cannot evaluate your evidence.

Use a more formal academic style
There are several places where the tone becomes conversational or emotionally phrased:
“Blood is thicker than water.”
“saints” and “evildoers”
“It is a shame...”

Some of this can work rhetorically, but overall your essay will be stronger if you use more precise academic language. Also avoid rhetorical questions unless they serve a very clear purpose.

Revise sentence-level issues
There are some grammar and style problems to correct:
agreement: “each cases” → “each case”
word choice: “could possible happen” → “could possibly happen”
pronoun consistency: avoid switching between “him,” “his/her,” and “their”
awkward phrasing: “proponents for adoptive parents” → “proponents of adoptive parents”
typo: “on could argue” → “one could argue”

Also watch for long sentences that could be broken into two for clarity.

Make the conclusion more decisive
Your conclusion repeats earlier points but does not leave the reader with a strong final insight. Since your argument is that both sides are weakened by oversimplification, end by emphasizing what a better approach would look like: for example, a framework centered on the child’s best interests, legal clarity, and case-specific evidence.

Suggested revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis so it matches the essay’s actual purpose.
Reorganize body paragraphs by evaluative criteria.
Fill in all missing evidence and citations.
Strengthen analysis of why certain arguments are weak.
Edit for consistency, tone, and grammar.

Overall, this essay has a thoughtful core idea: public debates about adoption rights often rely on emotionally charged but incomplete arguments. That is a strong foundation. Your revision should focus on making that idea sharper, better organized, and better supported.

Feedback Report

You have a clear topic, a recognizable argumentative structure, and a strong personal stake in the issue. Your essay shows that you are comparing solutions to academic cheating rather than simply describing the problem, which is a good foundation for argument. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your thesis, strengthening organization, integrating evidence, and editing for style and correctness.

Clarify and sharpen the thesis
Your introduction suggests several different claims:
cheating is common,
current consequences are ineffective,
two methods are strongest,
those methods have flaws,
those flaws can be improved.

By the conclusion, however, you argue that the honor code is the best solution and that revamping academic codes is weaker. This creates inconsistency.

Revision goal:
State one precise thesis early and make the essay follow it. For example:
> Although stricter academic penalties can reduce cheating, establishing a strong honor code is the most effective solution because it builds a campus culture of honesty, increases student accountability, and addresses cheating before it occurs.

This gives you a clear position and criteria for comparison.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Your body paragraphs generally follow a logical sequence, but some paragraphs try to do too much. For instance, the paragraph on honor codes includes:
explanation of honor codes,
evidence from Virginia,
your personal opinion,
personal anecdote,
a counterargument about peer pressure.

That is a lot for one paragraph. Breaking ideas into separate units would make the essay easier to follow.

Suggested structure:
Introduction + thesis
Honor codes: what they are and why they work
Evidence/example: University of Virginia
Counterargument: peer pressure limits honor codes
Response: why honor codes still remain stronger overall
Stricter academic code / XF grade
Weaknesses of the XF approach
Role of faculty
Weak alternatives such as legal action
Conclusion

Also, several places marked with  suggest missing transitions, examples, or explanation. Be sure each paragraph has:
a topic sentence,
evidence,
analysis,
a link back to the thesis.

Use evidence more fully and consistently
You mention useful examples—University of Virginia, Harvard, University of Maryland, Delaware, and South Carolina—but often the evidence is not fully explained. For example, when you say cheating dropped by fifty percent, explain why that matters and how it supports your claim.

Also, you repeatedly refer to “the authors,” but you do not name them or identify the sources clearly. In academic writing, readers need to know:
who is making the argument,
what source the information comes from,
why that source is credible.

Revision goal:
Introduce sources more specifically. For example:
> According to [author name], the University of Virginia’s honor system works because students encounter it at multiple stages of admission and enrollment, which reinforces shared expectations about honesty.

Then analyze the evidence rather than just reporting it.

Strengthen analysis
At times the essay summarizes solutions but does not fully explain why one is better than another. Your best analytical move appears in the contrast between:
honor codes as prevention through culture and student responsibility,
XF penalties as deterrence through punishment.

This comparison could become the core of your argument.

Ask yourself:
Does an honor code prevent cheating more effectively than punishment after the fact?
Why is student buy-in more powerful than fear of penalties?
If honor codes depend on reporting peers, how can that weakness be reduced?

If you answer these questions directly, your argument will become more persuasive.

Reduce reliance on first person unless it adds value
Your personal perspective gives the essay sincerity, but some sentences weaken the academic tone:
> “As a student, I believe…”
> “I have seen others cheat…”

If personal experience is required, keep it brief and connect it directly to the argument. Otherwise, rely more on evidence and analysis.

Edit for grammar, wording, and sentence clarity
There are many sentence-level issues that distract from your ideas. A few patterns to address:

Word choice:  
  “major question of value” → awkward; consider “major ethical issue”  
  “when taken in account” → “when taken into account”  
  “less reluctant” → likely “more likely”  
  “to eliminated” → “to eliminate”

Agreement and parallelism:  
  “the range of punishments are” → “the range of punishments is”

Articles and prepositions:  
  “on a first hand basis” → “firsthand”  
  “ensure and honest academic environment” → “ensure an honest academic environment”

Sentence completeness:  
  Some sentences are incomplete because of missing material near the  markers. Make sure every quotation or reference is introduced and completed.

Make the conclusion match the essay
Your conclusion is stronger than your introduction because it clearly chooses honor codes over other methods. Revise the introduction so the essay builds toward that same claim. Also, avoid saying the authors “prove” the system is best; in argument, it is safer to say they “strongly suggest” or “demonstrate.”

Final priority list for revision
Write a clear, arguable thesis that consistently supports honor codes as the best solution.  
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one main purpose.  
Add complete source references and explain your evidence.  
Expand analysis comparing prevention (honor codes) versus punishment (XF grades).  
Edit carefully for grammar, missing wording, and formal style.

Your essay already has a strong topic and a clear position. With a more focused thesis and stronger evidence-based analysis, it can become a much more convincing argument.

A stronger thesis will help you decide what each paragraph needs to prove.

Improve organization and paragraph focus

Opposition/defense argument  
   - Explain credibility concerns about Fuhrman and procedure  
   - Show how inconsistencies weaken trust in the evidence

   - Explain which is more persuasive and why

Conclusion  
   - Return to the larger constitutional issue

This will make your analysis easier to follow.

explain the quotation,
connect it directly to your argument.

For example, instead of saying an article “attempts to get readers to put themselves in detective Fuhrman’s shoes,” explain how the quoted language creates sympathy or urgency.

Distinguish legal, ethical, and rhetorical analysis
At times, the essay moves between legal judgment, ethical judgment, and analysis of persuasion without clearly separating them. For example, “It is irrelevant whether the detectives obtained the evidence legally or not” sounds like your own claim, but it may actually reflect the prosecution’s perspective. Make sure readers always know whose position is being described.

Useful revision strategy:
Legal question: Was the search justified under exigent circumstances?
Ethical question: Should evidence be used if rights may have been violated?
Rhetorical question: How do each side’s arguments persuade readers?

Keeping those categories distinct will make your essay more sophisticated.

“they did a little homework” is too casual
“scratching to find anything” sounds biased
“After all, the detectives have earned their badge” is more emotional than analytical

Edit for accuracy, tone, and grammar

You have a strong topic and good instincts about the competing arguments. With a clearer thesis and tighter evidence-based analysis, this essay could become much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important analytical point: many anti-suicide arguments rely on assertion rather than evidence. That is a promising thesis for an argumentative paper because it focuses on *how* arguments are made, not just whether you agree with them. To strengthen the essay, revise for clearer thesis, more accurate use of sources, stronger organization, and a more careful tone.

Clarify your central claim
Your paper seems to argue that common arguments against suicide may be emotionally powerful but are often insufficiently supported by evidence. That idea is interesting, but the introduction does not state it clearly enough. Instead, it begins with broad and morally loaded claims such as “Suicide means the end of all problems” and “those problems are usually solved easily,” which weakens your credibility and oversimplifies the issue.

Revision goal: end the introduction with a precise thesis, such as:
> Although arguments against suicide often appeal to selfishness, family suffering, religion, and social consequences, many of these arguments are weakened when authors rely on emotional claims rather than verifiable evidence.

Use a more academically appropriate tone
Because this topic is sensitive, your language should be careful, precise, and respectful. Some sentences sound dismissive or speculative. For example:
“those problems are usually solved easily”
“The person really must be selfish to receive attention”
“For all the reader’s know, they could be car salespeople or accountants”

These statements risk sounding judgmental or sarcastic. Academic writing works better when it avoids exaggeration and assumes complexity.

Revision goal: replace absolute claims with measured ones:
“some problems may be treatable or temporary”
“some suicidal behavior may be interpreted as a plea for help”
“the source’s expertise should be identified more clearly”

Integrate sources correctly
A major problem is that several quotations are missing and replaced with “.” This makes it impossible to evaluate your analysis and seriously weakens the paper. In addition, you often summarize what authors “intend” without showing the exact language that supports your interpretation.

Revision goal:
Insert the actual quotations.
Introduce each source fully: author, title, publication, and relevance.
Analyze the wording of the quotation rather than guessing the author’s intention.

For example, instead of:
> Rauscher states that . Mr Rauscher hopes to convey…

Try:
> Rauscher argues that “[quote].” This wording frames suicide primarily as a social harm rather than an individual act of suffering, but he provides little empirical evidence to support that claim.

Distinguish between “lack of evidence” and “weak argument”
Often you say an argument is “good” but also “does not hold up well.” Be more precise. An argument can be ethically compelling, emotionally persuasive, or logically structured, yet still lack evidence. You should separate these criteria.

For instance, your best recurring point is:
the argument may be understandable,
but the author does not substantiate it with research, data, case studies, or expert testimony.

That distinction will make your critique sharper and more sophisticated.

Improve paragraph structure
Most body paragraphs follow this pattern: present a source, summarize it, then say it lacks evidence. Because this pattern repeats, the essay begins to feel circular.

Revision goal: organize body paragraphs by argument type and make each paragraph do something distinct:
Selfishness argument — what it claims, why people find it persuasive, what evidence is missing.
Cry-for-help/psychological distress argument — stronger because clinical evidence could exist; discuss what kinds of evidence would help.
Family grief/social stigma argument — assess whether the authors use grief research.
Religious argument — explain that this depends on shared beliefs and may not function as universal evidence.
Slippery slope argument about assisted suicide — this is your strongest logic-based critique; develop it more fully.

Develop your strongest analytical moments
Your discussion of the slippery slope fallacy is one of the clearest parts of the essay because you identify a specific logical problem. That is excellent argumentative analysis. Build on that strength by applying the same approach elsewhere:
Is the “selfishness” argument an emotional appeal?
Is the “family grief” argument relying on anecdotal reasoning?
Is the religious argument persuasive only for those who share the premise?

This would move the paper from general disagreement to careful rhetorical analysis.

Address complexity more fairly
At times, the essay seems to assume that if opponents do not provide firsthand testimony from suicidal individuals, then their arguments fail. But evidence can come from many places: psychology research, interviews with survivors, grief studies, religious ethics, or medical literature. Acknowledging that would make your critique more balanced.

Also, be careful with claims like “The topic of suicide will somehow affect every person on earth.” This is too broad. More measured claims are more persuasive.

Sentence-level editing

“what does the person attempting suicide thinking?” → “what is the person attempting suicide thinking?”
“When there little safety” → “When there is little safety”
“For all the reader’s know” → “For all the readers know”
“so lution” → “solution”

You also rely heavily on repetitive phrasing such as “This argument is good, but…” Try varying sentence structure.

Overall
You have a worthwhile argument: opposition to suicide should be examined critically and supported with evidence rather than moral assertion alone. To revise successfully, focus on:
a precise thesis,
complete and accurate source use,
careful tone,
stronger organization,
more specific analysis of logic and evidence.

With these changes, your essay could become a thoughtful critique of how arguments about suicide are constructed.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important and difficult topic, and one clear strength is that you try to move beyond description into theoretical analysis. Using Durkheim and Marx gives the paper an academic direction, and your central concern—that adolescent suicide deserves more public attention—is meaningful. To revise successfully, focus on clarifying your argument, organizing your ideas, and improving sentence-level accuracy.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the introduction presents adolescent suicide as a major problem, but the paper’s main argument is not fully clear. By paragraph two, the essay shifts into theory, so your thesis should announce that focus early.

For example, you could revise the thesis to something like:

“Adolescent suicide is a serious social problem that can be better understood through sociological theory, especially Durkheim’s structural functionalism and Marx-inspired conflict theory, because both explain how social isolation, instability, and pressure shape suicidal behavior among youth.”

This gives the paper a clear purpose: not just to show that suicide is serious, but to argue that sociological theory helps explain it.

Improve organization and paragraph unity
The paper currently moves from statistics, to theory, to examples, but the structure is sometimes hard to follow. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction
   - Explain the issue
   - Present its significance
   - State your thesis

Background on adolescent suicide
   - Use a few key statistics
   - Avoid too many numbers in one paragraph

Durkheim / Structural Functionalism
   - Explain the theory clearly
   - Define egoistic and anomic suicide
   - Show how each applies to adolescents

Conflict Theory / Marx
   - Explain the theory more carefully
   - Show specifically how inequality, family conflict, or social pressures affect adolescents

Common factor: depression
   - Be careful here: depression is important, but this paragraph should connect clearly to the sociological theories

Conclusion
   - Restate the argument
   - Explain why theory matters for understanding and prevention

Also, some paragraphs contain repetition or unclear transitions. For instance, the paragraph on anomic suicide repeats “adolescents who have experienced the death of a parent.” Tighten these areas.

Develop analysis more than summary
Much of the essay summarizes theory rather than analyzing how it explains adolescent suicide. Your revision should do more “why” and “how.”

For example:
Don’t just define egoistic suicide; explain why adolescents may be especially vulnerable to social isolation.
Don’t just mention conflict theory; explain how social inequality, family instability, or social powerlessness may contribute to despair in youth.

The Marx section especially needs more development. Right now, the connection between dialectics and adolescent suicide feels general. Ask:
What kinds of social conflict affect adolescents?
How do class, family stress, race, or unequal access to mental health care relate to suicide risk?

That will make the theory section more convincing.

Use sources more effectively
You refer to sources with markers like `<R>` and , but the essay often mentions evidence without integrating it fully. In revision:
Introduce the source
Present the evidence
Explain how it supports your point

For example, instead of dropping in a statistic and moving on, follow it with interpretation:
“This increase suggests that adolescent suicide is not only an individual issue but also a broader social pattern, which supports Durkheim’s view that suicide must be understood in relation to society.”

Also, be cautious with absolute claims such as:
“There are no theories…that argue adolescent suicide isn’t a direct result of depression.”
This sounds too broad and likely inaccurate. In academic writing, avoid universal claims unless you can prove them. You could say:
“Many researchers identify depression as a major factor in adolescent suicide.”

Revise for grammar, wording, and tone
There are frequent grammar and spelling errors that make the essay harder to read. Some recurring issues:

Word choice:

  - “contraversal” → “controversial”
  - “publically” → “publicly”
  - “appropiatly” → “appropriately”
  - “programed” → “programmed”

Apostrophes:  
  - “its” should often be “it’s” for “it is”

Subject-verb agreement:  
  - “These works and studies has” → “have”
  - “adolescents who has moved” → “adolescents who have moved”
  - “rates…are phenomenal” may also need more precise wording

Sentence clarity:  
  Some sentences are very long and awkward. Break them into shorter sentences.

For example, this sentence:

could become:

Reorganize the essay around background, Durkheim, Marx, and conclusion.
Add more analysis linking theory to adolescent suicide.
Use evidence more carefully and avoid overstatements.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay addresses an important issue and shows genuine concern for the social consequences of water pollution. You also make an ambitious attempt to connect an environmental topic to sociological theories, which gives your paper a clear academic purpose. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis clarity, organization, theory application, use of evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengths
Relevant topic: Water pollution is timely and significant.
Clear intention to argue: You want to show that water pollution is not just environmental, but also social.
Use of theory: Bringing in Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory gives the essay a stronger analytical frame than a purely descriptive paper.
Concrete examples: The opening examples of antifreeze, oil leaks, and fertilizer are effective and engaging.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Strengthen your thesis
Right now, the essay announces the topic, but the central argument is too broad. A stronger thesis should state what you are arguing about water pollution and how the two theories help explain it.

For example:
> Water pollution persists because individuals and companies often prioritize short-term convenience and reward over long-term environmental costs; Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory help explain why these decisions are made and how behavior might be changed.

This gives the paper a sharper direction.

B. Improve organization
The essay sometimes shifts between background information, theory, law, and examples without a clear structure. Try this outline:

Introduction
   - Hook
   - Importance of water pollution
   - Thesis

Background on water pollution
   - Types of pollutants
   - Point vs. non-point sources

Rational Choice Theory
   - Define the theory clearly
   - Explain how it applies to individuals and companies
   - Use evidence

Exchange Theory
   - Define the theory clearly
   - Explain costs, rewards, and reinforcement
   - Apply to pollution behavior

Conclusion
   - Restate argument
   - Emphasize social responsibility and policy implications

This structure will make your argument easier to follow.

C. Explain the theories more accurately
At times, the paper mentions the theories but does not fully define them or apply them consistently.

Rational Choice Theory: Usually suggests that people make decisions based on perceived costs and benefits. In your essay, explain that polluters may choose harmful behavior because it seems cheaper, easier, or more convenient in the short term.
Exchange Theory: Focuses on social behavior shaped by rewards, costs, and reinforcement. You should show more clearly how repeated behavior is reinforced when there are no immediate consequences.

Be careful not to treat the theories as simply “personal” or “individualized.” Focus instead on decision-making, incentives, and consequences.

D. Develop analysis instead of listing facts
You include many statistics, but sometimes they appear without interpretation. After each major fact, explain:
Why it matters
How it supports your claim
How it connects to the theory

For example, if beaches are closed due to pollution, explain how this shows a social cost that polluters may ignore when making short-term decisions.

E. Replace placeholders and incomplete citations
Your draft contains multiple placeholders such as “ <R>” and “Contemporary Journal reports that .” These make the essay feel unfinished. You need to:
Insert the missing source material
Cite all statistics properly
Identify the “text” you refer to

Without complete evidence, your argument loses credibility.

Style and Sentence-Level Issues

A. Word choice and grammar
There are many small errors that weaken the academic tone. Examples:
“its'” should be its
“then into four categories” should be them into four categories
“war pollution threat” should likely be water pollution threat
“to to pollute- water” should be corrected
“ones' behavior” should be one’s behavior

B. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are awkward or repetitive. For example:
> “Personal beliefs are propounded by these studies and rational choices are consciously made by these beliefs.”

This is hard to follow. A clearer version:
> Studies can influence personal beliefs, and those beliefs may shape rational choices about environmental behavior.

C. Formal academic style
Avoid conversational phrasing such as:
“like the text says”
“awakened in a sense”

Use more precise academic language instead.

Content Suggestions
Distinguish more clearly between individual pollution and corporate/industrial pollution. Your essay discusses both, but sometimes blends them together.
Consider adding a counterargument, such as the claim that regulation is expensive or that some pollution is accidental, and then respond to it.
Clarify your final example about Milwaukee: explain how it supports your larger claim about social consequences and institutional responsibility.

Overall
You have a meaningful topic and a strong foundation, but the essay currently reads more like a rough draft than a finished argument. Your next step is to turn description into analysis: define each theory clearly, apply it directly to water pollution, and organize the essay around one focused claim. Also, revise carefully for grammar, clarity, and missing citations.

If you revise with those goals in mind, your essay will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: marijuana should be legalized, especially for medical, industrial, and personal use. You also show strong effort in gathering historical background and multiple lines of argument. The paper is ambitious, and your use of sociological theories gives it an interesting academic angle. For revision, focus on making your argument more focused, better organized, and more credible.

What is working well

Clear overall claim  
   Readers can easily identify your stance on legalization.

Wide range of reasons  
   You discuss medicine, prisons, crime, industry, judges, and government revenue. This gives your paper breadth.

Attempt to use theory  
   Bringing in structural functionalism, exchange theory, and conflict theory shows you are trying to analyze the issue rather than just express opinion.

Historical context  
   The legal history helps show that marijuana policy has changed over time.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis and narrow the scope
Right now, the paper tries to argue too many things at once. The introduction promises discussion of medical use, industrial fabrics, prison overcrowding, violent crime, judges, and tax revenue, all through three theories. That is too much for one essay.

Revise by choosing 2–3 strongest points and making them your central argument. For example:

> Marijuana should be legalized because criminalization harms public health, wastes criminal justice resources, and prevents the government from regulating and taxing a widely used substance.

This thesis is more focused and easier to prove.

Improve organization
The essay often moves from one idea to another without clear transitions. Some paragraphs are long and contain multiple claims.

Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Brief background
   - Clear thesis

Medical benefits and public health
   - Explain medical uses
   - Address counterarguments
   - Show why legalization improves patient care

Criminal justice effects
   - Prison overcrowding
   - Misallocation of police/court resources
   - Social inequality in enforcement

Economic/industrial benefits
   - Tax revenue
   - Hemp/industrial uses
   - Regulation replacing black markets

Counterargument
   - Risks of abuse, health concerns, youth access
   - Explain why regulation is a better solution than prohibition

Conclusion
   - Reaffirm argument
   - End with strongest insight

Use sociological theories more accurately
At times, the theories feel added on rather than truly applied. For example, you sometimes repeat that “rewards outweigh costs,” but do not fully explain how exchange theory helps interpret marijuana policy.

To improve:
Structural functionalism: Explain how laws are supposed to promote social order, and argue that marijuana criminalization may now disrupt that order through prison overcrowding and uneven enforcement.
Conflict theory: Focus on how drug laws may disproportionately affect lower-income groups while more privileged users avoid punishment.
Exchange theory: Use it to compare perceived social costs and benefits of legalization versus criminalization.

Each body section should clearly connect evidence to one theory.

Make the tone more academic
The opening list of slang terms and some wording (“pot,” “getting high,” “crazed on pot”) make the essay sound informal. In an academic argument, use more neutral language.

Prefer:
“marijuana” or “cannabis” instead of “pot” or “weed”
“consumption” instead of “getting high”
“patients” or “users” instead of casual labels

Also avoid rhetorical questions such as:
> “Would it not make sense to reduce crime by simply legalizing marijuana?”

Instead, state the claim directly.

Improve credibility of evidence
Many claims are interesting, but some sound exaggerated or unsupported. For example:
“Not a single death has ever been credibly attributed...”
“People are going to obtain pot any way they can.”
“The black market would also very soon disappear.”

These need careful sourcing and cautious wording. Avoid absolute claims unless your evidence is very strong.

Also, several references are marked only as <R> or , so the reader cannot tell what the sources are. In revision, provide proper citations and use up-to-date, reliable academic or government sources.

Address counterarguments fairly
The paper mentions opposition, but usually dismisses it quickly. A stronger argument will acknowledge concerns such as:
impaired driving
adolescent use
dependency
respiratory effects of smoking

Then explain why legalization with regulation may address these concerns better than criminalization.

Edit for grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity
There are many sentence-level errors that weaken the authority of your argument. Examples include:
“cannavis sativa” → *Cannabis sativa*
“heroine” → *heroin*
“win also focus” → *will also focus*
“foe the DEA” → *for the DEA*
“mar on drugs” → *war on drugs*
subject-verb agreement: “Overcrowded prisons is” → *Overcrowded prisons are*

Also watch punctuation, run-on sentences, and capitalization.

Final revision advice
Your strongest revision strategy is to cut material, sharpen the thesis, and develop fewer points more deeply. This essay has good raw material, but it needs focus and stronger academic presentation. If you revise for organization, evidence, and tone, your argument will become much more persuasive.

If you want, I can also help you turn this into a revised outline or a stronger thesis statement.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows real ambition. You address homelessness as a historical and social problem, and you try to analyze it through two sociological theories rather than simply describing it. That gives your paper intellectual scope. For revision, the main task is to turn this broad, thoughtful draft into a clearer, more focused argument.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the essay opens with urgency, but the central claim is still too general. You say homelessness is a serious problem and later suggest that symbolic interactionism is the better lens, but this point does not appear clearly at the beginning.

Revise toward a sharper thesis, for example:
> Although homelessness has structural causes such as inequality and housing costs, symbolic interactionism provides the most useful framework for understanding why homelessness persists because social labels and public perceptions shape how homeless people are treated and how they see themselves.

This kind of thesis gives your essay direction and prepares readers for the comparison between conflict theory and symbolic interactionism.

Improve organization
The paper currently has three large parts:
history of homelessness,
conflict theory,
symbolic interactionism.

That structure makes sense, but the sections are too long and sometimes repetitive. The history section also takes up a lot of space without always connecting directly to your argument.

Suggestions:
Shorten the historical background to only the details that support your main claim.
Give each body section a clear topic sentence.
End each section by linking it back to the thesis.

A stronger outline might be:
Introduction + thesis
Brief historical/contextual background
Causes of homelessness
Conflict theory analysis
Symbolic interactionist analysis
Comparison: why symbolic interactionism is more useful
Conclusion

Focus on argument, not just summary
A major issue is that much of the essay describes theories rather than argues with them. For example, several paragraphs explain conflict theory in general terms, but they do not always show specifically how it explains homelessness better or worse than symbolic interactionism.

How does this support my thesis?
What example proves the point?

For instance, instead of broadly saying the powerful exploit the poor, you could argue:
> Conflict theory explains homelessness by showing how unequal access to housing, jobs, and political influence benefits those already in power while leaving poor people vulnerable.

federal housing support declined,

That placeholder must be replaced with an actual quotation or removed.

When revising:
add statistics, quotations, or examples;
cite them clearly;
explain how the evidence supports your claim.

Be careful with overgeneralization
Some statements are too absolute or unsupported, such as:
“The powerful people in society are immensely greedy and take everything for themselves.”
“Any time one person has more than another problems will occur.”
“The government... tagged the homeless as lazy.”

These claims sound forceful, but academically they need more nuance. Avoid language that sounds sweeping, emotional, or impossible to prove.

Instead, use phrasing like:
“Conflict theorists would argue that…”
“Public discourse often portrays homeless people as…”
“Government policy can reinforce negative stereotypes…”

This keeps your tone analytical.

Your discussion of both theories is promising, but some terminology is inaccurate or inconsistent:

“Social Interactionists” should be symbolic interactionists.
Labeling theory should be explained more precisely.

subject-verb agreement: “People living without shelter has been” → “have been”
word choice: “lobs” → “jobs”; “mayor cause” → “major cause”
awkward phrasing: “a percentage of the population of the citizens of America”
repetition: “problem” and “homeless” appear excessively
punctuation and run-on sentences

Read the paper aloud slowly. If a sentence sounds confusing, too long, or repetitive, rewrite it.

Reorganize around that thesis.

Proofread carefully.

You have the foundation of a strong sociology paper here. The key is to make your essay more focused, more evidence-based, and more analytical.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong ambition. It tackles a complex social issue, uses historical background, and tries to evaluate the debate through two theoretical lenses—Herbert Spencer and Robert Merton. That gives the paper intellectual range. For revision, focus on sharpening your argument, improving organization, and making your tone and evidence more balanced and precise.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, the essay raises many questions and presents both conservative and liberal views, but your own main claim does not become fully clear until very late. A strong argumentative essay needs a thesis that states your position early and guides the whole paper.

Your conclusion suggests this claim: welfare should not be abolished, but it should be reformed in ways that increase opportunity while also addressing abuse. If that is your position, state it clearly in the introduction.

For example:
> Although the welfare system has serious flaws and can foster dependency in some cases, abolishing it would ignore the structural barriers many recipients face; therefore, the most effective response is reform that combines accountability with education, childcare, and realistic job training.

Improve organization and reduce repetition
The essay is very long and sometimes feels like two separate papers: one on Spencer/conservatives and one on Merton/liberals. A clearer structure would help.

Consider organizing it like this:
Introduction + thesis
Brief history of welfare
Conservative/Spencer argument
Liberal/Merton argument
Your evaluation of both views
Specific reforms you support
Conclusion

This would also help you avoid repeating similar points, especially about laziness, lack of opportunity, and welfare dependency.

Be careful with tone and word choice
Some parts of the essay sound judgmental rather than analytical. Phrases such as “their laziness,” “illegitimate kids,” and “not fit to survive” are emotionally loaded. Even when you are presenting Spencer’s or conservative views, you should signal clearly that these are claims being analyzed, not facts you endorse.

Try using more precise academic language:
“children born outside marriage” instead of “illegitimate kids”
“some critics argue that welfare may reduce incentives to work” instead of “society pays for their laziness”
“structural barriers” or “systemic disadvantage” instead of vague phrases like “unfortunate social conditions”

A more neutral tone will make your argument stronger and more credible.

Strengthen your use of evidence
You include many statistics and examples, which is a strength. However, several problems need attention:

A. Missing/incomplete quotations and citations
There are multiple placeholders such as  and `<R>`. These make the essay feel unfinished and seriously weaken credibility. You need to:
insert the missing quotations,
explain why each quote matters,
use a consistent citation style.

B. Overreliance on anecdotal examples
Cases like Aletha Townsend are vivid, but one or two individual stories cannot prove broad claims about millions of recipients. Use anecdotes only to illustrate patterns already supported by reliable data.

C. Question some assumptions
At times, the essay treats correlation as proof. For example, statistics about single motherhood, low education, or job loss do not by themselves prove that recipients are unwilling to work. Be careful to distinguish between:
evidence of barriers,
evidence of abuse,
evidence of system design problems.

Engage more critically with the theories
Using Spencer and Merton is a good idea, but the essay sometimes oversimplifies them.

Spencer: You explain Social Darwinism well, but you could more clearly critique its limits when applied to modern public policy. Human societies are not identical to natural competition in the animal world.
Merton: The Matthew Effect is useful, but explain more directly how cumulative advantage/disadvantage affects education, employment, and family stability.

Most importantly, after presenting each theory, add a sentence evaluating it:
What does this theory explain well?
What does it fail to explain?

That would make your paper more analytical and less descriptive.

Make the conclusion more decisive
Your final paragraphs move toward a nuanced position, which is good, but they still sound somewhat uncertain. End by stating clearly:
welfare abolition is too extreme,
pure sympathy is not enough,
reform should combine support with responsibility.

Then name 2–3 concrete reforms you believe would work best.

Sentence-level editing
There are a number of grammar and style issues to correct:
“Me United States” should be “the United States”
“exploded in to” → “exploded into”
“deferent men” → “different men”
“Spencer. if” → punctuation error
“he no useful place” → likely “has no useful place”
“Consider, for example. the father” → punctuation error
“<R>..” → extra period

Also watch for comma splices, unnecessary rhetorical questions, and overly long paragraphs.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Remove placeholders and complete all citations.
Reorganize around your argument, not just around accumulated information.
Use a more balanced academic tone.
End with specific reforms and a firm conclusion.

Overall, this essay has strong potential because it addresses an important issue with energy and substantial research. Your revision should focus on turning that material into a clearer, more controlled argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear effort, broad knowledge of sociological theory, and a serious attempt to explain divorce from multiple perspectives. The strongest feature is your organization by theory: symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory. This gives the paper a logical structure and shows you understand that social problems can be analyzed in different ways.

What is working well

Clear topic and significance  
You establish early that divorce is a major social issue and explain why it matters, especially for children.

Use of multiple theoretical frameworks  
Discussing several theories is a strong academic move. It helps your essay go beyond personal opinion and into sociological analysis.

Attempt to move from analysis to solutions  
Your final sections do more than describe the problem; they suggest what might be done. That gives the essay purpose.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the essay presents many ideas, but the central argument is not fully clear. You seem to argue that divorce has risen because social changes have transformed marriage, family roles, and expectations, and that sociological theories help explain this. Make that your thesis near the end of the introduction.

For example:  
“Although divorce rates may have stabilized, divorce remains a major social problem because changes in expectations, family functions, gender roles, and power relations have made marriage more difficult to sustain; symbolic interactionist, functionalist, conflict, and exchange theories each help explain this rise.”

Distinguish summary from argument
Much of the essay summarizes what theories say, but your own argument sometimes disappears. After each theory section, explain:
what that theory contributes,
what specific cause of divorce it highlights,
and how convincing you find it.

For instance, after symbolic interactionism, you might say that changing meanings of love and divorce help explain why people leave marriages more readily than in earlier generations.

Improve coherence between paragraphs
Some paragraphs feel like lists of causes rather than steps in an argument. Add transitions that show how ideas connect.

Examples:
“In addition to changing expectations, shifting gender roles have also altered marital stability.”
“Where symbolic interactionism focuses on meaning, functionalism emphasizes changes in the family’s social role.”

Be careful with unsupported or outdated claims
A few claims need more caution or better framing:
“two-thirds of all first marriages will end in divorce”
“less often, homosexuality” as a cause
broad claims about African American families without context

These statements can sound overly general or dated. If you include them, explain the evidence and avoid implying that certain groups are inherently more prone to divorce. Also, some points reflect older assumptions and should be revised into more neutral academic language.

Make the conclusion more analytical
The conclusion mostly repeats earlier points. Instead, synthesize the theories:
symbolic interactionism explains changing meanings,
functionalism explains weakened family functions,
conflict theory explains inequality,
exchange theory explains decision-making.

Then state which combination seems most persuasive.

Style and sentence-level issues

Grammar and word choice
There are many small errors that weaken credibility. Examples:
“coning” → “coming”
“fob” → “job”
“decided” → “decide”
“wilt” → “will”
“then own job” → “their own job”
“it has involved through history” → “it has evolved through history”
“me changing sex roles” → “the changing sex roles”
“me family” → “the family”
“possible lower” → “possibly lower”

Proofread carefully.

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are awkward or imprecise:
“One partner cannot take for granted of what to expect from the other.”  
Better: “Partners can no longer take for granted what to expect from one another.”
“This places greater demands on the parents in providing for their children.”  
Better: “This increases the demands placed on parents.”

Formal academic tone
Avoid phrases like:
“okay”
“get out of the marriage”
“many times”

Use more formal alternatives:
“socially acceptable”
“leave the marriage”
“often”

Present the issue
Give brief context
End with a clear thesis
