Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that coaches accused of “running up the score” are often responding to the ranking system, player development, financial pressure, and fan expectations rather than acting out of cruelty. That is a strong argumentative starting point because it gives you a debatable claim and several reasons to support it.

What’s working

Clear stance
You consistently argue that coaches such as Steve Spurrier, Bobby Bowden, and Tom Osborne are “not guilty.” The reader always knows what side you are on.

Good use of examples
You include specific football situations, especially the Florida–Carolina example, to make the issue concrete. This helps the reader understand why the debate matters.

Multiple lines of reasoning
You do more than make one point. You discuss rankings, second-string players, Heisman candidates, job security, bowl money, and fan support. This gives your paper breadth.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your thesis is spread out and a little unclear. The essay would be stronger if the introduction ended with one sentence that clearly states your argument and main reasons.

Possible revised thesis:
*Although the NCAA criticizes coaches for running up the score, top college football coaches are often justified in continuing to score because rankings, player development, financial pressure, and individual awards all reward dominant victories.*

This gives your paper a roadmap.

Improve organization
Your ideas are interesting, but the essay sometimes feels like a series of connected thoughts rather than a carefully structured argument. Try organizing by reason, with one body paragraph per main point:

Rankings encourage large margins of victory
Second- and third-string players still deserve to compete
Star players need statistics for awards like the Heisman
Coaches and schools face financial and job pressure
Even smaller programs benefit from exciting wins

Add topic sentences to each paragraph so the reader knows the purpose immediately.

Develop analysis, not just examples
At times you describe what happened, but you do not fully explain how it proves your argument. For example, after discussing Florida’s ranking, explain more directly that the system itself rewards impressive scores, which pressures coaches to keep scoring. In other words: move from what happened to why it matters.

Use a more academic tone
Your voice is energetic, but some phrases are too informal for an academic essay. Examples include:
“getting their butts beat bad”
“Steve was a nice guy”
“bench warmer”
“little pay-cut”

Replace these with more formal language:
“losing badly”
“attempted to reduce embarrassment”
“reserve player”
“financial benefit”

Also avoid rhetorical comments such as “I know I made Steve Spurrier sound like a jerk.” Instead, present your point directly and objectively.

Address the opposing view fairly
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the other side. Right now, you mostly defend the coaches. Consider adding a paragraph that admits why critics object: continuing to score can appear unsportsmanlike, embarrass weaker teams, and increase injury risk. Then respond by explaining why, despite those concerns, the system still pressures coaches to do it. This will make your argument more persuasive.

Sentence-level issues to fix

Run-on sentences and punctuation
Many sentences are too long or joined incorrectly with commas. Break them into shorter sentences.

For example:
> “Florida held the number two ranking at the beginning of the season, but was eventually knocked down to number three because Florida State was beating their opponents worse than Florida.”

This is understandable, but several nearby sentences become too long and hard to follow. Read each sentence aloud and see where it naturally needs to stop.

Word choice and correctness
A few words are incorrect:
“unsurmountable” should be insurmountable
“it’s ranking” should be its ranking
“to” should sometimes be too
“do to” should be do too
“tables up” should be adds up

Grammar and agreement
Watch for subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency. Example:
“If one of these top ranked teams are winning” should be “If one of these top ranked teams is winning.”

Quotation issue
You have a sentence:
> “You could give them the ball and say .”

This looks unfinished. Either complete it or remove it.

Suggested revision strategy

Rewrite the introduction with a direct thesis.
Reorganize the body into separate reason-based paragraphs.
Add one counterargument paragraph.
Replace informal language with academic wording.
Edit carefully for punctuation, grammar, and spelling.

Final thought

You have the basis for a persuasive argument because you clearly understand the pressures of college football and can explain why coaches might keep scoring. The next step is to turn that strong opinion into a more formal, organized, and carefully edited academic essay. If you revise for structure and tone, your argument will be much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and engages seriously with a controversial issue. One of its strengths is that you acknowledge opposing views instead of ignoring them. You also try to make a distinction between the Confederate battle flag and the national Confederate flag, which gives your argument a specific angle rather than repeating a common debate. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving accuracy and evidence, and making your reasoning more consistent.

Clarify your thesis

At first, your essay argues that the Confederate battle flag should not fly over the South Carolina State Capitol. However, later you argue that the “real” Confederate flag should replace it. This shift changes the essay’s claim. A reader may ask: are you arguing against the battle flag specifically, or against Confederate symbolism at the capitol in general?

To revise, state one precise thesis early and stick to it. For example:

If your claim is about the battle flag specifically:  
  “The Confederate battle flag should be removed from the South Carolina State Capitol because its historical use links it more strongly to slavery, racism, and resistance to civil rights than to shared Southern heritage.”

If your claim is about replacing it with another symbol, you will need much stronger justification for why any Confederate flag should represent the state.

Right now, the second position is harder to defend because many readers will see both flags as tied to the Confederacy and slavery.

Strengthen historical accuracy

Several claims need verification or correction. This is important because your argument depends heavily on history.

For instance:
“This was the year that the civil rights movement started” is inaccurate. The Civil Rights Movement began well before 1964.
“the flag was set up the day after the civil rights movement started” is also inaccurate and too vague.
Your descriptions of the flags are informal and not fully precise.

You should research:
when and why the battle flag was raised at the capitol,
what state officials said at the time,
how historians interpret that decision,
the meanings and uses of both the battle flag and the Confederate national flags.

Using reliable sources will make your argument much more persuasive.

Address a major logical problem

Your essay says the battle flag represents hatred, but the “real Confederate flag” represents heritage and should fly instead. This creates a weak point in your reasoning because both flags are connected to the Confederacy, which defended slavery.

A skeptical reader might respond: if the battle flag is offensive because it represents a pro-slavery cause, why would another Confederate flag be acceptable?

To strengthen the essay, you need to do one of the following:
either argue only for removing the battle flag and avoid proposing another Confederate flag,
or directly address why the national Confederate flag should be understood differently despite its historical context.

The first option would likely produce a stronger essay.

Use evidence instead of broad assertions

You often make strong claims without support, such as:
“The battle flag stands for nothing but racism and hatred.”
“That flag stands for a way of life.”
“Many values and beliefs are still held sacred by true southerners.”

These statements are emotionally forceful, but they need evidence and explanation. Also, phrases like “true southerners” may alienate readers and sound exclusionary.

Instead, support claims with:
historical facts,
quotations from lawmakers or public figures,
polling data,
examples of how the flag has been used in public life,
scholarly interpretations.

Improve organization and paragraph development

Your essay has a basic structure, but some paragraphs repeat points rather than develop them. Try this structure:

Introduction: present the controversy and your thesis.
Background: explain the difference between the flags and why that distinction matters.
Historical argument: explain why the battle flag’s placement at the capitol is politically significant.
Counterargument: acknowledge heritage arguments fairly.
Response: explain why public government spaces require symbols that represent all citizens.
Conclusion: restate your claim and explain its broader significance.

This structure would help your argument build logically.

Revise tone and style

Your tone is generally earnest, but some wording is imprecise or overly absolute. Examples:
“short bursts of fury”
“all the wrong reasons”
“nothing but racism and hatred”

These phrases sound passionate, but academic argument is stronger when it is precise and measured. Also, watch grammar and wording:
“South Carolina succeeded from the union” should be “seceded from the Union.”
“flagrant sign of hatred” may not be the right word; perhaps “blatant” or simply “symbol.”

Final suggestion

Your most promising argument is not that one Confederate flag is acceptable and another is not, but that a state capitol should not display a symbol so closely associated with slavery and resistance to civil rights. If you revise around that idea, add accurate historical evidence, and reduce unsupported generalizations, your essay will become much stronger.

If you want, I can also help you rewrite your thesis and outline a stronger revised version.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: premarital sex is socially destructive. You also attempt to support that claim with multiple categories of evidence—history, disease, pregnancy/family structure, and social/emotional effects. That broad scope gives the essay ambition. For revision, though, the main priorities are strengthening the argument’s logic, improving organization, and adopting a more academically credible tone.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Your thesis appears in the first paragraph, but it is too broad and absolute: you claim premarital sex has damaged “this nation, and the world as a whole.” That is much larger than your evidence can support. In academic argument, a narrower claim is usually more persuasive.

Consider revising to something like:
“Premarital sex can have harmful social consequences when it is connected to inadequate sexual responsibility, unstable relationships, and objectifying attitudes toward partners.”

This kind of claim is more precise and easier to prove. It also avoids overstatement.

Improve logical connections
A major issue is that some claims do not fully follow from the evidence you provide.

Examples:
You cite statistics about multiple sexual partners, then use them to argue that premarital sex itself causes disease. But the evidence more directly supports a claim about unprotected sex or multiple partners, not all premarital sex.
You argue that single-parent or nontraditional families are “illegitimate” and linked to crime or lower education, but you do not explain causation. Academic readers will ask: is the issue family structure, poverty, access to education, or something else?
You connect premarital sex to disrespect toward women and men, but much of your evidence comes from media representation. That may reflect larger cultural sexism, not necessarily premarital sex alone.

For revision, separate your ideas more carefully:
What problems come from premarital sex?
What problems come from casual sex?
What problems come from lack of contraception, lack of commitment, or media objectification?

These are not automatically the same thing.

Use a more academic tone
Your essay often relies on emotionally charged or sarcastic phrasing, which weakens credibility. Examples include:
“when America headed down the toilet”
“ritual parties to get free stuff”
“the Norman Rockwell… 2.5 children (.5 being the dog of course)”
“immoral slaughter of babies”

These phrases sound conversational or polemical rather than analytical. In an academic essay, your strongest tool is careful reasoning, not ridicule.

Try replacing loaded language with neutral phrasing:
instead of “headed down the toilet,” say “contributed to a decline in traditional sexual norms”
instead of “ritual parties to get free stuff,” say “some critics argue that marriage has become less distinct from cohabitation”

Strengthen paragraph structure
Many body paragraphs contain several different ideas at once. For example, the paragraph on pregnancy shifts from shame, adoption, abortion, single parenthood, family values, and contraception. That makes it hard to follow.

Try this structure for each paragraph:
Topic sentence stating one claim
Evidence from sources
Explanation of how the evidence supports the claim
Transition to the next paragraph

Possible revised structure:
Paragraph 1: historical/cultural changes after the sexual revolution
Paragraph 2: health risks associated with multiple partners and unsafe sex
Paragraph 3: unintended pregnancy and social consequences
Paragraph 4: objectification and relationship expectations
Conclusion: restate argument and discuss implications

Be careful with evidence and citations
You use quotations and statistics, which is good, but some evidence needs more context. A few concerns:
Some statistics seem outdated or unexplained.
Some quotations are inserted without analysis.
A claim such as “99.91% ruled out” sounds overly exact and may undermine trust if not carefully documented.
Citations are inconsistent and sometimes unclear (for example, “Janus 13 5”).

After each quotation, explain:
Who is the source?
What does the evidence actually show?
Why is it relevant?

Also, avoid using one statistic to make a sweeping claim about all people or all families.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay acknowledges opposing views. Right now, the essay assumes that the harms are obvious. Consider engaging with other perspectives:
Some argue that the problem is not premarital sex itself but lack of education and contraception.
Others argue that committed premarital relationships differ from exploitative casual encounters.
Some would dispute the idea that only marriage creates healthy families.

If you address these views and explain why you still disagree, your essay will seem more thoughtful and persuasive.

Revise sentence-level errors
There are many grammar and word-choice problems that distract from your ideas. A few examples:
“One the most potent solvents” → “One of the most potent causes”
“have long ranging affects” → “have long-ranging effects”
“the lost of respect” → “the loss of respect”
“there bad side affects” → “their negative side effects”
“far to many” → “far too many”
“planed” → “planned”
“bare the brunt” is correct, but many surrounding sentences need punctuation and clarity.

I strongly recommend reading the paper aloud sentence by sentence. That will help you catch awkward phrasing, missing words, and run-on sentences.

Final advice
Your essay has a clear opinion and a real attempt at support, but to revise successfully, focus on precision, fairness, and organization. Narrow the claim, distinguish between different sexual behaviors, reduce emotional language, and explain evidence more carefully. If you do that, your argument will become much stronger and more academically persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong personal voice: you argue that women should serve in the military but not in combat roles because sex differences matter. That gives your essay a definite claim, which is a good starting point for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your argument with evidence, clarifying your logic, and improving organization and style.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Right now, your thesis is somewhat broad and repeated in different forms. Try to state one precise claim near the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible revision:  
“Although women can serve successfully in many military roles, combat positions should be assigned according to rigorous physical and psychological standards rather than the goal of symbolic gender equality.”

This version is more specific and arguable. It also shifts the focus from general statements about “women” and “men” to standards and policy, which will make your argument more persuasive.

Replace generalizations with evidence
Your essay relies heavily on assertions such as:
women are physically weaker,
women are emotionally different,
standards are lowered for women,
combat effectiveness would decline.

These are central claims, but you do not provide proof. In academic argument, readers need evidence, not only belief. To strengthen the essay, include:
military studies on physical performance and injury rates,
official policies on combat standards,
research on whether standards are or are not lowered,
evidence about unit readiness and combat effectiveness.

Also, be careful: some of your claims are stated as universal truths (“a woman’s body… cannot function in every capacity as a man’s does”). Broad statements like this invite readers to challenge your credibility unless you support them carefully.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay acknowledges the opposing side. Right now, references to “society” and “feminists” feel dismissive and oversimplified. Instead, summarize the strongest opposing argument fairly:
some argue that equal opportunity should include combat roles,
some argue that standards should be identical and sex-neutral,
some point to women who have succeeded in demanding military roles.

Then respond to those points directly. For example, you might argue that if standards remain identical and mission-based, access is acceptable, but the military should not alter requirements for the sake of representation. This would make your essay more nuanced and academically credible.

Improve logic and avoid unsupported assumptions
Several parts of the essay jump from difference to limitation without fully explaining why. For instance, you say women’s “emotional makeup” is different, but you do not define what that means or explain how it affects combat specifically. Be careful here: if you make claims about emotional traits, you need strong evidence and precise language.

Also, some of your reasoning is based on personal preference (“I love having feminine characteristics”) rather than public-policy analysis. Personal voice can be useful, but an academic argument needs reasons that apply beyond your own experience.

Reorganize by argument points
Your essay repeats similar ideas across paragraphs. A clearer structure would help:

Introduction  
   Present the issue and your thesis.

Body Paragraph 1: Physical standards  
   Explain the physical demands of combat and argue that mission effectiveness requires strict standards.

Body Paragraph 2: Military readiness and policy  
   Discuss whether changing standards affects readiness, with evidence.

Body Paragraph 3: Counterargument and response  
   Acknowledge equality arguments and explain why you disagree.

Conclusion  
   Reaffirm your claim and explain the policy implications.

This structure will reduce repetition and make your points easier to follow.

Revise tone for an academic audience
Some phrasing may alienate readers because it sounds absolute or value-laden, such as:
“the weaker sex”
“feminists”
“destroying the uniqueness”
“we were created different”

These phrases bring in emotional or ideological assumptions that are not explained. For an academic essay, choose more neutral wording:
“average physical differences between men and women”
“advocates of gender equality”
“sex-based differences”
“social and biological distinctions”

If your argument is rooted in religious belief (“we were created different”), you should either develop that framework clearly or avoid relying on it in a general academic essay.

Edit for sentence-level correctness
There are several grammar and style issues to fix:
repeated sentence in paragraph 2,
sentence fragments (“and goes into the military…”),
agreement errors (“a males,” “a women”),
punctuation problems,
overly long sentences.

For example:
“a males” → “a male’s”
“for a women” → “for a woman”
“try deny” → “try to deny”

Read the essay aloud and look for places where one sentence contains too many ideas. Breaking long sentences into shorter ones will improve clarity.

Final advice
Your strongest revision strategy is to shift from opinion-based claims about men and women to evidence-based claims about military standards and combat readiness. That will make your essay more persuasive, more focused, and more suitable for academic writing. You clearly care about the topic; now your task is to support your position with clearer reasoning, fair treatment of opposing views, and reliable evidence.

Body paragraphs: mostly argue that traditional gender stereotypes are unrealistic and harmful.

traditional gender roles should be preserved in limited form, or

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear, passionate position: residence hall rules can limit students’ independence and make it harder for them to develop responsibility. That central idea gives your paper energy and purpose. You also do an effective job identifying specific policies—visitation, gender-based rules, and quiet hours—rather than speaking only in general terms. This gives you a solid foundation for revision.

What is working

Specific examples: The USC visitation policy and roommate contracts help ground your claims.
Strong voice: Your tone shows conviction, which is useful in argumentative writing.

Main areas to revise

Sharpen the thesis
Right now, your introduction expresses frustration, but the main claim is somewhat broad. Try to state your argument more precisely at the end of the first paragraph.

Current idea: dorm rules are too strict and prevent maturity.  
Stronger thesis example:  
*Although residence halls are meant to protect students, overly restrictive policies on visitation, gender, and quiet hours can undermine students’ independence and should be revised to allow more personal responsibility while still respecting safety and shared living.*

This version makes your position more focused and previews your main points.

Improve organization
Your essay has good material, but some paragraphs become repetitive or move between ideas too quickly. A clearer structure would help:

Introduction: background + thesis
Body paragraph 1: visitation rules restrict independence
Body paragraph 2: roommate contracts offer a better alternative
Body paragraph 3: gender-based rules create fairness problems
Body paragraph 4: quiet hours should be more flexible
Conclusion: restate argument and propose balanced reform

Make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence that clearly states its main point.

Develop argument with stronger reasoning
Many of your points rely on personal frustration, which is understandable, but academic argument becomes stronger when it also uses logic, evidence, and acknowledgment of other perspectives.

For example, when discussing visitation, you could explain:
why colleges create these rules,
why those reasons may be valid,
and why the current rules still go too far.

This would make your argument sound more balanced and credible.

You do briefly acknowledge safety and respect for roommates, which is good. Build on that more. Showing that you understand the opposing side strengthens your case.

Reconsider some claims and language
A few statements may weaken your credibility because they sound too absolute, emotional, or unsupported. For example:
“The rules are worse than your parents.”
“Nine times out of ten…”
“It’s nothing but discrimination.”

These are strong claims, but without evidence they may seem exaggerated. Replace them with more measured academic phrasing.

Also, the section about sexual orientation and guest policies raises an interesting fairness issue, but the discussion needs to be handled more carefully and respectfully. Focus on policy inconsistency rather than making assumptions about individuals or situations.

Use a more formal academic style
Your paper often shifts into second person (“you”), conversational questions, and spoken expressions. In an argumentative essay, a more formal tone will help.

For example:
“You feel like you are walking on egg shells” → *Students may feel closely monitored in residence halls.*
“That’s ridiculous!” → *This policy appears unnecessarily restrictive.*
“Heaven forbid…” → remove or replace with a formal explanation.

Try to avoid directly addressing the reader with “you” unless your instructor allows that style.

Edit grammar, usage, and mechanics
This draft needs careful proofreading. Some common issues include:

Word choice/confusion:  
  - “rolls” should be roles  
  - “resident halls” is usually residence halls  
  - “atleast” should be at least  
  - “there opinions” should be their opinions
Subject-verb agreement:  
  - “policy… state” should be policy… states
Sentence structure: some sentences are run-ons or awkwardly phrased.
Punctuation: there are missing commas and inconsistent apostrophes.
Capitalization: be consistent with “University of South Carolina” and “USC.”

Reading your paper aloud can help you catch unclear or overly long sentences.

Revision priorities
If you revise in stages, do this in order:

Write a clearer thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around one main point each.
Strengthen reasoning and include more balanced discussion.
Replace informal or exaggerated language with academic wording.
Proofread for grammar and spelling.

Final thought
You have a strong topic and a genuine argument about how colleges should balance safety with student independence. The next step is to turn that strong opinion into a more polished academic argument. If you focus on structure, tone, and careful editing, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Your essay has a clear position and strong passion: college football should adopt a playoff system. To revise effectively, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving organization, and strengthening style and mechanics.

What’s working

Clear argumentative purpose
   - Your main claim is easy to identify: a playoff system would better determine a true national champion.
   - You also give reasons beyond fairness, such as revenue and fan excitement.

Relevant historical examples
   - The 1992–93, 1993–94, and 1994 seasons provide useful evidence that the existing system created controversy.
   - These examples help show that the problem is recurring, not isolated.

Attention to counterarguments
   - You recognize objections about tradition, bowl games, and season length.
   - This is an important feature of effective argumentative writing.

Main revision priorities

Strengthen and narrow the thesis
Your introduction is energetic, but the thesis is somewhat repetitive and broad. Try to state your argument in one precise sentence.

Current idea: a playoff system would create revenue, excitement, and a true champion.

*College football should adopt a playoff system that preserves bowl games because it would resolve championship controversies more fairly, increase fan interest, and generate additional revenue.*

This gives the essay a clearer roadmap.

Reorganize for clarity
Right now, the essay often reads like a long sequence of season summaries. The evidence is relevant, but the paper needs a stronger structure so the reader always sees how each example supports your argument.

A clearer organization might be:

Introduction + thesis
Problem with polls and bowls
   - Brief explanation of why the current system fails

   - One paragraph each on 1992–93, 1993–94, 1994
   - End each paragraph by explicitly connecting the example to your claim
Benefits of a playoff system
   - Fairness / true champion
   - Revenue and television ratings
   - Fan excitement and dream matchups
Address counterarguments
   - Tradition of bowls
   - Season too long
   - Cost concerns
Your proposed solution
   - Explain the 16-team format more clearly

Many paragraphs are very long and contain several different ideas. Aim for one main idea per paragraph. Begin each paragraph with a topic sentence that states the point.

For example, instead of launching directly into details about a season, start with something like:
*The 1994 season shows how the bowl system could leave two undefeated teams without a direct championship game.*
*The 1993 season demonstrates that polls could ignore head-to-head results and still leave fans unconvinced.*

This helps your reader follow your logic.

Analyze evidence more, summarize less
You include a lot of factual detail, but sometimes the essay spends too much time retelling seasons. After each example, explain why it matters.

For instance, after discussing Nebraska and Penn State, you might add:

That kind of analysis makes the argument stronger.

Style and sentence-level issues

“undisputed champion”
“clear title winner”
“postseason tournament”

“absolutely ridiculous”
“whipped up on”
“dream match-ups”

“highly problematic”
“defeated decisively”
“compelling interconference matchups”

  “their was” → “there was”
Word choice:  
  “implied this rule” → “applied this rule”
Missing words:  
  “couldn’t really Florida State ahead” → “couldn’t really rank Florida State ahead”
Sentence fragments or awkward phrasing:

  This is fine, but surrounding sentences sometimes become run-on or repetitive.
Pronouns and possessives:

Final advice

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: to persuade riders to follow go-cart safety rules. The strongest part of the paper is your firsthand experience. Because you work at the amusement park, you have credibility, and your examples make the topic feel real rather than abstract. Readers can tell that you care about safety and that you know what happens when rules are ignored.

What’s working well

Strong personal authority
You establish that you work at Frankie’s Fun Park and have seen injuries happen. This gives your argument authenticity.

Clear practical topic
Your essay stays focused on one main idea: people should follow go-cart rules for safety.

Effective use of examples
Details such as people forgetting the brake, workers getting hit, and accidents in the pit help readers understand the consequences of unsafe behavior.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction explains that rules are important, but the thesis could be sharper and more argumentative. Right now it mainly says rules matter because people can get hurt.

Try making your claim more specific:
Example revision:  
*Riders should follow all go-cart safety rules because these rules prevent injuries, protect workers and passengers, and reduce damage to the cars.*

This version previews the main points of your essay and gives it a stronger structure.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay reads like a list of rules in the order they are announced. That makes sense practically, but for an argumentative essay, you should group ideas into clear categories.

A stronger structure might be:
Introduction + thesis
Body paragraph 1: Rules that protect riders (seatbelts, brakes)
Body paragraph 2: Rules that protect others on the track (no bumping, swerving, reckless driving)
Body paragraph 3: Rules that protect workers and riders during accidents and in the pit
Conclusion: Reaffirm why rules matter

This will help the essay feel more organized and purposeful.

Use a more formal academic tone
Your voice is passionate, which is a strength, but some phrases sound too conversational for an academic essay.

Examples:
“Believe me”
“I should know”
“We hate throwing people off the track”
“you got to remember”

These can be revised into a more formal tone:
*Workers are also at risk when riders ignore instructions.*
*My own experience being hit by a go-cart shows how dangerous these situations can be.*

You do not need to remove your personal experience—just present it more formally.

Focus on sentence clarity and grammar
There are many sentence-level errors involving word choice, punctuation, and grammar. These make the essay harder to read. Common issues include:

Incorrect word forms: “taking off” should be “taken off”
Spelling: “annoucements” → “announcements”; “their” → “there”; “When ever” → “Whenever”
Subject-verb agreement: “The only reason the bumpers are… are” should be “is”
Run-on or crowded sentences: several sentences try to say too much at once

For example, this sentence is long and repetitive:
*The workers are at great risk when bringing the cars in, because there are about thirty-eight cars riding at one time and the workers have to direct all the cars in one lane and slow them all down as to keep people from being hit from behind hurting people's back or neck.*

A clearer version:
*Workers face serious risk when the race ends. Because as many as thirty-eight cars may return to the pit at once, workers must direct them into one lane and make sure they slow down to prevent rear-end collisions and injuries.*

Develop body paragraphs with topic sentences
Each paragraph should begin with a sentence that states the paragraph’s main point, not just the next rule.

For example:
*Seatbelt and brake rules are essential because they prevent avoidable injuries.*
*Rules against bumping and reckless driving protect both riders and equipment.*
*Instructions from workers are especially important during accidents and at the end of the race.*

This helps connect each paragraph back to your thesis.

Style suggestions

Avoid repeating “important” too often; use alternatives like *essential, necessary, critical,* or *vital*.
Be careful with emotional wording like “innocent, little kid” unless you support it clearly. A more neutral phrase such as *young child* is more appropriate in academic writing.
Consider using transitions such as *Furthermore, In addition, As a result,* and *Finally* to improve flow.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong foundation because it is based on real experience and genuine concern for safety. The next step is to shape that experience into a more polished argument. Focus on:
a clearer thesis,
better paragraph organization,
a more formal tone, and
careful editing for grammar and sentence clarity.

With revision, this could become a convincing and effective argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: Susan Smith should not have received the death penalty. You also try to support that position by discussing her background, mental state, and the idea that life imprisonment may be a harsher punishment than death. That gives your paper a definite argumentative purpose. To revise effectively, focus on strengthening your thesis, evidence, organization, tone, and audience awareness.

Clarify and sharpen your thesis
Right now, your main claim appears gradually instead of being stated directly and precisely. A stronger thesis should tell readers exactly why you believe the sentence was just.

For example, you might revise your thesis into something like:

*Susan Smith should not have received the death penalty because her history of trauma and mental illness reduced her moral responsibility, and life imprisonment provides a more just punishment than execution.*

This kind of thesis is arguable, specific, and gives your essay a structure.

Improve the argument’s logic
Some of your reasoning is persuasive, but parts of the essay rely too much on emotional language or religious belief rather than evidence that would convince a broad audience.

For example, these ideas need stronger support:
that life in prison is “greater than death”
that her guilt will punish her more than execution
that God’s forgiveness should influence legal sentencing

These points may reflect your sincere beliefs, but in an argumentative essay, you need to distinguish between:
legal reasoning
personal morality
religious conviction

If your audience includes readers who do not share your faith, arguments about heaven, hell, and divine forgiveness may not persuade them. If you keep those points, use them carefully and as part of your personal perspective, not as the main basis of public justice.

Use evidence more carefully
You include important background information about Smith’s childhood trauma, abuse, depression, and relationships. This material can support your case, but you need to explain more clearly how it relates to sentencing.

For instance, instead of saying:
*These past behavior patterns are a few reasons why the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty.*

You should correct the factual issue and explain the significance:
Was it the jury that decided sentencing?
How did testimony about depression or trauma function as mitigating evidence?

Be careful with facts. Referring to the “Supreme Court” appears inaccurate in this context. Errors like that weaken credibility.

Also, avoid assuming motives unless you can prove them. Statements such as *“Smith assumed that the boys were the reason the affair ended”* should be presented more cautiously unless supported by evidence.

Reduce sensational and repetitive language
Your essay often uses vivid, graphic descriptions:
*“hideous picture etched in the back of her mind”*
*“pleading and crying for help”*
*“cold-hearted murderer”*

These create emotional intensity, but too much can make the essay sound more like a personal reaction than a reasoned argument. In revision, aim for a more formal academic tone. Let facts and analysis do more of the work.

For example, instead of dramatizing what she hears in her cell, you could write:
*A life sentence ensures that Smith must live permanently with the consequences of her actions, which some may regard as a more severe punishment than execution.*

This keeps your point but sounds more analytical.

Organize paragraphs more clearly
Your essay would be stronger if each paragraph had one main idea.

A possible structure:
Introduction: case background + thesis
Mitigating circumstances: childhood trauma, abuse, depression
Why life imprisonment is just: punishment, accountability, public safety
Counterargument: why some support the death penalty, and your response
Conclusion: restate claim and explain why this sentence is just

Right now, some paragraphs mix biography, legal judgment, emotional reaction, and religious claims. Clearer paragraph focus will improve readability.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay shows awareness of the other side. Many people believed Smith deserved the death penalty because the crime was deliberate and involved two children. You should acknowledge that view fairly, then explain why you still disagree.

For example:
*Although many argue that the severity of the crime justified execution, mitigating evidence about Smith’s mental health and personal history suggests that life imprisonment was the more appropriate sentence.*

This will make your essay sound more balanced and mature.

Edit for grammar, word choice, and mechanics
There are several errors that need correction:
recieve → *receive*
prision → *prison*
comma splice: *“The news media and many individuals have stated facts as well as opinions, I would like…”*
awkward phrasing: *“At the age of fifteen years old”* → *“At age fifteen”*
avoid unnecessary repetition, such as repeating that her boyfriend dumped her

Also, remove placeholder quotations like *“In the Bible God says ”* unless you insert the actual verse and cite it properly.

Final advice
Your essay shows strong engagement with a difficult issue and a sincere attempt to argue for mercy rather than execution. For revision, focus on making your case more precise, factually accurate, logically structured, and less dependent on emotional or exclusively religious appeals. If you do that, your argument will become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0003.3.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that lowering the drinking age to eighteen would reduce crime, promote responsibility, and improve public health. That gives the paper a strong argumentative purpose. You also organize the body around three main claims, which is a good foundation for revision. To make the essay more persuasive, focus on strengthening your evidence, sharpening your logic, and improving sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the thesis and focus
Your introduction presents several related claims, but the thesis is somewhat repetitive. Try to state one clear, precise argument instead of restating the same idea in several sentences.

For example, your thesis could become:
“Lowering the drinking age to eighteen would reduce illegal alcohol-related behavior among young adults, encourage safer drinking habits through normalization, and lessen binge drinking by removing the appeal of alcohol as a forbidden activity.”

This version is more direct and gives the reader a clear roadmap.

Improve the quality and credibility of evidence
This is the biggest area for revision. Many of your sources are incomplete, outdated, or not academically credible. Email addresses, “homepage” references, and broken URLs weaken your authority. In a college-level argument, use reliable sources such as government reports, peer-reviewed studies, or reputable organizations.

Examples of problems:
“Rick Finley@rfinleygmail.vt.edu” is not a proper source citation.
Some web addresses are incomplete or not formatted as sources.
Several claims are broad but unsupported, such as “Alcoholism is not a problem in other countries.”

Also, some of your evidence does not fully support your point. For instance, saying that lowering the drinking age would reduce crime because fewer young people would technically be breaking the law is true in one sense, but it does not prove that harmful crime would decrease. A reader may argue that alcohol-related assaults, accidents, or DUIs could increase. You need stronger evidence directly tied to the outcomes you claim.

Address counterarguments
Right now, the essay mostly presents one side. A stronger argumentative essay acknowledges opposing views and responds to them. Many readers will immediately think of concerns such as:
increased drunk driving,
alcohol-related injuries,
immaturity among eighteen-year-olds.

If you address those concerns directly, your argument will seem more balanced and persuasive. For example, you might argue that education, regulation, and supervised introduction to alcohol could be more effective than prohibition for this age group.

Clarify reasoning and avoid overstatement
Some claims are too absolute and need to be made more carefully. For example:
“The common problem of binge drinking would be cured” is too strong.
“Alcoholism is not a problem in other countries” is factually doubtful.
“Once these rights are given back, young adults will act responsibly” assumes a result without proof.

Use more cautious language such as “may reduce,” “could help lessen,” or “might encourage.” Academic arguments are stronger when they avoid sweeping generalizations.

Improve paragraph development
Your body paragraphs often repeat ideas instead of building them. Try using this structure in each paragraph:
clear topic sentence,
evidence,
explanation of how the evidence supports the claim,
transition to the next idea.

For example, in the paragraph about crime, move beyond “young people would no longer be breaking the law” and explain how legal access could shift drinking from hidden, risky settings to more regulated environments.

Revise style, grammar, and word choice
There are many mechanical errors that distract from your argument. Some common issues:

Capitalization: “on most college campuses” should be “On most college campuses.”
Word choice: “effect” should often be “affect.”
Agreement: “Responsible drinking would be encourage” should be “encouraged.”
Spelling: “amoung” should be “among.”
Possessives/plurals: “eighteen-year old's” should be “eighteen-year-olds” in most cases.
Sentence clarity: “Eighteen-year old Is would not feel a need…” appears to contain a typing error.

You should also reduce repetition. Phrases like “Lowering the drinking age would…” begin many sentences. Vary sentence openings to create a smoother style.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion mostly repeats earlier points. Instead, use it to reinforce the significance of your argument. Summarize the strongest reasons and leave the reader with a final insight about policy, public health, or youth responsibility.

Revision priorities
Replace weak or improper sources with credible evidence.
Revise the thesis for clarity and precision.
Add a counterargument paragraph.
Remove exaggerated claims and improve logical support.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and citation format.

Final comment
You have a clear argument and an understandable structure, which gives you a solid starting point. The next step is to make the essay more academically persuasive by using stronger evidence and more careful reasoning. With substantial revision, this paper could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and worthwhile argument: schools should include swimming instruction in physical education because it can save lives. The topic is practical, socially important, and easy for readers to understand. You also show strong awareness of audience by opening with a vivid drowning scenario that immediately establishes urgency.

What is working well

Strong sense of purpose  
Your position is clear: swimming should be part of required P.E. This gives the essay focus.

Effective use of urgency and emotion  
The opening scene makes the danger of not knowing how to swim feel immediate. This is a strong rhetorical strategy for an argumentative essay because it helps readers care about the issue.

Practical solutions  
You do more than identify a problem; you suggest how schools could implement swim instruction by using existing pools, local pools, and certified instructors. This strengthens the argument.

Main areas to revise

Sharpen the thesis
Right now, your thesis appears in the third paragraph, but it is somewhat buried among several ideas. Make it more direct and specific.

For example:  
“Swimming instruction should be a required part of middle school or high school physical education because it teaches a life-saving skill, improves water safety awareness, and can be implemented through existing school or community resources.”

This version clearly states both your claim and your main reasons.

Improve organization
The essay would be stronger if each paragraph focused on one main point. Right now, some paragraphs combine multiple ideas or shift suddenly.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: drowning problem + thesis  
Body Paragraph 1: why swimming is a necessary life skill  
Body Paragraph 2: benefits beyond swimming itself, such as rescue awareness and water safety  
Body Paragraph 3: how schools could realistically implement the program  
Conclusion: restate claim and emphasize importance

This would make your reasoning easier to follow.

Add evidence, not just opinion
You make persuasive claims, but many of them need support. For example:

“This is a common story in the summer time.”
“Swimming is a skill that is easy to learn.”
“Many school systems have pools that are under-used.”

These statements may be true, but in an argumentative essay, you need evidence such as statistics, expert testimony, or examples from credible sources. Consider adding:

drowning statistics from the CDC or WHO  
research on swim instruction and drowning prevention  
examples of schools or districts that already require swimming successfully

Evidence will make your argument much more convincing.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should anticipate objections. For example, some readers may argue that:

schools cannot afford swim programs  
not all districts have access to pools  
students may have medical, cultural, or religious concerns

You briefly address the pool-access issue, which is good. Expand this by acknowledging these concerns and explaining how schools could provide alternatives, accommodations, or partnerships.

Strengthen the academic tone
The dramatic second-person opening (“you”) is engaging, but much of the essay remains conversational. For a university-level argumentative essay, aim for a more formal tone after the introduction.

For example, instead of:  
“How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone's life?”  
You could write:  
“Unlike many traditional P.E. activities, swimming provides a practical skill that can directly prevent injury or death.”

This sounds more academic while keeping your point.

Sentence-level concerns

There are frequent errors in capitalization, spelling, punctuation, and grammar that distract from your ideas. Examples include:

“your” should be “Your” at the start of a sentence  
“panicing” → “panicking”  
“drownding” → “drowning”  
“cericulum” → “curriculum”  
“evryone” → “everyone”  
“instuctor” → “instructor”  
“thaech” → “teach”  
“employent” → “employment”  
“simlpe” → “simple”  
“remidied” → “remedied”

There are also several comma splices and run-on sentences. For example:  
“This is not uncommon, most of your friends do not know how to swim.”  
This should be revised into two sentences or joined correctly.

You should proofread carefully or read the essay aloud to catch these errors.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear, direct thesis.  
Reorganize body paragraphs around distinct reasons.  
Add factual evidence from reliable sources.  
Address possible objections.  
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

You already have the foundation of a persuasive essay with a meaningful argument and a strong practical focus. With better evidence, clearer structure, and more polished language, this could become a much more compelling academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: society should move away from rigid gender identities because they are harmful and outdated. The paper has a recognizable argumentative structure, and you use examples about women’s roles, men’s pressures, and single parenting to support your claim. The topic is important and your thesis is arguable, which gives the essay strong potential. For revision, focus on sharpening the thesis, improving evidence, and refining style and logic.

Thesis and central claim
Your thesis appears in the introduction: society should “abolish distinct gender identities” and recognize that so-called masculine and feminine traits can occur in any person. This is a strong starting point, but the wording needs more precision. “Abolish gender identities” sounds absolute and may imply erasing personal identity, which may not be what you mean. From the essay, your real argument seems to be that rigid gender stereotypes should be abandoned because traits should not be limited by sex.

Suggestion: revise the thesis to make the claim more exact:
*Society should reject rigid gender stereotypes because traits such as sensitivity, ambition, strength, and compassion are human qualities rather than qualities limited to men or women.*

This wording better matches your body paragraphs.

Use of evidence
Your essay refers to sources such as Betty Friedan, Warren Farrell, and Masters/Johnson/Kolodny, which is a good strategy. However, the evidence is incomplete because several quotations or details are missing (, `<R>`). As written, the paper cannot fully support its claims.

To strengthen revision:
Insert the actual quotations or paraphrased findings.
Introduce each source more clearly.
Explain how each source supports your point rather than just mentioning it.

For example, after citing Friedan, explain specifically how her work shows that restricting women to wife-and-mother roles caused dissatisfaction. After Farrell, explain how masculine success norms create emotional pressure for men.

Also, be careful not to let sources stand in place of analysis. A body paragraph should follow a pattern:
topic sentence,
evidence,
explanation of the evidence,
connection back to the thesis.

Organization and paragraph development
The essay generally follows a logical order:
definition of the issue,
effects on women,
effects on men,
example of single parents,
conclusion.

That structure works well. However, some paragraphs are repetitive. For instance, several sentences restate that men can have “feminine” qualities and women can have “masculine” ones without adding new analysis. Try to make each paragraph contribute a distinct point.

A possible revised structure:
Introduction: define gender stereotypes and state thesis.
Women: traditional expectations limited women and caused psychological/social harm.
Men: masculine expectations create pressure and emotional restriction.
Contemporary life: work and single parenting show that human traits cross gender lines.
Counterargument/rebuttal: acknowledge that some people value traditional roles, but argue these roles should be choices, not requirements.
Conclusion: restate argument with broader significance.

Including a counterargument would make the essay more persuasive.

Reasoning and precision
Some claims are too broad or depend on outdated assumptions. For example:
“strong, courageous, dependent, and self-reliant” includes a contradiction: “dependent” likely should be “independent.”
Calling certain qualities “strictly male” or “distinct female” weakens your argument, since your essay is trying to show that these labels are artificial.
“There would be no psychological impact” is too absolute. It would be better to say rigid stereotypes can reduce psychological harm.

Try to avoid overgeneralizations such as “the male gender identity requires” or “women were taught.” Instead, use more careful language:
*Many societies have associated…*
*Traditional expectations often encouraged…*
*Rigid stereotypes can pressure…*

This makes your argument more credible.

Style and sentence-level issues
Your tone is appropriately formal, but some sentences are wordy and repetitive. You can improve clarity by shortening and simplifying.

For example:
“We, as an ever changing society, should abolish distinct gender identities…”  
  could become  
  *As society changes, we should reject rigid gender stereotypes…*

Watch punctuation and capitalization:
Missing space after a quotation mark: `gender."Distinct`
“if these traits” should be capitalized: *If*
“it would be ridiculous” should be capitalized: *It*
“on order to succeed” should be *in order to succeed*

Also, avoid awkward phrasing such as “emanate these qualities” or “radiate these qualities.” Simpler verbs like *show*, *demonstrate*, or *develop* are more natural.

Conclusion
The conclusion restates your main point effectively, but it mostly repeats earlier claims. Strengthen it by ending with a broader insight about why this matters—for example, greater personal freedom, healthier families, or a more equal society.

Revision priorities
Replace missing quotations and citations.
Refine the thesis to focus on rejecting rigid stereotypes, not eliminating identity itself.
Reduce repetition and strengthen analysis in each body paragraph.
Use more precise, less absolute language.
Edit for grammar, capitalization, and word choice.

Overall, you have a solid argumentative foundation. With clearer evidence, more precise language, and deeper analysis, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and passionate argument: media and social beauty standards harm women by promoting unrealistic ideals and encouraging unhealthy behaviors. The strongest part of the paper is your strong voice. The reader can tell you care deeply about this issue, and that emotional investment gives the essay energy and purpose.

What’s working well

Clear position
You clearly argue that media-created beauty standards are damaging and that women should value inner worth over appearance.

Strong engagement with the topic
Your use of rhetorical questions and personal reflection helps show why this issue matters. The paragraph beginning “I understand that every woman may not look at the famous models…” is especially effective because it makes the argument more personal and relatable.

Variety of supporting points
You discuss several consequences of beauty pressure: eating disorders, plastic surgery, advertising, discrimination against larger women, and low self-esteem. This gives the essay breadth.

---

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis and overall structure
Right now, your main claim appears in pieces across the introduction. Try to end the introduction with one precise thesis statement that previews your main points.

For example:
“Media and society promote unrealistic standards of female beauty, and these standards damage women’s physical health, mental well-being, and social opportunities.”

Then organize the body so that each paragraph develops one part of that thesis:
unrealistic standards in media
effects on health
effects on self-image
effects on social/economic treatment
conclusion with your solution or final reflection

This will make the essay feel more focused and logical.

Use evidence more carefully
You include many statistics, which is good, but several claims need credible sources and more precise wording. In academic argument, unsupported or exaggerated claims weaken your credibility.

For example, these claims need verification:
“there is no cure” for anorexia
implants are “a leading cause of Cancer”
chemicals “break down skin, and bone”
“girls as young as fourteen are having breast implants” everywhere
obese women are less likely to be married and earn “6,000 dollars less every year”

If you keep these points, cite reliable evidence. If you do not have sources, revise to more cautious language:
“can contribute to serious health risks”
“may increase anxiety and body dissatisfaction”
“some women pursue cosmetic procedures despite possible complications”

Distinguish emotion from argument
Your passion is a strength, but sometimes the essay relies on emotional language more than analysis. Phrases like “bleeding to death” or “fatally ill” may sound dramatic unless supported by evidence. In revision, aim to explain how media messages influence women rather than only condemning the results.

For instance, you could analyze:
repeated exposure to edited images
advertising that links beauty to success or love
pressure during adolescence when identity is still developing

This kind of explanation will make your argument more persuasive.

Improve tone and avoid overgeneralization
Some statements treat “women” as one single group or assume all women respond similarly. Try to avoid broad claims such as:
“every woman who doesn’t resemble Barbie is perceived as imperfect”
“we women think that we need them”
“most obese women have a low self esteem”

Instead, use more accurate phrasing:
“many women”
“some girls and women”
“media often suggests that…”

This makes your writing sound more fair and academically credible.

---

Sentence-level concerns

Grammar and word choice
There are frequent errors with:
their/there/they’re (“they are doing their job”)
woman/women (“the average woman is…”)
place/places (“society places on women”)
spelling: “adolescence,” “Naomi Campbell,” “tooth enamel”

Careful proofreading will help a lot.

Run-on sentences and punctuation
Many sentences are too long and contain multiple ideas. Break them into shorter, clearer units.

For example, this sentence:
“Plastic surgery is on an increase these days, it seems that everywhere you look…”
could become:
“Plastic surgery has become more common in recent years. Many young women are encouraged to see cosmetic procedures as a normal solution to insecurity.”

Paragraph unity
Some paragraphs contain too many different ideas at once. For example, the paragraph on anorexia also includes bulimia, plastic surgery, liposuction, whitening products, and dentures. Consider dividing this into separate paragraphs:
eating disorders
cosmetic surgery and beauty products

---

A few specific revision suggestions

Correct factual and spelling errors in the opening names and dates.
Replace “The 1990’s base these standards…” with a clearer sentence. It currently reads awkwardly.
Define key terms like “true beauty,” “media pressure,” or “unrealistic standards.”
Add transitions between paragraphs so the argument flows more smoothly.
Consider ending with a stronger final statement that echoes your thesis rather than repeating earlier ideas.

---

Final thoughts

This essay has a strong foundation because it addresses an important issue with sincerity and conviction. Your next step is to turn that passion into a more polished academic argument by improving organization, verifying evidence, and revising for clarity and precision. If you do that, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting question about “trash TV” talk shows and shows that you understand the complexity of the issue. You do a good job presenting both sides: some viewers see these programs as vulgar and harmful, while others see them as entertaining and sometimes even helpful. This balance is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear engagement with the topic  
You clearly care about the issue, and your voice comes through. The essay does not feel flat or mechanical.

Recognition of multiple perspectives  
You acknowledge that talk shows can be sensational and exploitative, but you also point out that they may provide support, information, and visibility for real problems. This makes your argument more thoughtful.

Strong use of vivid language  
Phrases like “on the edge of their seats” and “deep, dark secrets” create energy. This can be effective in persuasive writing when used carefully.

---

Main areas to revise

Clarify your thesis

Right now, your essay discusses the issue broadly, but your main claim is not fully clear until the last paragraph. An argumentative essay needs a precise thesis early in the introduction.

For example, your thesis could be something like:

“Although many daytime talk shows rely on sensational conflict, they should not be removed from television because participation is voluntary and these programs can also provide entertainment, public discussion, and support for people facing personal problems.”

This would help unify the whole essay.

---

Improve organization

The essay has strong ideas, but the paragraphs sometimes move in too many directions at once. Try a clearer structure:

Introduction: background + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: why people defend talk shows  
Body paragraph 2: criticisms of talk shows and their harmful effects  
Body paragraph 3: response to critics / discussion of personal responsibility  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why it matters

For example, your long third paragraph mixes:
how the shows create drama,
how audiences react,
how guests may reconcile,
how critics judge the shows.

These should be separated into more focused paragraphs.

---

Develop your argument more logically

At times, the essay seems undecided about whether talk shows are harmful or beneficial. That is fine if your goal is a nuanced argument, but you still need to guide the reader to your final position.

Your conclusion suggests that guests are responsible for the consequences of appearing on shows. If that is your main argument, make sure earlier paragraphs support it more directly. Right now, much of the essay describes the drama but does not always connect back to that claim.

Ask yourself:
Are you arguing that talk shows are valuable?
Or that they are dangerous but should remain on air?
Or that personal responsibility matters more than censorship?

Choose one main position and make every paragraph serve it.

---

Use evidence more effectively

The essay would be much stronger if it included more specific and credible evidence. You mention a violent incident involving a talk show guest, which is a useful example, but it needs clearer explanation and context. Also, one example is not enough for a full argument.

Consider adding:
statistics about talk show viewership,
examples of shows that provide expert advice,
evidence from critics about harmful effects,
discussion of free speech or media responsibility.

Even if this assignment does not require formal research, more concrete support will improve your credibility.

---

Revise for sentence clarity and grammar

There are many errors in spelling, punctuation, and sentence structure that make the essay harder to follow. Examples include:

“audeinces” → audiences
“agressively” → aggressively
“thier” → their
“heath” → health
“fueding” → feuding
“nieghbor” → neighbor
“The guest are willing” → The guests are willing

There are also many punctuation problems, especially with periods replacing commas or appearing in the middle of sentences:
“trash t.v..”
“vulgar. and inappropriate”
“Many faithful viewers. feel”

Be sure to proofread carefully. Reading the essay aloud may help you hear where sentences break awkwardly.

---

Maintain a more formal academic tone

Some wording is vivid, but parts of the essay sound too conversational or emotionally loaded for academic argument. For example:
“trash t.v.”
“naysayers”
“flying fist”
“dark skeletons taking over their daily lives”

Try to keep your style strong but more precise. Academic writing is most persuasive when it is controlled and clear.

---

Final suggestion

For revision, focus first on thesis and organization, then on sentence-level editing. If you can make your central argument clearer and divide your ideas into focused paragraphs, the essay will become much more persuasive.

You have the foundation of a compelling argument here. With a clearer position, better structure, and careful proofreading, this essay could become much stronger.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0004.4.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: the legal drinking age in the United States should be lowered to eighteen. That gives you a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. You also use an emotionally powerful line of reasoning—if eighteen-year-olds can serve in the military, they should have other adult rights. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: clarity of argument, strength of evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengths
Clear thesis: You state your position directly in the introduction: “Eighteen should be the legal age to consume alcohol in the United States.”
Relevant arguments: You bring up military service, adulthood, high school graduation, and crime. These are all possible points for an argument on this topic.
Personal engagement: Your voice is invested in the issue, which can make persuasive writing more compelling.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Make the argument more logical and consistent
Some claims need clearer reasoning or are contradicted later. For example, your final paragraph says, “Drinking should be prohibited to to kids eighteen or older,” but this is the opposite of your thesis. You likely meant that drinking should not be prohibited for people eighteen or older.

Also, some points need stronger explanation:
Why does military service necessarily mean a person should legally drink?
Why would allowing eighteen-year-olds to drink reduce crime?
Why should a high school diploma determine alcohol rights?

Right now, some claims feel asserted rather than proven. In revision, explain the logic step by step.

B. Use evidence instead of assumptions
Many sentences make broad claims without support:
“Most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving.”
“Most kids eighteen to twenty are out of high school, and their parents do not care what they do.”
“Maybe a higher percentage of teenagers will graduate…”

These statements need evidence. In an academic argument, readers will expect facts, statistics, or credible sources. For example, you could cite:
data on military enlistment age,
research on alcohol laws in countries or U.S. states,
statistics on underage drinking and drunk driving,
studies about whether a lower drinking age affects safety.

Without evidence, the essay reads more like opinion than argument.

C. Improve organization
Each body paragraph should have one main claim connected to the thesis.

A possible structure:
Introduction with background and thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Eighteen-year-olds are legal adults with major responsibilities, including military service.
Body paragraph 2: Lowering the drinking age could reduce unsafe hidden drinking.
Body paragraph 3: Counterargument—some people argue that younger drinkers are less mature; respond with evidence.
Conclusion: Restate the thesis and explain why the change would benefit society.

Including a counterargument will make your essay sound more mature and persuasive.

Style and Tone
Try to use a more formal academic tone. Phrases like “drink a beer,” “kids are dieing for our country,” and “I would feel more like an adult” sound personal or emotional. Emotion can help, but academic writing is stronger when emotion is balanced with reasoning and evidence.

For example:
Instead of “kids are dying for our country,” write:  
  “If eighteen-year-olds are considered mature enough for military service, the law should also recognize them as capable of making decisions about alcohol.”

Also avoid making the essay about only your own feelings in the conclusion. Focus on the broader public issue.

Sentence-Level Issues to Correct
There are frequent grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors that make the essay harder to follow. Examples:

“Every since” → Ever since
“Independance” → Independence
“dieing” → dying
“two young” → too young
“affects of drinking alcohol” → effects of drinking alcohol
“containing alcohol in the car” → possessing alcohol in the car
“gonvernment” → government
“convince the gonvernment” → likely convince the government
“prohibited to to kids” → prohibited to kids or better prohibited for those under eighteen/twenty-one

You also have several comma splices and awkward sentences. For example:
“People under the age of twenty one are considered to be minors, when it comes to drinking alcoholic beverages in the United States.”
  - Remove the comma after minors.
“At eighteen you are considered a man, therefore you should be able to decide your own fate…”
  - Better: “At eighteen, a person is legally considered an adult; therefore, that person should be able to make independent decisions.”

What to Do Next
When revising, try these steps:
Rewrite your thesis clearly and precisely.
Make each paragraph focus on one reason.
Add evidence from reliable sources.
Remove unsupported generalizations.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
End with a conclusion that emphasizes public benefit, not just personal preference.

Final Thought
You have a debatable topic and a clear opinion, which is the foundation of an argumentative essay. Your next step is to turn strong feelings into a stronger academic argument by improving logic, adding evidence, and editing carefully.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important issue: how ideas about gender are formed early and can shape adult identity, relationships, and life choices. You also make an ambitious move by connecting several sources (Pollitt, Ferrell, Friedan, and Gray). That gives your paper the potential for a strong synthesis argument. For revision, the main priorities are to clarify your thesis, improve organization, and strengthen accuracy and style.

Clarify your main argument
Right now, your thesis is not fully consistent. At some points, you argue that children need a “core” understanding of what makes them male or female; at other points, you argue that stereotypes are harmful and roles should be flexible. These ideas may be compatible, but the paper does not clearly explain how.

For example, you write that “men and women today need to understand and respect the fact that they are different,” but later you criticize rigid gender expectations. Ask yourself:

Are you arguing that gender differences are real and valuable?
Or that society exaggerates them into harmful stereotypes?
What exactly should children be taught?

Try revising your thesis into one clear, arguable claim. For instance:  
Children should be taught that gender exists, but they should not be forced into rigid stereotypes that limit their choices, relationships, and identities.

That would give the whole essay a clearer direction.

Improve organization and paragraph unity
Your essay has a broad structure, but some paragraphs drift between ideas. Each body paragraph should focus on one main claim that supports the thesis.

A possible structure:

Introduction: present the issue and state your thesis clearly.
Pollitt paragraph: explain how children’s media teaches limiting gender stereotypes.
Ferrell and Friedan paragraph: show how those stereotypes affect adults later in life.
Gray paragraph: discuss whether understanding gender differences can help relationships.
Conclusion: explain what changes society and parents should make.

Right now, some transitions are abrupt. For example, “Now that our children are off to a wonderful start they are ready for adulthood” sounds sarcastic, but it also feels sudden. Use transitions that clearly explain the connection between childhood messaging and adult outcomes.

Use sources more accurately and critically
You bring in multiple authors, which is good, but several references are unclear, misspelled, or oversimplified.

Examples:
Katha Pollitt, not “Pollit.”
Betty Friedan, not “Frieden,” “Friedon,” or “Fredian.”
Warren Farrell, not “Ferrell.”
John Gray, not “Grey.”
Men Are from Mars, Women Are from Venus, not “Venue.”

More importantly, be careful not to misrepresent your sources. For example, when you say, “Their approach is that gender roles are needed,” that may not accurately reflect all of these authors, especially Pollitt or Friedan. You need to explain how each source supports your position rather than grouping them together too broadly.

Also, integrate quotations more smoothly. For instance, “Pollit contends:that the message is more straight forward for the boys” needs proper punctuation and context. Introduce the quotation, explain it, and then connect it to your argument.

Strengthen analysis, not just summary
Much of the essay summarizes what the authors say, but stronger argument essays go further by explaining why the evidence matters.

For example, after discussing Pollitt’s examples of television, explain:
How do cartoons shape children’s assumptions?
Why are those assumptions harmful later?
What should parents or educators do differently?

After discussing Ralph and Friedan’s homemakers, explain:
How do these cases show the long-term consequences of imposed gender roles?
Are these examples still relevant today?

Analysis is where your own argument becomes most persuasive.

Revise tone and word choice
Some phrasing is too informal, exaggerated, or imprecise for an academic essay. Examples include:
“wonderful start” (sarcastic and unclear)
“roller coaster ride”
“scarry confession”
“bad message delivered at a bad time through a bad medium”

Try to use more precise academic language. Instead of “bad,” say harmful, limiting, distorted, or misleading depending on the meaning.

Also avoid broad generalizations such as “parents are not interested in justice, they just want quiet.” That claim is too sweeping and weakens your credibility.

Edit carefully for grammar and mechanics
This draft has many spelling, punctuation, and sentence-level errors, and they distract from your ideas. Some recurring issues:

Spelling: “ammend,” “relazed,” “pints,” “scarry,” “metophor,” “Califorian”
Apostrophes: “their's” should be “theirs”; “its” vs. “it’s”
Comma use: “but, there are” should not have a comma there
Sentence fragments: “Although the method takes on many forms, the message is the same .”
Citation formatting: keep citations consistent and complete

I strongly recommend reading the essay aloud and proofreading line by line, or asking a classmate/tutor to review it.

Final advice
You have a strong topic and a real attempt at synthesis. Your next draft should focus on:
writing one clear thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one claim,
using sources accurately,
adding more analysis,
editing for correctness and academic tone.

With revision, this essay could become a much stronger argument.
