Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear and debatable position: you argue that legalizing some drugs, especially marijuana, could benefit society more than prohibition does. That is a strong topic for an argumentative essay because it invites debate and requires evidence. You also raise several meaningful points, including regulation, prison overcrowding, tax revenue, crime reduction, and public health. These are good foundations. For revision, the main goals are to clarify your thesis, improve organization, strengthen evidence, and edit for grammar and style.

Strengthen and focus your thesis
Right now, your central claim shifts. Sometimes you argue for legalizing marijuana, sometimes “some drugs,” and sometimes all drugs. Because of this, the essay can feel inconsistent.

For example, you write:
“The legalization of some drugs like marijuana would improve many different aspects of society.”
Later, you discuss “marijuana among other drugs.”
Later, you suggest the government could sell drugs generally.

Choose one precise claim and build the essay around it. For example:
Stronger thesis option: “Legalizing and regulating marijuana would reduce crime, ease pressure on the prison system, and improve public health more effectively than current prohibition policies.”

If you want to argue for more than marijuana, you will need to define exactly which drugs and why.

Improve organization
Your essay includes many ideas, but they are not always arranged logically. Some paragraphs jump from health, to age restrictions, to fake IDs, to alcohol laws, which weakens your argument.

Try organizing your essay into clear body paragraphs, each with one main point:
Introduction: background + thesis  
Public health/regulation: legalization allows quality control and safer distribution  
Crime and prisons: legalization could reduce illegal markets and overcrowded jails  
Economic effects: tax revenue, jobs, law enforcement savings  
Counterargument: concerns about addiction, youth access, and harm  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why legalization is the better policy

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that states the paragraph’s main idea.

Use stronger evidence and reasoning
Many of your claims are interesting, but they are often stated without enough support. In academic argument, readers need facts, studies, examples, or statistics—not only opinion.

For example:
“Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs.”
“Hopefully, the number of drug users would decrease.”
“Intravenously used drugs would not likely lead to the spread of AIDS.”

These are big claims and need evidence. You should ask:
What studies show this?
Are there examples from places where marijuana or drugs were decriminalized/legalized?
What do public health experts say?

Also, some reasoning needs more care. For example, the statement “If people want to hurt themselves, let them because they are only hurting themselves” ignores the fact that addiction can affect families, children, driving safety, employment, and public health costs. You should acknowledge this counterargument and respond to it thoughtfully. That will make your essay more persuasive.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay shows awareness of the other side. You do mention health risks and age limits, but the discussion is underdeveloped.

Consider adding a paragraph like:
Opponents argue legalization may increase addiction or make drugs easier for young people to access.
However, regulation, age restrictions, education, and treatment programs may manage these risks better than the illegal market does.

This will help your essay sound more balanced and credible.

Tone and word choice
At times, the language becomes too informal or emotionally extreme for academic writing. For example:
“If somebody does not want to live on this earth, why make them?”

This statement is too harsh and distracts from your argument. Stay focused on policy, health, law, and society. Academic readers respond better to careful reasoning than to shocking statements.

Also, avoid overgeneralizations such as:
“People are going to use drugs anyway.”
“Everyone who is using alcohol or drugs are just trying to achieve one goal.”

These claims are too absolute. Use more precise language like “some people,” “many users,” or “prohibition has not eliminated drug use.”

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are many grammar problems that make the essay harder to read. Common issues include:
Subject-verb agreement: “The side effects of marijuana is” → “The side effects of marijuana are”
Word choice: “lease harmful” → “least harmful”; “stimulate from” → “stem from”
Sentence fragments: “Issues such as permanent damage to the brain and other parts of the anatomy.”
Missing words or incorrect forms: “what anyone say” → “what anyone says”; “hann your body” → “harm your body”
Punctuation and quotation formatting need attention as well.

Read the essay aloud slowly, or revise sentence by sentence for correctness and clarity.

Citation
You include one quotation from “Wibotsky 17,” but the citation is not fully integrated or explained. Introduce the source, quote it correctly, and explain how it supports your argument.

Final advice
You have the beginnings of a strong argument, especially because you identify multiple social effects of drug policy. Your next draft should focus on precision, structure, evidence, and academic tone. If you narrow your claim to marijuana legalization and support each point with credible sources, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: USC should require professors to demonstrate sufficient English ability before teaching. The paper is strongest when it stays focused on students’ ability to learn and on the university’s responsibility to provide effective instruction. You also include a concrete opening story, which helps readers see why the issue matters.

What’s working

Clear thesis and argument
Your central claim is understandable: professors should be able to communicate well enough in English to teach their subject effectively. The essay maintains this argument consistently.

Effective use of anecdote
The story about your friend gives the issue a human face. It provides a real consequence—having to drop the course or delay graduation—which makes the problem feel urgent.

Strong sense of audience
You write as if addressing a university community that should care about fairness, tuition, and educational quality. That gives the essay purpose.

Main revision priorities

Refine the thesis so it is more precise and less absolute
Right now, some sentences make broad claims such as “When professors can’t speak English, they can’t communicate… therefore they cannot teach them.” This creates an all-or-nothing argument. A stronger thesis would focus on functional proficiency rather than perfection.

Possible revision:
“Before professors are assigned to teach at USC, the university should require them to demonstrate sufficient spoken English proficiency to explain course material, answer student questions, and hold office hours effectively.”

This version is more specific, reasonable, and easier to defend.

Reduce overgeneralization and potentially biased wording
Be careful not to let the essay sound as if it is attacking foreign professors rather than addressing a communication problem. Phrases like “Ph.D.s from other nations” and repeated references to “students who speak English” can make the paper seem culturally narrow. The issue is not nationality; it is classroom communication.

Instead of: “The Ph.D.s from other nations should be more selective…”
Try: “Any instructor assigned to teach in English should meet a clear standard of spoken English proficiency.”

That revision keeps the argument fair and policy-based.

Strengthen evidence beyond the anecdote
The personal example is a good opening, but one story is not enough to prove a university-wide problem. Add broader support:
university policy on teaching assistants and language testing
student expectations for instructional quality
examples of what effective communication in teaching requires
perhaps data or a campus policy comparison, if available

Even without outside research, you can strengthen the logic by explaining why lecture, discussion, office hours, labs, and assessment all depend on clear communication.

Acknowledge complexity and address counterarguments
Right now, the essay presents only one side. Consider acknowledging that:
many international professors are excellent teachers
accents are not the same as poor teaching
subject expertise is valuable
universities also hire for research and specialization

Then explain your position:
expertise and research matter, but effective communication is still essential in a teaching role.

This will make your argument more credible and mature.

Improve organization and avoid repetition
Several body paragraphs repeat the same point: students pay tuition and therefore deserve instruction they can understand. Instead of repeating, give each paragraph a distinct job.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: anecdote + thesis  
Body 1: why communication is essential to teaching  
Body 2: consequences for students when communication fails  
Body 3: why current university policy is inconsistent  
Body 4: proposed solution and why it is fair  
Conclusion: restate significance

This would make the essay feel more intentional and less circular.

Style and sentence-level concerns

Fix grammar, punctuation, and typing errors
There are many mechanical problems that distract from your ideas: missing quotation marks, sentence fragments, spacing problems, random capitalization, and typographical errors. For example:
“It s” should be “it’s”
“b t U” appears to be a typo
“began. To ask” should be one sentence
“her only alternatives” needs punctuation between clauses

Careful proofreading is essential.

Use more formal academic language
Some word choices sound conversational or imprecise:
“Hey, math is a universal language”
“squander through complicated material alone”
“domain over a classroom”

Try clearer, more academic phrasing:
“struggle through difficult material independently”
“be assigned to teach”
“communicate course concepts effectively”

Keep pronouns consistent and inclusive
The essay often uses “he,” “his,” and “him” to refer to professors and students generally. Use more inclusive language such as “they,” “their,” or “the professor/the student.”

Final advice
Your essay has a strong core argument and a compelling topic, but it needs revision to become more persuasive. Focus on:
making the thesis more precise
reducing broad or biased language
organizing paragraphs around distinct reasons
adding broader evidence and counterargument
carefully proofreading for clarity and correctness

If you revise with those goals in mind, your paper will sound more balanced, credible, and academically effective.

Your essay has a clear sense of urgency and strong emotional commitment. You make it obvious that the Browns’ move matters not just as a business decision, but as a betrayal of a city and its fans. Clarify your central claim
Right now, the essay asks several questions—*Is this right? Is business just business? Do loyalty and tradition count?*—but your actual argument should be stated directly in one clear thesis.

A stronger thesis might be:
Arthur Modell’s decision to move the Cleveland Browns was unjustified because Cleveland supported the team financially, politically, and emotionally, and Modell acted dishonestly by refusing compromise before accepting Baltimore’s offer.

This kind of thesis tells readers exactly what you will prove.

Improve organization
The essay currently moves back and forth between examples, emotion, and background. Try restructuring it into clear paragraphs with one main purpose each:

Introduction: explain the move and present your thesis.  
Context: describe the larger trend of NFL franchise relocation.

Conclusion: explain why the move harms more than a business—it harms a community.

This structure will make your argument easier to follow.

Use a more academic tone
Your strongest weakness is tone. Many phrases are emotionally powerful, but in an argumentative essay they can sound exaggerated or unsupported. Examples include:

“proudly gave Cleveland the one finger salute”
“Arthur B. Modell is a liar”
“the only people who hurt are the fans of Cleveland”

These statements show how you feel, but they weaken your credibility because they sound personal rather than analytical. Instead, use evidence to let readers reach that conclusion themselves.

For example:
Instead of “Arthur B. Modell is a liar,”
  write “Modell contradicted his earlier public commitment to keep the team in Cleveland.”

That sounds stronger because it is factual.

Strengthen evidence and explain it
You include many useful details: the Gateway Project, the sin tax, attendance numbers, Baltimore’s offer. These are good pieces of evidence. However, sometimes they are listed without enough explanation of how they prove your point.

For example, when you mention Cleveland’s spending on downtown development, explain why that matters:
It shows Cleveland was investing in its future.
It weakens the argument that the city did not support professional sports.
It gives context for why the move felt like a betrayal.

Also, be careful with facts and names. A few details appear inaccurate or misspelled, which hurts trust:
“Tennessee Cherokees”
“Los Angeles Rains”
“late march of 1983”
“it's” instead of “its”

Make sure all team names, dates, and financial figures are correct.

Work on sentence clarity and grammar
Many sentences are run-ons or contain punctuation problems. Shorter, clearer sentences will make your ideas more persuasive.

For example, this sentence is hard to follow:

apostrophes: its vs. it’s
capitalization: March, Rock and Roll Hall of Fame
spacing and punctuation
comma splices and run-on sentences

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important and engaging argument: people should not let social expectations dictate major life choices. The discussion of gender roles, work, and family gives the piece relevance, and your central concern about regret and self-betrayal is clear. To strengthen the essay, focus on sharpening the thesis, improving organization, and supporting claims more carefully.

Clarify and narrow the thesis
At present, your main argument is implied rather than stated precisely. The essay seems to argue that both men and women are often pressured into socially approved life paths, and this can lead to dissatisfaction; therefore, people should make choices based on their own values rather than external expectations.

Try stating that directly near the end of the introduction. A clearer thesis will help guide the reader and keep each paragraph connected to your main claim.

Possible revision of thesis:
“Although social expectations often pressure men and women into conventional career and family roles, lasting fulfillment is more likely when individuals choose paths that reflect their own values rather than the demands of others.”

Improve organization
The essay has good material, but the structure feels somewhat repetitive. You move from Friedan, to Ralph, to women’s choices, and then back to Ralph and general reflections. Consider a more deliberate progression:

Introduction: define the problem of letting others shape one’s life.
Example 1: women: discuss Friedan and the pressure to “have it all.”
Example 2: men: discuss Ralph and the pressure to prioritize status and income.
Comparison: show that both cases reveal the same problem—social expectations over personal conviction.
Counterargument/complexity: acknowledge that practical constraints such as money matter.
Conclusion: argue for thoughtful self-determination and a “middle road.”

This structure would reduce repetition and make the comparison between men and women more persuasive.

Use evidence more precisely
You refer to *The Feminine Mystique*, “The Problem That Has No Name,” and Warren Farrell’s “Ralph’s Story,” which is a strong start. However, the essay often summarizes broadly instead of analyzing specific evidence. Also, the placeholder marks (“”) suggest missing quotations or citations. Those need to be completed.

When using sources, do three things:
introduce the source clearly,
quote or paraphrase accurately,
explain how the evidence supports your argument.

For example, instead of only saying Friedan influenced women to pursue careers, explain how her ideas contributed to a conflict between social ambition and domestic expectations.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some claims are too sweeping, such as:
“nothing has changed”
“most men helped around the house”
“many young women would say definitely yes”

These statements may weaken your credibility because they sound absolute without evidence. Qualify them more carefully:
“some pressures remain similar today”
“many families still struggle to divide labor fairly”
“for some young women, giving up motherhood would feel like a major loss”

This makes the argument more balanced and academically persuasive.

Strengthen tone and analytical focus
At times the essay becomes conversational or rhetorical in a way that reduces its authority. Questions like “But what if it’s wrong?” or “What’s not to like...?” can be effective sparingly, but too many rhetorical questions make the argument feel less formal.

Also, some sections sound judgmental or assume one solution is best, especially when discussing women choosing motherhood first. Be careful not to replace one social prescription with another. Your strongest insight is that individuals should choose freely; keep that principle central.

Revise for sentence clarity and style
There are many sentences with awkward phrasing, grammatical errors, or punctuation problems. A few examples:
“being ,pressured” → remove comma
“erected of them” → likely “expected of them”
“reouired” → “required”
“actuality prefer” → “actually prefer”
“computer tine-in” → “tie-in”

Some sentences are also very long and could be split for clarity. For instance, your opening sentence could be divided into two shorter sentences to improve readability.

Be careful with fairness and nuance
Your essay addresses gender expectations, which is a complex issue. It will be stronger if you acknowledge that there is no single ideal arrangement for all men or women. For example, instead of implying that motherhood first and career later is the best answer, frame it as one possible alternative that may suit some people.

Conclusion
Your final sentence points in a strong direction: people must “search [their] own heart.” That is the core of the essay. To make the conclusion more powerful, briefly restate the problem, the examples, and the lesson:
social pressure can lead to regret,
both men and women suffer from imposed roles,
fulfillment requires deliberate personal choice.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around that thesis.
Complete and properly integrate quotations/citations.
Replace broad generalizations with precise, qualified claims.
Edit carefully for grammar, diction, and punctuation.

You have a meaningful topic and a sincere argumentative voice. With clearer structure and more precise support, this essay could become much more compelling.

Your essay has a clear and energetic central interest: you want to argue that men and women can appreciate their differences without giving up legal equality. These are not the same argument. A stronger essay would make one precise claim and then build around it. For example:

“Although men and women should receive equal legal rights and opportunities, society should distinguish between unfair discrimination and the harmless appreciation of sex differences in entertainment, advertising, and some athletic contexts.”

That kind of thesis would help unify the paper.

Improve organization
The essay currently reads as a series of loosely connected examples. Try organizing by clear body-point paragraphs, each tied directly to your thesis.

A possible structure:

The football/volleyball example depends entirely on personal experience and assumes the football team “deserved” more money.

The comparison between Madonna and Charles Barkley oversimplifies public attitudes and does not account for important differences in how men’s and women’s bodies are marketed.

Have I explained why my example proves my point?

You do not necessarily need formal research for every point, but you do need fuller explanation and fairer reasoning.

An argumentative essay becomes stronger when it acknowledges objections. Right now, the essay often presents only one side and sometimes caricatures the opposing view.

For example, people who criticize strip clubs, Hooters, or sexualized ads are not always trying to “disintegrate” male-female differences. They may be concerned about objectification, workplace exclusion, or unequal power. If you respond to those concerns directly, your argument will sound more mature and persuasive.

“Women and men are, of course, different physically and emotionally.”
“Both are, beyond any doubt, superhuman.”
“Most guys do not want to have men in small outfits serving them food.”

These broad generalizations should be qualified. Academic writing usually benefits from words like many, often, in some cases, generally rather than universal claims.

Your essay would improve if you separated these ideas more carefully:
difference vs. inequality
sexual attraction vs. objectification
consumer preference vs. legal fairness
biological differences in sports vs. social stereotypes in employment

Right now, the paper often treats them as interchangeable, which creates logical confusion.

were / where
vice / vise

sells job should be sales job

Clearer:

Final advice
Your essay has a bold viewpoint and strong voice, but to make it persuasive in an academic setting, you need a narrower thesis, clearer organization, more careful reasoning, acknowledgment of counterarguments, and substantial proofreading. Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: welfare reform should combine ideas from both Democrats and Republicans. That is a strong starting point because it gives your paper a definite argument rather than just a summary of the debate. You also use sources throughout, which shows you are trying to build your claims with evidence. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, strengthening your reasoning, and improving style and correctness.

What’s working

Clear topic and stance
   - You identify welfare as a contested political issue and take a position early.
   - Your thesis proposes specific reforms: time limits, required training, reduced funding for some programs, and ending additional benefits for more children.

Use of evidence
   - You bring in multiple sources and examples, including Project Match and Princess Rowell.
   - These examples help move the paper beyond general opinion.

Organized by policy areas
   - The essay has body sections on training, time limits, and spending cuts.
   - This gives the paper a logical overall structure.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more precise and arguable
Right now, the thesis lists several policies, but it does not fully explain why this combination is the best solution or what principle connects them. A stronger thesis would do more than list reforms; it would make a claim about effectiveness and fairness.

Possible revision:
“Welfare reform should combine limited time-bound assistance with required job training and targeted spending reductions so that the system supports families in crisis without encouraging long-term dependency.”

This version gives the essay a clearer central idea: balancing support with personal responsibility.

Strengthen the logic of your argument
At several points, the essay assumes that cuts or time limits will automatically solve dependency. That is a large claim and needs more support. For example:
“If these time limits were enforced there would no chance for recipients to become dependent...”
“This would save the government a lot of money and make people support themselves.”

These statements oversimplify the issue. A reader may ask: What if jobs are unavailable? What about childcare, transportation, disability, or low wages? To make your argument stronger, acknowledge these complications and explain why your proposed combination of training and limits still works better than the alternatives.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should consider objections. For example:
Critics may argue that strict time limits hurt children.
Others may say cutting AFDC punishes poor families rather than solving poverty.
Some may argue that having more children is not simply caused by welfare incentives.

You do not need to agree with these views, but you should acknowledge them and respond. Doing so will make your paper sound more balanced and persuasive.

Use evidence more carefully
Some of your evidence is relevant, but parts of the essay rely on assumptions or emotionally loaded conclusions. For instance, the claim that mothers on welfare are “having more babies at the government’s expense” is a strong interpretation, but the quoted evidence does not fully prove that welfare causes births.

Try to separate fact from interpretation. After each quotation, explain exactly how it supports your argument. Also, be cautious about using one anecdote, like Princess Rowell, as proof of a national solution. Personal stories are useful, but they should support broader evidence, not replace it.

Improve paragraph development
Many paragraphs begin with a topic sentence, but some need fuller analysis. A strong body paragraph should do this:
State the claim.
Present evidence.
Explain how the evidence supports the claim.
Link back to the thesis.

For example, your paragraph on Project Match could be expanded by explaining why that program succeeded and how it supports your broader proposal for national reform.

Style and grammar issues

There are many sentence-level errors that weaken your credibility. Focus on:

Spelling: “Sliverstein” should be “Silverstein”; “highschool” should be “high school.”
Capitalization: “Whitman” should be capitalized consistently.
Punctuation with quotations: Several quotations have misplaced commas, apostrophes, or quotation marks.
Grammar:  
  - “there would no chance” → “there would be no chance”  
  - “she get on welfare” → “she gets on welfare”  
  - “for long periods of time” is clearer than some repetitive phrasing.
Word choice: Avoid informal or biased phrasing such as “quit butting heads for power.”

Also, check citation format carefully. Some parenthetical citations and quotation punctuation are incorrect or inconsistent.

Suggestions for revision
When you rewrite, try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce the debate.
   - End with a focused thesis that emphasizes both support and responsibility.

Body Paragraph 1: Training programs
   - Explain why training is essential.
   - Use Project Match and Princess Rowell as evidence.
   - Analyze why training helps recipients gain independence.

Body Paragraph 2: Time limits
   - Argue that time limits can prevent long-term dependency.
   - Acknowledge exceptions or concerns and explain how training makes limits more reasonable.

Body Paragraph 3: Spending reform
   - Argue for targeted cuts rather than broad reductions.
   - Be careful not to overstate claims about mothers and childbirth incentives.

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument

   - Explain how your combined plan avoids that problem better than either party’s plan alone.

Conclusion
   - Do more than repeat that compromise is needed.
   - Reinforce why your mixed approach is the most effective and fair.

Final note
You have the foundation of a real argument here. The next step is to make it more nuanced, better supported, and more polished. If you revise for clarity, fairness, and correctness, this essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important issue—television violence and its effects on children—and it shows that you have begun researching public debates about the V-chip and media regulation. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your argument, improving organization, and strengthening evidence and analysis.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your essay presents several ideas but does not commit to one clear position. For example, you suggest that television violence is a “serious concern,” but later you argue that violence “adds texture to the show and not to poison children’s minds.” These claims point in different directions.

Ask yourself: What exactly am I arguing?
Possible directions:
TV violence harms children and should be regulated more strongly.
TV violence is concerning, but parental responsibility matters more than government censorship.
The V-chip and similar proposals are ineffective responses to a complex problem.

Once you decide, write a thesis that makes a clear claim. For example:
Although television violence is widespread, government censorship tools such as the V-chip are less effective than active parental guidance and media education.

This gives the paper a focused purpose.

Improve organization
The essay currently reads more like a list of facts than a developed argument. Try organizing it into clear paragraphs with one main point each.

A stronger structure might be:
Introduction: explain the issue and present your thesis.
Extent of television violence: summarize evidence showing how common violent content is.
Concerns about children: explain why some people believe TV violence affects behavior.
Responses and policies: discuss the V-chip and congressional proposals.
Your evaluation: explain whether these policies work and what should be done instead.
Conclusion: reinforce your main claim.

Also, use transitions so readers can follow your logic. For example:
“In response to these concerns…”
“However, critics argue…”
“A more effective solution may be…”

Develop analysis, not just summary
You include sources, but often you report information without explaining how it supports your argument. For instance, when you mention Senate proposals or violent cartoons, you need to explain why that matters.

Example:
Instead of writing only that the Senate approved bills, add analysis such as:
These proposals show that lawmakers viewed television violence as a public issue, but they also raised concerns about censorship and government overreach.

Your reader needs to see your interpretation, not just the fact.

Use evidence more carefully
Some of your claims are too broad or unsupported. For example:
“Many children watch violent tv shows because the action thrills them and can sometimes cause the child to become violent.”
This is a major cause-and-effect claim and needs stronger evidence.
“how many parents actually watch television with their teenager?”
This is a rhetorical question, but it does not prove anything unless you provide evidence.

Try replacing vague statements with specific, cited support. Also, explain the credibility and relevance of each source.

Address inconsistencies
One of the biggest revision needs is logical consistency. For example:
Early in the essay, television violence is described as dangerous.
Later, you say violence is included for storytelling and that children will eventually have to face violence anyway.

These ideas are not necessarily incompatible, but you need to reconcile them. You might argue:
While violence can serve legitimate dramatic purposes, repeated exposure may still affect young viewers, which is why supervision matters.

That kind of nuance makes your argument more persuasive.

Strengthen style and tone
Your tone is sometimes informal or imprecise. Examples:
“the infamous V-chip”
“well that is strictly up to the viewer to decide”

In an academic argument, aim for a more neutral, formal tone. Also avoid phrases like “whomever is writing the television show” when a more precise phrase such as “the writers and producers” would work better.

Correct grammar and mechanics
There are several sentence-level issues to revise:
Capitalization: “tv” should be “TV.”
Word choice: “Some of the proposals to antiviolence” should be “Some anti-violence proposals.”
Date error: “1995-1995 season” is likely incorrect.
Quotation/punctuation: “sinister combat violence'” has incorrect punctuation.
Sentence clarity: “The most violent cartoons are 'Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles', 'Spider Man', and some 'Bugs Bunny' episodes with Elmer Fudd.” This should be formatted and punctuated more carefully.

Read the essay aloud as you revise; this can help you catch awkward phrasing and run-on logic.

Revise the conclusion
Your conclusion currently introduces a new claim rather than fully concluding the essay. Instead of ending with “children will have to deal with it,” try summarizing your position and suggesting a reasonable response.

For example:
Television violence is a real concern, especially for young viewers, but regulation alone cannot solve the problem. Parents, broadcasters, and policymakers must work together to limit harmful exposure while preserving creative freedom.

Final recommendation
For your next draft, focus first on choosing one clear thesis. Then reorganize the essay so every paragraph supports that thesis with evidence and explanation. You already have useful material; the main challenge is turning it into a focused, coherent argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: government should continue, and possibly increase, support for the arts. You show genuine commitment to the topic, and your paper offers several different lines of argument—historical, social, economic, educational, and therapeutic. That range is a strength. To revise effectively, focus on making your central claim sharper, organizing your reasons more logically, and supporting them with more precise evidence.

What’s working

Clear argumentative purpose  
From the introduction, your stance is easy to identify. You are not merely describing the value of art; you are arguing for public funding of the arts.

Breadth of reasons  
You do a good job showing that the arts matter in multiple ways: they preserve culture, build community, support communication, stimulate the economy, and promote mental health and learning.

Strong sense of voice  
The essay sounds engaged and sincere. Your personal interest in art gives the paper energy, especially in the paragraph where you discuss your own experience.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your thesis is somewhat general: the arts should be supported by the government. Make it more specific by naming your main reasons.

For example:

A thesis like this gives the reader a roadmap and helps you organize the essay.

Improve organization
Several paragraphs introduce valuable ideas, but the essay sometimes moves loosely from one point to another. Try grouping related ideas into clearer sections.

A possible structure:

Cultural/historical value of the arts
Communication and community-building
Economic benefits
Educational and therapeutic benefits
Counterargument and response
Conclusion

This structure would help the essay feel more cumulative and persuasive.

Use stronger evidence
Many claims are expressed in broad terms: “psychologists have told us,” “researchers have found,” “continual research has been done.” These phrases suggest evidence, but no actual sources or examples are given. In an argumentative essay, readers need proof.

For revision, add:
a real example of a publicly funded museum or arts program
data about tourism, jobs, or revenue generated by the arts
a study about arts education and cognitive development
an example of prison or therapy-based arts programming

Even one or two concrete examples per major point would make the essay much more convincing.

Address counterarguments more directly
You briefly mention budget cuts and later discuss pornography, but neither point is fully developed as a counterargument. A stronger paper would directly engage objections such as:
taxpayer money should go to essential services first
private donors can support the arts without government involvement
public funding may support controversial work

Then respond to those objections. For example, you might argue that arts funding is a small part of budgets but produces broad public benefits that private funding cannot distribute equally.

Clarify and refine some claims
A few statements are too absolute or unclear. For example:
“art is something which produces a positive, constructive analysis of aspects of human existence”
“some works are not meant be valued as art”

These claims raise difficult philosophical issues and could distract from your main argument about funding. Since the essay is about government support, you do not need to define art so narrowly. Be careful not to weaken your argument by including debatable claims that are not essential.

Style and sentence-level issues

Tone
At times the style becomes overly dramatic or wordy (“loom on the horizon like an ominous storm cloud”). Aim for a more direct academic tone.

Word choice and grammar
There are several sentence-level issues to edit:
“which should keep” → “which should be kept” or “which should continue to receive”
“the United States no longer is faced” → “the United States is no longer faced”
“in a ever-more complex” → “in an ever more complex”
“not meant be valued” → “not meant to be valued”
spelling errors such as “oftentiines,” “pschosanalysts,” and “analysing” (depending on your preferred variety of English, be consistent)

Pronoun and diction choices
The repeated use of “man” and “his” as universal terms sounds dated. Use more inclusive language such as “human beings,” “people,” or “artists.”

Paragraph unity
Some paragraphs contain multiple ideas that could be separated. For example, the paragraph beginning “One of the most important reasons…” combines creativity, clearer thinking, stress relief, personal experience, and color theory. Consider focusing each paragraph on one main point.

Final recommendation
Your essay has a strong foundation because it is clearly opinionated and rich in possible arguments. The next step is to make it more academic and persuasive: sharpen the thesis, organize the body more clearly, add concrete evidence, and address objections directly. If you do that, your paper will move from a thoughtful personal argument to a much stronger academic one.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and strong energy: you firmly argue that professional football players’ salaries should be capped, and you give several reasons for that view. The paper also has a recognizable structure, with an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion. That gives you a solid starting point for revision.

What is working
Clear thesis and purpose  
   Early in the essay, you make your claim clear: there should be a salary cap. Readers always know where you stand.

Multiple supporting reasons  
   You do not rely on only one point. You discuss fairness, fan facilities, greed, and competitive balance. This shows an attempt to build a full argument.

Strong voice  
   Your writing sounds engaged and passionate. That can be effective in argumentative writing when paired with evidence and careful reasoning.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the logic of your argument
Right now, many claims are based on opinion, exaggeration, or weak comparisons. For example, comparing football players’ salaries to doctors’ salaries may create emotional impact, but it does not fully prove that a salary cap is the right policy. A stronger essay would explain why the labor market in professional sports should be regulated differently from other professions.

Ask yourself:
What problem specifically would a salary cap solve?
How would a cap improve the league, fans, or society?
Why is limiting player pay better than limiting owner profits or redistributing revenue in other ways?

Also, some claims are too broad or unfair, such as saying football players “don’t do anything that is considered rewarding” or that “all you have to be able to do is run and catch balls.” These statements weaken your credibility because readers know the profession requires rare skill, training, and risk. You can still argue that salaries are too high without dismissing athletic labor entirely.

Use evidence instead of generalizations
Your essay would be much more persuasive if you included facts, statistics, or examples from reliable sources. Right now, statements such as “A professional football player makes more money in one year than a doctor does in a lifetime” are likely inaccurate or at least need proof.

Consider adding:
Average NFL salary compared with average salaries in other professions
Examples of leagues that use salary caps and how those caps affect competition
Data on stadium spending, ticket prices, or team revenues
Information about labor strikes and their actual causes

Also be cautious with examples like O.J. Simpson. That example is emotionally charged and does not directly prove that high salaries cause injustice. Try to choose examples that more clearly connect to your claim.

Improve organization within paragraphs
Your overall structure is visible, but individual paragraphs often contain many different ideas at once. For revision, give each body paragraph one clear focus.

For example:
Paragraph 1: fairness and income inequality
Paragraph 2: effects of large salaries on player-owner labor disputes
Paragraph 3: fan experience and stadium investment
Paragraph 4: competitive balance between teams

Begin each paragraph with a topic sentence, then develop that one point with evidence and explanation.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should consider the other side. For example, someone might say:
Players deserve high salaries because they generate huge revenue.
A salary cap may unfairly restrict workers’ earning power.
Owners, not players, may be the ones keeping too much money.

If you acknowledge these arguments and respond to them, your essay will seem more thoughtful and persuasive.

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, but parts of it become judgmental or insulting. Words such as “greedy,” “does nothing but entertain,” or comments about intelligence can alienate readers. Academic argument is usually more effective when it sounds fair, measured, and reasoned.

Also, some terms are outdated or inappropriate, such as “mentally retarded.” Use respectful, current language.

Grammar and editing issues
There are frequent grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors that distract from your ideas. Some examples:
“wee” → “we”
“affective” → “effective”
“use to be” → “used to be”
“ant other team” → “any other team”

There are also sentence-level issues:
Repetition: “The one reason why they go on strike is because of the money.”
Informal phrasing: “So. why don't we stop giving them so much money.”
Awkward constructions: “which are much more important”

As you revise, proofread carefully for:
spelling
punctuation
subject-verb agreement
word choice
sentence clarity

Suggested next step
Try rewriting your thesis to make it more precise. For example:

“Professional football should have a salary cap because it would promote competitive balance among teams, reduce damaging contract disputes, and encourage more responsible use of league revenue.”

That thesis is more focused and easier to support with evidence.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong opinion and clear direction, which is a good foundation. The next step is to make the argument more credible by using evidence, avoiding overstatement, improving paragraph focus, and adopting a more balanced tone. If you revise with those goals in mind, your paper will become much more persuasive.

Your essay presents a clear pro–gun control position and attempts to support it with constitutional reasoning, statistics, and policy proposals. Main strengths

Conclusion with purpose: The final paragraph gives the paper a strong sense of urgency and civic importance.

Most important revision priority: sharpen the thesis

Right now, your essay begins with a claim, but the overall thesis could be more precise and arguable. Your main point seems to be:

Gun availability increases violence and death.
Gun control is constitutional.
Federal measures such as registration, licensing, background checks, and waiting periods would reduce harm.

Put these ideas into one focused thesis at the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Because easy access to firearms contributes to homicide, suicide, and accidental death, the federal government should adopt constitutional gun control measures such as universal background checks, waiting periods, licensing, and registration.*

This gives your paper a clear roadmap.

Organization needs improvement

The essay currently reads more like a collection of source-based points than a fully structured argument. Some paragraphs repeat similar ideas, and the order is not always logical.

Suggested structure

Body Paragraph 1: Constitutional argument—why gun control is legal.
Body Paragraph 2: Evidence that widespread gun access increases harm.
Body Paragraph 3: Why opposing claims are weaker or less supported.
Body Paragraph 4: Specific policies that could help.
Body Paragraph 5: Why federal action is needed instead of only state/local laws.
Conclusion: Restate argument and emphasize significance.

Also, use stronger topic sentences so each paragraph has one main point.

Work on source integration and citation

A major issue is that many paragraphs rely heavily on quotations or paraphrases without enough of your own analysis. After giving a fact or quotation, explain:
why it matters,
how it supports your thesis,
and what conclusion the reader should draw.

For example, after statistics about deaths and injuries, explain that these numbers demonstrate not isolated incidents but a large-scale public safety problem.

You also need to improve citation consistency and accuracy:
Some source names are spelled differently: “Cozic,” “Cosec,” “Cole,” “CUF.”
Quotations sometimes have punctuation errors or formatting problems.
A few page citations are unclear.

Make sure all sources are cited in one consistent style required by your class.

Be careful with factual accuracy

This is one of the most important concerns. Some legal claims in the essay are presented too absolutely, such as the statement that the Supreme Court ruled the Second Amendment protects only state militias. That may reflect older sources, but constitutional interpretation has changed over time. In an argumentative essay, legal claims must be current and precise.

Similarly, some statistics may be outdated. Because gun policy is a controversial topic, readers will question unsupported or old evidence. Revise by:
checking the date of every source,
replacing old numbers with recent data,
and avoiding broad claims unless you can fully support them.

Address the opposing side more fairly

You mention pro-gun arguments, but often dismiss them too quickly. A stronger argumentative essay should acknowledge counterarguments honestly and then respond to them with evidence.

For example, you could address:
self-defense arguments,
concerns that criminals ignore laws,
fears that regulation leads to confiscation.

Then explain why your proposed regulations are still reasonable and limited. This will make your argument more persuasive and academically credible.

Style and sentence-level issues

There are many grammar, spelling, and formatting problems that weaken your credibility. Examples include:
“lnl981” instead of “In 1981”
“W'hiteHousePresssecretarywasshot”
“definatly” instead of “definitely”
“riffles” instead of “rifles”
“licencing” should be “licensing” in American English
“prevent identification” likely should be “present identification”

Also watch:
unnecessary capitalization (“Gun Control,” “Federal Legislation”),
comma spacing,
quotation punctuation,
and awkward phrasing such as “the ant-gun control activists.”

Read the paper aloud slowly or use spell-check and peer review to catch these issues.

Avoid overstatement

Some sentences are too sweeping, such as “Law-abiding citizens do not need handguns.” That kind of claim may alienate readers unless it is carefully qualified. Academic argument is usually stronger when it is precise:
“Many law-abiding citizens rarely use handguns for self-defense”
is more defensible than
“Law-abiding citizens do not need handguns.”

Final revision goals

For your next draft, focus on these steps:

Write a stronger, unified thesis.
Reorganize the essay around 4–5 clear main points.
Update and verify legal and statistical evidence.
Add more of your own explanation after each source.
Address counterarguments fairly.
Correct spelling, grammar, and citation errors.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position, strong personal conviction, and a willingness to engage a difficult issue. You also attempt to acknowledge exceptions, which is important in argumentative writing. To revise effectively, focus on tone, logic, organization, evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengths
Clear thesis: You argue that abortion should be strictly regulated.
Strong engagement with the issue: Your opinion is clear and consistent.
Use of personal example: The story about your cousin gives the essay emotional force.
Recognition of complexity: You allow exceptions for rape and danger to the mother’s life, which shows you understand the issue is not simple.

Main Areas to Revise

A. Improve your tone
At several points, your language becomes emotionally charged or insulting, which weakens your credibility. Phrases such as “play the game,” “horseplay,” “wild maniac,” “vicious sperm,” and “kill it” sound more like personal anger than reasoned argument. Academic argumentative writing is strongest when it sounds controlled, fair, and respectful.

Suggestion: Replace emotionally loaded wording with neutral terms:
“consensual sex” instead of “play the game”
“terminate the pregnancy” instead of “kill it”
“rape survivor” instead of highly graphic descriptions

A calmer tone will make readers more likely to take your argument seriously.

B. Strengthen your logic
Some of your claims are too absolute or unsupported. For example:
“a person has no excuse for wanting an abortion after consensual sex”
women who have abortions after consensual sex “should be put into jail for murder”

These are major legal and moral claims, but the essay does not fully explain or defend them. Also, your argument has some tension: if abortion is murder, why is it acceptable in cases of rape? That suggests the moral status of the fetus is being treated differently depending on the circumstances of conception. You should address this contradiction directly.

Suggestion: Clarify your reasoning. Ask yourself:
Is your argument primarily about personal responsibility?
Or about the fetus’s right to life?
If it is about the fetus’s right to life, how do you justify exceptions?
If it is about responsibility, how do you address contraceptive failure?

Your essay will be stronger if your central principle is consistent.

C. Use stronger evidence
You include contraceptive statistics and claims about welfare and abortion harm, but you do not cite any sources. In academic writing, unsupported statistics reduce credibility. The anecdote about your cousin is powerful, but one personal example cannot prove that abortion generally causes infertility or that policy should be based on that case alone.

Suggestion: Add reliable sources for:
contraceptive effectiveness
medical risks of pregnancy and abortion
adoption and welfare systems
legal and ethical debates about abortion

Use evidence from medical organizations, legal sources, or scholarly studies.

D. Organize the essay more clearly
Right now, the essay moves between points without a strong structure. A clearer organization would help readers follow your argument.

Possible structure for revision:
Introduction – present the issue and your thesis
Argument 1: Personal responsibility and contraception
Argument 2: Moral objection to abortion
Counterargument and exceptions – rape and danger to the mother
Personal example – your cousin, used carefully
Conclusion – restate your position

Add topic sentences to each paragraph so the reader knows the paragraph’s main idea.

Sentence-Level Issues

Your draft contains many grammar and mechanics errors that make the argument harder to follow. Common problems include:
subject-verb agreement: “women” not “women consents”
missing words: “is said to be ninety-five percent effective”
pronoun confusion: switching between “person,” “woman,” “they,” and “she”
run-on sentences
word choice errors: “case and point” should be “case in point”
spelling/typing errors: “govermnent,” “prevent if,” “up upstate,” “he family doctor”

Example of revision
Original:  
“Why can't these women just carry out the pregnancy and put the child up for adoption.”

Revised:  
“One alternative to abortion, in cases where the pregnancy does not threaten the mother’s life, is carrying the pregnancy to term and placing the child for adoption.”

The revised sentence is more formal and appropriate for academic writing.

Be careful with generalizations
Some statements assume that all women have the same motives or circumstances. For example, not everyone has equal access to contraception, health care, adoption support, or financial assistance. Strong arguments acknowledge complications rather than oversimplifying them.

Suggestion: Replace sweeping claims with more precise ones:
“In many cases…”  
“Some argue…”  
“Although contraception reduces risk…”

Final Advice for Revision
When you rewrite, aim for this:
state your position clearly
use respectful, academic language
make your reasoning consistent
support claims with credible evidence
organize each paragraph around one main point
edit carefully for grammar and clarity

You have the basis of a strong argument because your essay shows conviction and personal investment. Your next step is to turn that conviction into a more disciplined, evidence-based, and logically consistent academic argument.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of purpose: you argue that media overuses sex and that this affects teenagers’ attitudes and behavior. Your essay also shows good energy and conviction, which are important in argumentative writing. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, organizing your ideas more logically, and strengthening your evidence and style.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Right now, your main claim is understandable, but it shifts slightly. Sometimes you argue that sex is “overrated” in the media; other times you argue that media portrayals contribute to teen pregnancy and STDs; elsewhere you call for more “decent” programming. These are related, but not identical.

Try revising your thesis so it makes one precise argument. For example:

Possible thesis:  
*The media overuses sexual content in television, music, and advertising, and because these portrayals often ignore responsibility and consequences, they can give teenagers a misleading view of sex.*

This gives you two clear parts to prove:
sexual content is widespread in media, and  
those portrayals are misleading because they omit responsibility.

Improve organization
Your essay has examples from television, radio, and magazines, which is a good structure. However, the argument becomes repetitive because several paragraphs make the same point: sex is everywhere. Then later you move to the effects on teenagers. Consider organizing the essay into clearer sections:

Suggested structure
Introduction: Present the issue and your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Television examples.
Body paragraph 2: Music/radio examples.
Body paragraph 3: Advertising/magazine examples.
Body paragraph 4: Explain how these portrayals may affect teenagers, especially when safe sex and consequences are not shown.
Conclusion: Restate the argument and suggest what should change.

Also, use topic sentences that directly connect each paragraph to the thesis. For instance:  
*Television normalizes casual sex by making it central to popular shows while rarely showing consequences.*

Use stronger evidence
Your essay relies heavily on personal observation: “I can’t remember a time…” and “Every time I turn on the radio…” This gives your voice personality, but in academic argument, readers also expect evidence beyond personal experience.

To strengthen revision, you could:
cite studies about media influence on teen attitudes toward sex,
refer to statistics about the amount of sexual content in TV/music/advertising,
mention expert opinions from psychologists, educators, or public health researchers.

Even if you keep some personal examples, balance them with broader support. Also, when using examples like *Beverly Hills, 90210* or song titles, explain exactly how they support your point.

Develop the cause-and-effect reasoning carefully
Your essay suggests that sexual media portrayals lead teens to unsafe sex. This is an important claim, but it is a big one. Right now, the connection is asserted more than demonstrated.

Be careful not to overstate the case. For example, instead of saying media is the reason teens get pregnant or contract STDs, you might argue that media is one contributing influence, especially when it glamorizes sex without showing communication, contraception, or consequences.

That makes your argument more credible and nuanced.

Tone and word choice
Your voice is passionate, but some phrases sound too informal or judgmental for an academic essay, such as:
“everyone sleeps with everyone”
“half dressed body”
“that’s not reality”

You can keep your strong position while using more formal language. For example:
“Popular shows often depict frequent casual sexual relationships.”
“Advertisements use sexually suggestive imagery unrelated to the product.”
“These portrayals do not reflect the responsibilities that real sexual relationships require.”

Also, avoid rhetorical overstatements like “every time” or “almost every magazine I have ever read” unless you can prove them.

Sentence-level issues to revise
There are many grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors that distract from your ideas. A few patterns to fix:

Capitalization: “Sex Is used to do everything” → “Sex is used to sell everything.”
Spelling: “says,” “featuring,” “soaked,” “your” vs. “you’re,” “its” vs. “it’s.”
Sentence clarity: Some sentences are awkward or run-on, such as  
  “Then again some songs don't even have to be listened to, to realize they are talking about sex.”  
  This could become:  
  “Some songs make their subject obvious even before listeners hear the lyrics.”
Quotation and citation format: Song titles and slogans should be presented consistently and accurately.

Read the paper aloud during revision; this will help you catch unclear phrasing and repeated wording.

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points, but it could be more effective if it briefly summarizes your argument and leaves the reader with a stronger final thought. Instead of repeating that sex is “greatly overrated,” explain why this matters socially.

For example:  
*If media continue to present sex as glamorous while ignoring responsibility, they risk teaching young audiences that desire matters more than communication, consent, and protection.*

Final advice
Your essay has a clear argument, relevant examples, and strong concern for an important issue. The biggest improvements will come from:
creating a more focused thesis,  
organizing the essay around distinct claims,  
using more credible evidence, and  
revising for grammar and academic tone.

You already have the foundation of a persuasive argument. Now your task is to make it more precise, supported, and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting argument: clothing is not superficial, but deeply connected to identity, social judgment, conformity, and self-perception. You also use a wide range of sources, which shows serious effort and research. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your main claim, organizing your ideas more logically, and improving style and source integration.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, the essay’s main point is broad and somewhat descriptive. You discuss many things clothing does—signals identity, shapes self-image, affects adolescents, influences consumers, relates to modesty—but the essay does not clearly state what exact argument you want to prove.

A stronger thesis might be something like:

“Clothing influences both how others judge us and how we understand ourselves; this influence is especially powerful because fashion shapes social belonging, self-confidence, and conformity across different stages of life.”

This kind of thesis gives the paper a clear direction and helps you decide what belongs in each paragraph.

Improve organization
The essay currently reads more like a series of source-based observations than a unified argument. Consider reorganizing by major claims rather than moving source to source.

A possible structure:

Introduction
   - Explain the importance of clothing in social life
   - Present your thesis

Clothing shapes first impressions
   - How people judge others by dress
   - Limits/dangers of these judgments

Clothing affects self-image
   - How dress influences confidence and identity
   - Relationship between appearance and self-concept

Social pressure and conformity
   - Adolescents and peer approval
   - Consumer behavior and trends
   - Dress codes/uniforms

Clothing across the life span
   - Elderly people and continued concern with appearance
   - Broader point that fashion matters at all ages

Modesty and cultural values
   - Use this section only if you connect it clearly to your thesis

Conclusion
   - Restate the argument
   - Emphasize why clothing matters psychologically and socially

This structure would make the essay easier to follow.

Integrate sources more effectively
You rely heavily on quotations and paraphrases, sometimes listing ideas from sources without explaining them. Your reader needs more of your voice connecting the evidence to the argument.

For example, after quoting Herrick’s poem, explain:
Why is this poem relevant?
How does it support your point about “unstable fashion identities” or self-expression?
Does it show that disorder in dress can be attractive because it signals personality?

Also, be careful not to let quotations substitute for analysis. Every quote should be followed by commentary that explains its significance.

Use a more academic tone
Some wording is too casual, overly general, or inappropriate for an academic essay. For example:
“slut” and “pimp” are inflammatory and weaken your credibility.
“If she wears a mini-skirt, she probably feel sexy” is both grammatically incorrect and too speculative.
“this is where fashion designers feed” should probably be “capitalize on.”

Try replacing judgmental or sweeping claims with more precise language:
Instead of “One may judge them to be a slut,” say: “People often make quick moral or social judgments based on clothing.”

Avoid overgeneralization
Many claims are stated as universal truths when they are really tendencies:
“one can normally gather any of these characteristics from the way a person dresses”
“If the majority doesn’t approve, then that style generally is not worn”

These need qualification. Clothing can influence perception, but appearances are not always reliable. A stronger essay acknowledges complexity:
people use clothes to communicate identity,
but observers can misread those signals,
and fashion meanings vary across cultures, classes, genders, and time periods.

Adding nuance will strengthen the argument.

Revise sentence-level errors
There are frequent grammar, usage, capitalization, and punctuation problems. A few patterns to fix:

Subject-verb agreement:  
  “that person feel” → “that person feels”  
  “Clothing has strong affect” → “Clothing has a strong effect”

Word choice:  
  “indicator on” → “indicator of”  
  “fell self-pity” → “feel self-pity”

Capitalization:  
  “one doesn’t know” after a period should begin with One  
  “older men and women” should begin with Older if starting a sentence

Run-on sentences:  
  Several sentences are too long and need to be divided for clarity.

Pronoun consistency:  
  You shift between “one,” “he/she,” “she,” and “they.” Choose a consistent, modern approach, such as plural nouns with “they.”

Reconsider the conclusion
Your conclusion becomes broad and informal, especially in the final lines about “the excitement of walking down the street.” Instead, end by reinforcing your argument:

Clothing is a social language.
It influences identity and judgment.
Because of that, fashion deserves serious psychological and cultural attention.

Revision priorities
Write a clearer, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around major claims.
Add more of your own analysis after source material.
Replace informal or judgmental language with precise academic wording.
Edit carefully for grammar and clarity.

You have a strong topic and plenty of evidence. The next step is turning the paper from a collection of observations into a focused argument with clear structure and polished style.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: abortion decisions should be left to the woman. That clarity is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. You also attempt to engage opposing arguments, which is important in persuasive writing. For revision, focus on strengthening your thesis, organizing your reasoning more logically, and improving sentence-level clarity and correctness.

Main strengths
Clear stance: Your position is consistent throughout the essay.
Engagement with counterarguments: You mention anti-abortion arguments and try to respond to them.
Sense of urgency: You convey that this issue matters because it affects people’s lives and choices.

Thesis and argument
Your thesis appears in this sentence: “the decision to have an abortion or not should be left entirely up to the woman.” This is a workable claim, but it needs a stronger explanation of why. Right now, your reasons are present but underdeveloped.

Try revising the thesis so it includes your main supporting points. For example:

Possible revision:  
*Abortion should remain a legal choice because bodily autonomy belongs to the pregnant woman, because pregnancies caused by rape or other harmful circumstances require individual judgment, and because banning abortion can lead to dangerous outcomes for women and children.*

This version gives your essay a roadmap.

Organization
Your essay would be more effective with a clearer structure:
Introduction: introduce the issue and present your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain bodily autonomy and personal decision-making.
Body paragraph 2: discuss special circumstances such as rape.
Body paragraph 3: address and rebut a counterargument.
Conclusion: restate the argument and explain why it matters.

Right now, some paragraphs are difficult to follow because ideas are incomplete or jump suddenly. For instance, the paragraph beginning “Although if you analyze many of these arguments…” is confusing because the first sentence is incomplete and the example that follows is not clearly introduced.

Development and evidence
Your essay makes several strong claims, but many are not supported with evidence. In academic argument, readers will expect facts, examples, or sources.

Examples of claims that need support:
“Rape is something that the woman has no control over…”
“there would be higher death rates from self-induced abortions…”
“If a child is born unwanted, he is automatically psychologically damaged for life…”

Be careful with absolute statements like “automatically psychologically damaged for life.” That is too broad and may weaken your credibility. Instead, use more careful wording:

Stronger version:  
*Children born into unsupported or unwanted circumstances may face greater emotional and social challenges.*

Also, if you refer to historical comparisons such as the Nazi/genocide argument, explain where that argument comes from and respond to it more precisely.

Use of counterarguments
You are right to include opposing views, but represent them fairly and accurately. At times, your essay dismisses opponents too quickly by saying their arguments are “not substantial” or “unrealistic.” Instead of attacking the people, focus on the reasoning.

For example, rather than writing:
*“Views like this by prolifers make their whole argument seem very unrealistic…”*

Try:
*“This argument is less convincing because it does not account for pregnancies resulting from rape or other coercive circumstances.”*

This keeps the tone more academic and persuasive.

Style and clarity
Many sentences need revision for grammar, punctuation, and wording. Some examples:

“These debates began over two decades ago”  
  This is inaccurate; abortion debates have existed much longer.
“The two names for this categories”  
  Should be: *“The names for these two categories…”*
“pro-choiceor”  
  Add a space: *“pro-choice or”*
“its hard”  
  Should be: *“it’s hard”*
“some anti-abortionist”  
  Should be plural: *“some anti-abortionists”*
“even woman women who have abortions”  
  This appears to be an editing error and must be corrected.
“there tendencies”  
  Should be: *“their tendencies”*
“true meaning of ,-genocide”  
  Remove stray punctuation.

Read the draft aloud during revision. That will help you catch incomplete or awkward sentences.

Tone and word choice
Aim for more formal academic language:
Replace “proffers” with “pro-lifers” if that is what you mean.
Instead of “who really obtains enough knowledge”, say “who has the authority or understanding to judge the situation.”

Also, avoid rhetorical questions too often. One or two can be effective, but too many can make the essay sound less formal.

Priorities for revision
Rewrite the thesis to include clear reasons.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each paragraph has one main point.
Add evidence or examples to support major claims.
Revise counterarguments fairly and logically.
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and wording.

Final encouragement
You have a clear opinion and a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. The next step is to turn that conviction into a more structured, evidence-based academic argument. If you revise for clarity, support, and organization, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear concern and a strong sense of purpose: you want to argue that television exposes children to sexual content, violence, and profanity too often, and that this harms their development. Your essay also benefits from a passionate voice and from concrete examples drawn from everyday life. That gives the paper urgency. For revision, the main work is to strengthen your thesis, organization, evidence, and tone, while also correcting sentence-level issues.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Right now, your main claim appears in several forms, but it is not stated as one precise argument early in the essay. A stronger thesis would help unify everything that follows.

For example, you might revise your thesis into something like:

“Because children are often exposed to adult television content after school and at home without supervision, television networks should provide more child-appropriate programming during those hours, and parents should more carefully monitor what children watch.”

This works better because it:
identifies the problem,
explains why it matters,
and suggests a solution.

Improve organization
The essay has many related ideas, but they are repeated and sometimes presented in a loose order. Consider organizing the paper into clear sections:

Introduction
   - Introduce the issue
   - Present your thesis

Why children are exposed
   - Parents are busy
   - Children have access to TVs in bedrooms
   - After-school hours are a vulnerable time

Why this exposure is harmful
   - Children imitate behavior
   - Children misunderstand what they see
   - Sexual content, profanity, and violence may influence attitudes

Examples
   - Specific shows or cartoons
   - Personal examples from your children

Solutions
   - More child-friendly programming after school
   - More parental supervision
   - Better ratings/warnings

Conclusion
   - Restate the argument
   - End with a strong final point

This structure will prevent repetition and make your argument easier to follow.

Use stronger evidence
A major weakness in the essay is that many claims are unsupported or too absolute. For instance:
“Approximately ninety eight percent of television programs are not for children.”
“That only leaves a small two percent…”
“Children… once interested then wants to experiment.”

These are big claims, but you do not cite evidence. In an academic argumentative essay, readers will expect research, statistics from reliable sources, or expert opinions. Try to add:
studies about children’s media exposure,
research on imitation or social learning,
evidence about children’s understanding of sexual content,
data on television scheduling or ratings.

Also, be careful with exact percentages unless you can document them.

Balance emotional appeal with fairness
Your essay is persuasive because it shows concern for children, but some statements sound overly broad or one-sided. For example, saying children will see a show and then “think it’s all right to have sex and get pregnant at an early age” may feel too simplistic to readers.

Try using more measured language:
instead of “television makes children want to experiment,” say “television may encourage curiosity or imitation in children who are too young to understand what they see.”
instead of “approximately ninety eight percent,” say “much of daytime and evening programming is aimed at adults rather than young children.”

This makes your argument sound more credible and academic.

Reconsider some examples and wording
Your personal examples are useful, especially the story about your daughter assuming that kissing means marriage. That example effectively shows how children can misunderstand adult behavior. Keep examples like that.

However, some examples need clearer explanation. When you mention specific programs such as *Ricki Lake*, *Oprah*, or *The Simpsons*, explain exactly how they support your point. Avoid assuming all readers already agree.

Also, be careful with phrasing around topics such as homosexuality. That paragraph may come across as prejudiced rather than analytical. If you want to discuss difficult subjects children may not understand, frame the issue more broadly: young children may struggle to interpret complex adult relationships presented on television.

Revise sentence clarity, grammar, and mechanics
There are many sentence-level problems that make the essay harder to read:
incorrect word forms (“suppose” instead of “supposed”)
spelling issues (“women” for “woman,” “some where” for “somewhere”)
punctuation errors
repeated words (“the the”)
awkward phrasing (“make the children to want to experiment”)

You also have several very long sentences that should be divided. Read the essay aloud and look for places where you naturally pause. Those may need periods or commas.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion introduces ideas already mentioned, but it should more forcefully bring the essay together. A good conclusion should:
restate the thesis,
summarize the key harms,
emphasize solutions.

For example: children need protection not only from explicit content but also from confusion and imitation; therefore both networks and parents share responsibility.

Final advice
Your strongest asset is your clear concern for children’s well-being. Now your goal is to turn that concern into a more structured and evidence-based argument. Focus on:
one clear thesis,
better paragraph organization,
fewer repeated points,
more credible evidence,
more careful wording,
and thorough proofreading.

With those revisions, your essay can become much more persuasive and academically effective.
