Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay shows clear commitment to an important topic, and one of its strengths is the wide range of recyclable materials you discuss. You also try to persuade readers by combining practical advice with environmental consequences, which is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your main argument, improving organization, strengthening evidence, and polishing sentence-level issues.

Clarify and narrow your thesis
Right now, your essay argues broadly that recycling should become common, but the exact claim shifts. At times you focus on saving landfill space, at others on pollution reduction, energy conservation, or proper disposal of hazardous items. These are all related, but the essay needs one central argument.

A stronger thesis might be:
“Recycling should become a routine practice because it reduces landfill waste, conserves natural resources, and prevents hazardous materials from damaging human and environmental health.”

This thesis gives you three clear body sections and helps readers follow your reasoning.

Improve organization
The essay currently reads more like a list of recyclable items than a structured argument. Try grouping ideas into larger categories instead of discussing one material after another without transitions.

A possible structure:
Introduction: Present the issue and your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Landfill reduction  
   Explain how recycling reduces pressure on limited landfill space.
Body Paragraph 2: Conservation of resources and energy  
   Discuss plastic, glass, paper, steel, and energy savings.
Body Paragraph 3: Public health and hazardous waste  
   Discuss refrigerators, batteries, motor oil, and household toxics.
Conclusion: Reinforce why recycling should be a routine responsibility.

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence and ends by connecting back to the thesis.

Strengthen your use of evidence
You include many facts and quotations, which is good, but the essay often drops in evidence without enough explanation. After each quotation or statistic, explain:
what it means,
why it matters,
how it supports your argument.

For example, after the statistic about landfill space saved by recycled paper, explain how this demonstrates that individual actions can have measurable environmental impact.

Also, some sources seem outdated or incomplete, and some claims are too sweeping. For example:
“Plastic bags are much better for the environment than paper bags” is a controversial claim and needs careful qualification.
The reference to the Twin Towers suggests the essay relies on older material.
“Within fifteen years, eighty percent of the existing landfills will be full” sounds dramatic, but it needs current and credible support.

When revising, use recent, reliable sources and cite them consistently.

Address complexity and counterarguments
Because this is an argumentative essay, it would be stronger if you acknowledged that recycling is not always simple. For example:
not all plastics are recyclable everywhere,
recycling systems vary by city,
reducing and reusing are sometimes better than recycling.

You could briefly mention a counterargument such as:
“Some critics argue that recycling is inconvenient or ineffective, but the small effort required from individuals is outweighed by the environmental and public health benefits.”

This makes your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Improve style and tone
Your tone is passionate, which helps persuasion, but some sentences are overly dramatic or imprecise. For example:
“A major uproar has arisen” sounds vague.
“the world would not be in danger of overfilling the landfills” is awkward.
“a few more minutes of one person’s time can give the [earth] countless years” is emotionally strong but needs clearer wording.

Aim for a more formal academic tone. Replace emotional phrasing with precise claims.

Revise for grammar, mechanics, and clarity
There are frequent grammar and editing errors that distract from your ideas. Pay special attention to:
sentence fragments
run-on sentences
punctuation around quotations
word choice
typographical errors

Examples:
“one of which being recycling” → “one of which is recycling”
“The human population needs to educate themselves” → “People need to educate themselves” or “The public needs to educate itself”
“Batteries are categorized in rechargeable and non-rechargeable” → “Batteries are categorized as rechargeable and non-rechargeable”
“eighty percent of the existing landfills will be fun” → likely “full”
“light bulb” is misspelled as “fight bulb”
Some quotation formatting and apostrophes appear inconsistent.

Read your draft aloud slowly, or have someone else read it, to catch unclear or awkward sentences.

Focus your conclusion
Your conclusion should not introduce new ideas such as future trash-bin systems. Instead, briefly restate your main claim and summarize your strongest reasons. End with a concise final statement about why recycling is a necessary public habit, not just a personal choice.

Final thought
You have a strong topic, plenty of material, and clear concern for the environment. Your next step is to turn this from an informative list into a focused, well-organized argument. If you revise around a sharper thesis, stronger paragraph structure, and cleaner sentence-level writing, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a recognizable argument: society stereotypes pink as feminine and blue as masculine, especially through advertising and products for babies and women. You use several familiar examples, which helps readers understand your point. To revise successfully, focus on making your argument more precise, better organized, and more polished at the sentence level.

Strengthen your main argument
Right now, the paper mostly describes examples of pink and blue stereotypes. To become a stronger argumentative essay, it should make a clearer claim about why this matters.

For example, your thesis could go beyond “pink is associated with women” and argue something like:

*American advertising reinforces rigid gender stereotypes by linking pink with femininity and blue with masculinity, especially in products for women and babies.*
*Color-coding in advertising and consumer products teaches people to associate gender with specific colors from infancy.*

A stronger thesis will help the essay feel less repetitive and more analytical.

Improve organization
Your essay has several useful examples, but the ideas sometimes repeat rather than build. Try organizing the essay into clear sections:

Possible structure
Introduction
Introduce the topic of gendered colors
End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Advertising aimed at women
Skintimate shaving gel
Secret deodorant
Feminine product packaging
Explain how these examples reinforce femininity through color

Body Paragraph 2: Babies and early gender identification
Pink clothing for girls
Blue clothing for boys
Social pressure on parents to signal a baby’s gender

Body Paragraph 3: Baby products and commercialization
Huggies for girls/boys
Gerber bottles, dishes, brushes, combs
Explain how businesses profit from gendered stereotypes

Conclusion
Restate the argument
Explain the broader effect of these stereotypes on society

This structure will help readers follow your reasoning more easily.

Add analysis, not just examples
Many of your paragraphs list examples, but they need more explanation of what those examples mean. After each example, ask yourself:

What does this example show?
How does it support my thesis?
Why is this stereotype important or harmful?

For instance, after discussing Huggies, you could explain that gendered diapers suggest that even babies are expected to fit social ideas of masculinity and femininity before they can express identity themselves.

Work on style and tone
Some wording is too informal or repetitive for an academic essay. For example:

“of course” sounds conversational
“you would not catch a baby boy with pink on” is informal
“get away with it” could be replaced with more precise language

Try using a more formal style, such as:
*It is uncommon for baby boys to be dressed in pink because the color is widely coded as feminine.*
*These expectations limit how males and females are socially perceived.*

Also, avoid repeating the same idea in nearly the same words. For example, you say several times that pink is feminine and blue is masculine. State that clearly once, then develop it with new insights.

Revise grammar, punctuation, and sentence clarity
There are many sentence-level errors that make the essay harder to read. Some common issues:

Capitalization
“on the Secret deodorant commercial” → In the Secret deodorant commercial
“once a baby has developed its looks” → Once a baby has developed distinct features
“of course” at the beginning of a sentence should be capitalized

Sentence fragments / punctuation
“Why? because it is a very soft color...”  
  Better: Why? Because it is seen as a soft color, and women are often stereotyped as soft and tender.

Word choice
“pictured as being” → portrayed as
“brought from the hospital” → brought home from the hospital
“its looks” for a baby sounds awkward

Agreement and mechanics
“males can not wear pink” → males cannot wear pink
“jam referring to babies” seems to be a typo; likely I am referring to babies

Be careful with broad generalizations
Your essay relies on statements like “commercials always show this stereotype” and “baby girls are usually dressed in pink.” These ideas may often be true, but words like always can weaken your credibility if they are too absolute. Use more precise language:

often
frequently
commonly
in many advertisements

This will make your argument sound more thoughtful and accurate.

Consider a deeper conclusion
Your conclusion now mostly repeats earlier points. Try ending with a larger point about the effects of these stereotypes. For example, you might argue that color-coding influences how children are treated from birth and shapes expectations about gender roles.

Final revision priorities
Write a stronger thesis with a clear argument.
Reorganize paragraphs by topic.
Add analysis after each example.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Correct grammar, capitalization, and punctuation errors.

You have a solid topic and recognizable examples. With clearer structure and deeper analysis, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that drug legalization would benefit the United States. That strong stance is a good starting point for an argumentative essay. You also identify several possible benefits—reduced crime, lower prison populations, tax revenue, and public health improvements—which gives your paper a broad foundation. For revision, the main priorities are clarifying your argument, improving organization, and strengthening evidence.

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction shows your opinion clearly, but the thesis is currently too broad and informal. It also shifts between emotional language and argument.

Current idea:
legalization would save money and help America focus on more important things

Revise toward a thesis that previews your main reasons:
Drug legalization should be adopted in the United States because it would reduce black-market crime, lower incarceration for nonviolent offenders, generate tax revenue, and allow drug abuse to be treated as a public health issue.

This version is more specific and gives your essay a roadmap.

Improve organization and paragraph structure
Right now, many paragraphs begin with a claim but do not fully explain it. Some also contain incomplete sentences marked with , which interrupt the paper’s logic. Each body paragraph should follow a clear pattern:

Topic sentence stating one reason
Explanation of how or why this reason supports legalization
Evidence from a credible source
Analysis connecting the evidence back to your claim

For example, your paragraph on crime could be reorganized like this:
Topic sentence: Legalization could reduce crime by weakening illegal drug markets.
Explanation: When drugs are illegal, high prices and black-market profits encourage theft, gang activity, and street dealing.
Evidence: Add a source with statistics or expert analysis.
Analysis: Explain how removing black-market profits could reduce incentives for crime.

Use stronger evidence
The essay makes many large claims, but most are not supported with reliable evidence. For example:
“drug prices would decrease”
“crime would decrease”
“legalizing heroin, cocaine, and marijuana would produce a reduction in the use of tobacco and alcohol”

These are major claims that need sources. Also, some statements seem too absolute or speculative, such as “saving thousands of lives every year, maybe even more.” Avoid guessing. Instead, cite research, government reports, or academic studies.

You quote Duke once in the introduction, but the rest of the essay needs more source support. Make sure each major argument includes evidence.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the other side. Your paper assumes legalization would only have benefits, but many readers will wonder about possible harms, such as increased use, addiction, or impaired driving. Add a paragraph that fairly presents a counterargument and then responds to it.

For example:
Some critics argue that legalization would increase drug use.
However, supporters of legalization contend that regulation, age restrictions, quality control, and treatment programs could reduce harm more effectively than prohibition.

This will make your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Clarify confusing or inaccurate passages
Several sentences are hard to understand or incomplete. For instance:
“If drugs were to increase by a factor of two or three, the deaths and diseases caused by such drug use would be much lower…”
“Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers.”

These ideas need to be rewritten clearly. Also, check whether the content is accurate. If you mean that regulated access could reduce overdose deaths or contamination, say that directly.

Be careful with logic as well. Sometimes the essay repeats points without fully developing them. For example, you mention lower prices in multiple paragraphs, but you should explain the link between price, addiction, and crime more carefully.

Revise tone and style
Some wording is too informal or emotionally loaded for an academic essay. Examples:
“it’s time we stop wasting money”
“come to reality”
“big money”
“hooked up in the drug business”

Use a more formal tone:
“reconsider current policy”
“recognize”
“substantial profits”
“become involved in drug trafficking”

Also avoid sweeping statements like “Americans as a whole” unless you have evidence.

Edit grammar and mechanics
Your draft needs careful proofreading. Watch for:
punctuation errors
spacing problems (“it' s”)
sentence fragments
awkward phrasing
subject-verb agreement
word choice (“beneficiary” should be beneficial)
spelling (“imprisonmemt”)

Example revision:
“If drugs were legal America would not be wasting money on keeping them illegal.”
Better: If drugs were legalized, the government could reduce spending on enforcement and redirect funds toward treatment, education, and social services.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion restates your opinion, but it should do more than repeat earlier points. A stronger conclusion should:
restate the thesis in different words
summarize the main reasons
leave the reader with a final insight about policy, justice, or public health

Revision priorities
Replace incomplete sections and unclear sentences.
Rewrite the thesis to be specific and arguable.
Reorganize body paragraphs around one clear claim each.
Add strong evidence and citations.
Include a counterargument.
Proofread for grammar, style, and correctness.

You have the foundation of a persuasive argument, but the next draft should focus on clarity, evidence, and structure. With these revisions, your essay will become much more convincing and academically effective.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay shows strong personal investment in the topic, and that passion gives the paper urgency. You clearly believe gun control is necessary, and you provide several concrete examples from South Carolina to support that claim. The use of local incidents and personal experience helps readers see why the issue matters. For revision, the main areas to strengthen are thesis clarity, organization, evidence, tone, and mechanics.

Thesis and Focus
Your main claim appears in the introduction:

> “Gun Control needs to implemented because it would mainly reduce crime, save lives and lessen the fears of innocent ones.”

This is a solid starting point, but it needs to be more precise and grammatically correct. Also, the essay shifts between discussing South Carolina specifically and gun control in general. Decide which argument you want to make:

State-level claim: South Carolina needs stricter gun laws.
National claim: The U.S. needs stricter gun control.

A stronger thesis might be:

“South Carolina needs stricter gun control laws because they could reduce violent crime, limit youth access to firearms, and improve public safety.”

This version is specific and gives you a clear structure for body paragraphs.

Organization
Right now, the essay relies on a series of examples, but the argument does not always build logically. Try organizing the body paragraphs by main reasons, not just by separate incidents.

For example:

Youth access to guns
   - Blackville-Hilda High School case  
Guns used in public crimes
   - Bank robbery in Branchville  
Personal and community harm
   - Mr. Glover’s killing and your uncle’s death  
Need for legal reform
   - Brady Bill, opposition, and proposed changes  

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects directly to your thesis.

Example:
“One reason South Carolina needs stricter gun control is that young people can gain access to firearms too easily.”

Use of Evidence
You do a good job bringing in news reports, personal recollection, and an interview. That variety is a strength. However, some evidence needs better explanation and more careful use.

What to improve:
Explain how each example proves your point. Don’t assume the reader will make the connection.
Some claims are too broad, such as “heinous crimes involving guns are at an all-time high.” You need statistics or a reliable source to support statements like that.
The statistic about hate crimes, robbery, and murder seems confusing and may not actually support your argument about general gun violence. Make sure your evidence is relevant and accurate.
Your personal examples are emotionally powerful, but academic arguments also need credible, broader evidence such as crime statistics, state laws, or research studies.

Ask yourself after each quotation:  
What does this show, and how does it support my claim about gun control?

Addressing Counterarguments
You briefly mention that some people oppose gun control, especially the NRA, but you do not fully engage with the opposing side. In argumentative writing, your essay becomes stronger when you acknowledge and respond to counterarguments fairly.

For example, some opponents argue:
Guns are needed for self-defense.
Criminals will get guns regardless of laws.
The Second Amendment protects gun ownership.

You do not need to agree, but you should respond thoughtfully. This will make your essay sound more balanced and persuasive.

Tone and Style
Your voice is sincere and forceful, which works well for argument. However, some phrases sound too informal or emotionally charged for academic writing, such as:
“people killing others over nonsense”
“create trouble”
“wrong reasons”
“use their heads instead of the trigger”

Try replacing these with more formal language. For instance:
“personal disputes”
“commit acts of violence”
“misuse firearms”
“act responsibly”

Also, avoid overgeneralizations such as “anybody can just walk in with a gun and commit a robbery.” That kind of statement weakens credibility.

Grammar, Spelling, and Mechanics
There are many sentence-level errors that need careful proofreading. Some common issues include:

Spelling: “goverment,” “techers,” “memebers,” “misue,” “acquaintace”
Grammar: “needs to implemented” should be “needs to be implemented”
Capitalization: “Gun Control” should not usually be capitalized in the middle of a sentence
Punctuation and spacing: Several quotations and citations are awkwardly punctuated
Sentence clarity: Some sentences are run-ons or are worded awkwardly

Example revision:
Original:  
“There are some segments of society that are not taking gun control seriously, in light of these obstacles, Gun Control needs to implemented…”

Revised:  
“Although some groups do not support stricter gun laws, gun control needs to be strengthened because it can reduce crime, save lives, and improve public safety.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion introduces a new idea—that only police and military should carry guns. Since this is a major claim, it should be developed earlier in the essay if you want to include it. Otherwise, the conclusion should mainly restate your thesis and summarize your main points rather than add new arguments.

Revision Priorities
Rewrite the thesis to make it specific and focused.  
Reorganize body paragraphs by reasons, not just examples.  
Add stronger explanation after each piece of evidence.  
Include at least one fair counterargument and rebuttal.  
Revise for formal tone and correct grammar/spelling.  

You have a strong foundation because your paper is passionate and rooted in real examples. With clearer structure and stronger evidence, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position against capital punishment, and one of its biggest strengths is that it is organized around three counterarguments: cost, retribution, and deterrence. That structure gives your paper a solid foundation. You also write with strong conviction, which helps communicate your stance clearly. For revision, the main areas to improve are tone, evidence, clarity, grammar, and precision.

Strengthen your thesis
Your introduction makes your position clear, but the thesis is buried under emotional language. Try to state your argument more directly and academically.

Current idea:
Capital punishment is irrational and has no place in the justice system.

A stronger thesis might be:
Capital punishment should be abolished because it is more costly than life imprisonment, promotes revenge rather than justice, and fails to deter murder.

This version is clearer, more focused, and previews your three main points.

Use a more academic tone
At times, your language is too emotional or conversational for an argumentative essay. Phrases such as:
“obscene form of punishment”
“ridiculous”
“Let me throw a couple of statistics at you”
“Correct me if I’m wrong”

make the paper sound informal and weaken your credibility. Academic arguments are usually more persuasive when they rely on calm reasoning rather than insults or sarcasm.

Instead, use neutral phrasing such as:
“Supporters argue…”
“This claim is not supported by available evidence…”
“A closer examination suggests…”

Improve evidence and citation
Your essay refers to “statistics” and “studies,” but you do not identify any sources. This is the biggest weakness in the paper. Readers need to know:
where the data came from,
when the study was conducted,
who conducted it,
and how reliable it is.

For example, instead of saying:
“Currently to administer capital punishment upon a single individual it costs five times the amount…”

you should say something like:
According to [source], death penalty cases cost significantly more than life imprisonment because of lengthy trials and appeals.

The same applies to your deterrence paragraph. You mention studies in states with the death penalty, but without naming them, the claim feels unsupported. Add credible sources from government reports, legal research, or academic studies.

Clarify and deepen your reasoning
Your body paragraphs have good main ideas, but some points need fuller explanation.

Cost paragraph
You correctly explain that appeals make death penalty cases expensive. To improve:
explain *why* death penalty trials are more expensive from the start,
mention legal safeguards, expert witnesses, and longer jury selection,
connect the evidence back to your claim more clearly.

Retribution paragraph
This paragraph raises an important moral issue: the difference between justice and revenge. However, the argument would be stronger if you defined those terms more carefully. Right now, the paragraph relies heavily on rhetorical questions. Instead, explain:
what justice should accomplish,
why state execution may conflict with respect for life,
whether punishment should focus on accountability rather than retaliation.

Deterrence paragraph
Your reasoning here is promising, especially the point that many murderers do not expect to get caught or act rationally in the moment. However, your explanation should be more formal and precise. Avoid casual wording like “drawing up pros and cons for murder raps.” Replace that with a clearer sentence such as:
Many murders are committed impulsively or under emotional distress, making the threat of execution unlikely to influence behavior.

Work on paragraph coherence
Most paragraphs follow a clear pattern, but transitions between ideas can be smoother. Begin each body paragraph with a strong topic sentence that directly supports your thesis. Then present evidence, explain it, and end by tying it back to the argument.

A useful structure:
Topic sentence  
Evidence  
Explanation/analysis  
Concluding sentence

Revise grammar, spelling, and word choice
There are many mechanical errors that distract from your ideas. A few examples:
“untill” → until
“it’s existence” → its existence
“Ounce society” → Once society
“inprison” → imprison
“recieves” → receives
“midevil” → medieval
“deterent” → deterrent
“murdeers” → murders
“deference” → deterrence
“detramental” → detrimental

Also watch for sentence-level issues such as run-ons, awkward phrasing, and incorrect apostrophes. Careful proofreading will make your argument much stronger.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion summarizes your points, but it could be more powerful if it restated the thesis in a polished way and ended with a broader implication. For example:
Because capital punishment is costly, morally problematic, and ineffective as a deterrent, it should not remain part of a modern justice system.

Final advice
You already have a workable argumentative structure and a strong sense of purpose. For revision, focus on:
creating a sharper thesis,
replacing emotional language with academic wording,
adding credible sources,
expanding your analysis,
proofreading carefully.

With those changes, your essay will become much more persuasive and much more effective as an academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important ethical question—whether physician-assisted suicide should be legalized—and it shows clear engagement with the debate through discussion of euthanasia, Dr. Kevorkian, William F. May, and the Hippocratic Oath. You have a strong topic and some relevant examples, but the paper needs revision to become a convincing argumentative essay.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your essay introduces the issue well, but your main argument is unclear and somewhat contradictory. For example, the conclusion says doctors do not have the moral authority to decide for patients, but also says it is a physician’s option to cooperate with a patient’s decision. This leaves the reader unsure of your actual position.

What to revise:
State a clear thesis at the end of the introduction.
Make sure every body paragraph supports that thesis.
Decide whether you are arguing:
  1. physician-assisted suicide should be legal,
  2. it should not be legal, or
  3. it may be justified under limited conditions.

Example of a clearer thesis:
“Although terminally ill patients deserve compassion and autonomy, physician-assisted suicide should remain limited because it conflicts with medical ethics and gives doctors too much power over life and death.”

Or, if you want the opposite position:
“Physician-assisted suicide should be legal for terminally ill patients because it respects patient autonomy and relieves unbearable suffering when no effective treatment remains.”

Improve organization
The essay contains useful material, but the structure feels more like a list of information than a sustained argument.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: define the issue and present your thesis.
Background: explain forms of euthanasia and distinguish them carefully.
Argument 1: patient suffering/autonomy or medical ethics, depending on your position.
Argument 2: Kevorkian case and legal controversy.
Argument 3: Hippocratic Oath and moral limits of physicians.
Counterargument: address the strongest opposing view.
Conclusion: restate your position and explain why it matters.

Your paragraph on the “three forms of euthanasia” is helpful, but it should connect more directly to the argument. Also, the transition from May’s four forces to Kevorkian is abrupt. Add topic sentences that explain why each paragraph matters.

Develop analysis, not just summary
Much of the essay summarizes sources or events, but argument essays need analysis. For example, you mention May’s four forces, but you do not explain whether you agree with them or how they support your thesis. Similarly, the Kevorkian section gives dates and charges, but not enough interpretation.

Ask yourself after each example:
What does this show?
Why does it matter?
How does it support my claim?

For instance, instead of only stating that Kevorkian’s charges were dismissed, explain what that reveals about legal uncertainty around assisted suicide.

Use sources more effectively
You refer to May and Fletcher, but several quotations are missing and marked with “.” Those gaps seriously weaken the essay. A reader cannot evaluate your evidence if key quotations are absent.

What to revise:
Insert the missing quotations or paraphrase the ideas accurately.
Introduce each source clearly.
Explain the significance of each source after quoting.
Cite consistently in the format your instructor requires.

Also, be careful not to rely too heavily on quotation. Use sources to support your own reasoning.

Strengthen precision and terminology
Some definitions are too broad or imprecise. For example, describing suicide as “the easiest way out” sounds judgmental and weakens academic tone. Also, “euthanasia” and “physician-assisted suicide” are not always the same thing, so you should distinguish them carefully.

Try to define terms more precisely:
Passive euthanasia / withdrawal of life support
Refusal of treatment
Physician-assisted suicide
Active euthanasia

Using accurate terminology will make your argument stronger and more credible.

Revise style and grammar
There are multiple sentence-level issues that distract from your ideas.

Common problems:
Apostrophes: “patient's” should often be patients.
Word choice: “Christains” → Christians; “break throughs” → breakthroughs.
Pronoun agreement: shift between “one,” “his,” and “they.”
Awkward phrasing: “would rather die than to continue to live in pain” could be more concise.
Informal/biased wording: “vegetable” and “easy way out” are not appropriate in formal academic writing.

Example revision:
Original: “When the pain becomes unbearable and the medication stops working some people would rather die than to continue to live in pain.”
Revised: “When pain becomes unbearable and treatment no longer provides relief, some terminally ill patients may prefer death to continued suffering.”

Make the conclusion stronger
Your conclusion introduces an important idea—that doctors should not decide for patients—but it needs to connect more directly to the thesis and summarize the argument. Avoid ending with ambiguity.

Final priorities for revision
Write a clear, debatable thesis.
Fill in the missing quotations/evidence.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one supports the thesis.
Add analysis after each example.
Revise for formal tone, precision, and grammar.

You have a strong foundation and a meaningful topic. With a clearer position and more analytical development, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose and a strong overall position: the wild card is good for baseball. You also offer multiple reasons—more teams in the playoffs, more fan interest, and more competition—which gives the paper a solid structure. The essay shows good knowledge of baseball and uses timely examples from the 1995 season to support your claims. For revision, focus on sharpening the thesis, improving organization, and polishing sentence-level clarity.

What’s working well

Clear stance
You leave no doubt about your position. The opening paragraph states your claim directly, and the conclusion returns to it effectively.

Logical categories of support
Your three main reasons are easy to follow:
more teams/players in the playoffs,
more excitement and fan interest,
more competition.

These are strong argumentative categories for this topic.

Effective use of concrete examples
The discussion of specific teams and players helps make the argument vivid. The Yankees, Rockies, Mariners, Angels, and Reds give the essay real-world grounding.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen and simplify the thesis
Your thesis is present, but the introduction spends a long time explaining playoff realignment before arriving at the argument. Try to separate background from claim more clearly.

For example, your introduction could end with a thesis like:
> Although the wild card was created because of divisional realignment, it improves baseball by expanding postseason access, sustaining fan interest late in the season, and increasing playoff competition.

This version is more concise and previews the body structure.

Improve organization and paragraph focus
Right now, the essay spends too much space explaining playoff mechanics, including rules about home-field advantage. That material is informative, but not all of it helps prove that the wild card is beneficial. Ask yourself: does this paragraph support my argument, or just explain the system?

In particular:
The detailed explanation of the Division Series rules and home-field advantage feels excessive.
The long quoted list of playoff scenarios is interesting evidence, but it should be summarized and analyzed rather than reproduced at length.

A stronger structure would be:
Introduction + thesis
Brief background on old vs. new playoff system
Body paragraph: more teams and more star players
Body paragraph: more excitement/fan interest
Body paragraph: greater competition
Conclusion

Add analysis after evidence
Often you provide evidence, but the explanation of *why it matters* could be stronger. For example, you list many star players who would have missed the playoffs, but the paragraph would be stronger if you connected that more directly to revenue, television interest, or national attention.

Similarly, in the competition paragraph, you compare the Rockies and Reds against the Braves, which is useful. But explain more clearly what that proves:
that non-division winners can still be legitimate contenders,
that the playoff field becomes stronger overall,
and that stronger postseason series benefit fans and the sport.

To make the essay more persuasive, briefly acknowledge an opposing view. Some readers might argue that the wild card rewards teams that did not win their division and weakens the importance of the regular season. You could address that concern and argue that the added excitement and competitive balance outweigh that drawback.

Even one short paragraph or a few sentences would make the argument feel more mature.

Sentence-level clarity
Some sentences are awkward or grammatically incorrect. A few examples:

“The two winners of the division series in each league then meet in second round action.”

“people are fascinated by star athletes as made evident by the use of star athletes…”

Read aloud for missing words and awkward phrasing.

Tone

This weakens your academic credibility. Try for a more neutral tone.

“Andres Galaragga” -> likely “Galarraga”
inconsistent capitalization of division names
unnecessary line breaks and spacing
the “” section disrupts the essay’s presentation

Add a brief counterargument
Proofread for grammar, word choice, and formatting

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear and passionate position: you argue that the media often abuses the public’s “right to know” and damages privacy, fairness, and public trust in the process. Your strongest asset is your engagement with the topic. The reader can tell this issue matters to you, and that conviction gives the essay energy. You also use a range of examples—war coverage, political appointments, the O. J. Simpson trial, and campus journalism—which shows you are trying to build a broad case rather than relying on one incident.

To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your central claim, organizing the argument more logically, and strengthening your evidence.

Clarify the thesis
Right now, your main idea is present, but it is not stated in one precise, arguable sentence early in the essay. The opening starts with a personal anecdote about refusing a newspaper subscription, which is interesting, but it delays the main argument.

Consider ending the introduction with a thesis such as:

“Although the media has an important duty to inform the public, it often goes beyond that duty by invading privacy, sensationalizing events, and prioritizing profit over responsible journalism.”

A thesis like this gives the essay a clear direction and helps unify your later examples.

Improve organization
At present, the essay reads somewhat like a series of frustrations placed one after another. The examples are relevant, but the progression is loose. Try grouping your points into clear body paragraphs by category, not just by example.

For instance, you might organize the essay this way:
Media and national security — examples about Grenada, Desert Storm, and Bosnia.
Media and public figures’ privacy — Surgeon General candidate, Colin Powell.
Media sensationalism and profit — O. J. Simpson trial, tabloid-style reporting.
Consequences — erosion of trust, harm to democracy, invasion of privacy.

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects back to the thesis. That will help the essay feel like a structured argument rather than a list of complaints.

Strengthen evidence and explanation
Your essay relies heavily on assertion and rhetorical questions. These create emotional force, but in an argumentative essay you also need specific evidence and analysis.

For example:
When you say the media endangered troops by broadcasting movements, explain what happened, which outlet did it, and how it affected military operations.
When discussing the Surgeon General candidate, identify the person and explain why the reporting was unfair or disproportionate.
In the O. J. Simpson paragraph, distinguish between legitimate reporting and sensational excess.

In revision, try to follow this pattern:
claim → example → explanation of how the example proves the claim.

Right now, you sometimes give an example but do not fully analyze it.

Address complexity and counterargument
Your essay would become more persuasive if you acknowledged that the media does serve necessary democratic functions. At the moment, the argument can sound one-sided, as if all intrusive reporting is harmful. A strong argumentative essay often anticipates objections.

You might add a paragraph such as:
The public does need information about elected officials or public institutions.
Investigative journalism can expose corruption or abuse.
However, that responsibility should be balanced against privacy, accuracy, and public safety.

Including this kind of counterargument will make your position seem more thoughtful and credible.

Revise tone and style
Your voice is strong, but sometimes the tone becomes overly emotional or hyperbolic. Statements such as predicting cameras and microphones in every home may weaken your credibility because they sound exaggerated. Likewise, rhetorical questions are effective in moderation, but too many can replace analysis.

Aim for a tone that is firm but measured. Let the evidence persuade the reader, not only the intensity of your frustration.

Edit for grammar and mechanics
There are a number of sentence-level issues that need attention:
typo and spelling errors (“successfiil,” “fives,” “hwnburgers,” “dodi,” etc)
punctuation problems with quotation marks and apostrophes
awkward phrasing (“the reason for these things is a personal preference”)
occasional sentence fragments or run-on sentences
inconsistent formatting of names and titles

Also watch for places where wording is unclear or informal. For example, “The past is past. Let it be.” is understandable, but in an academic argument you may want a more formal phrasing.

Opening and conclusion
The personal opening can work, but make sure it quickly connects to the larger issue. Right now it spends too much time on your subscription decision. Shorten that anecdote and transition earlier to the broader argument about media ethics.

Your conclusion does restate your concern, but it could be stronger if it returned to your main claim more directly and ended with a thoughtful call for ethical journalism rather than only frustration.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong topic, clear concern, and passionate voice. The next step is to shape that passion into a more disciplined argument. As you revise, focus on:
stating a precise thesis
organizing examples into clear categories
using specific evidence
explaining how each example supports your claim
adopting a more balanced, academic tone
proofreading carefully

With those changes, your essay can become much more convincing and polished.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you believe marijuana should be legalized. That strong stance gives your paper energy, and your personal voice makes it engaging. You also raise several possible lines of argument—medical use, hypocrisy in law enforcement, economic motives, and changing public attitudes. These are good starting points. To revise successfully, focus on building a more credible, organized, and evidence-based argument.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your main claim appears early (“I think it should be legalized”), but it is too broad. A strong argumentative thesis should state what you believe and why.

For example, you might revise it to something like:
*Marijuana should be legalized because it has medical benefits, is less harmful than many illegal drugs, and current laws encourage hypocrisy and unfair enforcement.*

This gives your paper direction and helps readers know what your main reasons will be.

Improve organization
The essay currently moves from childhood memories, to politicians, to Europe, to a conversation with a Jamaican man, to police busts, to celebrities. Because of this, your ideas feel scattered.

Try organizing the essay into clear body paragraphs, each focused on one reason:
Medical and practical benefits of marijuana
Problems with current laws and enforcement
Public hypocrisy and changing attitudes toward marijuana

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and then give evidence and explanation. This structure will make your argument easier to follow.

Replace personal anecdotes with stronger evidence
Personal experience can help create interest, but it cannot serve as your main proof. Statements such as “I have friends who do” or “former classmates lit up before study session and aced their tests” are not reliable evidence in an academic argument.

Also, some claims are too speculative, such as:
government officials keep weed illegal for “their cut”
politicians use drugs themselves
legalization would stop the government from profiting from busts

These claims may sound forceful, but without evidence they weaken your credibility.

Instead, use research-based support:
medical studies on marijuana’s effects
statistics on arrests and incarceration
examples of legalization policies in specific states or countries
expert opinions from doctors, policy analysts, or legal scholars

Be careful with factual accuracy
Several claims need verification or more precise wording. For example:
“in european countries you can buy weed like you buy a pack of cigarettes” is too general and not accurate for all of Europe
“It is highly non habit forming” is too absolute
“there are nearly no long lasting side effects” is also too broad

In academic writing, avoid sweeping statements unless you can prove them. Use more careful language:
*Some studies suggest…*
*In certain places…*
*Compared with other drugs…*
*Marijuana has been used medically for…*

Precision will make your paper more persuasive.

Develop a more formal tone
Your paper has a conversational style, which makes it lively, but some phrases are too informal for an academic essay. Examples include:
“talking out of his head”
“mega bucks”
“light up”
“weed”

Depending on your instructor’s expectations, you may want to use more formal terms such as marijuana or cannabis, and avoid slang. You can still keep your voice, but aim for a tone that sounds thoughtful and credible.

Address the other side
A strong argument acknowledges counterarguments. Right now, the essay mainly dismisses anti-marijuana views without engaging them seriously.

Consider adding a paragraph like:
Some people oppose legalization because they worry about addiction, impaired driving, or youth access.
Then respond by explaining how regulation, age limits, and public education could address those concerns.

This will show maturity and strengthen your argument.

Revise grammar and sentence clarity
There are many sentence-level issues that distract from your ideas:
run-on sentences
comma splices
incorrect word choices (“their” instead of “there”)
capitalization errors (“european,” “Columbian” should be “Colombian”)
spelling errors (“critisize,” “c ession”)

A few examples:
“Although, that is the most political stance…” → “Although that may be the most politically acceptable stance…”
“Then one day I a conversation…” → “Then one day I had a conversation…”
“their are nearly no long lasting side effects” → “there are few documented long-term side effects” (if supported by evidence)

Read the essay aloud slowly, or have someone help you proofread. That will help you catch awkward or incomplete sentences.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your ending relies on a Bob Marley quote, which gives personality, but it does not fully summarize or reinforce your argument. A stronger conclusion should:
restate your thesis
summarize your main reasons
leave the reader with a final thought about policy or fairness

For example:

Final advice
Your essay’s biggest strengths are its strong opinion and authentic voice. Your biggest areas for revision are organization, evidence, and formal academic style. Keep your passion, but support it with clear structure and reliable facts. If you do that, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of protecting journalists’ confidential sources, and it shows strong engagement with the issue. You use historical background, legal examples, and well-known cases to support your argument. The paper’s main strength is that it consistently returns to the central idea that trust between reporter and source is essential. For revision, focus on sharpening your thesis, improving organization, and making your reasoning more precise and formal.

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your main claim is present, but it is spread across several opening paragraphs and becomes strongest only near the end. Try stating one focused thesis at the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Shield laws should strongly protect journalists from being forced to reveal confidential sources because source confidentiality supports investigative reporting, serves the public’s right to know, and preserves press independence even when courts or prosecutors seek disclosure.*

This gives your essay a clear roadmap.

Improve organization
The essay includes useful material, but the structure feels somewhat list-like. You move from Maryland history, to South Carolina law, to Ohio, Watergate, Boston, California, and then back to general claims. Instead, group points by purpose.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction: explain the issue and present thesis  
Why confidentiality matters: trust, public information, investigative reporting  
History and purpose of shield laws: Maryland, development in states  
Limits and challenges: South Carolina case, court orders, subpoenas  
Examples showing the stakes: Watergate, Swasy, Serrano  
Counterargument and response: courts need evidence, but broad forced disclosure harms the public  
Conclusion: restate significance

This would help the reader follow your argument more easily.

Develop analysis, not just examples
You provide many examples, but sometimes you summarize events without explaining how they prove your argument. After each example, add 2–3 sentences of analysis.

For instance, after the Twila Decker case, explain:
why the case reveals weaknesses in shield laws,
how judicial power can override source protection,
why this matters beyond one reporter.

Make sure each example directly supports a claim.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the opposing side. Your paper suggests that courts and police should not pressure reporters, but it does not fully engage with why judges might require disclosure. Add a paragraph recognizing that:
courts may need information to enforce orders,
criminal investigations sometimes involve urgent public interests,
confidentiality is not always absolute.

Then explain why, despite these concerns, compelled disclosure should remain limited. This will make your argument more persuasive and academically balanced.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some sentences sound conversational or overly absolute. For example:
“The journalist’s job is to get the story no matter what.”
“If the justice system were better…”
“Don’t make people scared to talk.”

These weaken the academic tone. Replace them with more precise phrasing, such as:
*Journalists rely on confidential sources to report matters of public importance.*
*Frequent subpoenas may discourage whistleblowers from coming forward.*
*Excessive judicial pressure can undermine the press’s watchdog role.*

Also avoid emotional claims unless supported by evidence.

Revise for clarity and correctness
There are several sentence-level issues to correct:
punctuation errors,
repeated commas,
awkward quotation integration,
inconsistent capitalization,
spacing problems,
some unclear wording.

Examples:
“especially in a courtroom setting has always been a problem” → add commas or revise for flow.
“In a very recent example,,” → remove double comma.
“deep Throat” → Deep Throat
“reporters right” → reporter’s right
“U.S. faced almost 4,500 subpoenas” → clarify who in the U.S. faced them.

Also, avoid overusing “he/she.” You can write “reporters… they” or “journalists… they.”

Integrate sources more smoothly
You do cite sources, which is good, but sometimes quotations are dropped in without introduction or explanation. Try using signal phrases and follow-up commentary.

For example:
Instead of placing a long legal passage in full, summarize most of it and quote only the key language. Long block quotations can overwhelm your own voice. In an argumentative essay, your analysis should remain central.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion is passionate, but it becomes slogan-like: “Protect the Shield Laws.” Instead, end by reinforcing your argument in a mature academic way. For example:
summarize the importance of source confidentiality,
emphasize the public interest in investigative reporting,
note that weak protections endanger not only journalists but also democratic accountability.

Final revision priorities
Write a sharper thesis.  
Reorganize by argument rather than by a series of cases.  
Add analysis after each example.  
Include and answer counterarguments.  
Edit for formal tone, grammar, and citation clarity.

You have a strong topic and a clear sense of conviction. With better structure and deeper analysis, this essay could become a much more persuasive academic argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position in favor of admitting women to The Citadel, and you give several reasons: equality, public funding, educational access, and preparation for real life in coeducational workplaces. These are strong starting points for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on organization, clarity of argument, development of evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen your thesis
Right now, your introduction presents the issue, but your main claim is not stated sharply enough. A stronger thesis should clearly say what you believe and preview your reasons.

Current idea: women should be allowed to attend The Citadel.  
Possible revision:  
*Women should be admitted to The Citadel because it is a publicly funded institution, equal opportunity should apply to both men and women, and claims that women would weaken or disrupt the school are not convincing.*

This gives the essay a stronger direction.

Improve organization
Your body paragraphs contain good points, but some ideas run together. Try using one main reason per paragraph.

A possible structure:
Introduction: introduce the debate and state your thesis.
Paragraph 1: tradition is not a sufficient reason to exclude women.
Paragraph 2: not all men meet the physical demands either, so women should at least have the opportunity to try.
Paragraph 3: because The Citadel is publicly funded, excluding women is unfair and inconsistent with equal access.
Paragraph 4: coeducation does not automatically create distraction; schools and workplaces already function this way.
Conclusion: restate your argument and explain why the issue matters.

This structure will make your essay easier to follow.

Develop your evidence
Your essay relies mostly on opinion and general statements. Argumentative writing becomes much stronger when you include specific evidence, such as:
legal principles about public institutions and discrimination
examples of other military or public colleges that admit women
facts showing women succeed in demanding academic or military settings
court cases or policy decisions related to The Citadel

For example, instead of saying, *“There is not a problem with it there,”* you could explain that many coeducational colleges operate successfully without loss of academic quality or discipline.

Make your reasoning more precise
Some of your ideas are persuasive, but they need more careful wording.

For example:
*“traditions are meant to be broken”* is a strong phrase, but it may sound too absolute. A more academic version would be:  
  *Tradition alone is not a good reason to continue an unfair policy.*
*“By having the men separate you are teaching them that women are less superior.”*  
  This phrase is confusing because “less superior” is awkward. You likely mean “inferior.”  
  Better version: *Separating men from women can suggest that women are less capable or less worthy of the same opportunities.*

Also, be careful with comparisons to slavery and other historical injustices. These comparisons can be powerful, but they need to be used thoughtfully and explained clearly.

Address opposing arguments more fully
You mention the main opposing claims: tradition, physical weakness, and distraction. That is a good strategy. To improve, respond to each one more directly and logically.

For example:
Opposing claim: women cannot handle the physical demands.  
Response: admission standards should be applied equally; those who can meet them, regardless of gender, should be allowed to attend.

This kind of rebuttal makes your argument stronger and more balanced.

Revise for grammar, spelling, and word choice
There are many sentence-level errors that make the essay harder to read. Be sure to proofread carefully. Here are some examples:

*“appearred”* → appeared
*“That questions is should women...”* → The question is whether women should...
*“There has been many objections”* → There have been many objections
*“as a women”* → as a woman
*“publically/publically funded”* → publicly funded
*“desciniinate”* → discriminate
*“pubfically”* → publicly
*“eqaul”* → equal
*“Every deserves”* → Everyone deserves
*“learening”* → learning

Also watch for run-on sentences and comma splices. For instance:

Original:  
*That may be true in some cases, but others might be able to handle the challenges, Everyone does deserve the chance to try.*

Revised:  
*That may be true in some cases, but some women might be able to handle the challenges. Everyone deserves the chance to try.*

Add a conclusion
Your essay ends suddenly. A conclusion should restate your main point and leave the reader with a final thought.

Possible conclusion idea:  
*In the end, The Citadel should judge applicants by ability and commitment, not by gender. As a publicly funded institution, it should offer equal opportunity to all qualified students.*

Final suggestion
You have a solid argument and a clear sense of fairness. Your next step is to turn that strong opinion into a more polished academic essay by improving structure, adding evidence, and editing carefully. The ideas are here; now focus on presenting them more clearly and convincingly.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and passionate position: a stay-at-home wife should be treated as an equal partner in marriage and should share ownership of family property. That central claim is strong, relevant, and arguable, which gives your paper a solid foundation. You also show commitment to the topic, and your concern for fairness comes through clearly.

What is working well

Clear stance
From the opening, your essay makes its position obvious. Readers know exactly what you are arguing.

Moral urgency
You effectively emphasize that unpaid domestic labor has value and that respect within marriage matters. This gives the essay emotional force.

Variety of support
You use several kinds of evidence: a TV example, a personal family example, hypothetical financial reasoning, and social observations. This shows you are trying to support your argument from multiple angles.

Main revision priorities

Strengthen the thesis
Right now, your thesis is somewhat spread across several sentences. Try to combine your main points into one focused claim near the end of the introduction.

For example:
> In marriages where the wife stays home by mutual agreement, she should be regarded as an equal partner because domestic labor is valuable, the decision is typically shared, and both spouses should have equal claim to family property.

This gives your essay a roadmap and makes the structure easier to follow.

Improve organization
The essay repeats similar ideas in several places, especially that the wife is not inferior and that the husband should respect her. Try organizing the body into clear sections, with one main point per paragraph.

A possible structure:
Domestic labor has real economic and emotional value
Staying home is often a mutual marital decision
Equal partnership should include legal/shared ownership
Outsiders unfairly judge stay-at-home wives

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and stay focused on that single idea.

Use stronger evidence
Many of your examples are personal, hypothetical, or based on television. These can help, but they are not as persuasive as more concrete evidence. For revision, consider adding:
statistics about unpaid household labor
examples of childcare or housekeeping costs
legal or financial reasons for shared ownership in marriage

Also, some quotations are marked with , which leaves parts of your evidence incomplete. Be sure to replace or remove those sections.

Address audience and tone
At times, the essay uses informal or stereotypical phrasing, such as “watch the soaps,” “the guy,” or “best of both worlds.” These weaken your credibility and may unintentionally oversimplify the lives of stay-at-home wives.

Also, some sentences assume traditional gender roles without questioning them. If you want a stronger academic argument, focus less on “wives” specifically and more on spouses who perform unpaid domestic labor. Even if you keep the current focus, use more precise and respectful phrasing.

Clarify and qualify claims
Some statements are too absolute, such as “nine times out of ten the marriage wouldn’t have taken place.” Unless you can prove a claim like that, it is better to soften it:
> In many cases, couples discuss work and family expectations before marriage.

This sounds more reasonable and persuasive.

Style and sentence-level issues

Grammar and wording
There are frequent errors with:
missing spaces after commas
“stay home” vs. “stay-at-home”
“can not” → “cannot”
“loose” → “lose”
“newly wed” → “newlywed”
sentence fragments and run-on sentences

Example:
> “Taking care of a house just as well as taking care of an income are both sources of income.”
This is unclear. You might revise it to:
> Managing a household provides economic value, even if it does not produce a paycheck.

Word choice
Some phrases are awkward or imprecise:
“fixing on an automobile”
“marriage was entered into”
“the newly acquired husband”

Choose simpler, more direct language.

Punctuation and formatting
Watch quotation marks, apostrophes, and paragraph breaks. Several places have stray punctuation marks or inconsistent formatting, which distracts from your ideas.

Content suggestions for revision

Explain more fully why unpaid domestic labor counts as labor.
Develop the point about legal ownership—this is one of your strongest and most original ideas.
Consider one counterargument: for example, some people may argue that the wage earner contributes more financially. Then explain why financial income is not the only meaningful contribution in a marriage.
Revise the conclusion so it does more than repeat earlier points. End by reinforcing the broader principle of equality, respect, and shared responsibility.

Final thoughts

This essay has a strong core argument and genuine conviction. Your biggest task in revision is to make the paper more focused, organized, and academically persuasive. If you tighten the thesis, reduce repetition, use clearer paragraph structure, and replace weak examples with stronger evidence, your argument will become much more effective.

If you revise carefully, this could become a thoughtful and compelling essay about equality, labor, and respect within marriage.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important public-policy issue and shows clear engagement with the debate over marijuana legalization. You also attempt to consider history, health effects, and social consequences, which gives the paper a broad scope. For revision, the main priorities are: clarifying your argument, improving organization, strengthening evidence, and correcting style/mechanics.

Clarify your thesis and purpose
At the start, you say the essay is not meant to convince the reader, but to “inform you on all the facts.” However, the conclusion clearly argues that “there can only be one logical answer,” which is persuasive. This creates a contradiction.

What to revise:
Decide whether this is an informative essay or an argumentative essay.  
Since the paper clearly takes a side, write a direct thesis such as:  
  “Marijuana should be legalized because its medical uses are recognized, its harms are less severe than those of legal substances like alcohol and tobacco, and legalization could reduce crime and increase tax revenue.”

A clear thesis will help the whole essay feel more focused.

Improve organization and paragraph structure
Right now, the essay moves through several topics, but the structure is loose. Some paragraphs begin with broad statements (“The next step…”) rather than making a specific claim. A stronger argumentative essay should have body paragraphs centered on reasons that support the thesis.

Possible structure:
Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Medical and historical context  
Health effects and comparison with alcohol/tobacco  
Crime and black-market consequences of prohibition  
Economic/social benefits of legalization  
Counterargument: explain concerns about legalization and respond to them  
Conclusion

Also, make sure each paragraph starts with a topic sentence that states the paragraph’s main point.

Use stronger and more accurate evidence
You include statistics and quotations, which is good, but many claims need clearer sourcing or more careful wording. Some statements are too absolute, such as “NO ONE has ever died from marijuana.” Even if your point is that overdose deaths are extremely rare, an absolute claim can weaken your credibility if readers question it.

What to revise:
Provide reliable, current sources for statistics and quotations.
Explain the source’s relevance.
Avoid unsupported generalizations like “any doctor will inform you.”
Be careful with factual accuracy. For example:
  - The FDA does not “prescribe”; doctors prescribe medications.
  - Some claims about marijuana cigarettes, glaucoma, or DEA statements should be checked carefully.
  - “More than 400,000 people are arrested each year” needs a source and date.

Try using evidence in this format: claim + source + explanation.

Develop analysis, not just lists of facts
Several paragraphs list effects or facts but do not explain how they support legalization. For example, the discussion of short-term and long-term effects is useful, but you need to connect it to your argument.

Ask yourself after each piece of evidence:  
What does this show?  
Why does it matter?  
How does it support legalization?

For instance, if marijuana has risks but they are comparable to or lower than those of legal substances, explain why that comparison matters for public policy.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge opposing views. Your essay briefly mentions that some people may want marijuana to remain illegal, but it does not fully address their concerns.

Possible counterarguments to address:
Marijuana can impair coordination and judgment.
Legalization might increase youth access.
Long-term use may affect memory or mental health in some users.

Then respond by explaining regulation, age restrictions, public-health education, or comparisons to current prohibition.

Revise tone and style
Some sentences sound informal or conversational, such as “look at what generation is office now” and “ask yourself, how different is it?” These make the essay sound less academic.

Aim for a more formal tone:
Replace rhetorical questions with analytical statements.
Avoid direct address (“you”) unless your instructor permits it.
Use precise vocabulary: for example, “reduce” instead of “elevate” crime.

Correct grammar, usage, and mechanics
There are many sentence-level issues that distract from your ideas. These include:
comma splices
awkward phrasing
capitalization errors (“Marijuana” does not need to be capitalized mid-sentence)
spelling errors (“government,” “sufferers,” “Psychology,” “amount,” “in office”)
possessive error: “it’s purpose” should be “its purpose”
unclear or incomplete sentences, such as:  
  “The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States.”

Example revision:  
Original: “The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States.”  
Revised: “One important factor in forming a position on marijuana is its history in the United States.”

Final advice
Your essay has a clear interest in the subject and the foundation of a persuasive position. To improve it, focus on:
writing a clear argumentative thesis,
organizing body paragraphs around reasons,
verifying and citing evidence carefully,
explaining how each fact supports your claim, and
editing thoroughly for grammar and academic tone.

With revision, this could become a much stronger and more convincing argument.

media beauty ideals,

What’s working well

Clear stance
   You strongly argue that banning books harms education and freedom of expression.

Use of examples
   Referring to *Ms.* magazine, *Cary v. Board of Education*, Madonna’s *Sex*, and Maya Angelou gives the essay concrete evidence.

Sense of urgency
   Your voice shows commitment to the issue, which helps engage the reader.

Improve organization

Make sure names, dates, and organizations are accurate.
Distinguish between a challenge, a removal, and a ban if possible.

> These numbers suggest that censorship was not isolated, but part of a widespread national effort to restrict access to reading materials.

Madonna’s *Sex* is a book, not a novel.

Some phrases are emotionally powerful, but they weaken your authority in an academic essay because they sound conversational or exaggerated.

For example:
“try to stop the world for turning”
“burning it to ashes”
“they will see it”

This keeps your argument strong while sounding more credible.

> Supporters of book removal often argue that schools should protect students from material they consider sexually explicit, offensive, or morally harmful.

This will make your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Spelling: “fireedom,” “Gutenburg,” “bum,” “curriculwn,” “baimed”

Word choice: “provided with the authority” could become “given the authority”

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay addresses a meaningful and controversial topic and shows that you have gathered a range of sources. You include polls, quotations, and historical evidence, which is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. The main area for revision is not effort or topic choice, but clarity of argument, organization, and use of evidence.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your essay presents many viewpoints, but your own position is not clearly stated until the end, and even then it remains vague. An argumentative essay needs a specific, debatable thesis early in the introduction.

For example, your final claim seems to be that the Confederate flag should remain because it represents heritage and removing it would create more conflict. If that is your position, say so directly in the introduction.

A stronger thesis might look like:
Although the Confederate flag is offensive to many Americans because of its connection to slavery, it should not be removed from all public memory because it also represents regional heritage and historical ancestry.

Or, if you want to argue the opposite, make that equally clear. The key is to choose one position and organize the essay around defending it.

Improve organization
At the moment, the essay reads more like a collection of quotations than a structured argument. Try using this pattern:

Introduction
Brief historical background
Present the current controversy
End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Heritage argument
Explain why some people see the flag as heritage
Use one or two quotes as support
Explain how the evidence supports your claim

Body Paragraph 2: Racism/slavery argument
Acknowledge the opposing side fairly
Use Alexander Stephens’s speech and other evidence
Explain why this evidence matters

Body Paragraph 3: Public opinion/polls
Present polling data carefully
Analyze what it suggests
Be cautious about overgeneralizing

Body Paragraph 4: Your rebuttal
Address the strongest opposing argument
Explain why your position is still more convincing

Conclusion
Restate your thesis
Summarize your strongest reasons
End with a thoughtful final point, not just “there will always be controversy”

Analyze evidence instead of stacking quotations
You rely heavily on long quotations. In college-level writing, quotations should support your analysis, not replace it. After each quote, explain:
Who is speaking?
Why is this source credible or important?
How does the quote support your argument?
Are there limitations to the evidence?

For example, the Stephens quote is one of your strongest pieces of evidence because it comes from a Confederate leader and directly connects the Confederacy to white supremacy. But you need to explain its significance, not just include it.

Address contradictions in your argument
One issue is that some of your evidence works against your apparent conclusion. For instance, the Alexander Stephens quote strongly suggests that the Confederacy was explicitly founded on racial inequality. If you argue that the flag is only about heritage, you must directly respond to this evidence. Ignoring it weakens your credibility.

Similarly, the South Carolina polling paragraph needs more careful interpretation. If only 40% wanted the flag to remain on the dome, that does not strongly support keeping it there. Be honest about what your evidence shows.

Strengthen tone and fairness
Try to avoid emotionally vague statements such as:
“There is no reason to fight over something that was established so many years ago.”
This dismisses the real concerns of people who see the flag as tied to slavery and racism.

A stronger academic tone would acknowledge complexity:
Because the Confederate flag carries both historical and painful racial meanings, debates over its public display are unlikely to disappear.

This sounds more thoughtful and persuasive.

Revise sentence-level issues
There are many grammar, spelling, and formatting errors that make the essay harder to read. Examples include:
“fory” → forty
“siad” → said
“an the Capital grounds” → on the Capitol grounds
“unacceptabe” → unacceptable
“united Daughter” → United Daughters
inconsistent capitalization of Confederate, Senate, State House, etc

Also watch for:
comma splices
missing quotation marks
awkward phrasing
incorrect punctuation with citations

Proofread carefully, and if possible, read the essay aloud.

Use citations consistently
Your citations are incomplete and inconsistent. Some include page numbers, some do not, and some source names are unclear (“USA,” “Davis,” “Riley”). Follow the citation style your instructor requires and include a Works Cited or References page if needed.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Reorganize the essay by argument, not by source.
Cut down long quotations and add more of your own analysis.
Address opposing evidence honestly.
Correct grammar, spelling, and citation problems.

You have strong source material and a topic with real complexity. The next step is to shape that material into a focused argument led by your own reasoning.
