Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that frivolous lawsuits waste public money and that lawmakers should reform the civil court system. The paper also ends with specific proposals, which is a strength. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: argument development, evidence, and tone/style.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Right now, your main claim is present, but it is somewhat spread across the introduction. A stronger essay would state the argument in one precise sentence near the end of the first paragraph.

For example, you might revise the thesis to say something like:

“Lawmakers should reduce frivolous civil lawsuits because they waste taxpayer money, delay legitimate cases, and increase costs for workers, schools, and insurance customers.”

This kind of thesis gives your essay a clear roadmap.

Organize by reasons, not by stream of examples
The essay currently moves quickly from one example to another: inmate lawsuits, fake accidents, workers’ compensation, schools, police, criminal courts, and insurance companies. This creates energy, but it can also feel scattered.

Try reorganizing into clear body paragraphs, each centered on one main reason:
Frivolous lawsuits waste public resources
They increase costs for businesses and consumers
They delay legitimate civil and criminal cases
Reform is needed

Then place examples under each reason. This structure will make the argument easier to follow.

Use stronger evidence
Much of the essay relies on generalizations such as “everybody is suing everybody” or “I heard of a case last week.” These statements sound conversational, but they are not persuasive in academic argument unless supported by evidence.

To strengthen your credibility:
Replace anecdotes with statistics, studies, or documented cases
Define what counts as a “frivolous” lawsuit
Distinguish between legitimate claims and abusive claims

For example, instead of saying inmates sue over “everything you can imagine,” you could cite data on how many prisoner lawsuits are filed annually and how many are dismissed. Instead of saying accident scams cost “millions,” provide a source.

Ask yourself:
How common are frivolous suits really?
What percentage of court costs come from such cases?
What evidence shows they delay other cases?

Without evidence, readers may see the paper as opinion-driven rather than well argued.

Avoid overgeneralization and unsupported assumptions
Some claims are too broad:
“Seems like everybody is suing everybody these days.”
“Lawsuits seem to have become a way for a lot of people to make a living.”
“Students… are not properly disciplined because teachers and principals are afraid of being sued.”

These statements may contain some truth, but they need proof and qualification. Consider using language such as:
“Some critics argue…”
“In certain cases…”
“One consequence may be…”

This makes your argument sound more careful and thoughtful.

Improve tone for an academic audience
Your essay has a strong voice, but parts of it sound informal, emotional, or dismissive. Phrases like “ridiculous cases,” “begging for people to call them,” and “their time should be otherwise occupied” may weaken your credibility because they suggest bias rather than analysis.

Aim for a more formal tone:
Replace “ridiculous” with “meritless” or “frivolous”
Replace “everybody is suing everybody” with a more precise claim
Replace “you and I” with “consumers” or “the public”

An academic argument is often more persuasive when it sounds measured.

Consider counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the other side. Right now, the essay assumes that reducing lawsuits is obviously good. But some readers may argue that civil lawsuits:
protect injured people
hold corporations and institutions accountable
safeguard constitutional rights, even for prisoners

You do not need to agree, but you should respond. For example, you might say that while access to the courts is essential, reforms should target only clearly frivolous cases, not valid claims. This will make your argument more balanced and sophisticated.

Sentence-level editing and proofreading
The draft contains many spelling and typing errors, including:
“govermnent”
“taxpayers dollars” → taxpayers’ dollars
“corporation of lawyers” → cooperation of lawyers
“payoff” → usually pay off as a verb
“then” → than in comparisons
“ffivolous” → frivolous

There are also several unclear phrases caused by typing or scanning errors. Carefully proofread the essay so readers can focus on your ideas.

You should also revise some punctuation and sentence structure. For example, several short sentences in a row create a choppy effect. Try combining related ideas to improve flow.

Revise the conclusion
Your conclusion contains useful policy suggestions, but it would be even stronger if it first restated the core argument and then presented the reforms as a logical result.

For example:
Restate the problem
Summarize the harms
End with the reforms you recommend

Revision priorities
Write a clear one-sentence thesis
Reorganize body paragraphs by major reasons
Add credible evidence and definitions
Replace emotional language with formal academic wording
Address at least one counterargument
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and clarity

You have a strong topic and a clear opinion. With better organization, evidence, and a more formal tone, this essay could become a much more persuasive argument.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0017.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and a strong sense of urgency. You argue that partisan conflict caused the 1995 government shutdown and harmed ordinary Americans, and that central claim comes through consistently. You also use a real historical event to support your position, which gives the essay relevance and seriousness. For revision, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument, organization, and style/mechanics.

Thesis and argument

Your essay’s main claim is understandable, but it needs to be stated more precisely. Right now, the opening relies on a Jefferson quotation and then moves into the shutdown. A stronger thesis would directly state your argument in your own words. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*The 1995 government shutdown shows how intense partisanship can damage the functioning of American democracy because it prevents compromise, disrupts public services, and hurts citizens who depend on the government.*

This would help your essay sound more analytical and less like a personal reaction.

What is working:
You clearly blame partisan conflict for the shutdown.
You try to explain why the issue matters to the public, not just politicians.

What to improve:
Avoid making claims that are too broad or unsupported, such as “This could cause a depression.” That is a very large economic claim and would need strong evidence.
Be careful with absolute statements like “Neither side is right” or “political parties eliminate stability.” These can be persuasive, but they need more explanation and evidence.
Your argument would be stronger if you acknowledged complexity. For example, instead of saying parties should not exist at all, you could argue that extreme partisanship is the problem.

Organization

The essay has useful information, but the order sometimes feels uneven. Some paragraphs explain the budget process, some describe the shutdown’s effects, and others return to the broader issue of political parties. Try organizing the essay around clear points.

A possible structure:
Introduction
   - Introduce the issue of partisanship
   - Briefly mention the 1995 shutdown
   - End with a clear thesis

Background
   - Explain the fiscal year and budget deadline
   - Briefly explain what caused the conflict in 1995

Main argument: how partisanship caused the shutdown
   - Clinton’s proposal
   - Congress’s proposal
   - Refusal to compromise

Effects on Americans
   - Furloughed workers
   - Closed services and public institutions
   - Economic and social consequences

Conclusion
   - Reaffirm argument
   - Explain why compromise is necessary in democracy

This structure would help readers follow your reasoning more easily.

Evidence and analysis

You include many specific details, such as dates, numbers of workers, and examples from South Carolina. That is a real strength. However, some points need more explanation. Don’t just present facts—show how they support your argument.

For example, after listing closed services like museums, passports, and veterans’ benefits, explain why that matters:
What does this reveal about government responsibility?
How does it show the consequences of political conflict?

Also, check factual accuracy carefully. Some historical details may need verification, especially dates and claims about the first shutdown. Accurate evidence is essential in an argumentative essay.

Tone and style

Your voice is passionate, which helps the essay feel engaged. However, some wording is too informal for academic argument, such as:
“like two kids fighting on a playground”
“So who’s right?”
“I say no.”

A more formal tone would make your argument stronger. Instead of conversational questions, try declarative analysis. For example:

Informal: *Both sides were stubborn like two kids fighting on a playground.*
Formal: *Both parties prioritized political advantage over compromise, which prolonged the shutdown and intensified its consequences.*

Also, limit repeated phrases like “I feel.” In argumentative writing, confidence is stronger than personal phrasing. Instead of “I feel the American people…,” write “The American people were unfairly forced to bear the consequences…”

Grammar, mechanics, and sentence clarity

This is the area that most needs revision. There are frequent errors in punctuation, capitalization, spelling, and sentence structure. These errors sometimes distract from your ideas.

Common issues:
Sentence fragments/run-ons  
  Example: “This event is the shutdown of the United States government and it will not end until government officials. decide to work together...”  
  Remove the period after “officials.”

Capitalization  
  “united States,” “republicans,” “democrats,” and “constitution” should be checked carefully.

Spelling
  Examples: “govermnent,” “figthing,” “effect” vs. “affect,” “Thurrnond.”

Word choice/grammar
  “representatives of the people will be divided” is awkward; consider “elected representatives will become divided.”

Punctuation and spacing
  Several dates and quoted passages use incorrect commas, hyphens, or spacing.

Final advice

Your essay has a strong topic, a clear position, and useful historical details. The next step is to turn that passion into a more controlled academic argument. Focus on:
Writing a sharper thesis in your own words  
Reorganizing paragraphs around clear claims  
Explaining your evidence more fully  
Adopting a more formal tone  
Carefully editing grammar and mechanics

With revision, this essay could become a much stronger and more persuasive argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: abortion should remain legal, and individuals should retain the right to choose. You also attempt to support your claim with several arguments, including rape, poverty, unsafe illegal abortions, and government overreach. These are strong areas to build from. For revision, focus on three main priorities: clarifying your thesis, improving organization and evidence, and correcting sentence-level errors.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis
Your introduction establishes the topic, but the thesis is weakened by unclear wording: “Hapeo povertyg personal choice and illegal abortions…” appears to contain typing errors, so the reader cannot fully understand your roadmap.

Try revising the thesis so it clearly states both your position and your main reasons. For example:

Possible revision:  
*Abortion should remain legal because forcing people to continue unwanted pregnancies can deepen trauma, disproportionately harm those in poverty, and drive women toward dangerous illegal procedures.*

This version is specific and gives your essay a structure to follow.

Improve organization and paragraph development
Right now, the essay reads as a list of points rather than a fully developed argument. A stronger structure would help:

Introduction: present the issue and thesis
Body Paragraph 1: abortion in cases of rape/assault
Body Paragraph 2: economic hardship and poverty
Body Paragraph 3: dangers of illegal abortion
Body Paragraph 4: legal rights/government interference
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why it matters

Each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence and then provide explanation and evidence. For instance, in the rape paragraph, you ask emotional questions (“Would [you] be able to love him or her?”), which show feeling, but the paragraph needs more analytical support. Explain why access to abortion may matter to survivors and support that with reliable evidence.

Use evidence carefully and accurately
Several claims need support or are presented in ways that may sound insensitive or unconvincing.

Claims that need evidence:
“One in four women experience rape or attempted rape…”
“Most unwanted pregnancies occur in poverty stricken areas…”
“Lower income families also tend to have five children on average…”
“The average cost to support a child is one hundred twenty-five thousand dollars…”
“Infant mortality rates would sky rocket…”

If you use statistics, you should cite credible sources. Also, make sure the numbers are accurate and current.

Be careful with ethically problematic reasoning
This sentence is especially troubling:

> “By offering abortion to the lower income people you are helping to maintain a balance…”

This suggests abortion is useful for controlling the number of poor people, which raises serious ethical concerns and could alienate readers. If your point is that people in poverty may need more reproductive autonomy because raising a child is financially difficult, say that directly and respectfully.

For example:

*People living in poverty are often affected most by limited access to reproductive healthcare, so preserving abortion rights helps protect their ability to make decisions about their futures.*

Strengthen legal and factual accuracy
You mention “Rowe versus Way,” but the correct case is Roe v. Wade. This kind of error hurts credibility. Also, your legal point could be more precise. Instead of saying women “won this right,” explain that the case recognized a constitutional protection for abortion access for many years, though abortion law has changed significantly in recent years. If your assignment allows it, you may want to acknowledge those changes.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some sentences rely heavily on rhetorical questions or emotionally charged phrasing. Emotional appeal can be effective, but in an argumentative essay, it should be balanced with reasoned analysis.

For example:
“Would [you] be able to love him or her?” → too personal and speculative
“primitive tool like a coat hanger” → vivid, but should be paired with evidence about unsafe abortion risks

Aim for a tone that is persuasive but measured.

Revise grammar, punctuation, and wording
There are many sentence-level issues that make the essay difficult to follow. Examples include:

“today�s” → likely an encoding error; should be “today’s”
“being force to give birth” → “being forced to give birth”
“Would be able to love him or her?” → “Would you be able to love him or her?”
“they won this right” → “They won this right”
“if the remove this right” → “if they remove this right”
“practice’?” → remove incorrect punctuation
“sky racket alsop” → “skyrocket also”
“eightteen” → “eighteen”

Also correct capitalization, especially at the start of sentences (“they” should be “They”).

Add a conclusion
The essay ends abruptly. A conclusion should not introduce new evidence; instead, it should reinforce your main argument. For example:

*Ultimately, abortion should remain a legal choice because reproductive freedom protects bodily autonomy, reduces harm, and allows individuals to make deeply personal decisions without government coercion.*

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis clearly.  
Reorganize the essay into focused body paragraphs.  
Replace weak or problematic claims with accurate, cited evidence.  
Correct factual errors like Roe v. Wade.  
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and clarity.

You have a clear stance and several relevant ideas. With better organization, stronger evidence, and more careful phrasing, this essay could become much more persuasive.
